# A House of Graces

*A Novel of Rome* — **Trajan's Rome, AD 111–112**

> A freedwoman who built a house is summoned by the family that sold her, and the past she has spent twenty-four years paying for comes due in the Senate of Rome.

## Chapter One

The house woke before its mistress, as houses do. By the fourth hour of the December morning the water carrier had come and gone, leaving the courtyard flags dark with spill; the baker's boy had left his basket at the back door and been paid in copper by the cook; and Bathyllus, who kept the door and had once kept the peace in a gladiator's barracks, had already turned away two beggars, a woman selling Egyptian charms, and a young man who claimed to have an appointment and did not.

Antonia Cassia heard all of it from her room above the garden, the way a captain hears his ship. The creak of the well rope. The shuffle of the kitchen slaves sweeping yesterday's ash. The thin, irritable clatter of the secretary's stylus being sharpened on the stone sill. She lay a while longer under the coverlet, because the morning was cold and because lying still was one of the few luxuries she had never learned to be bored by. Then she rose, and Sabina came in with the basin, and the day began.

Sabina was twelve, small for it, with a slave's careful hands and a free child's habit of asking questions. She held the towel while Cassia washed, and said, "There was a man at the door before light. Bathyllus wouldn't wake you."

"What kind of man?"

"A man with a lantern. He had a horse."

"Then he was somebody's man, not his own." Cassia dried her face. "What did he want?"

"He said his master would send word at the proper hour."

"He said it in Latin?"

"He said it in Greek," Sabina said, "and then he said it again in Latin, louder."

Cassia smiled at that, and the smile made her look younger than forty-two and also more dangerous, because it was the smile of a woman who had learned that most men repeat themselves. She let Sabina dress her in a plain stola the colour of undyed wool and a mantle the colour of wine lees, and she put on no gold, and she did not look at the mirror longer than it took to pin her hair. The mirror was silvered glass from Alexandria, and it had been a gift from a man who had wanted something, and she had forgotten which man.

At the desk in the small room behind the atrium, Hermeros was waiting with the tablets. He was fifty-seven, a freedman of the house, bald as an egg and neat as a receipt, and he had been with her since the year she had bought the house and discovered that a fortune is not a thing a woman can carry alone.

"Show me," she said.

He showed her. The wine from Campania, forty amphorae, of which six had soured on the road and would have to be sold to a tavernkeeper who did not ask questions. The silk merchant from Berytus, who had promised payment on the Ides and would not pay. The knight who owed forty thousand and had sent a gilt cup instead of money, which was an insult with a handle on it. The baker, whose rent was three months late and who had a sick daughter. The sum that would be needed for the Saturnalia, which was less than the sum that would be needed for the Kalends, which was less than the sum that would be needed if certain people in the city decided to remember that the lady of this house had once been something other than a lady.

"Call the knight's debt," Cassia said.

"He is a knight."

"He is a knight who owes me forty thousand and thinks a cup will do. Call it. Politely. In front of his wife's uncle, if you can arrange it."

Hermeros made a small mark. "And the baker?"

"Let him be until the spring. The daughter?"

"Still ill."

"Send Narcissus with the green jar. Not the blue. The green." She turned a tablet over. "The house is full tonight?"

"Senator Proculus, four at his table, and the grain man Comptus with two. Proculus has asked for the garden room."

"Proculus always asks for the garden room. It is February in his soul." She set the tablet down. "Give him the painted room and tell him the garden is being replanted. He will complain, and then he will be pleased to have been lied to. Put Comptus next to him. Comptus talks when he is nervous, and Proculus cannot resist correcting a man who talks."

"And the women?"

"Ione at Proculus's shoulder. Chrysis on the other side, if she will. Dama to sing after the second course, not before. The man has no ear before the second course." Cassia stood. "And tell Celer that if the fish sauce is as thick as it was at the Ides, I will give his kitchen to the boy and let him watch."

Hermeros wrote it down, although he had written it down a hundred times before, because writing it down was his way of loving her.

The house was not the largest on the Esquiline. It was not even the most beautiful, though it had a peristyle garden that had cost more than some senators' estates and a bath suite that had ruined a marble merchant. It was simply the most quietly expensive house in Rome, and the price of entering it was not money. Money was the least of it. Men came to the House of the Graces because they could not say certain things in the Senate, or to their wives, or to their own mirrors; and Cassia had built her fortune on the truth that a man will pay almost anything for a room in which he is not required to be himself.

She had bought the house with money that had been made in a brothel and laundered through a shipping venture and a brickworks on the Tiber, and she had bought it from a man who had lost it at dice and had wept at the sale. She had kept his cook and his doorkeeper and his gardener, and she had changed everything else. The garden she had made larger. The atrium she had left plain, because a plain atrium made the garden look richer. The strongroom she had built herself, behind a false panel in the tablinum, and in it she kept the household's money, the deeds, the wills of three men who trusted her more than their own families, and a cushion that was not a cushion.

Her name had been given to her by a man who had bought her and then, in the way of such men, decided that he preferred to own a partner than a slave. Antonius Felix had been twenty years older and had had no children and had believed, wrongly, that gratitude was a debt that could be collected. He had freed her in his will and left her his fortune and the name. She had buried him in a tomb on the Via Appia and had paid for the inscription herself, because she had discovered that the dead are easier to be generous with than the living.

The city knew all of this, or most of it, and the parts it did not know it invented. The city said she had been a whore, which was true. The city said she had poisoned her patron, which was not; he had died of a dropsy and a bad temper. The city said she kept a book of every secret told in her house, which was true, and which was the reason half the men who came to her door came a little afraid. The city said she had no family, no name, no past. On that point the city was wrong in the way that mattered, but it was a long time since she had corrected anyone.

There were three Graces painted in the dining room, standing in a row with their hands on one another's shoulders. Aglaia, Euphrosyne, Thalia. Splendour, good cheer, abundance. The painter had been paid to make them look like Cassia and two of her women, and he had done it badly enough that it was a joke and well enough that it was a compliment. Guests liked to name which was which. They were always wrong. Cassia had learned long ago that men see what they have come to see, and that the skill of a hostess is not to correct them but to arrange the room so that what they see is useful.

At the fifth hour the household assembled in the atrium to hear the day's orders, and Cassia stood at the foot of the impluvium with her hands folded and told them. There were twenty-three people in the house, counting the children, and she knew every name, every debt, every ailment, and every grudge. She knew that Philumena the hairdresser had a brother in the vigiles who could be asked to forget a fire. She knew that Celer's wife in the Subura was pregnant again and that Celer was frightened. She knew that Bathyllus had a bad knee and would not admit it. She knew that Ione had been saving money for two years and had not yet said what for, and that Chrysis had a brother somewhere, sold south, and had not yet asked her to look for him.

She knew all of this the way she knew the accounts: because she had learned that people are not so different from money, and that both are best understood when you know where they came from and what they are doing to be spent on.

"Tonight," she said, "the senator Proculus is our guest. He is a vain man, and he has a good memory for slights. Do not give him one. He will drink more than he should and say less than he means. If he asks you about the emperor, you admire the emperor. If he asks you about the Senate, you admire the emperor. If he asks you about his son, you have heard that the young man is the hope of his house, and you have heard it from someone who knows."

A ripple of laughter. She let it run, then raised one finger.

"Mistress Chrysis will not be alone with anyone tonight. If a guest asks, he is told she is promised to the house. If he insists, he is told to ask me." Her eyes moved to Chrysis, who was standing with her hands clasped and her gaze on the floor, and who was nineteen and had been in the house for five years and still flinched at the word *promised*. "And no one goes into the strongroom, for any reason, at any hour, even if I send them. If I send them, it is not me."

They dispersed. Chrysis lingered. Cassia had known she would.

"Domina," she said.

"Not now."

"It is only—"

"Not in the atrium, where the walls have ears and the ears have mouths. Come to the garden before the guests."

The garden was winter-thin, the roses cut back to black sticks, the box hedges dark and wet. A fountain played in the middle of it, a bronze boy holding a dolphin, and the water came out of the dolphin's mouth with a sound like distant conversation. Cassia sat on the stone bench and put her hands in her sleeves.

Chrysis stood before her. She had been born somewhere north of the Danube, in a village whose name she no longer remembered, and she had been sold twice before she came here. She had black hair and grey eyes and the kind of beauty that made men lower their voices. She was also, Cassia thought, the only person in the house who did not know how beautiful she was.

"Well?"

"My brother," Chrysis said. "I have heard where he is."

Cassia said nothing.

"A man came to the kitchen door. He says he has seen him. In Puteoli. He is with a fuller. He is not well. The man says for two thousand sesterces he can be bought and brought."

"Two thousand is not a small sum."

"I know it."

"And the man who tells you this is a man who sells information, and information about a boy he has never met is worth exactly what a frightened sister will pay for it." Cassia watched her. "What is his name?"

"Gaius."

"His name. Not the boy's."

Chrysis hesitated. "I do not know his name. I did not ask."

"Then you have not heard where your brother is. You have heard a story that has your brother in it." Cassia's voice was not unkind. "I will have Hermeros ask. If the boy can be found, he will be found. But I will not buy a rumour, and I will not have you buying one either. If this man comes again, he is to be sent to me."

"He will not come again if he thinks there is nothing to be had."

"Then he was not selling your brother. He was selling your hope, which is cheaper and never runs out." Cassia stood. "Tonight you will sit beside the senator and you will be charming and you will not think about Puteoli. Tomorrow we will think about Puteoli."

Chrysis's eyes filled, but she did not cry. She had learned that too. "Yes, domina."

"Chrysis."

"Yes?"

"You are not to give that man money. Not a copper. If he is what he says, he will come back. If he is not, he will find easier fish." Cassia touched her cheek, once, briefly, the way one might touch a statue to see whether it was warm. "Go and have Philumena do your hair. The braid, not the coils. The braid makes you look like a girl who has not yet decided what she is, and men pay for that."

Chrysis went. Cassia stayed in the cold a while longer, listening to the fountain, and thought about the price of a brother, and about the fact that she had once had a price put on her own head. It had been reckoned in sesterces. It had also, in a way she had never been able to explain to anyone, been reckoned in something else.

***

The guests came at the ninth hour, which in December was already dusk. Proculus arrived first, as a senator should, with his son and two clients and a litter that had been cleaned for the occasion. He was sixty, with the soft hands of a man who had never held a tool and the hard eyes of one who had held a grudge. His son, Aulus, was twenty-four and had the look of a man who had been told all his life that he was promising.

Comptus came next, alone but for his secretary, a narrow man with inky fingers. Numerius Vibius Comptus was an equestrian who moved grain for the annona and had made himself useful to men who moved armies. He was forty-four, going grey at the temples, and he had a wife and three children and a habit of looking at Cassia as though she were a question he had almost answered.

The last to arrive was a knight named Felix, who had not been invited and had sent word that he would be grateful for a word. Cassia had him shown into the small room off the atrium, where the light was bad and the seats were low, and she kept him waiting long enough to look at the ceiling and admire the paint.

Marcus Aquilius Felix was thirty-five and handsome in the way a knife is handsome. He had been a delator for six years, and he had grown rich on other men's terror, and he had the manners of a man who had learned them from a book. He wore a signet ring with a bird on it, which was not his family's, because he had no family worth a bird.

"Domina," he said, and kissed her hand.

"You are not on the list."

"I am aware."

"Then you will understand if I ask what you want."

"A word. Not tonight. A word at your convenience." He smiled. "I have recently come into possession of a document that I think would interest you."

"Documents are rarely interesting. People are."

"This one concerns a physician."

Cassia did not move. Her face did not change. But something behind her eyes closed like a door.

"Physicians are always interesting," she said. "They know too much and they charge too much. Which physician?"

"One who is dead," Felix said. "And one who is not."

She looked at him for a long moment. Then she said, "You may have five minutes after the second course. Not before. And you will not speak to my women."

"I would not dream of it."

"You would dream of it. Every man dreams of it. But you will not do it." She rose. "Come. The senator will be wondering whether I have forgotten him, and a senator who wonders is a senator who talks."

The dining room was warm and smelled of rosemary and roasting fish and the cedar oil the slaves had rubbed into the tables. The walls were painted with scenes of the countryside: vines, a villa, a hunter with a spear, a woman in a yellow dress carrying a basket. The three Graces stood in the alcove, their painted hands on one another's shoulders, their painted eyes on the guests. Lamplight moved over them, so that they seemed to breathe.

Cassia took her place at the head of the middle couch, and the room arranged itself around her. Proculus reclined at her right, his son at his feet. Comptus at her left, with Ione. Chrysis on the outer side, where she could be seen and not touched. Dama stood behind the wine, young and dark and beautiful in the way that made men uncertain of their own taste. The first course came: eggs, olives, oysters, a dish of mushrooms in garum, bread still warm from the bakery next door.

"Tell me," Proculus said, when the oysters were gone, "is it true what they say about the emperor's new forum?"

"That depends what they say."

"That it cost more than the Dacian war."

"The Dacian war cost the emperor a great deal of trouble," Cassia said. "The forum cost him money. He has more money."

Proculus laughed too loudly. "They say you have more money than the Senate."

"Then they say too much, and you should not repeat it."

"I am among friends."

"You are among my friends," Cassia said, "and I am not sure you have been introduced."

Comptus laughed into his cup. Proculus, who was not stupid, decided to be amused. "I have missed you," he said.

"You have missed my cook."

"That too." He leaned closer. The wine had loosened him. "I am told the Asian command will be decided before the spring. Corvinus wants it."

"Does he?"

"He has the votes. He has the name. He has that mother of his, who is worth three tribes on her own." Proculus bit an olive. "But there is talk. There is always talk."

"Talk is the cheapest thing in Rome," Cassia said. "I sell it myself."

"This is not my talk. This is talk with a document in its hand."

She did not look at Felix, who was seated at the far end of the room with a cup he had not touched. She picked up a mushroom and ate it slowly.

"Documents," she said, "are like mushrooms. You should know where they were picked before you put them in your mouth."

Proculus laughed again, and the laugh turned into a cough, and his son thumped him on the back with more enthusiasm than was required. The talk moved on to the games, the price of mules, a Vestal who had been seen at a wedding she should not have attended. At the second course the fish came in on a silver platter, and Dama sang, and Ione leaned into Proculus's ear and said something that made him put down his cup.

Cassia watched the room the way a general watches a field. Proculus was talking now, and the wine was talking with him. Comptus was quiet, which meant he was worried. His secretary had told Hermeros that the grain fleet had lost two ships off Sicily, and that Comptus had been asked to explain it to a man who did not accept explanations.

Young Aulus had had too much wine and was leaning toward Chrysis, not quite touching her, but close enough that his intention was visible from across the room. Cassia saw Chrysis's hands tighten on the edge of the couch. She saw the girl look toward the door, and then toward her. She saw her decide to endure it, because enduring it was what she had been trained to do.

"Aulus," Cassia said.

The young man turned, flushed. "Domina?"

"Your father is telling us about the grain fleet. It is a subject on which he is very fine. You should listen. You may be asked about it one day, when you are in the Senate, and it would be a pity if you had spent your youth learning only one thing."

The room went quiet. Proculus's face did something complicated. Aulus's flush deepened. He started to speak, and his father put a hand on his arm.

"The lady is right," Proculus said. "Sit back. Listen. You might learn something."

"I was only—"

"Sit back."

He sat back. Chrysis breathed. Cassia lifted her cup to Proculus and said, "Your son has spirit. It is a fine thing in a young man. It is a better thing when it has a rein."

"You have a gift," Proculus said, "for making a compliment sound like a sentence."

"I have a gift for both. It is the same gift."

Comptus said, quietly, "The grain fleet lost two ships."

"I heard."

"One of them was carrying my name."

"Then your name is on the bottom of the sea, and you should be glad it is not on the Senate's list." She turned to him. "What do you need?"

"Time."

"Time is what I sell least of."

"Then a friend on the prefect's staff."

"I have no friends on the prefect's staff. I have acquaintances who owe me. It is better." She thought. "Come to the house on the day after the Ides. Bring the manifest. Bring the names of the men who signed it. Do not bring your secretary, because your secretary has been talking to someone, and I do not yet know who."

Comptus's face did not change, but his hand did. "You are sure?"

"I am never sure. I am often right." She touched his wrist. "Eat. The fish is good, and a worried man should eat."

When the plates were taken away, Ione came and sat beside her, which she did not usually do in front of guests. Ione was twenty-seven and had been in the house for nine years, and she had the beauty that improves with use, like a knife. She was Greek, from a family that had once had a name in Ephesus and had lost it to a war and a bad loan. She spoke three languages and could do sums in her head and had never once asked Cassia for anything outright, which was how Cassia knew that she wanted a great deal.

"You gave the girl to the senator's son," Ione said, quietly, watching the room.

"I gave her nothing. I took her away from him."

"He will be back."

"Then I will take her away from him again."

"And when you are not here?"

Cassia looked at her. "I am always here."

Ione smiled, and it was not a warm smile, though it was a beautiful one. "You are always here, domina. But the house is not only you. The house is all of us, and some of us will be here when you are not, and some of us will have to make our own arrangements." She turned her cup by the stem. "I am not asking for anything."

"You are asking me to think about it."

"I am asking you to think about what happens to a girl like Chrysis when the house is sold, or the house is closed, or the house is raided by men with a warrant and a grudge. You think about the future. You must have thought about it. You have thought about everything else."

"I have thought about it," Cassia said.

"And?"

"And it is not a conversation for the dinner table."

Ione inclined her head. "No. It is not." She rose, and then, because she was Ione, she said one more thing, softly, with her back to the room: "You should know that the grain man has been to see Pactus twice this month. I have it from his secretary's woman. He is not as frightened as he looks. He is frightened of the wrong thing."

She went back to her place. Cassia sat with her cup and did not look at Comptus, and thought about the wrong thing, and about how a man who is frightened of the wrong thing is more dangerous than a man who is frightened of the right one.

At the third course Felix rose and came to her, and she led him into the little room off the atrium, where the lamps had been turned down and the painted ceiling was lost in shadow.

"Five minutes."

He did not waste them. "There is a man in the city who is preparing an accusation against Lucius Sulpicius Corvinus. The accusation concerns the death of his father, twenty-four years ago, at Tibur. The man has a witness. The witness is old and frightened, and has been paid a little, and will be paid more. The accusation will be laid before the consul after the Kalends. I am not the man. I am the man who knows the man."

"Why tell me?"

"Because everyone knows that the lady of this house remembers everything, and because I would rather be your friend than your enemy."

"You have been my enemy for six years."

"I have been your competitor," Felix said. "It is not the same."

"It is the same when you lose." She looked at him. "What do you want?"

"Two hundred thousand sesterces."

"That is a great deal for a rumour."

"It is a small sum for a life."

"Whose life?"

"Whosever you choose," Felix said. "The witness can remember the physician. Or the physician's daughter. Or the lady who carried the cup. Memory is a house with many doors. I am offering you the keys."

Cassia stood very still. In the other room, Dama was singing, and the sound came through the wall as though from another world. She thought of a summer twenty-four years gone, of a closed door, of the taste of honey and bitter almond. She thought of the way a man's face changes when he is certain he has won.

"Five minutes are over," she said.

Felix smiled. "I will come for your answer on the day after the Ides."

"Come on the Ides. I do not like to be kept waiting, and I do not like to be hurried, and a man who arrives early shows that he does not trust his own case."

He bowed and went out. She stood alone in the little room for a moment, and then she went back to her guests, and her face was the face of a woman who had heard nothing at all.

***

The house emptied slowly. Proculus was the last to go, because Proculus was always the last to go, and at the door he took Cassia's hand and said, "You are wasted on this house."

"I am not wasted anywhere," she said. "I am invested."

He laughed, and his litter carried him away, and the doorkeeper closed the door and shot the bolt, and the house let out its breath.

The slaves cleared the tables. The women went up. Celer came out of the kitchen with flour on his arms and asked, with the dignity of an artist, whether the fish had been acceptable, and Cassia told him it had been excellent, and he went back to his kitchen satisfied. She walked the rooms as she did every night, checking the lamps, the locks, the shutters. In the dining room the three Graces looked down at the wreck of the feast. Someone had spilled wine on the floor, and the stain was already drying to the colour of old blood.

She was in the corridor when Chrysis came to her, wrapped in a mantle, her hair down.

"Domina."

"I told you. Tomorrow."

"I know what he is," Chrysis said. "The man who says he has my brother. I know what he is. I know he is probably lying. But if there is one chance in a hundred that he is not, and I do nothing, then I am the kind of person who does nothing." Her voice shook, but it did not break. "I do not want to be that kind of person."

Cassia looked at her for a long time. The lamp between them was burning low, and the smell of the oil was sharp.

"Neither do I," she said. "Go to bed. In the morning, we will find your brother. I will pay the man, and if he is lying, I will have Bathyllus explain to him the difference between a lie and a theft. If he is not lying, we will bring the boy here and Narcissus will look at him, and he will be fed, and he will sleep in a bed with a roof over it. And you will owe me nothing but the truth about what he is to you."

Chrysis stared at her. "Why?"

"Because I was once sold," Cassia said, "and no one came."

She had been nineteen when the man had come for her, and she remembered the cart, and the smell of the road, and the way the driver had counted the money twice. She remembered the door of the room at Puteoli and the sound of the bolt. She had spent a great deal of her life since then arranging things so that she would never again be on the wrong side of a door, and she had discovered that the arrangement was never finished, and that a bolt is a thing that can be put on either side.

She said it flatly, without pity, the way one reports the weather. Chrysis's eyes filled. She took Cassia's hand and pressed it to her lips, and Cassia let her, and then sent her to bed.

It was past midnight when Bathyllus knocked at the door of the tablinum. He had a folded tablet in his hand, sealed with wax the colour of rust.

"A man brought it," he said. "A steward. He would not wait."

"From whom?"

"He said his master would be known to you."

Cassia broke the seal. The wax was stamped with a raven, wings spread, a small thing and old. She knew the seal before she knew the hand. She had seen it on a letter once, a lifetime ago, a letter that had been read to her by a man who could read and who had been paid to read it slowly.

She unfolded the tablet.

*L. Sulpicius Corvinus to Antonia Cassia. I will do myself the honour of calling upon you on the day after the Ides, at the tenth hour. I trust the house receives.*

There was no greeting, no closing, no explanation. Twenty-four years, and four lines.

Cassia stood in the lamplight with the tablet in her hands. Her face was calm. Her hands were not. She set the tablet on the desk, and then she picked it up again and read it a second time, as though the words might have changed, and then she held the edge of it against the flame of the lamp and watched the wax soften and the wood begin to blacken.

She did not burn it. She put it down, and sat, and looked at the raven until the lamp guttered.

"Bathyllus," she said.

"Domina."

"When he comes, he is to be shown into the garden room. Not the atrium. Not the painted room. The garden room, where the light is behind him and the door is at his back."

"And if he brings his lictors?"

"He will not bring his lictors. He will bring his pride, and it is heavier." She turned the tablet over. "And Bathyllus—"

"Yes?"

"Have the boy bring more lamps. I want the room bright enough that I can see his face."

---

## Chapter Two

The Kalends of January opened grey and cold, with a wind off the Tiber that smelled of silt and smoke, and Rome put on its best face anyway. The city had been preparing for a month. The shopkeepers had washed their awnings and hung them with laurel; the wine sellers had chalked the prices of the good Caecuban on their boards; the priests of half a hundred temples had been up since dark, killing birds and reading their livers and arguing about the omens in the way that priests argue when the omens are good but not perfect. The emperor was to dedicate his forum. The emperor was to take the consulship for the sixth time. The emperor was to give games, and a distribution, and a speech that everyone would pretend to have heard and no one would remember.

Antonia Cassia had been invited to none of it. She went anyway.

They left the house before the third hour, while the streets were still full of carts and the vigiles were still arguing with the carters about the load limits on the Via Lata. Bathyllus walked in front with a staff and the expression of a man who had been told to keep the peace and was looking forward to failing. Behind him came Cassia in a plain litter, with Tertulla beside her and Ione on the seat opposite, dressed in a blue that was too expensive for the morning and exactly right for the afternoon. Hermeros brought up the rear with a purse and a face of mild disapproval. The purse was for bribes. The face was for the account books.

They went down the Clivus Suburanus into the old city, where the streets narrowed and the insulae leaned out over the road as though trying to hear the news, and the crowd thickened with every step. Porters with baskets. A man selling hot sausages from a brazier. Two women with a child between them, arguing about whether the child had been cured or had only stopped crying. A group of young men in matching cloaks, drunk already, singing a song about the emperor's bridge over the Danube that had never been finished and had cost a great deal. A beggar with no legs, who knew Cassia's litter by its curtains and called out to her by name, and to whom she gave a silver piece without stopping.

"You are too generous," Tertulla said.

"I am not generous. I am superstitious. He has been at that corner for eleven years, and he has outlived three of my enemies."

Tertulla laughed. Vibidia Tertulla was thirty-nine and had buried two husbands and had the kind of money that made her unmarriageable and the kind of wit that made her unwelcome at half the dinner tables in Rome. She had been Cassia's friend for six years, and her lover for two of them, off and on, in the way of two women who had both decided that the world had taken enough from them and they would keep this one thing for themselves. She was not beautiful, exactly, but she had a face that improved when she was interested, and she was interested in almost everything.

The crowd at the forum was already enormous. The new complex stood where the old slums had been, and the men who had lived in those slums had been moved somewhere else, and the men who had built the new complex had been paid, and the men who were now looking at it had been told that it was the greatest thing ever built by human hands, which, on that morning, in that light, with the incense and the garlands and the noise, it almost was.

They had a place in the portico of the Basilica Ulpia, arranged for them by Comptus, who had arranged it for a great many people and had been paid in favours by all of them. The basilica was vast. Its columns were grey granite from the quarries of Mons Claudianus, each one dragged across Egypt and floated across the sea and hauled up the Tiber and set upright by men who would never see the finished building. The roof was gilded bronze, and the floor was marble in a pattern that made the eye want to follow it, and the apse at the end held a statue of the emperor that was larger than the emperor and, Cassia thought, better looking.

Beyond the basilica stood the two libraries, one Greek and one Latin, facing each other across a small courtyard with a fountain. Beyond them, the column was still in its scaffolding, a great wrapped thing with workmen crawling on it, the spiral of carved soldiers and ships and bridges emerging from the top down as the masons worked. And in the middle of the new square, on a bronze horse, the emperor sat with his hand raised, and the horse had its mouth open, and the pigeons had already found it.

"Look at it," Tertulla said. "They have taken the heart out of the city and put a monument in its place."

"They have taken the slums out of the city," Cassia said. "It is not the same."

"It is the same to the people who lived in them."

"People who live in slums are used to being moved. It is the ones who live in houses who are surprised." Cassia watched the workmen on the column. "My father—" She stopped.

Tertulla looked at her. "Your father?"

"I have no father. I was thinking of someone else's." She turned away. "You are not afraid," Ione said, from the other side of the litter, "of being seen."

"I am afraid of being seen in the wrong company. It is not the same."

"You are a freedwoman in a city where the emperor's grandmother sold fish. There is no wrong company, only the wrong witnesses."

Cassia looked at her. "That is either the wisest thing you have said or the most dangerous."

"Can it not be both?" Ione turned her face to the light. "I want to see the libraries. I have heard there is a copy of Sappho there that cost more than a house."

"There is a copy of Sappho there that is kept in a box and shown to no one. It is the most Roman thing about the building."

"I should like to see it anyway."

"Then you will have to marry a senator, or become one. I do not recommend either." Cassia drew her mantle closer. "Do you know why I came today, Ione?"

"Because you were not invited."

"Because I was not invited." Cassia watched the standards go by. "A woman in my position is always being told what she is not. She is not respectable. She is not senatorial. She is not the sort of woman one invites to the dedication of a forum. If she stays at home, she agrees with them. If she goes, she makes them say it to her face, and a man who says a thing to your face is a man you can answer."

"And if he does not say it to your face?"

"Then he says it behind your back, and you find out from someone like Felix, and you charge him for the information." Cassia smiled, without warmth. "I have been in this city for twenty years. I have learned that the only thing more expensive than being seen is being invisible."

Ione was quiet for a moment. Then she said, "And the senator Corvinus? Is he here because you were not invited?"

Cassia did not look at her. "The senator Corvinus is here because it is the Kalends, and because he is an augur, and because a man like that would come to the opening of a door if there were a crowd to see him do it."

"That is not what I asked."

"No," Cassia said. "It is not."

"Where is Comptus?"

"With the grain men. He will be here. He is always here, and always where he should not be, and always sorry."

A trumpet sounded from the direction of the old forum, and the crowd turned like a field in the wind. The procession was coming. First the lictors, then the priests, then the colleges of the augurs and the pontiffs in their striped robes, then the consuls, then the senate, a long river of white wool and purple borders, and above it all the standards and the eagles and the smoke of the incense. The people cheered. Some cheered because they loved the emperor. Some cheered because they were being paid to. Most cheered because the man beside them was cheering, and because a crowd in Rome is a thing that runs on its own momentum until it hits something.

Cassia did not cheer. She stood with her hands folded and watched the faces go by, and she read them the way she read the room at a dinner: the ambitious, the frightened, the bored, the drunk. There was Proculus, sweating under his toga, his son beside him looking as though he had been dressed by a committee. There was Pactus, tall and lean and smiling, walking with the ease of a man who had never had to ask permission for anything. There was the young Publius Sulpicius Corvinus, twenty-one and trying to look older, his hair combed forward in the fashion, his shoes too new.

And there, behind the consuls, in the place of honour that his augurate gave him, was his father.

Lucius Sulpicius Corvinus walked with his head up and his hands at his sides. He was forty-five and looked it in the way that men who have spent their lives outdoors and in the Senate look it: weathered, straight, the grey at his temples cut short. His toga was white as a Vestal's, and his shoes were old and good. He had the face of a man who had been handsome at twenty and had never quite forgiven the world for noticing that he was handsome still. He did not look at the crowd. He looked at the middle distance, where men of his kind looked, and the crowd looked at him, and Cassia looked at him, and for a moment the noise of the forum seemed to her like the noise of water over stones.

Then his head turned. It was not a large movement. It was the smallest movement in the world, the movement of a man who has been told there is someone behind him and has decided to be certain before he looks. His eyes found her across the width of the basilica, through the crowd, through the smoke and the standards and the twenty-four years.

He looked away.

Cassia's hands were cold. She put them in her sleeves and kept her face as still as a painted wall.

"You know him," Tertulla said, quietly.

"Everyone knows him."

"That is not what I asked."

"No," Cassia said. "It is not."

***

The ceremony went on for a long time. There was a sacrifice, and a reading of the decree, and a speech by the consul, and another speech by a man from the treasury who had been told to keep it short and had not. The emperor spoke last. He was a big man, broad and grey and careful, and he spoke in the voice of a soldier who had learned to speak in public because it was part of the job. He spoke of the city, and of the generations who had built it, and of the duty of a man to leave behind him something better than he had found. The crowd cheered at the right moments. Cassia watched the faces of the senators, who were watching the crowd, and understood that every word the emperor said was being weighed by a hundred men who were each calculating what it would be worth to them.

When it was over, the crowd began to move, and the movement was the dangerous part. The porticoes filled with people looking for their friends, their patrons, their wives. A child was separated from its mother and began to scream. A litter was overturned and its occupant, a fat man in a green cloak, was helped up by three strangers and robbed by a fourth. The vigiles appeared, and then the Praetorian guard, and the crowd opened for the guard in the way that water opens for a keel.

Cassia kept her party together by main force, her hand on Ione's wrist and Bathyllus's shoulder in front of her. They were in the portico of the library now, where the light was dimmer and the noise was muffled by the shelves of scrolls beyond the wall, and the smell of cedar and dust and old papyrus was so strong that it seemed to be a kind of silence.

"Wait here," Cassia said. "Bathyllus, the door."

She had intended to go to Comptus. She had intended to find out what he had learned from the grain office, and to be seen finding it out, because a woman who is seen to be informed is a woman whom men think twice about crossing. Instead she found herself standing before a bust of the divine Augustus, looking at a face that had been dead for a hundred years, and hearing footsteps behind her that she had been hearing, in one form or another, since she was eighteen.

"Domina."

She turned.

He was alone. That was the first thing she noticed; a man of his rank was never alone in public, and the fact that he had made himself alone was a statement. The second thing she noticed was the smell of him. Wool and cedar and the faint, clean smell of the oil his mother used in the house. She had not smelled it in twenty-four years, and it went through her like a blade.

"Senator."

"You are well."

"I am rich. It is often confused."

He did not smile. His face had the closed, careful look of a man who had spent his life in rooms where every word was recorded. "I sent a letter."

"I received it."

"You did not answer."

"I answered by receiving. It is the custom."

"You are not a customary woman."

"No," Cassia said. "I am not."

But before Corvinus could say more, a woman came into the portico with two slaves behind her and the particular stillness of a person who has decided to be noticed without appearing to want it. She was thirty-seven or so, with dark hair dressed high and a face that had been beautiful and had settled into something more useful. Her stola was the colour of cream, and her jewels were old and heavy and worth more than the house Cassia lived in. She stopped a few paces away and looked at Cassia with an expression that was not hostility and was not welcome.

"Domina," Corvinus said, and there was something in his voice that had not been there before. "My wife."

Aemilia Celsa inclined her head. "I have been looking for you," she said to her husband. "The consul's wife is asking after the games. She has a scheme for a pantomime that will require a great deal of money and a great many virgins, and I have been sent to find out which of the two is in shorter supply."

"Tell her the virgins," Cassia said.

Aemilia's eyes came to her. They were grey, and steady, and not at all surprised. "You are the woman my mother-in-law prays about."

"I am honoured. I did not know she prayed."

"She prays for the destruction of her enemies. It is a form of religion." Aemilia's mouth moved, almost a smile. "I have never been inside your house. I am told it is beautiful."

"It is comfortable. Beauty is a word men use when they want to pay less."

"And what word do you use?"

"Profitable."

Aemilia laughed, a short, dry sound, and for a moment she looked younger and much more tired. "I have heard a great deal about you," she said. "Some of it from my husband, most of it from women who have never met you. I find that the women who have never met you are the ones who know the most."

"They are the ones with the most to say."

"Yes." Aemilia looked at Corvinus, and then back at Cassia, and something passed between the two women that had no name. "I am told you receive senators."

"I receive everyone. It is the business."

"Do you receive their wives?"

The question hung in the air. Corvinus said, "Aemilia," in a voice that was almost a warning, and she ignored him.

"I receive anyone who comes with good manners and a reason to be there," Cassia said. "Wives are rarer than senators. They are also usually better company."

"Then perhaps I will come," Aemilia said. "One day. When I have a reason." She turned to her husband. "The consul's wife is waiting. You should come and be seen to be bored. It is what you are for."

She went out, her slaves behind her, and the portico seemed larger and emptier for her going.

"She does not know," Corvinus said.

"She knows everything," Cassia said. "She is a senator's wife. It is her profession."

A group of senators came past the end of the portico, talking loudly about the sacrifice, and Corvinus waited until they were gone. When he spoke again, his voice was lower.

"Felix came to me. He came to me before he came to you. He wanted three hundred thousand from me and he told me he had already spoken to you, and that you had agreed to help him."

"Then he lied to you."

"Did he?"

"Which part?" She looked at him. "I did not agree to help him. I agreed to hear him. There is a difference, and it is the difference between a whore and a hostess."

His jaw tightened. "You always did know how to put a knife in a compliment."

"I learned from the best." She said it before she could stop herself, and saw the blow land. He took it without moving, the way he had always taken things, and she hated him for it, and hated herself more.

"The accusation is being prepared," he said. "It will be laid before the consul within the month. It concerns my father."

"I heard."

"From Felix."

"From a senator who had drunk too much. Felix merely confirmed it." She held his eyes. "You have nothing to fear from me, Lucius. I have kept your secret for twenty-four years. I have kept it through the fire and the plague and the year I had nothing to eat but bread and olives. I am not going to give it away now, when I have finally built something worth keeping."

"Something worth keeping," he repeated, and looked around the library, the shelves, the scrolls, the marble, the whole vast monument of it. "You have done well."

"I have done what I could."

"You always did." He said it without warmth. "My mother is ill."

"I am sorry to hear it."

"No, you are not."

"No," Cassia said. "I am not. But I am polite, and I am sorry to hear it in the way that one is sorry to hear of a storm at sea. It is a great deal of weather and none of it mine."

He looked at her for a long moment. In the dim light of the library, with the dust moving in the shafts of sun, he looked younger than he had in the procession, and tired, and something else that she did not want to name.

"I need to know what you will say," he said. "If they come to you. If they ask you about Tibur."

"They will not come to me."

"They will come to your household."

That stopped her. She had thought of Felix, and of the Senate, and of the slow machinery of the law, but she had not thought of the household. The household was hers. The household was the one thing in the world that was entirely hers, bought and paid for and built out of people no one else had wanted.

"On what charge?" she said, and her voice was steady.

"Parricide."

The word hung in the air between them. A man who killed his father was sewn into a sack with a dog and a cock and a viper and thrown into the Tiber. The law had been old when the Republic was young. It was not often used. It was never used against a man who could afford a good lawyer, unless someone with power wanted it used.

"They cannot touch me," Cassia said. "I am free. I have been free for sixteen years. Free women are not put to the question."

"No," he said. "But your slaves are."

She looked past him, through the doorway, to where Bathyllus stood with his back to them, and beyond him to the crowd, and beyond that to the house on the Esquiline where Sabina was learning her letters and Chrysis was waiting for news of her brother and Thais was counting the linens and Celer was shouting at a boy about a fish.

"Then you had better hope," she said, "that your secret is worth more to me than they are."

"That is not an answer."

"It is the only answer I have." She stepped closer to him, close enough that no one else could have heard, close enough that she could smell the cedar and the wool and the faint, clean oil. "Listen to me, Lucius. I have spent twenty-four years making sure that what happened at Tibur stayed at Tibur. I have paid for it. I have paid in ways you cannot imagine, and in some ways you can. I am not going to destroy my house to save yours. But I am not going to destroy yours to save mine, either, unless I have to. Do you understand?"

"Yes."

"Then we have an understanding."

"We have a truce."

"Call it what you like." She turned to go.

"Cassia."

She stopped. He had not used her name in twenty-four years. He had called her domina, and then he had called her nothing at all, and now he said her name like a man setting down a heavy thing he had been carrying for a long time.

"There is something else," he said.

"There is always something else."

Before she could answer, a shadow fell across the doorway. Decimus Iunius Pactus stood there with a cup in his hand and a smile that had been polished by years of public life. He was forty-nine, tall and lean, with the sort of weathered good looks that made men trust him and women regret it. He was a new man, which in Rome meant a man whose grandfather had not been a senator, and he had spent his whole career making the old families forget it and the new ones remember it.

"Corvinus," he said. "I did not know you were a patron of the libraries."

"I am a patron of nothing," Corvinus said, "except my own household."

"A harsh doctrine. The gods are patrons of everything, and look how well it has worked for them." Pactus's eyes slid to Cassia, and his smile changed, became more real and more careful. "Domina. We have not met. I am Pactus."

"I know who you are."

"Then I am flattered, and also slightly afraid."

"Men who say they are afraid are usually the least afraid men in the room."

"Not I. I am afraid of all sorts of things. Failure. Debt. My wife's cook." He sipped his wine. "I am told you keep the best table on the Esquiline."

"I keep a table. The rest is gossip."

"Gossip is the only currency that has never been debased." He glanced at Corvinus, and then back at her. "I hope you will permit me to call on you. I have a proposition that requires discretion, and I am told you are the soul of it."

"Come," Cassia said. "Bring your proposition. I will decide whether it is worth the salt."

"I would expect nothing less." He bowed, and went out, and the portico was quiet again.

"That man," Corvinus said, "is the reason the Asian command is not yet decided."

"I know."

"He will use you."

"Everyone uses me. I am a house. That is what houses are for." She looked at him. "The difference is that I decide what I charge."

Corvinus looked at the doorway where Pactus had stood, and then at her, and his face closed like a hand.

"The child," he said.

The library was very quiet. Somewhere beyond the wall, a man was laughing, and a cart went past on the stones, and the fountain in the courtyard played its endless, patient music.

"There was no child," Cassia said.

"Cassia—"

"There was no child. There was a girl who was told there was a child, and there was a liar who told her, and there was a grave with nothing in it, and there was a woman who has spent her life not thinking about it. That is all there is. If you have come to me for absolution, you have come to the wrong house. I sell pleasure. I do not sell that."

She walked away from him, past the bust of Augustus, past the shelves of scrolls, past Bathyllus, into the noise and the light. She did not look back. She had learned long ago that a woman who looks back is a woman who is followed.

***

They came out of the forum into the late afternoon, when the light had gone the colour of old bronze and the city was beginning to think about dinner. The crowd had thinned. The workmen on the column had gone home, and the scaffolding stood against the sky like a cage for a bird that had not yet been caught.

Cassia walked. She had sent the litter ahead and told the others to go with it, and Tertulla, who knew her, had not argued. She walked up the Via Lata with her veil over her hair and her hands in her sleeves, and the city closed around her the way it always did, indifferent and alive. A baker was taking bread out of an oven. A woman was shouting at a man on a balcony. A dog was eating something in the gutter that had once been a pigeon. Two boys were playing at gladiators with sticks, and one of them had tied a rag around his head like a retiarius, and the other was pretending to be the emperor.

She stopped at a fountain to drink and to let an old woman fill her jar first, and while she stood there she saw, across the street, a girl in a brown cloak buying figs from a stall. The girl was fifteen or sixteen, with a plain face and a sulky mouth and the unmistakable bearing of a house that had money. She was not alone. She had a slave with her, a thin woman with a basket, and the slave was looking around in the way of a slave who has been told to be somewhere she should not be.

The girl turned, and Cassia saw her eyes. They were her father's eyes. They were also, in the way that these things happen, the eyes of the portrait in the atrium of the house on the Palatine that Cassia had never seen, and the eyes of a man who had once stood in a garden at Tibur with his hands full of roses.

The girl saw Cassia looking and did not look away. For a moment they regarded each other across the street, the freedwoman and the senator's daughter, and the noise of the city went on around them, and neither of them moved.

Then the slave touched the girl's arm, and the girl turned, and they went off toward the Palatine, and Cassia stood with her cup of water and watched them go.

"Domina," Bathyllus said, behind her. "The house."

"Yes," she said. "The house."

She walked on. The water was cold in her stomach, and the memory of cedar was in her throat, and somewhere behind her, in the new forum, a workman was hammering at the column, fixing another piece of the emperor's glory into place. It would stand for a thousand years, she thought. It would outlast her, and Corvinus, and the Senate, and the whole squabbling city. It would outlast the secret. That was the thing about stone. It did not care what it was built on.

At the corner of the Clivus Suburanus, a man was waiting for her. He was a small man in a brown tunic, with the look of a clerk, and he stepped out of a doorway as she passed and said, quietly, "The lady is to know that the midwife is alive."

Bathyllus moved faster than a man his size should have. His hand closed on the man's collar, and the man did not resist. He looked at Cassia with the flat, patient eyes of a man who had been paid to deliver a message and had been paid enough to be calm about it.

"What midwife?" Cassia said.

"The one from Tibur," the man said. "The one who was at the birth. She is old, and she is not well, and she has been asking for the lady. She says she has something to give her."

"Who sent you?"

"No one sent me. She paid me with a ring." The man held out his hand. In his palm was a small bronze ring, worn thin, with a pattern of two snakes twined around a staff. Cassia had seen it before. She had seen it on the hand of a woman who had held her down on a bed in a cell at Tibur and told her to push, and had told her afterward that the child had never breathed.

She took the ring. It was cold, and lighter than it looked.

"Where is she?"

"At Tibur. In the village below the hill. The house with the fig tree." The man pulled free of Bathyllus's hand and straightened his tunic. "She says the lady should come before the spring. She says the lady will want to hear what she has to say."

He went away down the street, and Bathyllus looked at Cassia, and Cassia looked at the ring in her hand.

"Domina?"

"Go home," she said. "Tell Hermeros to bring the strongbox to my room. Tell Thais to come to me. And tell no one else anything."

She began to walk again, faster now, the ring closed in her fist. The city went on around her, buying and selling and eating and dying, and above the rooftops the new forum of the emperor shone in the last of the light like something that had been built by gods who had never had a mother, and never been told a lie.

---

## Chapter Three

The house of the Sulpicii stood on the slope of the Palatine where the hill turns its shoulder against the Circus, and it had stood there, in one form or another, for four hundred years. It had been burned in the year of the great fire and rebuilt; it had been seized in the civil wars and recovered; it had been mortgaged twice and redeemed twice, and the second redemption had cost the family a vineyard in Etruria and a great deal of pride. The walls were old republican stone, plain and massive, and the door was cedar and bronze and had been repaired so often that there was hardly an original piece of it left. A stranger might have thought it shabby. A Roman would have known that it was the opposite of shabby, because a house that does not need to look rich is a house that has been rich for a very long time.

The atrium was high and dark and cold in winter, and along one wall stood the imagines: the wax masks of the Sulpicii, each in its wooden cupboard, each with its name and its offices painted underneath. There were consuls there, and praetors, and a censor, and a man who had been sent as ambassador to a king in the East and had come home with an eye missing and a treaty. The masks were wax and would melt in a fire; they were also, in the strange arithmetic of Roman memory, more real than the men had been. On feast days a slave was paid to open the cupboards and anoint the masks with oil, and the whole atrium smelled of wax and old wood and the particular dust of a family that had been important for four hundred years.

Sulpicia Varia had the masks polished twice a year. She had done it herself when she was younger. Now she had a woman in to do it, and she sat in her chair and watched, and complained about the woman's technique.

She was sixty-seven and dying, though the household had agreed not to say so. The growth had begun in her breast two winters before, and the physician had wanted to cut it out, and she had refused, and then she had allowed him to cut into it, and then it had come back, and now it was a hard, hot thing under her collar that she carried around with her like a purse she did not intend to open. She had grown thin. Her face had become all bone. But her eyes were the same as they had always been, pale and cold and entirely without self-deception, and when she rang the little silver bell beside her chair, the whole house came running.

On the morning after the Kalends, the house came running.

Her son came first, because he was the eldest and because he had learned, at an early age, that the cost of not coming was higher than the cost of coming. He came in his formal tunic, with his hair combed and his chin shaved, and he stood at the foot of her couch and waited to be told what he already knew.

"You were seen talking to her," Sulpicia Varia said.

"Yes."

"In the portico of the library. In front of half the Senate."

"There were three senators and a man from the treasury. The rest was crowd."

"Three senators is a crowd in this city. Three senators is a pamphlet." She shifted in her chair, and the movement cost her, and she did not allow it to show. "What did she want?"

"She wanted nothing. She was there because she had not been invited."

"And you spoke to her because?"

"Because she is a source of information."

"She is a source of something." The old woman's mouth was a thin line. "You are forty-five years old, Lucius. You have a wife, a son, a daughter, and a career that is not yet over. You have the augurate and the consular rank and the Asian command within your reach. And you are standing in a public portico talking to a woman who could destroy you with a single sentence, and you are telling me it is because she is informed."

"She is informed. She knows about Felix."

"Then pay Felix."

"Felix wants three hundred thousand."

"Then pay him three hundred thousand."

"I do not have three hundred thousand."

Sulpicia Varia looked at him for a long moment. Then she laughed, a dry, papery sound that turned into a cough. The slave behind her chair moved forward and she waved him away.

"You do not have three hundred thousand," she said. "You have a house on the Palatine that is falling down, a vineyard in Etruria that has not made a profit since the drought, a son who owes money to half the moneylenders in the city, and a daughter who must be married in the spring to a man whose family will want a dowry that you do not have. And you have the Asian command, which would pay for all of it and which you will not get if this accusation reaches the consul. And you are telling me you cannot find three hundred thousand sesterces."

"I am telling you I cannot find them honestly."

"Honestly." She said the word as though it were a bad smell. "Your father would have found them."

"My father," Corvinus said, "would have found them by selling someone."

"Yes," his mother said. "He would. And the house would still be standing, and the name would still mean something, and I would not be lying in this chair watching my son lose everything his grandfathers built because he has developed a conscience at the age of forty-five."

The room was silent. The slave by the door studied the floor. Outside, in the corridor, a servant went past with a basket of linen, and the sound of her feet was very loud.

"I have not developed a conscience," Corvinus said, at last. "I have developed a memory. They are not the same."

"They are worse." His mother closed her eyes. "Send for the steward. Tell him to sell the Etrurian vineyard."

"It is the last thing we have."

"It is a vineyard. I am the last thing we have." She opened her eyes. "And tell your wife I want to see her. And send the girl to me. She has been at the mirror again, and I will not have her married with paint on her face like a Greek."

He went out. In the corridor he stood for a moment with his hand against the wall, and the cold of the stone went through his palm and up his arm. He thought about the portico, and the noise of the crowd, and the way she had said *I was once sold, and no one came,* which she had not said to him, but which he had heard, somehow, in the middle of everything else. He thought about the library and the smell of cedar and the twenty-four years.

Then he straightened his tunic and went to find the steward.

***

The women of the house were in the small room off the garden, where the sun came in through a window of clear glass and the loom stood against the wall. Aemilia Celsa was at the loom, because she was always at the loom in the morning, and because a woman who is always at the loom cannot be accused of idleness. She had been weaving the same length of cloth for a month. It was a good cloth, the colour of wheat, and it would make a fine mantle for her husband, and she had no intention of finishing it.

Sulpicia Varia Minor was on the stool by the window with a book in her lap. She was fifteen and had her grandmother's bones and her father's eyes and a mouth that was always on the edge of something. She was not reading the book. She was watching the garden, where a blackbird was pulling at the ivy, and she was thinking about the Lupercalia, and about a boy.

"Your grandmother wants you," Aemilia said, without looking up.

"I know."

"Then go."

"In a moment."

"Now."

Sulpicia put the book down and went, and Aemilia wove four more lines of the wheat-coloured cloth before she set the shuttle down and said, to the empty room, "She is going to be a great deal of trouble."

The girl came back a quarter of an hour later with her face washed and her hair bound and a sulky set to her shoulders that meant her grandmother had been at her. She sat down on the stool again and said, "She wants me to marry Aulus Proculus."

Aemilia did not stop weaving. "Does she."

"She says he is steady. She says his father is a fool but the son will inherit, and a fool's son is better than a clever man's daughter, whatever that means."

"It means she has been talking to the lawyers."

"I do not want to marry Aulus Proculus."

"Then you had better find a husband who will be more useful to your father and less annoying to you, and you had better do it quickly." Aemilia looked up. "You are fifteen. You have perhaps a year before the choice is made for you. If you want a say in it, you will have to be cleverer than the choice."

The girl stared at her. "Did you choose my father?"

"No."

"Do you regret it?"

Aemilia considered the question longer than a mother should. "I regret very little," she said at last. "It is a luxury I was not raised to afford. Your father is a decent man, and a proud one, and he has spent his life trying to be the man his father was and the man his mother wants, and he has failed at both. That is not the worst thing a husband can be." She picked up the shuttle. "If you want a reason to dislike him, you will find one. Everyone does. But do not pretend it is his fault that the world is arranged the way it is. It is not his fault. He merely profits from it, which is worse, and which he knows."

Sulpicia was silent. Then she said, "The woman at the forum. The one he was talking to."

"What about her?"

"Grandmother says she is a whore."

"Your grandmother says a great many things." Aemilia's hands moved. "The woman is a freedwoman with a fortune and a house full of senators. She is not received in any house on this hill, and every man on this hill has been to hers. That is not a whore. That is a mirror. When you are older, you will understand the difference, and you will find that the mirror is more frightening."

"Are you afraid of her?"

Aemilia looked at her daughter for a long moment. "Yes," she said. "But not for the reason you think."

***

In the afternoon Corvinus went down into the Forum to see a man about a loan. The man was a banker named Sittius, who had a stall under the Basilica Julia and a face like a closed door, and who had lent money to three generations of Sulpicii and had been repaid by two of them. The negotiation took an hour and ended with a rate of interest that was only slightly worse than ruin.

Coming out, Corvinus found his son waiting for him.

Publius Sulpicius Corvinus was twenty-one, and he had his father's height and his mother's colouring and none of either's restraint. He was dressed in the newest fashion, which meant his tunic was too fine for the season and his cloak was too short for the weather, and he had spent the morning, Corvinus suspected, doing something expensive.

"Father."

"Publius."

"I need to speak with you."

"You need money."

"That too." The boy fell into step beside him. They walked along the edge of the forum, past the stalls of the money changers and the lawyers and the men selling hot wine, and the boy talked, and the father listened, and neither of them looked at the other.

"It is the Asian command," Publius said. "Everyone says you will have it. Pactus is telling everyone you will not. He says a man with a son like you cannot be trusted with a province."

"And what does he mean by a son like me?"

"He means my debts. He means the gambling. He means the girl in the Subura." The boy's voice was carefully light. "He means that I am not serious. That is the word he uses. Not serious."

"And are you?"

"I am twenty-one."

"So was I, once."

"Yes, and you were serious. You were serious because grandfather made you serious. He made you stand in the atrium and look at the masks and tell him what each one had done for Rome, and if you got it wrong he beat you." Publius laughed, without humour. "I have heard the story. Everyone has heard the story. You tell it at dinner when you have had wine, and everyone laughs, and mother looks at the table."

Corvinus stopped walking. They were in the shadow of the basilica, and the noise of the forum went on around them, and a pigeon was pecking at something on the pavement between their feet.

"Your grandfather," he said, "was a hard man."

"He was a monster."

"He was a Roman of the old school, and he believed that the family was more important than any man in it, and he lived by that belief, and he made other people live by it, and he died by it." Corvinus's voice was flat. "I did not beat you. Perhaps that was my mistake. I have spent my life trying to be a different sort of father, and I have produced a son who thinks that a debt is a thing that happens to him."

Publius's face flushed. "I did not come here to be lectured."

"Then why did you come?"

"Because I need thirty thousand sesterces and you are the only man in Rome who can get them without asking me what they are for."

"What are they for?"

"For a girl."

Corvinus looked at him. His son looked back, defiant and frightened and twenty-one, and for a moment, in the middle of the forum, with the pigeons and the money changers and the noise, Corvinus saw himself at twenty-one in a garden at Tibur with his hands full of roses and a girl's laughter in his ear, and he felt the ground move under him.

"What girl?"

"None of your business."

"Publius."

"She is not a respectable girl. She is a girl in a house. Not that kind of house," he added, quickly, seeing his father's face. "A house of women. A decent house, kept by a freedman named Scaurus. Her mother is dead. She is free, and she has been promised to a sailor in Ostia, and the sailor has paid a deposit, and if I do not pay the rest he will take her." He stopped. "I have made a mess of it."

"Yes," Corvinus said. "You have."

They stood in the shadow of the basilica and looked at each other.

"I will not give you thirty thousand sesterces to buy a girl," Corvinus said. "I will give you ten thousand to pay your debts, and you will come with me to the moneylender and you will sign the agreement in your own name, and if you default he will take your horse and your rings and your cloak, and you will walk home in your tunic, and the whole city will see you do it."

"Father—"

"That is the offer."

The boy's jaw worked. Then he said, "And the girl?"

"The girl is not a transaction. If she is free, she cannot be bought. If her mother has promised her to a man in Ostia, then the promise is the business of her family and not of yours. If you want her, you will have to be a man who is worth wanting, and that is not something I can buy for you."

Publius stared at him. Then he laughed, a short, bitter laugh, and said, "You sound like mother."

"I have been told worse."

"No," the boy said, and turned away. "No, you have not."

He walked off into the crowd. Corvinus watched him go, and then he stood for a while in the shadow of the basilica, with the noise of the city going on around him, until a clerk came up and said, "Senator? The banker is waiting for the signature."

He signed. The rate was worse than he had feared and better than he had deserved. He came out of the banker's stall with the tablet under his arm and walked up the hill through the Forum and the Velia, past the arch of Titus and the vendors of fruit and the men selling copies of the new decree, and by the time he reached the Palatine his legs ached and his mind had gone quiet in the way it did after a day of small humiliations.

The house was cold. It was always cold in January. His mother kept her rooms warm out of a sense of her own importance, but the rest of the house was heated only when there were guests, and there were rarely guests now. He went through the atrium without looking at the masks, which was a habit he had developed as a boy and had never broken, and found his wife in the garden, wrapped in a mantle, watching the light go out of the sky.

"You are late," Aemilia said.

"The banker wanted to explain himself. Bankers always want to explain themselves. It is how they justify the interest."

"Did you get it?"

"Some of it. At a rate that would make a Carthaginian blush." He sat down on the bench beside her, and she moved a little to make room, which was the closest thing to affection that had passed between them in a week. "Publius came to see me."

"I know. He came home in a temper and kicked the door of his room and then asked the cook for bread and cheese, which is his way of apologising." She looked at him. "What did you say to him?"

"I told him I would not buy him a girl."

"Good."

"You do not ask who she is."

"I do not need to. He is twenty-one and he has been in love four times this year, and each time it has been the end of the world and each time the world has survived." Aemilia drew her mantle closer. "What else?"

"I told him he should be worth wanting."

She turned her head. "That was either very cruel or very kind."

"I do not know which. I have never known which." He looked at his hands. "My father would have beaten him. My father would have beaten him and then bought the girl and then beaten him again for the expense. And the terrible thing is that the house would have been stronger for it. The boy would have learned to be afraid, and fear makes men careful, and careful men keep their money. I have not beaten him, and he is not careful, and I am borrowing money at twelve per cent from a man who used to sell olives in the Subura."

"Your father is dead," Aemilia said. "You cannot keep measuring yourself against a dead man. He will always win. He has the advantage of not having to live with the consequences."

Corvinus laughed, once, without much sound. "You sound like her."

"I know. It is why I am still here." She said it without malice. "Your mother sent for me this morning. She wants the girl married to Aulus Proculus by the summer. She wants the vineyard sold. She wants the Asian command and she wants the accusation dealt with and she wants the masks polished before the Kalends of February, and she wants it all done by other people, because she cannot do it herself anymore."

"She is dying."

"Yes."

"She will not admit it."

"No. She will not." Aemilia was quiet for a moment. "I have been in this house for nineteen years. I came here at eighteen and I did not know what a family was. I thought it was a thing you joined, like a priesthood or a guild. I thought there would be rules and I would learn them and then I would belong." She looked at the dark garden. "There are no rules. There is only your mother, and the masks, and the money. And now there is this accusation, which no one will explain to me, and a woman on the Esquiline whom no one will name, and a husband who goes to the forum in the morning and comes home at night with a face like a closed book."

"I am sorry," Corvinus said.

"I did not ask for an apology. I asked for the truth." She turned to him. "Whatever it is, I can bear it. I have borne worse than the truth. What I cannot bear is being the last person in my own house to know it."

He looked at her. In the fading light, with her hair coming down and her mantle clutched at her throat, she looked like a woman who had been beautiful once and had put the beauty away in a chest, and had never taken it out again because there was no occasion to.

"Not tonight," he said.

"That is not an answer."

"It is the only one I have."

She rose. "Then it will have to do." At the door to the house she stopped. "Lucius. The girl. If she is not to marry Aulus Proculus, she will have to marry someone. Find her someone. Not for the family. For her. There is a difference, and it will matter to her, and it may one day matter to you."

She went inside. Corvinus stayed in the garden until it was fully dark, and the slaves came out with the lamps, and the house on the Palatine settled into the night with the sound of old stone cooling and an old woman coughing in her room above.

***

The house on the Esquiline had a visitor that night, and Cassia, who had been expecting one, was not surprised when Bathyllus came to the tablinum with his face set in the expression he reserved for young men with money.

"There is a party at the door," he said.

"Whose?"

"The Sulpicius boy. And two others. They have been drinking, and one of them has brought a jar of wine as a gift, and they are all wearing their cloaks like men who have never been told no."

Cassia put down her pen. She had been going over the accounts with Hermeros, and the lamp was low, and her eyes were tired, and she had spent the whole day thinking about a midwife in Tibur and a ring with two snakes on it.

"Where are they now?"

"In the street. The boy sent his name up as though it would open the door by itself."

"Did it?"

"Not yet." Bathyllus's mouth twitched. "I thought you would want to decide."

"Bring them in. The boy only. The others can wait in the garden and be looked at."

"And the wine?"

"Take it and thank them. It is probably the best thing they own." She stood and smoothed her stola. "And Bathyllus—"

"Yes?"

"Put them in the small dining room, not the painted one. Let them see the house but not the Graces. I want the boy to understand that there is a part of the house he has not been invited into."

The young man who came into the small dining room ten minutes later was handsome and drunk and trying to hide both. He had his father's height and his father's eyes, and he had the particular swagger of a boy who has been told all his life that the name he carries is a shield. He stopped in the doorway and looked at Cassia, and his face did something complicated.

"Domina," he said, and bowed too low.

"You are Publius Sulpicius Corvinus."

"I am."

"Then you are welcome in my house." She gestured to the couch. "Sit. You have come a long way, and you have brought wine, which is more than most men bring."

He sat. A slave brought cups, and the boy poured for both of them, and his hands were not quite steady. He drank too fast, and then he said, "You know my father."

"I know of your father."

"You know him."

The room was quiet. In the garden, one of his friends laughed, loudly, and was hushed.

"Yes," Cassia said. "I know him."

"He does not know I am here."

"I had assumed not."

"He would be angry." The boy turned his cup. "He is always angry, but he is quiet about it. He has made a career of being quiet about it. You know what he is like. You must know. Everyone knows what he is like except him."

"What is he like?"

"He is a man who has never made a mistake in his life, and who cannot understand why everyone else keeps making them." Publius drank. "He gave me ten thousand sesterces today and made me sign a paper. He thinks that is generosity. He thinks that is being a good father. He thinks that if he is careful enough and quiet enough and respectable enough, the world will not notice that the whole thing is a lie."

Cassia set her cup down. "What whole thing?"

The boy looked at her. The drink was in him, and the anger, and something else — a hurt that had been growing in him for a long time, like a tooth.

"The house," he said. "The name. The masks. My grandmother sitting in her chair talking about honour. My mother weaving the same cloth every day of her life. My father going down to the forum to borrow money from a man he despises so that he can keep up a house that is falling down, and then coming home and telling me that a man must live by the truth." He laughed. "The truth. The truth is that this city is a game, and the people who win are the ones who admit it, and my father has spent his whole life pretending not to play."

"And you?" Cassia said. "Are you winning?"

The boy's face changed. For a moment he looked very young, and very like his father, and not at all like a man who thought the city was a game.

"I came here," he said, "because I wanted to see what it looked like."

"What what looked like?"
