"Your house. You." He looked around the room. "My father will not say your name. My grandmother prays about you. My mother mentions you at dinner as though you were a disease. I have lived my whole life in a house where you are a thing that is not spoken of, and today I saw my father stand in a public portico and talk to you as if the whole world were not watching, and I thought — I thought I should see for myself."

"And what do you see?"

He looked at her for a long time. The lamp was between them, and the wine, and the noise of his friends in the garden, and the whole weight of the house above and around them.

"I see a woman," he said, "who is not a monster."

"No," Cassia said. "I am not."

"Then why does he hate you?"

The question was so naked, and so young, that she almost answered it. She looked at the boy's face, which was his father's face, and at his hands, which were not his father's hands, and she thought: he does not know. He does not know anything. He has been raised in that cold house with the masks on the wall and the mother in the chair, and no one has ever told him the truth about anything, and he is drowning in it.

"Your father does not hate me," she said. "He hates what I know."

"What do you know?"

"Everything," Cassia said, "that your family has spent a great deal of money to forget."

The boy stared at her. Then he said, quietly, "Tell me."

"No."

"Why not?"

"Because it is not mine to tell, and because you are drunk, and because the telling of it would cost more than you can pay tonight." She stood. "Go home. Tell your friends the evening was a success and the lady of the house was beautiful and nothing happened. It is the most useful lie you will tell this year."

He did not move. "If I come back? Sober?"

"Then I will consider it."

He rose, unsteadily. At the door he turned and said, "My father said something today. He said I should be a man who is worth wanting. What does that mean? What does it mean to be worth wanting?"

Cassia looked at him. In the lamplight, with his cloak askew and his face open, he looked like a boy who had come a long way to ask a question and had been given a riddle instead.

"It means," she said, "that you should find out what you are worth and then make sure it is true. Most men never do. They spend their lives being told what they are worth by other people, and they believe it, and they die believing it. Your father is one of them. He is worth a great deal to the Senate and the army and the name on the wall, and he is worth almost nothing to himself, and he has never once noticed the difference."

The boy's face worked. Then he bowed, and went out, and she heard him collect his friends in the garden, and the three of them go laughing and stumbling down the Clivus Suburanus into the dark.

Bathyllus came in to clear the cups.

"Domina."

"Yes?"

"The boy's father will hear of this. He has eyes on the hill, and eyes in the street, and the boy is not careful."

"I know."

"And the boy knows something, or he thinks he does. He will talk."

"He will not talk. He is too frightened of his father." Cassia picked up her pen. "But he will come back. And when he does, he will be sober, and he will ask me again, and I will have to decide what to tell him."

She looked down at the accounts. The figures swam. She set the pen aside and sat for a moment with her hands flat on the desk, and then she said, "Bathyllus, send word to Tibur. Tomorrow. Not through Hermeros. Through the woman who sells flowers in the Subura. Tell the midwife that I will come before the spring, and that she is to stay alive until I do."

"Domina."

"And Bathyllus—" She looked up. "The boy is not to be admitted again. Not unless I say so. Not unless I am here."

"Yes, domina."

She was alone then, with the lamp and the accounts and the ring on the desk before her, two snakes twined around a staff, worn thin by twenty-four years of someone else's hand.

---

## Chapter Four

The Baths of Agrippa opened to women at the fourth hour, and by the time Cassia arrived the dressing room was already full of the particular noise of Roman women pretending not to look at one another. There were senators' wives and bankers' widows and the daughters of men who had made their money inannounced and were now buying their way into the equestrian order one marriage at a time. There were two actresses, who were not respectable and did not care. There was a woman who had been a vestal until a scandal had retired her, and who now came to the baths every day and sat in the hottest room until she was red, as though she were trying to sweat out the memory. And there was Tertulla, already undressed and wrapped in a linen sheet, arguing with the attendant about the temperature of the oil.

"It is rancid," she said.

"It is the best oil in Rome, domina."

"Then Rome has gone off." Tertulla saw Cassia and waved. "There you are. Come and smell this. It is either oil or a mistake."

Cassia smelled it. It was oil, and it was not rancid, though it had been in the jar a long time. "It is fine. Stop tormenting the girl. She did not press the olives."

The attendant looked grateful. Tertulla looked betrayed. They went through into the tepidarium, where the air was warm and wet and smelled of steam and perfume and bodies, and the mosaic floor showed a Triton with a following of sea creatures, and somebody had dropped a bronze strigil and left it where it lay.

The bathing was a ritual, and like all rituals it was mostly about being seen. The actual washing took a quarter of an hour. The rest was politics. Cassia sat on the marble bench with her feet in the warm water and let the steam open her lungs, and the room arranged itself around her the way rooms always did.

"Did you hear about the grain fleet?" said a woman on her left, who had a face like a spoon and a husband in the annona.

"I heard two ships were lost."

"Three. The third went down off Corsica and the cargo was insured in Alexandria, and the insurer is refusing to pay, and the prefect is furious." The spoon-faced woman leaned in. "They say Comptus is finished."

"They have said that before."

"They have never said it with a warrant."

Cassia turned her head. "What warrant?"

"There is a man from the treasury asking questions. A man named Felix."

The steam curled between them. Cassia's face did not change.

"Felix asks questions for a living," she said. "He has been asking them for years and he has never once found an answer."

"He found one this time. He has been to the grain office twice, and to the prefect's house, and to the house of a man in the fourth cohort who signed the manifest." The woman lowered her voice. "They say he is not working alone."

"Who is he working for?"

"Nobody knows. That is the interesting part." The woman smiled, pleased to have something to trade. "You should be careful, domina. They say he has been asking about your house as well."

"Then he will find that it is very clean," Cassia said, "and that I charge by the hour."

The woman laughed, uncertain whether she had been insulted, and drifted away to tell someone else.

Two women came through from the dressing room, and one of them was the wife of a praetor and the other was the wife of a man who hoped to be a praetor, and they saw Cassia and made the small adjustment of the head that is not quite a greeting and not quite a cut. Cassia watched them go by with her hands folded in her lap.

"You could buy them both," Tertulla said, "and they know it."

"Buying is easy. It is the being bought that people resent." Cassia smiled at the praetor's wife, a wide, warm, entirely genuine-looking smile, and the woman faltered and then, because she was well brought up, smiled back before she could stop herself. "There. Now she will go home and be angry with herself all afternoon."

"You are a terrible woman."

"I am a student of human nature. It is the same thing with better manners."

A young woman came and sat down on the bench opposite, with a nurse behind her and a look of determined friendliness. She was perhaps twenty, and pregnant, and clearly at the baths on the advice of a physician who had told her that warm water was good for the child and had not told her what to do about the women who would look at her.

"You are Antonia Cassia," she said.

"I am."

"I am Lollia. My husband is in the fourth cohort of the vigiles. He says you are the most dangerous woman in Rome." She said it with pleasure, as though she had been given a present. "I have wanted to meet you for a year. My mother says I am not to speak to you and my husband says I am not to speak to you and the priest says I am not to speak to you, and I have decided that a woman with three men telling her what to do must have something worth hearing."

Cassia laughed. It was the first unguarded laugh she had given all morning, and it surprised her. "What do you want to hear?"

"How did you do it?"

"Do what?"

"All of it. The house. The money. The men who come and go and are afraid of you. My husband is a good man and he is afraid of his commander, and his commander is afraid of the prefect, and the prefect is afraid of the emperor, and the emperor is afraid of the army, and nobody in the whole city is afraid of me." Lollia's chin came up. "I would like to be afraid of something other than my mother-in-law."

The steam curled. Tertulla was watching them both with amusement and something like tenderness.

"You will not like the answer," Cassia said.

"I have not liked many answers."

"Then here it is. I did it by being useful to people who could hurt me, and by making sure that I knew one thing about each of them that they would not want known, and by never once asking for a favour without offering something in return. I did it by working when other people were sleeping, and by remembering every name and every debt, and by never letting a man see me cry. And I did it by being willing to be disliked." Cassia leaned forward. "You are pregnant, and you are young, and you are pretty, and you have a husband who is a good man. Do not do what I did. It is a poor bargain. Be liked. Be liked and be useful and be patient, and in twenty years you will have a house full of children and a husband who does not flinch when you come into the room, and that is worth more than all the gold in my strongroom."

Lollia stared at her. Then she said, "But you have the gold."

"Yes," Cassia said. "I have the gold."

She stood, and Tertulla stood with her, and they went through into the hot room, and behind them Lollia sat with her hands on her belly and her nurse fanning her and a look on her face like a woman doing arithmetic.

Tertulla came and sat beside her, close enough that their shoulders touched. "Felix," she said.

"Felix."

"You are not surprised."

"I am never surprised. It is bad for the digestion." Cassia closed her eyes. "He came to the house after the Saturnalia. He wants two hundred thousand and he offered me a document, and I told him to come back after the Ides, and I have been thinking about him ever since."

"Two hundred thousand for what?"

"For a memory. His words."

"Whose memory?"

"Mine," Cassia said. "Or near enough."

Tertulla was quiet. Then she said, "You could pay him."

"I could. And then he would come back next year, and the year after, and the price would go up, and by the time I was fifty I would have bought my whole life back from him three times over. That is not a bargain. That is a subscription." Cassia opened her eyes. "There is only one way to deal with a man like Felix."

"Which is?"

"To make him more afraid of you than he is greedy for your money." She stood. "Come. I want the hot room before the consul's wife takes it, because she will want it for an hour and she will talk about her son the whole time."

The caldarium was a furnace. The floor was too hot to stand on without wooden sandals, and the air was so thick with steam that the bathers were only shapes, pale and moving, like fish under ice. Cassia climbed to the highest bench, where the heat was worst, and lay back with a folded cloth over her eyes, and let the sweat run.

Tertulla climbed up beside her. Their hands found each other in the dark of the cloth.

"You are frightened," Tertulla said, quietly.

"I am warm."

"You are frightened. You talk about subscriptions and bargains when you are frightened. When you are calm you talk about the price of fish."

Cassia said nothing. The steam hissed. Somewhere below, a woman laughed, and someone else shushed her, and the attendant came through with the bucket, sloshing water on the coals.

"I have spent twenty-four years," Cassia said at last, "building a wall. Every brick is a favour, a debt, a secret, a friend. I have been very careful. I have never once asked for more than a man could give, and I have never once let a man leave this house without knowing that I knew something about him that he would not want his wife to know." She lifted the cloth from her eyes. "And now a man I have never liked is standing outside the wall with a chisel, and the wall is made of people. My people. They will not put me to the question. They will put them to the question. And they will break, because everyone breaks, and then they will say things about me that are true and things that are not, and the Senate will not care which is which."

"You could go away."

"Where?"

"Anywhere. You have money."

"I have money in Rome. In Alexandria I have a letter of credit and a cousin of Hermeros who may or may not be honest. In Antioch I have nothing. In Athens I have a debt I have never collected." Cassia sat up. "And if I go, I am a woman who ran. A woman who ran is a woman who was guilty. The whole city will say it, and they will be right, and my house will be sold for debts within the year, and the people in it will be sold with the furniture."

"What do you want?"

Cassia looked at her. In the steam, with her hair flat to her head and her face flushed and her eyes steady, Tertulla looked like the only solid thing in a room made of vapour.

"I want," Cassia said, "to be the one who decides."

She leaned over and kissed her. It was not a public kiss, though it was not exactly private either; the steam hid them, and the women around them were occupied with their own sweating and their own gossip, and in Rome a kiss between two women in a bath was not a scandal, provided that neither of them was married to a man who cared. Tertulla's mouth was warm and tasted of the mint they gave out at the door. Her hand came up and rested on Cassia's neck, and her thumb moved once, slowly, against the pulse there.

"Not here," Tertulla murmured.

"No," Cassia said. "Not here."

They went down to the small pool in the corner, where the water was cold, and Cassia stepped in and gasped, and the shock of it went through her like a knife and cleared her head. Tertulla followed, and they stood in the cold water with their arms around each other, and the noise of the baths was a long way off, and for a moment Cassia allowed herself to be held.

"Whatever happens," Tertulla said into her ear, "you are not that girl any more. You are not the girl in the cell. You are the woman who owns the house. Remember that when you are frightened. You are not the one who gets sold. You are the one who does the selling."

Cassia laughed, and it came out broken. "That is the most Roman thing anyone has ever said to me."

"I have been practising."

They came out of the pool and were drying themselves when the attendant came to say that a lady was asking for the domina. Cassia, with her hair still dripping and her skin still cold, wrapped herself in the sheet and went out into the colonnade, where the winter sun was coming in low and gold and the water of the great pool was green and still.

Aemilia Celsa was sitting on the stone bench by the pool, dressed, composed, with a fan in her hand that she did not need. She had come alone. That in itself was a statement, because a senator's wife did not go to the public baths alone unless she wanted to be seen doing it.

"Domina," Cassia said.

"Domina." Aemilia looked up at her, and her eyes were the eyes of a woman who had been awake all night. "I have come to ask you a question, and I would like you to answer it honestly, because I have had enough of being lied to by people who love me."

"Ask."

"Who is Psyche?"

The name went through Cassia like the cold water. She stood very still, with her hair dripping onto the mosaic, and the whole great colonnade seemed to hold its breath.

"Where did you hear that name?"

"My mother-in-law said it in her sleep. Three nights ago. She said it twice, and then she said, 'Tell her nothing,' and then she woke up and asked for her medicine and would not look at me." Aemilia's fan did not move. "I have been in that house for nineteen years. I know every name in it — the slaves, the freedmen, the tenants, the creditors, the cousins in Gaul. I have never heard the name Psyche. And when I asked the old steward, he went the colour of a fish and said it was a woman who used to do the mending."

Cassia sat down on the bench beside her. It was not a friendly gesture, and it was not meant to be; it was the gesture of a woman who has decided that the conversation will be long.

"Psyche was a midwife," she said. "She was at Tibur, in the summer of the year eighty-eight. She was at a birth."

"Whose?"

"Mine."

Aemilia's fan stopped. For a long moment she did not move at all. Then she said, "There was a child."

"There was a child."

"What happened to it?"

"I was told it was born dead. I was told it did not breathe. I was told it was buried in the garden at Tibur, and I was not allowed to see it, and three days later I was sold to a man in Puteoli and put on a cart, and I did not come back to Rome for four years." Cassia's voice was even. "The midwife is alive. She is old and she is ill and she has sent me a ring and a message, and she says she has something to give me. I do not know what it is. I do not know whether she is telling the truth or whether she wants money or whether she has gone mad in her old age. But I am going to Tibur before the spring, and I am going to ask her."

Aemilia turned to look at her. Her face was white. "Why are you telling me this?"

"Because you asked. Because you came here alone to ask. Because you have been lied to by people who love you, and I know what that does to a person, and I would not wish it on a dog." Cassia drew the sheet closer around her shoulders. "And because whatever is coming, it is coming for your house as much as mine, and I would rather have one person inside that house who knows the truth than a whole family who does not."

Aemilia was silent for a long time. The sun moved on the water. In the distance, in the women's hall, someone dropped a bronze basin and the sound rang off the marble.

"He is my husband," Aemilia said at last.

"Yes."

"And you hate him."

"I do."

"Why?"

Cassia looked at her. There were a hundred answers, and most of them were true, and none of them were ones she would give to this woman on a stone bench in a public bath.

"Because he was there," she said, "and he did nothing. And because he has spent twenty-four years being a good man in public in order to make up for the one thing he did in private, and it has worked. He has been consul. He is an augur. Men listen to him. And I have spent twenty-four years being a woman no one will listen to, and I have had to buy my hearing." She stood. "That is the answer. You may do with it what you like."

Aemilia rose too. She folded her fan and put it in her sleeve, and she looked at Cassia for a moment with an expression that was not friendship and was not enmity.

"I will not tell him I came here," she said.

"I know you will not."

"How do you know?"

"Because you did not bring a slave, and you did not use the north entrance, and you have been sitting with your back to the door the whole time we have been speaking." Cassia almost smiled. "You are a very good liar, domina. You have spent nineteen years learning it. It is the only thing that house teaches anyone."

Aemilia's mouth twisted. "My daughter thinks I am at the temple of Juno."

"Then you had better go to the temple of Juno, or the gods will be angry and your daughter will be suspicious, and I do not know which is worse."

Aemilia turned to go. At the edge of the colonnade she stopped and said, without turning round, "The girl. Publius's girl. Her name is Calpurnia. She is in a house in the Subura that belongs to a man named Scaurus, and she is not a whore, and her mother is dead, and she has been promised to a sailor in Ostia. He has paid a deposit. If my son does not pay the rest, she will be on a boat by the Kalends of March."

"Why are you telling me?"

"Because my son will ruin himself over her, and I have no money, and you have a great deal." Now she did turn, and her face was hard. "I do not like you. I do not approve of you. I think your house is a blot on this city and I would not let my daughter cross your threshold. But I am a practical woman, and my son is going to make a mistake, and there is no one else in Rome with money and no children who might understand."

Cassia looked at her for a long moment.

"Come to the house," she said. "Not the baths. The house. On the day after the Ides, in the morning, before the men are awake. We will talk about your son, and about the midwife, and about what your mother-in-law knows. And bring the girl, if you can do it without a scandal. It is time she saw a house that is not a tomb."

***

The house was quiet that evening. Cassia bathed again at home, in her own bath, with Sabina pouring the water and Philumena doing her hair afterward, and she ate a light supper in the small room with Tertulla and Ione and Thais, and she did not talk about the baths.

Thais was fifty-four and had been with her since Puteoli. She had been the woman who ran the house in those years, when the house was three rooms above a bakery and the guests were sailors and the money was bad. She was small and brown and neat, and she could read and write and do sums and had never in her life been surprised by anything a man did. She came to Cassia's room after supper with the keys at her belt and said, "You have not asked me about the boy."

"The boy?"

"The flower seller came. She had word from Tibur." Thais sat down without being invited, because she had earned that. "Psyche is alive. She is very old and her eyes are bad and she lives with her granddaughter's family in the village under the hill. She has a box that she keeps under her bed. The granddaughter does not know what is in it. The granddaughter is frightened of it."

"Frightened why?"

"Because the old woman talks to it." Thais's face did not change. "She calls it by a name."

Cassia sat down at her desk. The lamp was lit, and the shutters were closed, and beyond them the city went on with its noise, indifferent as always.

"What name?"

"'The little master,'" Thais said.

Cassia put her hands flat on the desk and looked at them. They were a woman's hands, jewelled, soft, and they had once been a girl's hands, red from the wash water, and there was a scar across the left thumb from a knife in a kitchen in Puteoli. She looked at the scar for a while.

"Thais," she said. "What do you remember of the day I came to Puteoli?"

"Everything."

"Tell me."

Thais was quiet. Then she said, "You came in a cart. You had a cloak that did not belong to you and no shoes. You had been bleeding for three days and you would not let anyone look at you. You sat in the kitchen and would not eat, and when the man who owned the house came in and told you what you were to do, you looked at him as though you were measuring him for a coffin. I thought: that one will either kill him or own him. I was right both times."

"He died of a fever."

"He died of a fever three years later and you had bought the house by then and you were not sorry." Thais's mouth moved. "I remember that you would not say the word 'child.' For two years you would not say it. If a customer's child came into the kitchen you would leave the room. If a woman on the street was carrying a baby you would cross the road. And then one day you said it — we were counting the wine and you said, 'The child of the wine merchant is sick,' and you went on counting — and after that you were all right, or you were better, or you were a different kind of not all right."

"What kind?"

"The kind that builds a house," Thais said, "and fills it with people, and never has a child of her own."

The lamp guttered. Cassia reached out and turned the wick up, and the light steadied.

"Go to bed," she said. "Tomorrow I want the strongbox brought up, and the black book, and I want you to write down everything you know about Felix. Every debt, every woman, every bribe. Every man he has ever ruined. I want it all."

"For what?"

"Because a man who has ruined other men has enemies, and enemies are cheaper than friends." Cassia opened a drawer and took out a small box of cedarwood, and set it on the desk, and did not open it. "And because I am going to make him choose between the money and his life, and I need to know which one he loves more."

Thais rose. At the door she said, "And the boy? In Puteoli. The fuller's boy. Chrysis's brother."

"What about him?"

"Two thousand sesterces is not a great deal of money."

"No," Cassia said. "It is not."

"You could buy him and give him to her, and she would be yours forever."

"I do not want her to be mine forever. I want her to be free and to know it." Cassia looked up. "There is a difference, Thais. I have spent my whole life learning it."

Thais went out. Cassia sat alone for a long time with the cedar box in front of her. Then she opened it.

There was nothing in it but a letter. The papyrus was brown at the edges and had cracked along two of the folds, and the ink was faded to the colour of rust, and the handwriting was the careful, upright handwriting of a young man of twenty who had been taught to write by a Greek tutor with a stick. She did not read it. She had read it so many times that she could have recited it in the dark, and had done, on bad nights, in Puteoli, when the clients were gone and the house was quiet and the only sound was the sea.

She held it for a while. Then she put it back in the box and closed the lid, and put the box back in the drawer, and locked the drawer, and put the key in the fold of her belt where it had lived for twenty-four years.

Sabina came in with the evening lesson, which was letters. She had learned her alphabet from Thais and her numbers from Hermeros and her manners from everyone, and she could now read a line of Virgil slowly and with great seriousness, as though each word were a coin she had been asked to test with her teeth. Cassia sat her at the little table with the wax tablet and the stylus and heard her read.

"The arms and the man I sing," Sabina read. "Who first from the shores of Troy..." She stopped. "Domina, why does it begin with arms? Is it a song about weapons?"

"It is a song about a man who carries them."

"But it says the arms and the man. As though the arms were the important part."

Cassia looked at her. Sabina was twelve, and thin, and her hair was tied back with a strip of leather, and she had the flat, direct gaze of a child who has not yet learned to be embarrassed by her own intelligence.

"Perhaps the poet thought they were," Cassia said. "The arms are what the man is for. The man is what the arms are used by. A line like that is a way of saying that you cannot separate the two."

"That is a sad thing to begin a song with."

"It is a Roman thing to begin a song with."

Sabina considered this, and then wrote the line again on her tablet, in careful letters, and Cassia watched her do it and felt the old, strange, painful thing that she had felt for twenty-four years whenever a child did something well.

"Domina," Sabina said, without looking up, "did you have a mother?"

The room was very quiet. The lamp hissed.

"I did," Cassia said. "She died when I was small."

"What was she like?"

"She smelled of lavender. She used to sing when she was working. She had a bad temper and a good laugh, and she could not sew, and she used to tell me that the gods were not in the temples, they were in the kitchen, because that is where the food was." Cassia's voice was steady. "I do not remember her face. I remember the smell of lavender and the singing and the bad temper. That is all."

"That is a lot," Sabina said.

"Yes," Cassia said. "It is."

"Will you teach me to write a letter?"

"Who would you write to?"

"Nobody," Sabina said. "I would just like to be able to."

Cassia put her hand on the girl's head for a moment, and then took it away, because tenderness in this house was a thing that had to be measured out in small doses, like medicine.

Ione came in as Sabina was leaving. She had been out, and she was still in her cloak, and her cheeks were pink with the cold, and she stood in the doorway and watched the child go with an expression that Cassia could not read.

"She is getting clever," Ione said.

"She is getting educated. It is not the same. Cleverness is what she was born with."

"You are fond of her."

"Yes."

"More than you are of the rest of us."

Cassia looked at her. "I am fond of all of you. It is not a competition."

"Everything is a competition." Ione came in and sat down, uninvited, in the chair by the desk. "I have been thinking about what you said at the Saturnalia. That you were not a customary woman. That you had built something worth keeping."

"I did not say that."

"You said something like it. It does not matter. What matters is that I have been in this house for nine years, and I have no house of my own, and no money of my own, and no name. I have a room, and a share of the gifts, and a future that depends on your health and your temper and the goodwill of men who think of me as furniture."

"You are not furniture."

"I know. That is why I am talking to you." Ione leaned forward. "I want to buy my freedom."

Cassia was silent.

"I have saved," Ione said. "Not enough. I have a man who will lend me the rest. He is a wine merchant in the Vicus Longus, and he is fat and he is fifty and he has a wife, and he wants me to keep a house for him, and I do not want to keep a house for him, but I want to be free more than I want anything else." Her voice did not shake. "I am telling you because I would rather buy my freedom from you than run away from you, and because I think you would rather have the money than the scandal."

"How much do you have?"

"Forty thousand."

"And the man will lend you forty more."

"Yes."

"And what will he want for it?"

Ione's mouth twisted. "What do men always want?"

Cassia looked at her for a long moment. Then she said, "I will not sell you to him."

"You will not—"

"I will free you," Cassia said. "For nothing."

Ione stared at her. "Why?"

"Because you asked me a fair question and because you have been honest with me for nine years, which is longer than most wives manage, and because if I keep you here against your will, you will poison the house, and I like the house." Cassia picked up her pen. "There will be conditions. You will stay until the spring, and you will train the girl who takes your place, and you will not take any of my clients with you, and you will not tell anyone what you know about me. And you will not take the wine merchant's money, because a woman who takes a loan from a man like that is not free, whatever the paper says."

Ione's eyes were bright. "I do not know what to say."

"Say nothing. It is not a favour. It is a transaction, and I am getting the better of it." Cassia looked down at her accounts. "Go and tell Thais. She will draw up the papers, and she will be unpleasant about it, and then she will cry in the kitchen, and you will pretend not to notice."

Ione rose. At the door she said, "You are not what they say you are."

"I am exactly what they say I am," Cassia said. "I am also other things. Most people are."

There was a knock at the door of the house, and Bathyllus's voice, and then Hermeros's, and then a third voice, young and high and frightened. Cassia went out into the atrium. A boy stood there with his cloak wet from the street and his hair plastered to his forehead, and in his hand he had a wax tablet with the seal broken.

"From whom?" Cassia said.

"From the senator Corvinus," the boy said. "He said to give it to you and no one else and to wait for an answer."

She took the tablet and turned it to the lamp.

*They have found the physician's assistant. He is in the house of Pactus and he is talking. F.*

No greeting. No closing. The same four lines, the same terrible economy, twenty-four years later.

Cassia looked at the boy, who was shivering, and at Bathyllus, who was watching her, and at Hermeros, who was pretending to be interested in the accounts, and beyond them at the dark garden where the roses were black sticks and the fountain played its patient music in the cold.

"Tell him," she said, "that I will come to him. Tomorrow, at the ninth hour, at the house of my friend Tertulla, which is on the Caelian and has two doors. Tell him to come alone and to come by the garden door. Tell him that if he brings a lictor, or a friend, or a slave who can read, I will have him thrown out of the house and he can explain to the Senate why he was there."

The boy nodded and ran.

"Domina," Hermeros said, when he had gone, "is that wise?"

"No," Cassia said. "It is necessary. Wisdom is what you do when you have a choice."

---

## Chapter Five

Tibur, in the summer of the year eighty-eight.

The villa stood on the shoulder of the hill above the falls, where the Anio came down out of the hills and broke itself white on the rocks and then ran green and slow through the meadows below. The house was old and cool and full of the sound of water. It had a portico with a vine on it and a garden of clipped box and a small bathhouse with a mosaic of dolphins that had been put in by the great-grandfather and repaired by every generation since. From the terrace you could see the whole wide plain and, on a clear day, the smoke of Rome, thirty miles off, hanging over the city like a stain.

Cassia had come to Tibur with the household in the month of June, as she had come every summer since she was twelve. She was eighteen. She was a maid to Sulpicia Varia, which meant that she carried the mistress's fan and her medicine box and her writing things, and stood behind her chair in the afternoons, and was invisible in the way that a good maid is invisible, which is to say that she saw everything and was seen by no one.

She had been born in Rome, in a room over a laundry near the Circus, to a woman named Daphne who had washed clothes for the Sulpicii and had died when Cassia was nine. She did not remember much of her mother. She remembered the smell of lye and lavender, and a song about a boat, and hands that were always red and cracked and always gentle. When Daphne died, the household had kept the child, because a child is an asset and the Sulpicii had never thrown away an asset. She had worked in the kitchen, then in the linen, then in the mistress's rooms, and she had learned to read and write from the steward's clerk, who had taught her in exchange for nothing more than her company and the occasional slice of bread and honey, and who had died of a fever in the year eighty-five and left her the notebook he had copied his accounts into.

The family came to Tibur in June, and the young master came in July.

Lucius Sulpicius Corvinus was twenty. He had been away for two years — a military tribune with the legion in Syria, then a winter in Athens, then a slow road home through the islands — and he came up the hill from the road in the late afternoon with dust on his boots and a sunburn on his neck and the look of a man who has discovered that the world is larger than the house he was raised in. He was tall and lean and there was a new scar on his forearm and a new way of standing. Cassia saw him from the terrace, where she was shaking out the mistress's cushions, and he looked up and saw her, and neither of them spoke.

That evening there was a dinner. The father presided: Marcus Sulpicius Corvinus, fifty-three, consul twice, a man with a face like a coin that had been in circulation too long. He had the family's height and none of its grace. He was a hard man and a proud one, and he had spent his whole life in the conviction that the world was divided into men who were equal to him and men who were not, and that the second category was very large and the first very small. His wife sat at his right, silent and beautiful, with her fan and her medicine box and her pale eyes. She was forty-three that summer. She looked ten years younger, and she had the kind of composure that comes from never having been contradicted by anyone who mattered.

They talked about the war in the north, and the emperor's temper, and the price of the villa's wine. The young master said little. He ate slowly and looked at the garden and once, when the conversation turned to the household and the steward listed the slaves who had been bought and sold that spring, he looked at his father with an expression that Cassia, standing behind the mistress's chair with the fan, could not read.

After dinner he found her in the garden.

"You are Daphne's girl," he said.

"I am."

"I remember you. You used to bring the water to the men in the vineyard."

"You used to drink it and say thank you."

"Did I?"

"Every time. The others did not."

He looked at her for a long moment. The light was going, and the cicadas were loud, and the water from the falls came up the hill as a low, continuous sound, like a crowd very far away.

"I am sorry about your mother," he said.

"Thank you."

"I was at school in Rome when she died. I heard about it. My mother said she had been a good servant." He paused. "It seemed a small thing to say."

"It is a small thing," Cassia said. "It is also more than most people said."

He laughed. It was a surprised laugh, a young man's laugh, and it changed his face entirely. "You are not what I expected."

"What did you expect?"

"I do not know. A maid."

"I am a maid."

"Yes," he said. "That is what surprised me."

***

The summer went like this: a garden, a fountain, a boy who came to the kitchen for bread and stayed to talk. It was not a seduction. That is what Cassia would have said afterward, if anyone had asked her, and no one did for twenty-four years. It was not a seduction, because a seduction is a thing one person does to another, and this was a thing that happened to both of them at once, the way a rainstorm happens, the way a fever happens.

He found excuses. He came to the kitchen for water, for bread, for the key to the bathhouse, for a message he could have sent a slave for. He sat on the bench by the door and watched her work and talked about Syria, about the desert, about the strange gods he had seen and the men he had met and the letters he had tried to write home and torn up. He asked about her, and listened to the answers, which was the most dangerous thing of all. He asked what she thought of the poems of Catullus, which he had been reading in Athens. She had not read Catullus. The next day there was a copy of Catullus in the kitchen, left on the bench as though it had grown there.

She read it at night by the lamp in the slaves' corridor, and when she came to the poem about the girl's sparrow she cried, and she could not have told anyone why.

He kissed her first in the store-room, between the jars of oil and the baskets of figs, in the hot dead hour of the afternoon when the whole house was asleep. It was a clumsy kiss, and brief, and afterward he stepped back and said, "I should not have done that," and she said, "No," and neither of them moved, and then he did it again.

They met in the vineyard, in the little stone hut where the pruner kept his tools. They met in the bathhouse after the house was asleep, in the cold pool, with the dolphins looking at them from the wall. They met in the meadow below the falls, in the long grass, with the water roaring and the sun on their backs, and he traced the scar on her thumb with his finger and asked where she had got it, and she told him, and he said, "You will never have another scar like that. I will see to it." He said it with the absolute seriousness of a boy of twenty, and she believed him, because she was eighteen, and because the light was gold, and because no one had ever promised her anything.

He told her about his father. He told her in pieces, in the dark, with his head in her lap. He told her about the atrium and the masks and the beatings that were not beatings but "lessons," and the way his father could make a room cold by walking into it, and the way his mother had learned to be a wall. He told her that he was afraid of becoming his father, and she said that he would not, and he said, "How do you know?" and she said, "Because you are afraid of it. Your father is not afraid of anything."

She did not tell him about herself. There was not much to tell. She had no story, only a life: the laundry, the kitchen, the corridor, the lamp, the book. She told him about her mother's song and about the steward's clerk who had taught her letters, and he listened as though these were the adventures of Odysseus.

The mistress watched them. Cassia knew it, the way one knows that a spider is in the corner of a room. Sulpicia Varia said nothing, did nothing, gave no sign. She sent Cassia to the village with a message and sent the young master to the vineyards with the steward, and arranged the days so that they were never alone in the house at the same time, and it made no difference at all.

One afternoon in the middle of August she kept Cassia behind after the other maids had gone and made her sit on the stool by the window and read to her from a book of letters. It was a book of Pliny's letters, and the one she chose was about a man who had lost his wife and could not stop writing about her.

"Read that again," the mistress said, when she had finished.

Cassia read it again.

"Now tell me what it is about."

"A man who is grieving."

"No," Sulpicia Varia said. "It is about a man who wants everyone to know that he is grieving. There is a difference. The first man weeps in the dark and says nothing. The second man writes a letter and has it copied and sends it to eleven friends." She took the book back and closed it. "You are clever. Cleverer than the maids I have had. Cleverer, I sometimes think, than my son."

"I am not, domina."

"You are. He is good and he is honest and he is not clever, and that is why he will be eaten alive in this city unless he learns to be quieter than he is." She looked out of the window at the garden. "You are clever, and you are pretty, and you are a slave, and those three things together are the most dangerous combination a woman can have. Do you know what happens to women like that?"

"No, domina."

"Either they are destroyed, or they learn to be the ones who destroy." The mistress turned back to her, and her pale eyes were steady. "I have been watching you since you were twelve. You have a good heart and a bad temper and you do not forget anything. Those are useful things. They will not save you, but they are useful." She stood. "Go and tell the cook that I want the fish plain tonight, with no sauce. He will be offended. Let him be offended. It is good for cooks."

Cassia went. At the door the mistress said, "And Cassia. Do not be alone with my son in the afternoons. The house is not as empty as it looks."

In the last week of August she missed her bleeding. She did not think about it at first. Then she thought about it, and counted, and thought about it again, and one morning she was sick in the gutter behind the kitchen, and Melissa, the under-cook, a sharp-eyed woman who had seen everything twice and been surprised by none of it, looked at her and said nothing, and then, later, left a cup of mint tea on the bench where Cassia would find it.

She told him on the terrace, at night, with the plain dark below them and the smoke of Rome gone and the stars very close.

He was quiet for a long time. She watched his face and saw, for the first time, the thing that she would see again and again for twenty-four years: the way he went still when he was afraid. Not pale. Not shaking. Still, like a man in a temple waiting for the augury.

"Then we will be married," he said.

"Your father will never permit it."

"Then we will go away."

"Where?"

"Anywhere. I have money of my own — not much, but enough. We could go to Syria. I have friends there. There is a farm near Antioch that belongs to a man who owes me a favour." He took her hands. "Cassia. Look at me. I am not my father. I will not do what he would do."

"I know," she said. She did not know. But she wanted to say something that would make it true.

They had four days. On the fifth day the steward came for her in the middle of the afternoon and said that the master wished to see her in the tablinum.

***

Marcus Sulpicius Corvinus was standing at the window with his hands behind his back. He did not turn when she came in. The room was hot and smelled of ink and old paper and the wax of the seals. There was a bust of his father on the desk, and a letter open beside it, and a cup of wine that had not been touched.

"Close the door," he said.

She closed it.

"You have been fucking my son."

The word was not shouted. It was placed on the desk like a coin, precisely, in the tone of a man who has already decided what everything is worth.

"I have not—"

"Do not." He turned. His face was calm. "I have eyes in this house. I have had eyes in this house since before you were born. The gardener saw you in the vineyard. The bath slave saw you in the pool. The girl who does the linen saw you on the terrace at night with your hands in his hair." He came around the desk. "You are pregnant."

She said nothing.

"You are pregnant," he said again, "and you will tell me the truth, or I will have the physician examine you in front of the household, and then I will have you whipped for insolence, and then I will sell you to a man I know in Puteoli who buys girls for his house and does not ask them questions."

"Yes," Cassia said. "I am pregnant."

"By my son."

"Yes."

He looked at her for a long moment. Then he sat down behind the desk and poured himself a cup of wine and drank it off, and set the cup down, and said, in the same calm voice, "You have been in this house since you were twelve. You have been fed and clothed and taught to read, which was not my doing and which I have always thought was a mistake. My wife is fond of you, in her way. You have had a better life than most of the people in that kitchen, and you have had it because we are a family that takes care of its own." He looked up. "And this is how you repay us."

"I love him."

"You are a slave."

"He loves me."

"My son is twenty years old and has been in the East for two years and has never in his life been told no by a woman. He does not love you. He wants you. There is a difference, and it is the difference between a man and a boy." He reached for the letter on the desk. "He will be sent to Syria at the end of the month. There is a staff posting with the governor. I had intended to refuse it. I will accept it now."

Cassia stood very still. The room was hot. Somewhere outside, a bird was calling, over and over, the same three notes.

"And me?"

"You will be sold. Not here — there is no market at Tibur, and I will not have the household talking. You will go to Puteoli, to a woman named Lusca, who keeps a house and is discreet, and who will pay me a fair price for a girl with a pretty face and a child in her belly. She will take the child off your hands. It will be exposed, or sold, or given to a farm; I do not care which. You will not see it." He set the letter down. "That is the arrangement. You may go."

She did not move. She was eighteen years old, and she had been a slave all her life, and she had learned in that life that there is a moment in every conversation when a person with power has finished speaking and is waiting for you to be afraid. She had seen it a hundred times. She had never been on the other side of it before.

"Does your wife know?" she said.

His eyes narrowed. "What?"

"Does she know about Puteoli? About Lusca? About the child?"

"That is not your concern."

"She will find out. She always finds out. She knew about the girl in the kitchen at Rome, the one you sent away in the spring. She knew about the wine merchant's daughter. She knows everything, and she says nothing, and one day she will say something, and it will not be to you."

The blow came fast and flat, an open hand across the face, and it knocked her against the door. She tasted blood. She stood up, and put her hand to her mouth, and looked at him.

"Get out," he said.

She got out.

***

Sulpicia Varia came to her that night, in the corridor by the linen room, with a lamp in her hand.

Cassia was sitting on the floor with her back against the wall. Her face was swelling. She had been sitting there for three hours, because there was nowhere else to go, and because the house was small and every corner of it had a door that led to a room where she had been happy.

The mistress stood over her. She was in her nightgown, with her hair down, and without her jewels and her fan she looked smaller and older and strangely ordinary.

"He has told you," she said.

"Yes."

"And you believe him?"

"Why would he lie?"

"He would not lie. He is many things, but he is not a liar." Sulpicia Varia set the lamp down on the floor between them. "My husband has made arrangements. I have come to make a different one."

Cassia looked up at her.

"I have no power in this house," the mistress said, "except the power of a woman who has been married to that man for twenty-six years and has learned where the keys are kept and which of the servants can be trusted and which of the physicians is in debt. I cannot stop him selling you. I cannot stop him sending my son away. But I can make it easier." She crouched down, her skirts folding on the stone, and her face came into the lamplight. "I can make it quick. There is a draught I can give you, on the night it happens. You will sleep. You will not feel the birth as you would otherwise feel it, and you will not remember much of it afterward. And when you wake, it will be over, and you will be on the road, and you will not have to carry the memory of it for the rest of your life."

"Why would you do that?"

"Because you are a person," Sulpicia Varia said, "and because I have watched you grow up in this house, and because I have no daughter of my own, and because I do not wish to think of you in that room at Puteoli remembering what was done to you here." She paused. "And because I am a practical woman, and a girl who screams is a girl who is remembered, and I do not want this house remembered."

Cassia looked at her. In the lamplight the mistress's face was kind, and tired, and entirely sincere.

"Will the child be buried?" Cassia said.

"Yes."

"Here? In the garden?"

"By the fig tree. I will see to it myself."

"Will you tell me when it is done?"

"I will tell you," Sulpicia Varia said. "I will tell you before you go."

She reached out and took Cassia's hand. Her own hand was warm and dry and very steady.

"Drink what I give you," she said. "Drink all of it. And when you wake, do not look back."

***

They came for her in the last week of September, on a night with no moon.

She had been having pains since the afternoon. The physician had been and gone, and the midwife had come at dusk with her bag and her bronze ring and her quiet, competent hands. Psyche was sixty that year, a small woman with grey hair and a face like a walnut, and she had delivered half the babies in the village and had never lost a mother. She put her hand on Cassia's belly and said, "Soon. Before morning. You will have an easy one; I can tell." And Cassia, who had been told that nothing about her life would ever be easy, had almost laughed.

The room was a cell at the back of the villa, next to the storeroom. It had a bed and a stool and a small high window, and the door had a bolt on the outside. There was a lamp on the stool and a basin on the floor, and the walls were whitewashed, and someone had scratched a fish into the plaster, a child's drawing, very old.

Sulpicia Varia came at the ninth hour with a cup in her hand.

It was a small cup, silver, and the liquid in it was dark and smelled of honey and something bitter underneath.

"Drink it now," she said. "All of it. It will make the pains easier, and it will help you sleep between them."

Cassia took the cup. She was eighteen years old and in pain and frightened, and the woman who had been kind to her was standing over her with a lamp, and outside the door a man was waiting to put her on a cart in the morning.

She drank it.

It tasted of honey and then of almonds, and then of nothing at all.

The room went soft at the edges. The lamp became three lamps, and then a smear of gold. She heard the door open and close, and voices, and the midwife saying something in a low voice, and the mistress answering, and then a long silence in which the only sound was the blood moving in her own ears.

The pains came and went, and she floated above them, and at some point she was pushing, and the midwife was telling her to push, and there was a great pressure and a tearing and a wet rush, and then a silence that went on too long.

Then a cry. Thin and furious and alive.

"A boy," Psyche said. "A fine boy. Look at him."

Cassia tried to open her eyes. The room was dark and turning. She saw, through a haze, the midwife's hands, and something small and red and moving, and then a shadow came between her and the light.

"Give him to me," Sulpicia Varia said.

The midwife said something. Cassia could not hear the words. She heard the tone — a question, a protest.

"Give him to me," the mistress said again, "and remember what you were paid, and what you were told, and what will happen to you if you forget it."

The baby stopped crying. Cassia heard the small, wet, working sounds of a newborn being wrapped in cloth, and then the sounds were muffled, and then they were somewhere else in the room, and then they were at the door.

"Psyche," the mistress said. "The cup. All of it. And then you will go to the steward and tell him it is done, and you will say nothing to the girl, and in the morning you will tell her the child did not breathe."

"Domina, he is breathing now—"

"He will not be breathing by the time she wakes," Sulpicia Varia said, "because he will not be here. Do you understand me?"

"Give him to me," the mistress said again, "and do what you were paid to do."

And then Cassia's body, which had been holding her up out of the dark for a long time, let go, and she went down into it, and the last thing she heard was the crying, going away, getting smaller, until it was only a sound in a dream.

***

She woke in the morning to a grey light and a pain like a knife and the taste of almonds in her mouth.

The room was empty. The basin had been emptied and the floor washed. The lamp had been taken away, and the bed had been changed, and she was clean and dressed in a plain shift, and there was a cup of water on the stool.

She got up. It took a long time. She went to the door and found it unbolted, and she opened it, and the corridor was empty, and the storeroom door was open, and the morning light was coming in through the colonnade at the end.

Psyche was in the kitchen, drinking a bowl of broth. She looked up when Cassia came in, and her face did something complicated.

"Where is my son?" Cassia said.

The midwife set the bowl down.

"Domina," she said.

"Where is my son?"

"Stillborn," Psyche said. "He did not breathe. It happens. He was a fine boy, but he did not breathe, and the mistress took him to be buried."

"You said he cried."

"The medicine makes you dream." The midwife's eyes were steady. "You were dreaming. I am sorry. He did not breathe."

Cassia stood in the kitchen with her hand on the table. The room was warm. The fire was lit. Somewhere a dog was barking, and a woman was singing, and the world was going on exactly as it had gone on before.

"I want to see him," she said.

"He is buried."

"Then dig him up."

"Domina—"

"Dig him up. I want to see him. I want to see his face."

Psyche looked at her for a long moment. Then she said, "The mistress has given orders. You are to be on the cart by the third hour. If you are not on the cart, the steward has been told to tie you to it." She picked up her bowl again. "I am sorry. I have delivered a hundred babies in this village and I have never lost a mother, and I have lost three children, and it never gets easier, and there is nothing I can say to you that would help." She drank her broth. "Eat something. The road to Puteoli is long."

The cart came at the third hour. It was a farm cart, driven by a man who did not look at her, with a load of wine jars and a folded blanket on the boards. The steward walked her to it. The household watched from the doors and the windows — the cook, the gardener, the girl who did the linen, the boy who fed the pigeons. No one said anything. There was nothing to say that would not be reported.

The last thing she saw of Tibur was the fig tree at the corner of the garden, with the morning sun on it, and the earth below it disturbed.

She was in Puteoli by the evening of the second day. Lusca's house was three streets up from the harbour, with a green door and a lamp over it, and a woman with dyed hair and a fat, kind, entirely merciless face who looked her over the way a farmer looks at a horse.

"Eighteen," Lusca said. "Pretty. Not too thin. What did she have?"

"A boy," the driver said. "Stillborn."

"Good. No brats to worry about." She took Cassia's chin in her hand and turned her face to the light. "You will do. You will do very well. Get her inside and fed, and then we will see about the blood."

The green door closed. The bolt went across. Somewhere in the house a woman was laughing, and a man was shouting about a cup, and the sea was three streets away and could be heard in every room, like something enormous breathing in its sleep.

A woman came out of the kitchen with a bowl of soup and a hunk of bread and stood in front of her. She was small and brown and neat, and she looked at Cassia the way a physician looks at a wound, without pity and without disgust, and she said, "Eat."

Cassia did not move.

"Eat," the woman said again. "You have been on a cart for two days and you have a fever coming and if you do not eat you will be no use to anyone, including yourself." She put the bowl into Cassia's hands. "My name is Thais. I run the kitchen and the accounts and the girls when Lusca is drunk, which is most of the time. You will do as I say and you will not cry in front of the clients and you will not run, because there is a man at the back door with a stick and he is quicker than he looks." She turned to go, and then turned back. "And you will not believe anything they tell you in the first month. Not the men, not the girls, not the mistress. Nothing. Do you understand?"

"Yes," Cassia said.

"Good." Thais looked at her for a moment longer. "What was his name?"

Cassia's mouth opened. No sound came out.

"The child's name," Thais said. "Or the father's. Either."

"I do not know," Cassia said. "I never gave him one."

"Then give him one now," Thais said, "and keep it to yourself, and never say it out loud in this house. A name is the only thing they cannot take from you if you do not tell them what it is."

Cassia stood in the dark of the entrance hall with her hands at her sides and thought about the cry. She had heard it. She knew she had heard it. And for twenty-four years afterward, whenever she was very tired or very ill or very close to sleep, she would hear it again — thin and furious and alive, going away from her, getting smaller, until it was only a sound in a dream.

---

## Chapter Six

Tertulla's house on the Caelian was smaller than Cassia's and older than the Republic, and it had two doors: one on the street, where the clients came, and one in the garden wall at the end of an alley where the rubbish was put out. Cassia came by the garden door. She came alone, with her veil over her face and a lamp under her cloak, and Bathyllus walked behind her at a distance of twenty paces with a knife in his sleeve and an expression of profound disapproval.

"An hour," he said at the gate. "If you are not out in an hour, I am coming in."

"Two."

"An hour and a half, and I will knock loudly and embarrass you."

"That is the most insubordinate thing anyone has said to me this year," Cassia said, "and it is January."

The garden was small and bare and smelled of wet earth and rosemary. Tertulla was waiting at the door of the little dining room with a lamp in her hand and a face that was doing its best to be neutral and failing.

"He is here," she said.

"I know."

"He came by the garden door and he stood in my atrium and looked at the ceiling for a long time and then he said, 'This is a very old house,' and I said, 'Yes,' and then neither of us said anything for a quarter of an hour." Tertulla's mouth twisted. "I do not like him."

"You do not have to like him."

"I have to watch him look at you. That is different." She put her hand on Cassia's arm. "I will be in the kitchen. If you need me, scream. If you do not need me, scream anyway, and I will come and be rude to him."

She went out. Cassia stood in the corridor for a moment, with the lamp and the cold and the smell of rosemary, and then she opened the door.

He was standing by the window with his back to her, in a dark cloak, his hands behind his back. It was the posture of his father; she had seen it in a tablinum at Tibur twenty-four years before, and the sight of it went through her like a blade.

"You came," she said.

"I said I would."

"Men say a great many things."

He turned. In the lamplight he looked older than he had in the forum; the lines around his mouth were deeper, and there was a greyness under his skin that had not been there at the Kalends. He had not slept. She recognized the signs because she had not slept either.

"Thank you for seeing me," he said.

"Sit down, Lucius. You are making the room smaller."

He sat. She sat opposite him, with the table between them, and for a moment neither of them spoke. The lamp burned. Somewhere in the house a door closed, and there was the sound of a woman laughing, very far away.

"Tell me what you know," Cassia said.

He told her. It took a quarter of an hour, and it was worse than she had feared.

Felix had come to him in December, before the Saturnalia, with a demand for three hundred thousand sesterces and a story. The story was this: a man named Faustus, who had been the assistant to the physician who had attended his father in his last illness, was now living in a room above a wine shop on the Aventine, and he had been talking. He had been talking to a woman named Psyche, a midwife, who had been at Tibur in the summer of eighty-eight. He had been talking to the old steward, who was retired and lived in a cottage near Ficulea and had a bad chest and a daughter who was not married. He had been talking, in fact, to anyone who would listen, and he had been paid to talk by a man who was not Felix, and Felix knew who the man was and would not say.

"And the accusation?"

"Parricide. That I procured the death of my father." Corvinus's voice was flat. "It is absurd. It is legally absurd. A man cannot be accused of parricide on the word of a physician's assistant and a midwife and a steward who is dying. But it does not have to be true to be laid. It only has to be laid before the consul, and then there is a trial, and a trial is a year, and in a year the Asian command is gone, the augurate is gone, the house is gone, and I am a man who was accused of killing his father and was acquitted, which in this city is worse than being convicted."

"Who is behind it?"

"Pactus. It must be Pactus. He has wanted the Asian command since he was praetor, and he has never had the name for it, and he knows that if I am out of the way the lot will fall to him."

"Pactus is a new man. He cannot afford a scandal. If it comes out that he hired a delator to destroy a consular, the emperor will eat him alive." Cassia shook her head. "No. If Pactus is involved, he is a partner, not the principal. There is someone behind him who wants the Sulpicii damaged and has the money to pay Felix and the patience to wait."

"Who?"

"I do not know yet. I will find out." She looked at him. "Now tell me the rest."

"What rest?"

"The part you have not told me. The part you have been carrying for twenty-four years. Tell me what happened that night. Tell me what you knew, and when you knew it, and what you did."

He was silent for a long time. The lamp guttered, and he reached out and turned the wick up, and the light steadied, and his face was closed and careful and entirely without defence.

"I knew my father had found out," he said. "He told me on the twenty-first of September. He called me into the tablinum and he told me that he knew, and that I was a fool, and that the girl would be sold and the child disposed of, and that I would be on a ship to Syria before the month was out. I argued with him. I told him I would marry you. He laughed at me." Corvinus's hands were flat on the table. "I told him I would go to the governor of the province and ask for a posting and take you with me. He said that if I did, he would have you sold to a house in Puteoli and he would make sure I never found you. He said he had done it before, with a girl in Rome, and that I would forget you in a year, and that in ten years I would thank him."

"And you believed him?"

"I believed he could do it. I did not believe I would forget you." He looked up. "I went to my mother. I thought — I do not know what I thought. I thought she might help. She was always the one who could manage him. She listened to me and she said that she would speak to him, and that I was to do nothing, and that she would send word to you. And then the next morning she came to me and told me that you had gone into labour in the night, and that the child was dead, and that you were dead."

Cassia sat very still. The room was cold. She could hear her own breathing, and the lamp, and the rain that had started outside, a thin, steady rain on the roof tiles.

"She told me you were dead," Corvinus said. "She told me you had a fever and the child was stillborn and you died before dawn. She had the steward bring me the physician's certificate. I did not see the body. I did not ask to see the body. I was twenty years old and I believed my mother, and I got on a ship to Syria three days later, and I did not come back to Rome for two years, and when I came back the villa at Tibur had been sold and the household had been broken up and there was no one left to ask."

"She sold me," Cassia said.

"I know."

"She sold me to a woman named Lusca, who kept a brothel in Puteoli, and I was there for four years, and I was beaten and I was used and I was made to do things that I will not tell you and that you will not ask about, and I survived because I am the kind of woman who survives, and I bought my freedom with money I earned on my back and with the help of a man who was kind to me and died of a dropsy, and I have spent twenty-four years building a life out of the wreck of that night." Her voice did not rise. "And you got on a ship."

He did not look away. "Yes."

"And you did not come back."

"I came back. Two years later. The house was empty. The fig tree was still there. I stood in the garden and I could not remember your face. I could remember your voice and the way you held your head and the way you read Catullus out loud in the kitchen, and I could not remember your face, and I thought that was the worst thing that had ever happened to me." He laughed, once, without any humour at all. "I was a fool. I was a boy. I have been a boy for twenty-four years, and I am tired of it."

Cassia looked at him across the table. The rain came down. The lamp burned. Somewhere in the house Tertulla was moving about in the kitchen, and the sound of her was small and ordinary and comforting.

"There is something you should know," she said.

"Tell me."

"The midwife is alive. Psyche. She is old and she is ill and she lives at Tibur, and she has sent me a message. She says she has something to give me." Cassia watched his face. "She was there that night. She delivered the child. She told me he was stillborn, and I heard him cry, and I have spent twenty-four years telling myself that I dreamed it."

Corvinus's face changed. It was not a large change. It was the change of a man who has been holding a door shut for a very long time and has just felt the wood begin to give.

"He cried," he said.

"He cried. And then your mother took him out of the room, and the midwife gave me something to make me sleep, and when I woke the room was clean and the child was buried under the fig tree and I was on a cart to Puteoli." Cassia leaned forward. "If she has something to give me, Lucius, it is not a ring. It is not a memory. It is either a grave with a body in it, or it is a name."

"A name," he said.

"A name and an age and a place. There is a boy somewhere in this world who is twenty-four years old and who does not know who his mother is. I am going to Tibur, and I am going to find out, and I am going to bring him home if he is alive." She said it without emphasis, as though she were discussing a shipment of wine. "And you are going to help me."

He was silent. She watched him and let the silence work, because silence was one of the tools of her trade and she had learned to use it as a carpenter uses a plane.

"Who else knows?" she said. "About the witnesses. About the accusation."

"Felix. Pactus, if he is behind it. My mother, though she will not admit it. The old steward at Ficulea, who is frightened and dying and has a daughter who is not married." He rubbed his face. "And a man in the prefect's office, I think. Felix said something about a manifest. I did not understand it at the time."

Cassia's head came up. "The grain manifest."

"What?"

"Nothing. Something else. Something that may be useful." She was already thinking — Comptus, the manifest, the clerk with the inky fingers who had been talking to someone. A chain of small men, each holding one link. "Go on."

"That is all. That is everything I know."

"Then we have something to work with. A frightened steward can be reassured. A clerk can be bought. A delator can be broken. Your mother is the only one who cannot be dealt with, because she does not want money and she does not want safety. She wants the past to stay where it is." Cassia's mouth tightened. "And the past is not going to stay where it is, because the midwife is alive and I am going to Tibur."

He was silent.

"You owe it," she said.

"I know."

"Do you? Because this is not a favour, Lucius. This is not a debt I am calling in to make you feel better. This is a child. If he is alive, he has been raised by whoever your mother sold him to, and he has been told whatever they told him, and he has no idea that there is a woman in Rome who has been listening for him for twenty-four years." Her voice was steady. "And if he is dead, then I want to stand at the grave and I want you standing beside me, because I did it alone the first time and I will not do it alone again."

He looked at her. His eyes were wet, and he did not wipe them, and she did not look away, and in the long silence that followed, the rain stopped as suddenly as it had begun, and the room was very quiet.

"Yes," he said. "I will help you."

"Then we have a bargain."

"We have a bargain."

She stood up. She did not know what she had intended to do — to leave, to call Bathyllus, to go into the kitchen and let Tertulla be rude to him. What she did instead was walk around the table and stand in front of him, and he stood up, and they were close enough that she could smell the cedar and the wool and the rain on his cloak.

"You are still wearing the same oil," she said.

"My mother's housekeeper buys it. I have never thought to change it."

"I hated it. For twenty-four years, I hated the smell of it. I would smell it in a crowd and I would have to stop walking."

"I am sorry."

"Stop saying that."

"I am sorry," he said, and then he laughed, a wrecked sort of laugh, and she put her hand flat on his chest, to stop him, or to steady him, or to feel whether he was real.

The kiss was not like the kisses in the vineyard. It was not young and it was not easy and it was not a promise of anything. It was a collision. He made a sound against her mouth, and his hands came up and held her face, and she gripped the front of his tunic and pulled him down and kissed him back as though she were trying to take something out of him by force.

They broke apart. Both of them were breathing hard.

"That is not part of the bargain," she said.

"No."

"It is a very bad idea."

"Yes."

She kissed him again.

***

Afterward they lay in Tertulla's bed, in the room with the blue ceiling, and the rain had started again and was ticking on the roof. The lamp was low. Corvinus's arm was under her head, and her hand was on his chest, and she could feel his heart going, slow now, steady, like a man who had run a long way and stopped.

"That was a mistake," she said.

"It was."

"We are going to do it again."

"Probably."

She laughed, and then stopped laughing, because the laugh had turned into something else in her throat, and she lay still until it passed. He did not say anything. He stroked her hair, and waited, and after a while she was able to breathe normally again.

"Tell me about your life," he said. "Not the war. Not the Senate. The life."

"There is no life. There is the house."

"Tell me about your wife."

He was quiet. "Aemilia is a good woman. She is cleverer than I am and she has known it since the second year of our marriage and she has never once said so. She came to me at eighteen with a dowry and a trunk of books and a great deal of hope, and I gave her a cold house and a mother who hated her and a husband who was somewhere else even when he was in the room." He looked at the ceiling. "I have tried to be kind to her. Kindness is not the same as love, and she knows the difference, and she has never complained. That is the worst of it. She has never complained."

"She came to see me at the baths."

He turned his head. "She did what?"

"She came alone, without a slave, and she asked me who Psyche was." Cassia watched his face. "She knows something is wrong. She does not know what. She has been in that house for nineteen years and she has been lied to by everyone in it, and she is beginning to understand that the lies are not for her protection."

Corvinus closed his eyes. "I should have told her."

"Yes."

"I did not know how."

"Men never do. That is why women learn to read faces." She propped herself up on one elbow. "If we survive this, Lucius, you will tell her. Not because it is good for the family, and not because it will make you feel better. Because she is your wife and she has earned the truth, and because a house built on a lie will fall on the people inside it, and I know that better than anyone in Rome."

"Tell me about the house."

So she told him. She told him about buying it with money from the brothel and the brickworks and the shipping venture, and about the strongroom behind the panel, and about Thais and Hermeros and Bathyllus, and about Sabina learning her letters and Celer and his fish sauce and Chrysis and her brother. She told him about the clients, and the games, and the parties, and the way a senator will tell a woman things at midnight that he would not tell his own brother at noon. She told him about the black book, though not where it was kept.

"And you are happy?" he said.

"I am busy."

"That is not an answer."

"It is the only answer I have." She turned her head on his shoulder. "Happiness is a thing that happens to other people. I have a house, and a fortune, and twenty-three people who depend on me, and a seat at every table in Rome that matters, and no one has called me a slave in sixteen years. That is not happiness. It is better than happiness. Happiness can be taken away."

"Can it?"

"Everything can be taken away. That is the first thing I learned and the last thing I will forget." She sat up, pulling the coverlet with her. "Now. The bargain. What do we do about Felix?"

He sat up too. The mood changed, the way it does when two people who have been lovers remember that they are also, in the eyes of the world, two parties to a negotiation.

"Felix wants three hundred thousand," he said. "I do not have it. If I sell the vineyard, I will have perhaps a hundred and fifty. My mother has jewellery. My wife has a dowry, which is not mine to touch. The house is mortgaged to a banker named Sittius."

"Then we do not pay him."

"He will go to the consul."

"Let him. A delator who goes to the consul with a story and no evidence is a delator who gets fined." Cassia pushed her hair back. "The question is not Felix. The question is who is paying Felix. Find that out and we can break him. A man like that has no loyalty. He has a price, and when someone else offers a better price, he will sell his patron in a heartbeat."

"How do we find out?"

"I will find out. I have people in this city who owe me favours, and one of them is a clerk in the office of the prefect of the grain supply, and another is a woman who does the hair of the wife of a man who is close to Pactus." She looked at him. "You will do something else. You will go to your mother and you will ask her what she knows about the midwife."

He was silent.

"She gave me the cup, Lucius."

"I know."

"She told me it would make it easier. She told me the child would be buried under the fig tree. She stood in the room while the midwife took him away, and she told her to remember what she had been paid." Cassia's voice was very calm. "I have spent twenty-four years believing that she was the only person in that house who was kind to me. I would like to know whether I was wrong."

"My mother is dying."

"I know."

"She has perhaps three months. Perhaps less. The physician will not say, but I have seen it before. She is in pain all the time now, and she does not sleep, and she has begun to talk about my father as though he were in the next room." He looked at his hands. "If I ask her, she will tell me the truth, and it will kill her."

"Then it will kill her," Cassia said. "She has had sixty-seven years and a house on the Palatine and a son who has spent his whole life trying to please her. I had a night in a cell and a cart to Puteoli. I am not going to be careful with her feelings."

He looked at her for a long moment. Then he nodded.

"I will ask her," he said. "But not tonight. Tomorrow. When she is awake and lucid and has her medicine." He got up and began to dress. "And Cassia—"

"Yes?"

"Whatever she says. Whatever she did. I did not know. I want you to know that I did not know."

"I know," Cassia said. "That is the worst of it."

He stopped with his cloak half on. "What?"
