"If you had known, you would have been a monster. If you had known and done nothing, I could hate you cleanly, and it would be simple, and I would be free of you." She pulled the coverlet up. "But you did not know, and you did not come, and you married a woman you do not love and you have spent twenty-four years being a good man in a bad house, and I cannot hate you cleanly. I have tried. I have tried for twenty-four years. It does not work."

He came back to the bed and stood over her. In the lamplight his face was tired and open and entirely without defences.

"I will come to Tibur with you," he said. "When you go. I will find a way."

"You will not. If you are seen on the road with me, the whole city will know by the Ides, and then the accusation will not matter because the scandal will be worse." She shook her head. "You will stay in Rome and you will do what I tell you. That is the bargain."

He bent down and kissed her forehead, and then her mouth, and then he went out. She heard him in the corridor, and the garden door, and the rain, and then Bathyllus's voice, low and unfriendly, and then nothing.

She lay in the bed for a while with her eyes open. Then she got up and dressed and went into the kitchen, where Tertulla was sitting at the table with a cup of wine and a face like a thundercloud.

"Well," Tertulla said.

"Well."

"Are you going to tell me, or am I going to have to guess?"

"You are going to have to guess," Cassia said, and sat down, and poured herself a cup, and drank it in one go. "And then you are going to be angry with me, and I am going to let you, and then we are going to have supper."

Tertulla looked at her for a long moment. Then she reached across the table and took her hand.

"I am not angry," she said. "I am frightened. There is a difference."

"Yes," Cassia said. "I know."

***

On the Palatine, the lamps were still burning in the old woman's room.

Corvinus came in out of the rain and stood in the doorway of the atrium, dripping, and looked at the masks in their cupboards, and at the small bronze lamp that burned before them, and at the slave who had come to take his cloak and was waiting, patiently, to be told what to do.

"Leave it," he said. "Go to bed."

Aemilia was standing at the end of the corridor, in her nightgown, with a lamp in her hand.

"You are wet," she said.

"It is raining."

"I heard the door. I thought it was a thief." She looked at him. "You have been with her."

He did not answer.

"I am not going to make a scene in the corridor at midnight, and I am not going to weep, and I am not going to ask you to choose, because I know what you would choose and I do not want to hear it said out loud." Her voice was perfectly steady. "I am going to ask you one thing. When this is over — whatever this is — will you tell me the truth? All of it? Not the version that protects me. The truth."

"Yes," he said.

She looked at him for a long moment. Then she stepped aside to let him pass.

"Your mother has been asking for you," she said. "She has been asking for you since the ninth hour. She will not say why. She has sent the boy out twice to look for you in the street." She turned away. "Whatever she wants, Lucius, hear her out. She is dying, and dying people are the only honest people in this city."

The slave went. Corvinus walked through the house to his mother's room and found the door open and the physician's boy asleep on a stool outside it. He went in.

Sulpicia Varia was propped up on her pillows with her eyes closed. Her face was the colour of candle wax, and her hands on the coverlet were so thin that he could see the shape of the bones. The room smelled of camphor and sickness and the lavender they put in the linen.

She opened her eyes.

"You went to her," she said.

"Yes."

"You smell of her."

"I know."

"Did she tell you that I poisoned your father?"

The room was very quiet. The lamp on the table burned without a flicker. Outside, the rain had stopped, and the night was cold and clear, and the stars over the Palatine were the same stars that had shone on a garden at Tibur twenty-four years before.

"No," Corvinus said. "She told me that you gave her a cup."

"I did."

"Did you kill him?"

His mother looked at him for a long time. Her eyes, in the ruin of her face, were still the eyes he had known since he was a child: pale and cold and entirely without self-deception.

"Not with the cup," she said. "The cup was for her, and it was only poppy and honey and a little of the stuff the physician used for the pain. It was not poison. She was not poisoned. She was asleep." She shifted, and winced, and went on. "Your father died of an apoplexy, in his bed, at the third hour of the morning, and the physician was called and he signed the certificate and he was paid for his signature, and I have never told anyone in this house a word of what happened before that, and I am not going to tell you now."

"Did you kill him?"

"I did not put anything in his cup," she said. "I put something in his wine at dinner, and he drank it, and he went to his room, and he was dead by morning. It was not enough to kill a strong man. He was not a strong man. He was a bully and a coward and a man who beat his son with a strap and called it education, and his heart was bad, and the physician had told him two years before that he must not excite himself." She closed her eyes. "I did not know whether it would kill him. I hoped it would. I have hoped it every day for twenty-four years, and I have confessed it every month to a priest who is now dead, and I am not sorry."

Corvinus stood by the bed. His hands were shaking. He was forty-five years old, and a consular, and an augur, and he had spoken in the Senate a hundred times, and he could not think of a single thing to say.

"The child," he said at last.

"What child?"

"Do not. Do not do that. The child. My child. The boy she was carrying." He heard his own voice rise and did not stop it. "The midwife is alive. She is at Tibur. She has something to give her. What did you do with my son?"

Sulpicia Varia opened her eyes. She looked at him with an expression that he had never seen on her face before, and that he would think about for the rest of his life: not guilt, not defiance, but calculation.

"He is dead," she said.

"Is he?"

"Yes."

"Then where is the grave?"

"Under the fig tree. As I told her."

"Then I will have it opened."

"No," his mother said, "you will not." And for the first time in the whole of his life, her voice was not cold; it was frightened. "You will not open that grave. You will not go to Tibur. You will not speak to the midwife. You will let this die, Lucius, as I have spent twenty-four years making sure that it dies. There is more at stake here than a girl and a brat. There is the house. There is the name. There is the Asian command, and the augurate, and everything your father and his father and his father's father built." She pushed herself up on her elbows. "If this comes out, we are finished. Not disgraced. Finished. There is no house, no name, no money, no marriage for your daughter, no future for your son. You will be the man who killed his father, or you will be the man whose mother killed his father and who said nothing. Either way, you are finished, and the Sulpicii are finished with you."

Corvinus looked at his mother. The rain had stopped. The lamp burned. Somewhere in the house, his wife was asleep, and his children were asleep, and the masks in the atrium looked out of their cupboards into the dark.

"Then I am finished," he said.

He turned and went out. Behind him, in the room, his mother called his name once, and then again, louder, and then the physician's boy woke up and came running, and there was the sound of a woman weeping, which was a sound he had never heard in that house before, and which he would hear in his dreams for years afterward.

---

## Chapter Seven

The Lupercalia fell that year on a day of thin sunshine and a wind that came off the hills and smelled of snow. By the second hour the streets around the Palatine were packed, and by the third the whole lower city had poured into the Circus Valley and the Forum and the slopes of the hill, and Rome was doing what Rome did best, which was to turn a sacred rite into an excuse for a party.

The house had been awake since dawn. The women were doing one another's hair, which on a festival day was a serious business and involved a great deal of shouting. Celer had been up since the third hour making honey cakes for the household and a dish of lentils for the workers, because Celer believed that a festival without lentils was a pagan festival and a pagan festival was bad for the digestion. The kitchen boys had been given the morning off and had spent it in the street throwing nuts at a cat.

Chrysis had not wanted to go. She had been quiet since the Saturnalia, and quieter since the news from Tibur, and she stood in the doorway of the kitchen with her mantle already on and her face set in the expression of a woman who has decided to be brave in public and is not looking forward to it.

"You will go," Cassia said.

"I do not want to."

"You will go, and you will laugh at the men with the goatskin, and you will eat something, and you will come home tired, and tomorrow you will feel better. That is what festivals are for." Cassia straightened her collar. "And Chrysis. If anyone speaks to you in the street, you will be polite and you will keep walking."

"Who would speak to me?"

"Nobody. That is the point." Cassia looked at her. "The man who came about your brother has not come back. That is either because he was lying, or because he is waiting for you to become frightened enough to pay him. Either way, he will come back, and when he does, he will come to the house, and I will deal with him. You will not."

Chrysis nodded. At the door she stopped and said, without turning, "Domina. If my brother is alive, and if he is bought and brought here, what will he be? To me, I mean. Will he be my brother, or will he be a boy I have bought?"

Cassia was silent for a moment.

"He will be both," she said. "That is the difficulty."

Cassia did not go to the Lupercal. She had gone once, at nineteen, and had been struck across the back with a goatskin thong by a young man in a mask who had laughed and run on, and she had felt the sting of it for two days and had not become pregnant, and had drawn her own conclusions. But the house went. The house always went. Thais took the women in a body, with Bathyllus in front and two of the kitchen boys behind, and even the ones who had to work that night were allowed the morning, because a festival is a thing that belongs to everyone, including the people who have nothing else.

Sabina went with Thais. She had been given a new cloak and a handful of nuts and a mask of a laughing satyr that was too big for her, and she came back at the fifth hour with her mask askew and her hair full of straw and a look on her face that Cassia recognized, because it was the look of a child who has seen the city for the first time and has begun to understand that it is not a thing that has been arranged for her benefit.

"There were men with no clothes on," Sabina said.

"There are always men with no clothes on. It is the point of the festival."

"They had a goat. They cut its throat and smeared the blood on two boys and then they laughed and ran." Sabina's eyes were wide. "One of them had a knife and he was laughing. And a woman was screaming because a man had hit her with the thong and she was bleeding."

"Did she want to be hit?"

"I do not know. She was laughing too." Sabina frowned. "I did not understand it. Everyone was laughing and there was blood. Is that what a festival is?"

Cassia looked at her. The child was twelve years old and had just discovered the central fact of the city, which was that it was built out of things that were funny and things that were cruel and could not always tell them apart.

"Some of it," Cassia said. "The rest is bread and wine and the day off."

"I liked the part with the bread," Sabina said.

***

Cassia watched the festival from the roof.

The house had a flat roof over the kitchen wing, reached by a narrow stair and a door that was kept locked, and from it you could see the whole valley of the Circus and the slope of the Palatine with its palaces and its temples, and the smoke rising from a hundred cooking fires, and the river shining away to the west like a length of bronze. Tertulla came up to join her, and Hermeros, and Chrysis, who had been silent for days and who stood at the parapet with her arms wrapped around herself.

"They will be at the Lupercal now," Tertulla said. "The priests will be sacrificing a dog and a goat, and the young men will be anointed, and then they will run."

"I know how it goes."

"You have not told me why you are watching."

Cassia looked at the crowd. It was a moving thing, a single organism with a thousand heads, surging along the valley and up the Via Sacra and around the hill. You could see the bright spots in it: a woman's yellow mantle, a litter with a gilded pole, a group of youths in matching red cloaks carrying a goat's head on a pole. You could hear it as a single sound, a low roar with laughter in it, and occasionally a scream.

"I am watching for a girl," she said.

"Which girl?"

"The senator's daughter."

Tertulla came and stood beside her. "Why?"

"Because she will come."

"How do you know?"

"Because she is fifteen and her grandmother is dying and her mother is unhappy and her father has a secret, and because a girl in that house will do anything to find out what the secret is." Cassia's eyes moved over the crowd. "And because she has been told all her life that my house is a place of sin, and there is nothing more attractive to a clever girl than a sin she has been told about."

"Cassia. If that girl comes to your door—"

"She will not come to the door. She is too well brought up for that. She will come to the festival with a slave and she will slip away in the crowd and she will stand in the street and look at the house from a distance, and then she will go home and lie about it." Cassia pointed. "There. By the fountain. In the blue cloak, with the plain mask."

Tertulla looked. "How can you tell?"

"Because she is standing still. Everyone else is moving. A girl who has run away from her slave does not move; she stands and looks and tries to decide whether she has made a mistake."

The blue-cloaked figure was standing at the corner of the street by the fountain, a hundred paces away, with a cheap clay mask over her face and her hood up. She was alone. She was looking at the house, and then at the street, and then at the house again.

Cassia went down the stair.

***

She came out of the garden door into the alley and walked around to the front of the house, and the festival crowd filled the street and the noise was enormous. The girl saw her coming and did not move. She was tall for fifteen, with her hood up and the mask covering the top half of her face, and her hands were clasped in front of her in the way of a girl who has been taught to stand.

"You are lost," Cassia said.

"I am not."

"Then you are waiting for someone, and the someone has not come, and you have been standing here for a quarter of an hour, and your slave is looking for you in the crowd and will find you in a moment and will be very frightened and will tell your mother." Cassia smiled. "Take off the mask."

The girl did not move.

"Take it off," Cassia said, "or I will call the vigiles and tell them there is a young woman of good family alone in the street at a festival, and they will take you home to your father, and you will have to explain why you were standing outside my house."

The mask came off. The face underneath was young and fierce and frightened, with her father's eyes and her grandmother's bones, and a mouth that was trying very hard not to tremble.

"I am Sulpicia Varia," she said.

"I know."

"How do you know?"

"Because I have seen your father's face, and because you have his eyes, and because you are standing exactly the way he stands when he is trying not to be afraid." Cassia looked at her. "What do you want?"

The girl's chin came up. "I want to know what you did to my family."

The noise of the festival went on around them. A man with a drum went past, and a group of women with garlands, and a dog running with something in its mouth. Cassia looked at the girl in front of her and felt, absurdly, a kind of tenderness.

"Nothing," she said. "I have done nothing to your family. Your family has done a great deal to me."

"What does that mean?"

"It means that you are fifteen years old and you have been sent here by a curiosity you do not understand, and that if I told you the truth you would not sleep tonight, and you would go home and say something to your mother, and she would say something to your father, and the whole city would know by the Ides." Cassia took her arm. "Come. You cannot stand in the street. There is a wine shop on the corner with a room at the back, and the woman who owns it owes me a favour, and we will have a cup of hot wine and you will tell me what you think you know."

The girl came. She came because she was fifteen and because the alternative was to go home, and because Cassia's hand on her arm was firm and warm and entirely without menace.

The back room of the wine shop was small and dark and smelled of wine and wet wool and the sausages that were frying in the front. Cassia sat the girl down on a bench and ordered hot wine with honey and a plate of bread and olives, and the girl sat with her hands around the cup and looked at her.

"You are not what I expected," Sulpicia said.

"What did you expect?"

"A monster."

"I am a woman who runs a business. It is the same thing to some people." Cassia drank her wine. "Now. What do you think you know?"

"I know that my father goes to your house."

"Your father has never been to my house."

The girl's eyes widened. "But—"

"He has never been to my house. He came to a house on the Caelian, once, to speak to me about the accusation that is being prepared against him, and before that I spoke to him for a quarter of an hour in a public portico in front of half the Senate. That is all. Whatever you have heard, whatever your grandmother has said, whatever the servants whisper, that is the truth." Cassia set her cup down. "Your father is not my lover. He was, once, a very long time ago, before you were born, and it ended badly, and he has spent his life since then trying to be a man his father would have approved of, and I have spent my life trying to be a woman who does not need anyone's approval. That is the whole of it."

"That is not the whole of it."

"No," Cassia said. "It is not. But it is as much as you are going to get from me today."

The girl was quiet for a moment. Then she said, "My grandmother is dying."

"I know."

"She talks in her sleep. She says a name. Psyche. And she says, 'Tell her nothing.' And my mother went to the baths and came home and would not speak to anyone, and my father came in at midnight soaked to the skin and went to my grandmother's room and there was shouting, and then my grandmother was crying, and my grandmother has never cried in her life." Sulpicia's voice was steady, and her eyes were bright. "I am not a child. I am fifteen, and I am to be married next year to a man I have met twice, and I am to spend the rest of my life in a house like that house, with the masks on the wall and the lies in the corridors. I would like to know what I am marrying into."

Cassia looked at her for a long time. The girl looked back. She had courage; that was certain. Whether she had judgment was another matter, and judgment was the thing that could not be taught in an afternoon.

"Not today," Cassia said. "Today you will go home, and you will tell your slave that you were separated in the crowd and that you are sorry, and you will be forgiven because you are fifteen and pretty and your family is important. And then, if you still want to know, you will come to me on a day that is not a festival, with your mother's knowledge, and I will tell you what I can." She leaned forward. "But understand me, Sulpicia. What I tell you will not make you happy. It will not make your family better. It will not make your marriage easier. It will only mean that you know, and knowing is a burden, and once you have it you cannot put it down."

"Tell me about my grandmother," Sulpicia said. "Not the illness. The other thing. The thing no one will say."

"Your grandmother is the cleverest woman I have ever met and the most frightened," Cassia said. "She has spent her whole life making sure that nothing in that house is ever out of place. Not the masks, not the linen, not the marriages, not the servants. She believes that if everything is in its proper place, nothing bad can happen. And when something bad does happen, she puts it somewhere, and she shuts the door, and she never opens it again." She turned her cup. "You are like her. You have her bones and her courage and her hardness. You will need all three. But you will have to learn to open doors, or you will end up in a chair in a cold room, counting the linen, with a son who is afraid of you."

The girl stared at her. "You are not afraid of my grandmother."

"I am terrified of your grandmother."

"You do not sound it."

"Because I have spent twenty years learning not to sound afraid. It is a skill, like spinning. It can be learned." Cassia leaned back. "You are to marry Aulus Proculus. That is what your grandmother wants."

"How did you know?"

"Because his father is a fool and his mother is dead and the boy is weak and will inherit, and because your grandmother has never in her life made a match that did not improve the family's position by at least one step." Cassia's mouth twisted. "Aulus Proculus is not a bad man. He is a boy who has been told all his life that he is promising, and he believes it, and he will spend his inheritance proving it. If you marry him, you will run his house and his money and his career, and he will be grateful for it until the day he decides he would like to be the one in charge, and then there will be a scene, and then he will have an affair, and you will have a son, and the whole thing will settle into a shape that is comfortable for everyone except you."

"You are very cheerful about it."

"I am honest about it. There is a difference." Cassia reached across the table and touched the girl's wrist. "If you must marry him, then marry him, and make yourself the one who holds the purse and the keys and the clients' names. Do not spend your life waiting for him to be worthy of you. He will not be. Spend it making sure you are never in a position where his worth matters."

"I would rather know," the girl said.

"Everyone says that at fifteen."

"I am not everyone."

Cassia looked at her. Then she smiled, and it was a real smile, and it made her look, for a moment, like the girl she had been in a garden at Tibur with a book of poems in her lap.

"No," she said. "You are not."

She walked the girl back to the edge of the crowd and watched her find her slave, a thin woman with a basket who fell on her with a cry, and then she stood for a while in the street with the festival going on around her. A group of young men went past with goatskin thongs, laughing, and one of them struck a woman on the shoulder and she shrieked and laughed and hit him back. A child was selling sparrows in a cage. A man was asleep in a doorway with a wreath on his head.

Then the shouting changed.

It happened quickly, the way it always did. A knot of men in the middle of the street, a scuffle, a scream that was not laughter, and then the crowd surging backward, and a woman shrieking, and the bright sudden smell of blood in the cold air. Cassia was pushed against the wall of the wine shop. Bathyllus was there, somehow, his arm across her, his staff raised. The crowd opened and closed like water, and in the middle of it a young man was lying on the stones with his hands around his belly and his mouth open, and another young man was standing over him with a knife, and then the knife was gone and the young man was running and the crowd was closing over the body.

"Keep moving," Bathyllus said. "Domina. Keep moving."

They got her back to the house. She stood in the atrium with her hands shaking and her veil torn, and Thais brought her a cup of wine, and she drank it and sat down on the edge of the impluvium and looked at the water.

"There was a boy," she said. "In the street. He was stabbed."

"I heard," Thais said. "They have taken him to the temple of Aesculapius. He will probably live. They usually live. It is the ones who are stabbed in the throat who do not."

"He was someone's son."

"Yes," Thais said. "He was."

Cassia put her face in her hands. She did not cry. She had not cried in twenty-four years, not properly, not since a cart on the road to Puteoli, and she was not going to start now, in the atrium, in front of the household, with the festival going on outside.

"Send word to Tibur," she said. "Today. Not tomorrow. Today. Tell the granddaughter that I am coming within the month, and that she is to keep the old woman alive, and that if she sends me word that the old woman is dead before I get there, I will know that she was paid to let it happen, and I will come anyway, and I will be less polite."

Thais went. Cassia sat by the water and watched the light move on it, and outside the festival went on, and the city ate and drank and laughed and killed itself in small ways, and the sun moved across the roof of the House of the Graces and began, slowly, to go down.

***

He came after dark, in a mask.

She was in the garden room with the lamps lit and the shutters closed, and Bathyllus brought him in without a word and shut the door behind him. He was wearing a dark cloak and a cheap clay mask of a comic actor, and he took it off and stood there with his hair flattened and his face tired, and Cassia felt her heart turn over in her chest like a fish in a net.

"You are mad," she said.

"I know."

"Half the city saw you at the Lupercal. If anyone recognized you—"

"Nobody recognizes a man in a mask at a festival. That is the point of the mask." He put it down on the table. "And I could not wait. I have been to Tibur."

She went very still.

"I did not go myself. I sent a man — a freedman of mine, a Greek, discreet, who used to be a courier for the governor of Syria. He went up the day after the Ides and he came back this morning." Corvinus sat down. "The midwife is alive. She is very old and she is blind in one eye and she lives with her granddaughter's family in a house with a fig tree in the yard. There is a box under her bed. She will not let anyone touch it. She says she is waiting for a woman from Rome."

"Did he see her?"

"He saw her. He told her he came from you. She wept. She said —" He stopped. "She said, 'Tell her I did what I could, and I have kept it all these years, and I am not afraid of her.'"

Cassia sat down slowly on the couch opposite him. The lamp between them was steady. She could hear her own breathing.

"And Felix himself?" she said. "What has he done since the Saturnalia?"

"He has been to the consul's clerk twice. He has been to the prefect of the grain supply. He has been seen at the house of Pactus, and at the house of a man named Scaurus in the Subura, who keeps a house of women and lends money." Corvinus's mouth tightened. "And he has been to my son. Publius came to me last week and told me that a man had approached him in the forum and asked him questions about his grandfather, and offered him money for letters."

"Did he take it?"

"No. He came to me. He was frightened, and he came to me, and I —" Corvinus stopped. "I was proud of him. It is the first time in his life he has done something I could be proud of, and it took a delator to bring it out of him."

"Bring him to the house."

"What?"

"Not to mine. To Tertulla's. He is twenty-one and he is frightened and he has begun to understand that his family is not what he thought it was. If we are to fight this, we need him on our side, and he will not be on our side if he finds out the truth from a stranger." Cassia looked at him. "Tell him something. Not everything. Enough that he knows he is trusted."

"He will hate me."

"He will hate you less than he will hate being lied to." She paused. "And Lucius. Your daughter came to see me today."

He went white. "She what?"

"She came to the festival with a slave and slipped away and stood in the street outside my house for a quarter of an hour, and I brought her into a wine shop and gave her hot wine and told her nothing." Cassia held his eyes. "She is fifteen years old and she is cleverer than her brother and she is frightened of nothing, and she is going to find out the truth whether we tell her or not. If you want to control how she finds it, you will tell her yourself, before someone else does."

"And Felix's men?"

"They have been there. Two of them, three days before my man, asking questions at the inn and the bathhouse and the shrine of Hercules. They did not get to the old woman. The granddaughter turned them away — she is frightened of them, but she is more frightened of the box." He looked at her. "They will go back. If they go back with money, or with a warrant, they will get in. We do not have much time."

"Then I will go tomorrow."

"Cassia—"

"Tomorrow. Or the day after. I will not wait for the spring." She looked up. "And you will not come with me."

"I said I would."

"And I told you what would happen if you did. The whole city is watching your house. If you leave Rome for two days in the middle of an accusation, Pactus will know by the evening of the first day, and he will send a man after you, and then it will not matter what the midwife says because the story will be that the senator fled to Tibur to silence a witness." She stood. "You will stay. You will be seen in the forum every day. You will go to the Senate and make a speech about the grain supply and be photographed in the act of being an ordinary, innocent, boring man. And I will go to Tibur with my own people, and I will come back with the truth, whatever it is."

He looked at her. In the lamplight, with the mask on the table between them and the shutters closed and the festival dying down outside, he looked like a man who had been given a sentence and was trying to decide whether to appeal.

"I hate this," he said.

"I know."

"I have spent twenty-four years letting other people decide what happened to you, and I am doing it again."

"No," Cassia said. "You are doing what I tell you to do. That is not the same thing." She came around the table and stood in front of him. "Lucius. Look at me. I am not the girl in the vineyard. I am a woman with a house and a fortune and twenty-three people who depend on me, and I have been making my own decisions since I was twenty-two years old. I do not need you to protect me. I need you to do what I say, and to be in the forum tomorrow, and to be dull. Can you be dull?"

"I have been dull for twenty-four years."

"Then it should come naturally."

He laughed — a real laugh, surprised out of him — and reached up and took her hand and pressed it to his mouth. Then he pulled her down and kissed her, and the mask fell off the table and broke on the floor, and neither of them cared.

Later, in the dark, with the festival finally over and the city gone quiet, she lay with her head on his chest and listened to the house settling around them. Somewhere a door closed. A lamp guttered and went out. The wind came off the hills and rattled the shutters and smelled of snow.

"Lucius," she said.

"Yes."

"If the boy is alive. If he is alive and we find him." She stopped.

"Yes?"

"What will you do?"

He was silent for a long time. She felt his chest rise and fall, and his hand move in her hair, and she thought he was not going to answer.

"I do not know," he said at last. "I have a son. I have a daughter. I have a wife who has done nothing wrong. I have a name that is four hundred years old and a house that is falling down and a mother who is dying and who has done something that I cannot yet think about without wanting to put my fist through a wall." His voice was low. "And I have a woman I have not been able to forget for twenty-four years, and a child I did not know I had, and no idea in the world what I am supposed to do with any of it."

"You will have to choose."

"I know."

"And you will not choose well. No one ever does." She turned her face into his shoulder. "But you will choose, and you will have to live with it, and so will I. That is what people do. They choose, and they live with it, and they die, and the house goes on."

"The house goes on," he said, and there was something in his voice that she could not read — grief, or bitterness, or the beginning of a decision.

She did not ask which.

In the morning he was gone before the light, and the mask was in pieces on the floor, and Bathyllus had swept up the worst of it and put the pieces in the hearth. Cassia stood in the garden room with a cup of hot water and looked at the broken clay in the ashes, a comic actor's grin in two halves, and thought about the fact that every mask in Rome was made of the same cheap stuff, and that underneath them all, the faces were all the same.

---

## Chapter Eight

The slave market stood in the shadow of the Temple of Castor, in the hollow of the Forum where the ground had been paved over the ruins of the old republican shops, and it opened at the third hour and closed at noon, because the dealers had discovered long ago that a man who is hungry is a man who bargains badly. There were pens along the portico and a raised platform in the middle, and on the platform, in the morning light, stood the merchandise.

Cassia came in a plain litter with Hermeros and two of the kitchen boys, and she stood at the edge of the crowd with her veil drawn and her hands folded, and she looked at the platform and did not allow herself the luxury of feeling anything.

There were forty of them that morning. A group of Gauls, big and fair and frightened, with the chalk marks on their feet that meant they were new to the city and had not yet learned to run. A Greek boy of sixteen with a lyre and a pretty face and the sort of eyes that had already learned what his price was. Two women from the Danube, sisters perhaps, holding hands. An old man with a cough who had been a gardener somewhere and would not last the year. A Nubian with a brand on his shoulder. A child of six or seven, alone, with a placard around his neck that said *good with animals.*

The placards were the worst of it. They listed everything: the origin, the skills, the languages, the flaws. *Runaway, twice.* *Epileptic.* *Barren.* *Speaks Greek and Latin, reads, writes, honest.* *Mute.* *Sings.* *Bad temper.* The dealers wrote them in chalk or paint and hung them around the neck, and the customers came and read them the way they read the edicts on the walls of the Forum, and the people wearing them stood very still and looked at nothing.

The dealer was a man named Verus, who had a stall under the portico and a slave to do the shouting and a bad chest and a good memory, and who had been in the trade for thirty years and had bought and sold, by his own estimate, nine thousand people. He knew Cassia by sight. Everyone in that business knew her by sight, because she had been sold twice herself and had once stood where his stock was standing now, and because there is no trade in the world with a longer memory than the trade in people.

"Domina," he said, when she came under the portico. "You do me honour."

"I do you business. There is a difference."

"There is. But honour is cheaper." He coughed into his hand. "Your man has been here. He wants the northern boy. The sick one."

"I want the northern boy."

"He is not much. He is fourteen, maybe fifteen, he has been beaten and he has a cough and he does not speak unless he is spoken to. I would not sell him to a farm. He would not last the season. I would sell him to a house, if the house were patient." Verus looked at her. "Four thousand."

"Two."

"Four. He cost me two and I have fed him for a month and I have had a physician look at him, which was a waste of money, because the physician said what I already knew." Verus spat. "Three and a half, and I will throw in the blanket."

"Three, and you will throw in the blanket, and you will tell me who else has been asking."

The dealer's face did not change, but his eyes did. "I do not talk about my customers."

"You do not have customers for a boy who is not on the platform. You have a boy in a back room and a knight who came asking and a woman who is offering you three thousand in cash." Cassia smiled. "I am not asking you to betray a confidence. I am asking you to tell me something I already know, so that I do not have to pay someone else to tell me. It is cheaper for both of us."

Verus looked at her for a long moment. Then he said, "Three thousand two hundred, and the blanket, and I will tell you that the knight had a bird on his ring and did not give his name and came twice."

"Three thousand two hundred," Cassia said. "Done."

She had been sold twice. The first time she had been twelve and had stood on a platform in Rome with her mother's song still in her head and a placard around her neck that said *quick, clean, honest, can sew.* The second time she had been eighteen and had not stood on a platform at all; she had been sold in a back room at Tibur, with a steward counting the money and a woman holding the lamp, and that had been worse, because a platform is at least public, and a public thing can be witnessed, and there is a kind of mercy in having witnesses. No one had witnessed the second sale. She had gone into that room a maid with a name and had come out of it a thing with a price, and the price had been written down in a book, and the book had been put in a drawer, and the drawer had been locked, and the woman who kept the key had gone to bed and slept.

She looked at the platform, and the placards, and the people wearing them, and she made herself look at each one, because she had learned that the only way to survive a thing like this was to see it clearly and not look away.

Hermeros had done the negotiating in the days before. He had found the fuller's household at Puteoli and had learned that the fuller had bought two boys from a dealer in Rome three years before, and that one of them had been sold on to a farm at Aricia, and that the other was still there, and that the fuller would sell him for three thousand sesterces, which was more than the boy was worth.

"He is not on the platform," Hermeros said. "The dealer is holding him in the back. He wants to sell him privately."

"Why?"

"Because he is sick, and a sick slave on the platform is bad for business." Hermeros's face was carefully blank. "And because a man came asking about him last week. A knight. The dealer would not say which one."

Cassia's hands tightened on her veil. "Felix."

"It is possible."

"Then we buy him today, and we buy him quietly, and we do not ask the dealer any questions that will make him raise the price." She looked at the platform. "How much does he want?"

"Four thousand now."

"Pay it."

"Domina, four thousand is—"

"Pay it," Cassia said, "and then take him to the house and have Narcissus see him before I do. I do not want to see him in this place. I want to see him in a bed with clean linen."

Hermeros went. Cassia stayed at the edge of the crowd and watched the platform, because she had learned a long time ago that a woman who is seen to be impatient is a woman who is charged more.

The auction went on. The Gauls were sold to a builder. The Greek boy went to a household on the Quirinal for a sum that made even the dealer raise his eyebrows. The two sisters were sold together, which was a mercy, and to a brothel keeper, which was not. The old gardener was not sold at all, and was led away at noon to be given bread and water and kept for the next market, and Cassia, who had seen the old gardener, and who knew what happened to old men who were not sold, found that she was looking at the child with the placard and thinking about a cell at Tibur.

The child looked back at her. He had a smudge of dirt on his cheek and the flat, patient eyes of a person who has learned that looking at the crowd is a way of surviving it.

Verus took her through the portico to the back, past a curtain and a counter and a room where a clerk sat with a ledger and a pot of ink, and into a yard where the stock that was not for the platform waited in the shade. There were eight of them. A woman with a baby at her breast. Two boys asleep against the wall. A man with a broken arm in a sling. And, in the corner, a boy sitting on a stool with a blanket around his shoulders and his hands in his lap.

He did not look up when they came in.

"Melanthus," Verus said. "This is the lady."

The boy looked up. He looked at Cassia for exactly as long as it took to see her, and then he looked at the ground again, and she understood in that moment that he had been looked at by a great many people in the past month, and that each of them had been deciding what he was worth, and that he had learned to make himself small so that the decision would be quick.

"Leave us," Cassia said.

Verus hesitated. "Domina—"

"Leave us. I will not steal him. I have a house full of witnesses and a doorkeeper who could break you in half." She waited until he had gone, and then she crouched down in front of the boy, which hurt her knees, and which she did not allow him to see. "I am not going to touch you. I am not going to ask you to stand up or turn around or show me your teeth. I am going to ask you one question, and you may answer it or not."

The boy said nothing.

"Did they take you from your village, or were you born a slave?"

"Taken," the boy said. His voice was a scrape.

"Did they take your sister too?"

The boy's head came up. For the first time there was something in his eyes that was not the flat patient waiting. It was fear, and under the fear, something else, something that had been kept alive in the dark for five years and had not quite gone out.

"Yes," he said.

"Domina," Hermeros said, at her elbow.

She turned. The boy was standing behind him, between two of the dealer's men, with a blanket around his shoulders and his feet bare. He was fourteen or fifteen, thin to the point of gauntness, with the pale skin and light hair of the north and a bruise fading on his cheekbone. He was not looking at her. He was looking at the ground.

"His name is Melanthus," Hermeros said. "That is what the fuller called him. His own name is something else. He does not say it."

"Melanthus," Cassia said.

The boy looked up. His eyes were grey and old, and there was nothing in them at all: no fear, no hope, no curiosity. She had seen that look before. She had worn it, once, in a house in Puteoli, in the first month, before Thais had taught her to keep her name to herself.

"Come," she said. "You are going to a house where no one will touch you."

The boy said nothing. He came.

***

The house on the Esquiline received him the way it received everything: with hot water and clean linen and a great deal of noise. Thais took charge. She had the boy put in the small room off the kitchen where the under-cook slept, and had the under-cook moved in with the kitchen boys, and had a bath drawn, and had Celer make broth, and had Narcissus come at once with his bag.

Narcissus was sixty-one, a Greek from Pergamon with a face like a walnut and the manners of a man who had once been rich. He had been the physician of a consular household until the consular had died and the household had sold him, and Cassia had bought him for the price of a good horse and had never regretted it. He examined the boy in the small room with the door shut, and came out after half an hour and sat down in the kitchen with his hands on his knees.

"Well?"

"His back is bad. There are scars, old ones, from a whip, and newer ones from a rod. He has been beaten on the soles of his feet. There is a cough that has been in his chest for a long time and could become something worse, and he is underfed, and he has a fever that comes and goes." Narcissus's face was impassive. "He will live if he is fed and kept warm and not beaten. He will not live if he is sent back."

"He will not be sent back."

"Then he will live." Narcissus reached into his bag. "There is something else. He does not speak much, but when he does, he speaks Greek with a northern accent, and he remembers a village and a mother and a sister. He remembers a raid, and men with swords, and a cart. He remembers his sister's name."

Cassia's heart turned over. "What is the name?"

"Chrysis," Narcissus said. "He says it the way a child says a prayer. He has been saying it for five years."

***

Chrysis was in the corridor when Cassia came out. She had been standing there for an hour, because no one had told her to go away, and because Thais had looked at her and decided that this was not a thing that could be managed by orders.

"Is it him?" she said.

"Go in," Cassia said. "He is tired and he is frightened and he may not know you. Do not rush at him. Do not cry. Sit down on the stool and talk to him about the village, and if he does not answer, keep talking."

Chrysis's face was white. "What if he does not remember me?"

"He remembers you," Cassia said. "He has been saying your name for five years."

Chrysis went in. Cassia stood in the corridor with her back against the wall and listened. There was silence for a long time, and then a sound, a single, terrible, broken sound, and then two voices, low and quick and urgent, in a language she did not speak. She stood there with her eyes closed and her hands flat against the plaster, and she thought about a cart on the road to Puteoli and a cry going away from her, getting smaller, until it was only a sound in a dream.

Thais came down the corridor with a lamp and stopped when she saw her.

"Domina."

"I am all right."

"You are not all right. You are standing in a corridor in the dark with your hands on the wall."

"I am all right," Cassia said again. "I am not the one who was sold."

Thais looked at her for a long moment. Then she set the lamp down on the bracket and put her arms around her, and Cassia let herself be held, which was a thing she allowed perhaps twice a year, and only in the dark.

***

They ate together that night in the small dining room, all of them: Cassia and Thais and Chrysis and the boy, and Hermeros, who had been invited because he had done the buying and because Cassia wanted him to see what he had bought. The boy had been bathed and dressed in a clean tunic and had been given a bowl of broth and a piece of bread, and he ate slowly, watching everyone, the way a dog that has been kicked watches a room.

"Melanthus," Cassia said.

The boy looked up.

"That is not your name."

He said nothing.

"Your name is your own. If you want to tell us, you will tell us. If you do not, we will call you Melanthus until you do." She set down her cup. "I bought you today for four thousand sesterces, which is more than you are worth, and I did it because your sister asked me to. I am not your mother. I am not your mistress. I am the woman who owns the house you are sitting in, and I own the roof and the bread and the bed, and I do not own you. Do you understand the difference?"

The boy looked at her. His eyes were old and grey and entirely without illusion.

"You bought me," he said. His voice was hoarse from disuse.

"Yes."

"Then you own me."

"No," Cassia said. "The law says I own you. The law is a thing men wrote down. It is not the truth." She leaned forward. "The truth is that when a person is bought and sold, something is done to them that cannot be undone by a piece of paper. You are not the same as you were before. You will never be the same. But that does not mean that I am the same as the man who sold you, or the fuller who beat you, or the men who took you from your village. It means that I have bought you, and that you are in my house, and that I will not beat you, and that in a year, if you want it, I will free you and give you money and a trade and a name that is your own. That is the difference."

The boy looked at her for a long time. Then he said, "Why?"

"Because no one did it for me," Cassia said.

Chrysis made a sound. Cassia did not look at her.

"Eat your broth," she said. "Tomorrow Narcissus will put something on your back and it will hurt, and the day after that it will hurt less. In a week you will be able to walk to the end of the street. In a month you will be able to work, if you want to work, and if you do not want to work you will have to learn to be bored." She picked up her cup. "There is no shame in being bored. There is a great deal of shame in the world, and most of it is deserved, but boredom is not one of the things anyone should be ashamed of."

"What is your name?" Chrysis said.

The boy looked at her. He had eaten most of the broth and his colour was better and his eyes were less like a dog's and more like a boy's, and he was tired in a way that had nothing to do with sleep.

"Senno," he said.

Chrysis's hand went to her mouth. "Senno."

"You said it wrong," the boy said. "You always said it wrong. It is Sen-no. Two n's. You said Seno, like a Roman."

"I was eight," Chrysis said. "I did not know how to say anything."

"You said it wrong," the boy said again, and then he began to cry, without any noise at all, the way a person cries who has learned that crying is a thing that attracts attention, and Chrysis got up and went around the table and knelt beside him and put her arms around him, and the boy held on to her and shook.

Cassia sat very still. Hermeros looked at his plate. Thais looked at the wall. Nobody said anything for a long time, and the lamp burned, and the house was quiet around them.

Chrysis wiped her face with the back of her hand and looked at him, and the boy looked back at her, and something passed between them that had nothing to do with Cassia and nothing to do with the house and nothing to do with the price that had been paid in the market that morning.

The boy almost smiled. It was a small thing, a flicker at the corner of his mouth, and it was gone almost at once, but Chrysis saw it, and she put her hand over her mouth, and her eyes filled.

***

Felix came at the ninth hour, when the lamps were lit and the house was warm and the streets outside were full of the noise of the evening.

He came with two men and a wax tablet and the smile of a man who has decided to be pleasant. Bathyllus showed him into the tablinum and stood by the door with his arms folded, and Cassia sat behind her desk with the accounts open in front of her and did not stand up.

"Domina," Felix said. "You are looking well."

"You are looking expensive."

"I have been fortunate."

"You have been paid." She closed the account book. "What do you want?"

"Straight to business. I admire that." He sat down without being invited. "You went to the market today."

"I go to the market every week. The household must eat."

"You bought a boy."

"I bought a boy. He was sick and I bought him. It is not a crime to buy a sick boy." Cassia's voice was mild. "What is it to you?"

The smile did not change, but something behind it did. "I have been looking for a boy," Felix said. "A boy of about that age, from the north, who was sold out of a household at Tibur some years ago. A boy who might be worth a great deal to the right person."

Cassia's hands were steady on the desk.

"There are a great many boys from the north who were sold out of households."

"There are. But this one is particular." Felix turned the tablet in his hands. "There is a potter on the Via Ostiensis, outside the Porta Trigemina, who has a son of twenty-four. The boy works in the shop. He has his father's name, which proves nothing, but he has the look of the family, which proves less. I have not been to see him yet. I have been saving him."

"I have a midwife's word for it. And a physician's assistant. And an old steward with a bad chest who is willing to say what he remembers in exchange for a pension and a promise that his daughter will not be told." He looked up. "I am assembling a case, domina. Not against you. Against the senator. But a case is a house, and a house needs a foundation, and the foundation is a story that a court will believe. And the story I am building is that the senator's father was killed by his wife, and that the wife had help, and that the help came from a girl in the household who was pregnant by the son and who was sold to a brothel in Puteoli and who has spent twenty-four years buying her way back into respectable society."

The room was very quiet. Bathyllus moved, and Cassia raised one finger, and he stopped.

"That is a very good story," Cassia said. "It has everything. A noble house, a wicked mother, a slave girl, a bastard child. The only thing it lacks is proof."

"I am getting proof."

"Then get it." Cassia stood. "And when you have it, bring it to me, and I will tell you what it is worth. Until then, you will not come to my house at night with two men and a tablet and a story. You will not speak to my household. You will not go to Tibur, and if you do, you will find that the road is longer than you think." She came around the desk. "And Felix. If you go near the boy, or the midwife, or the senator's daughter, I will not pay you. I will not bargain with you. I will find out what you love and I will take it away from you, and it will not be a thing you can buy back with a tablet and a smile."

Felix rose. He was taller than her, and he used it, the way men do.

"You are threatening a Roman knight," he said.

"I am promising a Roman knight. There is a difference." Cassia smiled. "You have three days. After that, the price is off the table and the game changes."

He looked at her for a long moment. Then he laughed, and it was a real laugh, and there was something in it that was almost admiration.

"You are the most dangerous woman in Rome," he said.

"So I am told."

He went out with his two men, and Bathyllus bolted the door behind them, and Cassia stood in the tablinum with her hands shaking and the lamp burning and the whole house breathing around her.

"Domina," Bathyllus said. "He will go to Tibur."

"I know."

"He will get there before you do."

"I know that too." Cassia went to the strongroom panel and pressed the catch and took out the black book and set it on the desk. "Which is why you are going tonight, and not in the morning. Take the flower seller's boy, and the fast horse from the baker's stable, and ride through the night. You will be at Tibur by dawn. You will go to the house with the fig tree and you will bring the old woman out, and her box, and her granddaughter if she will come, and you will put them in a cart and you will bring them to Rome by the back road, and you will not stop for anything."

Bathyllus looked at her. "And if the old woman will not come?"

"Then you will tell her that the lady from Rome is coming, and that she is coming with a warrant and a magistrate and the whole weight of the law, and that if she wants to tell her story to a court she can tell it in Rome, in a room with a fire and a chair and a cup of wine, instead of in a stable in Tibur with a man from the city asking her questions." Cassia opened the book. "She has kept something for twenty-four years, Bathyllus. She has kept it because she was afraid. Tell her that the woman she was afraid of is dying, and that the son is not his father, and that there is a house in Rome with a warm room in it and a door that locks from the inside."

Bathyllus nodded. At the door he said, "And you, domina?"

"I will be here," Cassia said. "I will be here, and I will be visible, and I will be at the Senate tomorrow with the senator's wife, and every man in this city will see me being respectable." She looked up. "Go. And Bathyllus—"

"Yes?"

"If the old woman has a box, do not open it. Not for any reason. Bring it to me sealed."

He went out. Cassia sat alone with the black book open in front of her and the lamp burning and the house settling into the night. Then she turned to a page near the end, where the entries were not accounts but names, and she wrote: *Felix. Wife deceased. Two children, a boy of nine and a girl of six, in the house of his sister on the Aventine. Sister's husband is a corn merchant named Sabinus, who owes money to three men and has been cheating the annona for two years.* She looked at it for a long time. Then she closed the book, and put it back in the panel, and went up to bed.

Thais came in with a cup of warm milk and set it on the desk without being asked.

"You have written something in the book," she said. "Something you are not proud of."

"I have written what I know."

"You know a great deal about a man's children."

Cassia looked at the page. "If he comes for my household, I will use it. A man who threatens a house should know that his own house is not safe."

"And will you use it? The sister on the Aventine? The boy of nine and the girl of six?"

"I do not know."

"You know," Thais said. "You are not that woman. You have never been that woman. You have done a great many things that would make a Vestal faint, and you have ruined men, and you have taken money from people who could not afford it, and you have kept secrets that ought to have been told. But you have never once hurt a child, and you will not start now, at forty-two, with a boy asleep in your kitchen."

Cassia was silent.

"I will keep the entry," she said at last. "It is useful. He does not know what I will do and neither do I, and that is the point of a threat." She closed the book. "But you are right. I will not touch the children. If it comes to it, I will find another way."

"Good," Thais said. "Drink your milk."

She did not sleep. She lay in the dark and listened to the house, and at some hour past midnight she heard the garden door open and close, and the sound of a horse on the stones, and then nothing. She thought about a boy with grey eyes and a bruise on his cheekbone, and about a midwife with a box under her bed, and about a cry in the night that had been going away from her for twenty-four years and was now, perhaps, beginning to come back.

---

## Chapter Nine

The dinner was Cassia's idea, and it was a dangerous one, and Tertulla told her so twice before the guests arrived.

"You are inviting a man into your house," she said, "who is trying to destroy the father of your child, who has threatened your household, and who has already been paid by someone we do not know. And you are giving him the best fish."

"I am giving him the best fish because the best fish costs money, and a man who is given the best fish understands that he is being given something." Cassia checked the arrangement of the couches for the third time. "A man like Felix has spent his whole life being let in through the service door. Tonight he comes through the front. He will be so grateful that he will tell me things he should not, and then he will go home and be unable to sleep, and in the morning he will write me a letter that begins 'Domina' and ends with an apology."

"And if he does not?"

"Then he is stupider than he looks, and I will have to do this the other way." Cassia straightened a cushion. "Which is more expensive and less pleasant for everyone."

The guest list was chosen with the care of a general disposing his troops. Proculus, because Proculus was a senator and a gossip and would tell the whole city that Felix had dined at the House of the Graces, which would make Felix feel elevated and make his patron nervous. Comptus, because Comptus owed her a favour and because his presence would suggest that the evening was about business, which would make Felix feel important. Tertulla, to leaven the company and to watch. Ione, because Ione was leaving in the spring and had asked to be present at one last dinner, and because a beautiful woman at the end of the table makes men talk. And, at the last moment, Cassia had sent a boy to the Palatine with a note for Publius Sulpicius Corvinus, asking him to dine.

The young man arrived early, nervous, in his second-best tunic, and stood in the atrium looking at the painted walls as though he had never seen a house before.

"Domina," he said. "My father does not know I am here."

"Your father is not on the guest list."

"He would not approve."

"Your father does not approve of most things. It is what makes him so restful." Cassia took his arm. "Come. You are seated at the end, next to the lady Tertulla, who will be rude to you and will make you laugh. When the man named Felix speaks to you, you will be polite and you will tell him nothing. When he asks about your family, you will say that your grandmother is unwell and your sister is to be married and your father is busy with the Asian command. You will say it as though you were bored."

"I am not bored."

"Then you will pretend. It is a skill, like swimming." She looked at him. "And Publius. Whatever happens tonight, you will not lose your temper. Not for any reason. If he insults your father, you will smile. If he insults me, you will smile. If he says something about your mother, you will smile and you will change the subject to the games. Do you understand?"

"Yes."

"Say it."

"I will smile and change the subject to the games."

"Good boy," Cassia said, and saw his face change at the words, and understood that no one had called him a good boy in a very long time.

The dinner was a success. It was always a success; Cassia had built her reputation on the fact that a dinner in her house was never dull and never quite safe. The gustatio came in on silver: eggs, olives, mushrooms in garum, oysters from Baiae, bread from the bakery next door. The wine was Caecuban, and Proculus, who had been raised to believe that Caecuban was the only wine worth drinking, drank a great deal of it and became jovial. Comptus talked about the grain fleet and was listened to politely. Felix, seated at Cassia's right hand, ate slowly and watched everything.

Proculus, by the second cup, had begun to tell the story of the time he had been insulted by a man in the theatre, which was a story he told at every dinner and which had improved with the telling to the point where it now involved an emperor, two Vestals and a performing bear. Comptus listened with the fixed courtesy of a man who needs something. Tertulla listened with the expression of a woman who has heard the story four times and intends to say so before the evening is out. Publius looked at Dama.

"Tell me," Cassia said to Felix, while Proculus was drawing breath, "how does a man get into your trade?"

Felix looked at her over his cup. "You are asking about my family."

"I am asking about your life. There is a difference."

"My father was a schoolmaster in the Subura. He taught letters to the sons of freedmen for a copper a day and he died of a cough when I was twelve. My mother took in washing. I had a good memory and no money and no name, and I discovered at seventeen that a man with a good memory can make a living out of other people's mistakes." He ate an oyster. "There is no mystery to it, domina. It is the same trade as yours. You sell what people want to hear about themselves. I sell what they want to hear about other people."

"That is a very good answer."

"It is a true one. They are not always the same."

"You are quiet," Cassia said, when the fish came.

"I am listening."

"To what?"

"To the room." Felix cut a piece of fish. "You have arranged this evening very carefully, domina. The senator on your left, to show that you are respectable. The grain man across the table, to show that you are useful. The old lady at the end, to show that you have friends who are not men. And the boy." He ate the fish. "The boy is the interesting one."

"He is a guest."

"He is a son. There are three kinds of men in Rome, domina: men who have sons and are proud of them, men who have sons and are disappointed in them, and men who have no sons and would give a great deal for one. I have been watching you all evening and I cannot tell which kind you are."

"I am a woman. There is a fourth kind."

Felix laughed. "I am corrected."

At the second course Dama sang, and the room went quiet, because Dama's voice was a thing that made even Proculus stop talking. She sang a song about a girl who is turned into a laurel tree to escape a god, and the room listened, and Publius, at the end of the table beside Tertulla, did not look at his wine or at the dancers, but at Dama, with his mouth slightly open, the way the young look at beautiful things.

Ione leaned across the table to Felix and said something that made him laugh — a real laugh, surprised out of him — and Cassia watched the two of them together and thought that Ione would do very well in whatever life she chose, because she had learned the one thing that could not be taught, which was how to make a man feel that he had been clever in front of other people.

"Your Ione is wasted on you," Felix said, when Ione had turned away.

"Everyone is wasted on me. It is the tragedy of my house."

"She is leaving."

"Yes."

"Why?"

"Because she asked, and because I said yes." Cassia turned her cup. "You are surprised."

"I am surprised that you would let her go. A woman like that is worth a great deal."

"A woman like that is worth more than a great deal. She is worth what she decides she is worth." Cassia looked at him. "That is the difference between us, Felix. You see people as property. I see them as investments. An investment that is allowed to grow is worth more than a thing that is kept in a box."

Afterward, when the plates had been cleared and the fruit brought in and the lamps trimmed, Cassia leaned back and said, in a voice pitched to carry exactly as far as she wanted it to, "Felix. Tell us about your work."

The room went still. Tertulla put down her cup. Comptus looked at the table. Proculus, who was too drunk to be discreet, said, "Yes, do. I have always wanted to know how it is done."

"It is not complicated," Felix said. He was smiling, but his eyes had gone careful. "A man has a secret. A woman has a secret. A whole family has a secret. I find out what it is and I tell a magistrate, and the magistrate is grateful, and the state is grateful, and sometimes there is a reward. It is a public service."

"It is a business," Cassia said.

"It is a public service that pays."

"And who pays you?"

"The state, when the case succeeds."

"And when it does not succeed?"

"Then I do not eat." Felix turned his cup. "It is a hard trade, domina. Not everyone can do it. It requires patience and a good memory and a certain indifference to being disliked."

"I have all three," Cassia said, "and I have never once had to inform on anyone to pay my cook. That is because I sell pleasure, and you sell fear, and the market for pleasure is larger." She smiled at the table. "But I have always thought that a man in your trade must have a great many enemies, and that a man with enemies ought to have a great many friends."

The room was silent. Proculus looked from one to the other with the fat, happy confusion of a man who suspects a fight and is looking forward to it.

"I have friends," Felix said.

"Do you? Name one."

He opened his mouth, and then closed it. The silence went on a beat too long.

"I thought so," Cassia said. "You have clients and payers and men who use you and men who despise you. You do not have friends. I am offering you the chance to have one, and I am doing it in front of witnesses, which means that if you accept, everyone in this room will know that you were received at my table, and the next time a magistrate looks at you he will see a man with a friend in the Esquiline, and he will think twice."

"Why would you do that?"

"Because I want something."

"Of course you do." Felix set down his cup. "What?"

"Pactus," Cassia said. "And the man behind Pactus. And the petition that is going to be laid before the consul, and the names of the witnesses, and the date."

The room had gone very quiet. Publius was staring at her. Proculus had stopped chewing. Comptus was looking at his plate as though it might save him.

"That is a great deal," Felix said.

"It is. And I am offering a great deal in exchange. Not just money. Money is the least of it. I am offering you a place at this table, and the protection of this house, and the kind of introduction that no amount of money can buy." Cassia leaned forward. "You are a clever man, Felix. You have spent your life climbing a wall that has no footholds, and you have climbed it with your fingernails, and you are still on the wrong side. I am telling you that there is a gate, and that I am standing beside it, and that the price of the gate is one name."

Felix looked at her. In the lamplight his face was not handsome; it was tired and sharp and quite intelligent, and there was something in it that Cassia recognized, because she had seen it in her own mirror: the look of a person who has been alone for a very long time and has learned to call it independence.

"Think about what, exactly?" Cassia said. "About whether you would rather be a man who is feared or a man who is received? About whether the money you are being paid is worth more than the protection I am offering? About what will happen to you when Pactus has what he wants and no longer needs a delator?" She set her cup down. "Because I will tell you what will happen. He will pay you, and then he will be a consular with an Asian command, and he will look at you one day across a room and he will think: that man knows what I did. And then he will find a magistrate who owes him a favour and he will have you charged with extortion, and you will lose everything, and the man who paid you will be the man who signs the order."

Felix's face did not change. But his hand did, and Cassia saw it, and she knew she had found the place where he was soft.

"You do not know that," he said.

"I know men. Your Pactus is a new man who has spent his whole life climbing, and a new man who has climbed is more afraid of falling than any old family in Rome. He will not tolerate a witness to his own beginning. He will pay you and then he will bury you, and he will do it politely, in the Senate, with a speech about the dignity of the equestrian order." She leaned forward. "I will not bury you, Felix. I have no reason to. You are useful to me, and useful men are worth keeping. That is not friendship. It is arithmetic. But arithmetic is more reliable than friendship, and I have found that it lasts longer."

"I will think about it," he said.

"That is not an answer."

"It is the only one you are getting at the table." He rose. "You have been very generous, domina. The fish was excellent. I will send my answer tomorrow."

He went out. Bathyllus showed him to the door, and the room let out its breath, and Proculus said, "Well," and reached for the wine.

***

The garden was cold and the fountain had been turned off for the winter, and Cassia stood by the dry basin with her arms wrapped around herself and watched the last of the guests go.

Tertulla came out with a cloak and put it around her shoulders. "He will not take it."

"He might."

"He will not. He is a man who has been bought by other men all his life. He does not know how to be bought by a woman." Tertulla stood beside her. "What did you see in his face?"

"Fear. And something else." Cassia pulled the cloak closer. "He is proud. That is the thing about men like Felix. They are not poor and they are not stupid and they are not without courage. What they are is proud, and the world has spent their whole lives telling them that they are nothing, and they have built a career out of proving the world wrong in the worst possible way." She looked up at the sky. "If I can persuade him that there is another way to be a man than the one he has chosen, he will come to me. If I cannot, he will go to Pactus and tell him everything I said tonight, and we will have a war."

"You should have let me be in the room."

"You were in the room."

"I mean with a knife."

Cassia laughed. It was a tired laugh, and it hurt somewhere in her chest, and she stopped.

"I am too old for this," she said.

"You are forty-two."

"That is exactly what I mean." She turned. "When I was twenty-five I thought that by the time I was forty I would be safe. I thought I would have a house and a fortune and a wall around me and no one would be able to touch me. And here I am, at forty-two, standing in a cold garden bargaining with a delator for the life of a man I have spent twenty-four years trying to forget."

"You are not bargaining for his life."

"I am bargaining for his life, and for the truth about my son, and for the safety of a household that depends on me, and for the future of a girl who came to me in a wine shop because she wanted to know what her family was." Cassia shook her head. "They are all the same bargain. That is what no one tells you about power. You think it is a thing you hold. It is a thing that holds you."

Tertulla took her hand. "Come inside. It is cold, and there is wine, and there is a boy in the dining room who is pretending to be a man and doing it badly, and he needs to be sent home before his father finds out he was here."

They went in. Publius was standing by the table with a cup in his hand, looking at the three Graces in the alcove. He turned when they came in, and his face was flushed with wine and with something else.

"Domina," he said. "I want to ask you something."

"Ask."

"Felix said something tonight. About a potter on the Via Ostiensis. A boy of twenty-four." He looked at her. "I have heard my grandmother say something like it. She said it to my mother, when she was ill. She said, 'He is not to be found.' And my mother said, 'Who?' and my grandmother said, 'The potter's boy.'"

Cassia's face did not change. She had had years of practice.

"Your grandmother is dying," she said. "Dying people say a great many things."

"She is not mad."

"No. She is not mad." Cassia sat down on the couch. "Sit, Publius. This is going to be a long conversation, and I would rather have it sitting down."

He sat. He was twenty-one years old, and he had his father's eyes and his grandmother's jaw and his mother's stubbornness, and he was frightened, and he was trying not to show it.

"There is a story," Cassia said, "that belongs to your father and to me and to your grandmother, and it is not a story I am going to tell you tonight. But I will tell you this much, because you have earned it: there may be a man in this city who is your brother. Not your mother's son. Not your father's lawful son. A child born before you were born, to a woman who was told he had died." She looked at him. "If he is alive, he does not know who he is. If he is alive, he has been raised by people who may not know the truth either. And if he is alive, then everything you have been told about your family is a lie, and you will have to decide what to do with that."

Publius stared at her. His face had gone the colour of the plaster.

"Is he alive?" he said.

"I do not know. I am going to find out."

"Does my father know?"

"Your father knows as much as I do. Perhaps less." Cassia leaned forward. "And Publius. This is the most important thing I will ever say to you. You will not tell your grandmother. You will not tell your sister. You will not tell your mother until your father and I have decided how to tell her. If you tell anyone — anyone at all, even a friend, even a slave — you will put the boy in danger. Do you understand?"

"Yes."

"Say it."

"I understand."

"Good." Cassia stood. "Go home. And Publius—"

"Yes?"

"Your father loves you. He has a bad way of showing it, and he has spent your whole life being afraid that you would turn out like him, and it has made him cold. But he loves you." She said it, and knew it was true, and knew also that she had spent twenty-four years believing the opposite. "It is a poor sort of love that cannot say its own name. But it is not nothing."

He went out. Cassia stood in the dining room with the three Graces looking down at her and the lamps burning low, and Tertulla came and stood beside her and said nothing at all.

***

She did not tell Corvinus about the box. She told herself it was because a message sent to the Palatine would be read by a dozen people before it reached him, and because his mother's household was full of eyes, and because the accusation was still hanging over him and any letter from her was a risk. All of that was true. It was not the reason.

The reason was that she wanted to open it alone.

Bathyllus came back on the third day, at dawn, with the box.

Cassia was in the tablinum when he came in. She had not slept; she had spent the night going over the accounts and the black book and the household lists, because there is a kind of comfort in columns of figures, and because she had found that a woman who is waiting for news can either wait well or wait badly, and waiting well consists of finding something to do with the hours.

He was dirty and tired and had a cut over one eye, and he set the box on the desk and stood back.

"Domina."

"You are hurt."

"A stone. On the road. It is nothing." He wiped his face with his sleeve. "The men from the city were at the inn at Tibur the night before we came. Two of them. They had been to the house with the fig tree and the granddaughter had told them nothing, and they had gone away, and they had come back with a magistrate's man and a piece of paper that said they had the right to search the premises."

"And?"

"And the magistrate's man was a boy of nineteen who did not know what he was doing, and I gave him a cup of wine and a little money and told him that the old woman was my mother's aunt and that she had a chest of my uncle's things that I had come to collect, and he looked in the chest and found linen and a broken lamp and a doll, and he went away." Bathyllus's mouth moved. "The men from the city were not pleased. They are still at the inn, waiting."

"The box?"

"The old woman gave it to me herself. She would not let the granddaughter carry it. She held it on her lap all the way to the gate and then she put it in my hands and said, 'Tell her I kept it. Tell her I kept it every day, and I am not afraid of her.'" He paused. "She would not come. She said she has lived in that house for fifty years and she will die in it, and that if the lady wants to hear the truth she can come and hear it in the place where it happened."

Cassia looked at the box. It was a small chest of dark wood, no bigger than two hands, with a bronze lock and a strip of leather around it, and it had been polished so many times that the grain had worn smooth.

"And the granddaughter?"

"Outside. In the kitchen. She is frightened, domina, and she has walked a long way, and she says she will not go back without the box."

"She will not have to." Cassia reached out and put her hand on the lid. It was cold. She thought about the night in the cell, and the cry, and the cart, and the fig tree. "What is her name?"

"Marcella."

"Bring her in."

Bathyllus went out. Cassia sat with her hand on the box and did not open it. She had waited twenty-four years. She could wait a quarter of an hour more.
