Marcella was perhaps nineteen, with her grandmother's quick eyes and a countrywoman's hands and the look of a person who has been told all her life that there is a thing in the house that must not be spoken of. She stood in the doorway and looked at Cassia and then at the box and then at Cassia again.

"Domina," she said. "My grandmother says to tell you that the little master lived."

The room was very quiet. Outside, a cart went past on the stones, and a bird sang in the garden, and the house went on with its morning, and Cassia sat behind her desk with her hand on a wooden box and her heart going like a bird in a fist.

"Say it again," she said.

"The little master lived," Marcella said. "She gave him to a man. She has the paper. It is in the box."

Cassia's hand closed on the lock. She did not open it.

"Does she know where he is?"

"She knows the name of the man. She does not know if the boy is alive. She says the man was a potter, and that he lived in Rome, outside the gate, and that she has prayed for him every day for twenty-four years." Marcella's chin trembled. "She says she is sorry. She says she has been sorry every day, and that she could not tell you before because the old lady would have had her killed, and because she was afraid, and because she did not know whether you were alive."

Cassia sat very still. Then she said, "Take her to the kitchen and give her something to eat. Thais will find her a bed. She is to stay until I say otherwise."

Marcella went out. Cassia sat alone in the tablinum with the box in front of her, and the morning light coming in through the window, and the whole house going on around her, and she did not open it.

She did not open it because she was afraid, and because she had been afraid for twenty-four years, and because a woman who has spent her life building walls does not know what to do when a door opens.

Thais came in without knocking, which she had earned the right to do, and stood looking at the box on the desk.

"Is it what she says it is?"

"I do not know. I have not opened it."

"You should open it."

"I know."

"Then why do you not?"

Cassia looked at the box. "Because when I open it, I will know. And as long as I do not open it, he is alive and he is dead at the same time, and I have had twenty-four years of living with the dead, and I know how to do it. I do not know how to do the other thing." She put her hand flat on the lid. "There is a woman in a village at Tibur who has been keeping this box for twenty-four years. She kept it because she was afraid of an old woman in a chair. And I have been afraid of a wooden box on a desk."

"That is not the same."

"No," Cassia said. "It is worse."

She sat there until the light changed, and then she locked the box in the strongroom, and went out, and told Hermeros to have the travelling carriage prepared, and the horses fed, and a room taken at the inn at Tibur for the day after tomorrow.

And then she went into the garden, where the roses were beginning to put out their first green shoots, and she knelt down in the wet earth at the foot of the fig tree that she had planted there herself, six years ago, for no reason she had ever been able to explain to anyone, and she stayed there for a long time.

---

## Chapter Ten

The Senate met in the Curia Julia on a morning in March when the wind came up the Forum with dust in it and the light through the high windows was the colour of old honey. The house was full. It was fuller than it had been for a month, because the lot for the Asian command was to be drawn that day, and because the emperor's letter was to be read, and because every man in the chamber had heard, in one form or another, that there was going to be trouble.

Corvinus came in early and took his place on the benches with the other consulars. He had been in the Senate for twenty years and he knew its smells and its sounds the way a man knows his own house: the scrape of shoes on stone, the coughs, the murmur of the clerks, the way the noise dropped when the consul rose. He had spoken in this chamber a hundred times. He had never once come into it afraid.

He was afraid now. He could feel it in his hands. He put them in the folds of his toga and looked at the opposite benches and made himself breathe slowly, the way the physicians had taught his mother.

Pactus was already there, at the front of the benches, talking to a group of men with the easy confidence of a man who has arranged everything. He was dressed in a white toga of excellent wool, and his hair was combed forward, and he looked like a man who had never had a doubt in his life. He looked up as Corvinus came in, and smiled, and inclined his head, and turned back to his friends.

The consul called the house to order. The clerk read the prayer. The sacrifice had been made outside, and the augur had pronounced the omens favourable, and Corvinus, who was himself an augur, and who had spent his life reading the flight of birds and the appetite of chickens with a straight face, thought about how easy it was to make the gods say what you wanted them to say.

Then the consul read the emperor's letter.

It was a short letter. It praised the Senate, and the consuls, and the army, and the prosperity of the age. It spoke of the burden of empire and the need for men of experience in the provinces. And it recommended, for the proconsulship of Asia, the name of Lucius Sulpicius Corvinus, consular, augur, legate of the Second Dacian War.

There was a murmur of approval. Several men turned to look at him. Corvinus inclined his head. He did not allow himself to smile.

The consul folded the letter. "The house will proceed to the lot," he said.

Then Pactus rose.

He rose slowly, the way a man rises when he wants everyone to watch him do it, and he stood in the middle of the floor with his hands clasped behind his back and his chin lifted, and he said, "Conscript fathers. Before the lot is drawn, there is a matter that the house must hear."

The chamber went quiet. The consul looked at Pactus, and then at Corvinus, and then at the clerk.

"Speak," he said.

"I have this morning been informed," Pactus said, "that a formal accusation is to be laid against the man whose name the emperor has just recommended to this house. The accusation is one of parricide. It concerns the death of Marcus Sulpicius Corvinus, consul, censor, in his villa at Tibur, in the year of the consulship of Domitian and Silanus, twenty-four years ago."

The silence in the chamber was total. Corvinus sat very still. He had known it was coming and he had not known how it would feel, and it felt like a hand closing on his chest.

"The accuser," Pactus said, "is a Roman knight named Marcus Aquilius Felix, who has laid his information before the consul in the proper form. The witnesses are three: a physician's assistant, a steward, and a midwife. I do not know whether the accusation is true. It is not for me to know. But I know that this house cannot draw the lot for the richest province in Asia while one of the candidates stands accused of the murder of his father." He turned and looked at Corvinus. "I say this with regret. I have known Lucius Sulpicius all my life. But the dignity of this house is worth more than any man's career, including my own."

Old Servilius Paetus rose first, a consular of seventy who had been in the chamber since the year of the fire and who was known for two things: an inability to be brief and an absolute contempt for new men.

"Conscript fathers," he said, "I have known this house for fifty years, and I have never heard a man's name read out by the emperor and a charge of parricide read out in the same hour. It is indecent. It is worse than indecent; it is convenient. I do not know the knight Felix. I do not wish to. I know that a delator is a man who sells other men's misfortunes, and I know that in my father's time such men were whipped out of the Forum, and I know that the empire was no worse for it." He turned and looked at Pactus with the slow, deliberate contempt of a man who has nothing left to lose. "If the emperor has recommended Lucius Sulpicius for Asia, then the emperor has his reasons, and this house would do well to remember who recommended whom."

There was a rumble of approval from the old benches. Then a young man rose on the other side — a tribune, thirty, with the sort of voice that had been trained by a rhetor and the sort of confidence that had been trained by nothing at all — and said that the dignity of the house required that no man, however distinguished, should be permitted to stand for a province while under accusation; that the law was the same for the consular and the cobbler; and that he was sure the senator would welcome the opportunity to clear his name.

"He is sure," Corvinus murmured, to the man beside him. "He has been sure since he was born."

The man beside him, a fat consular named Rufus who had been his father's friend, did not laugh. "They will have you," he said, quietly. "Not today. Not this year, perhaps. But they will have you, and they will do it in the proper form, and the proper form is slower and crueller than a knife."

There was uproar. The consul called for order twice. Corvinus sat on his bench and listened to the noise, and it was like listening to the sea from a cliff: enormous, indifferent, and a long way down.

He rose.

The chamber went quiet again, in the way that chambers do when a man who has been accused stands up.

"Conscript fathers," he said. His voice was steady. He was surprised by how steady it was. "I will not waste the time of this house on a defence of my father's death. I will say only that I loved my father, that I was in Syria when he died, and that I have never in my life done anything to harm him. I will say further that the accusation comes at a convenient moment, from a source that has every reason to wish me harm, and that I do not believe this house will allow a man's career to be destroyed by a rumour and a delator before a single witness has been heard."

He looked at Pactus.

"But I will not ask the house to draw the lot while the accusation stands. I will ask instead that this matter be decided quickly, in public, before this house, in the proper form. I have nothing to hide. Let the accuser bring his witnesses. Let them be examined. Let the truth be known." He paused. "And let the house remember, when it is over, who brought this accusation and why."

He sat down. There was a moment of silence, and then the benches erupted, and it was not all hostile. Several men came to him and shook his hand; others looked away. The consul struck the floor with his staff and called for order, and the clerk read out the procedure: the accusation would be received, a day would be set for the preliminary hearing, the witnesses would be summoned, and the lot for Asia would be deferred until the matter was resolved.

The lot was not drawn. The house rose. Corvinus went out into the wind and the dust of the Forum with his friends around him and a smile fixed on his face, and he did not allow himself to look up at the Palatine, where his mother was dying in a cold room, and he did not allow himself to think about a woman in a house on the Esquiline who was, at that moment, probably already being told what had happened by one of her dozen informants.

He walked as far as the steps of the basilica and then stopped, because his legs would not carry him any further, and stood with his hand against a column while the city went past him.

He had been sixteen when his father had taken him into the atrium and made him recite the offices of the masks. He had been twenty when his father had told him that the girl would be sold and the child disposed of, and he had believed, in that moment, that he had reached the bottom of the man. He had been wrong. The bottom was deeper, and it had taken him twenty-four years and a dying woman's confession to find it.

*I put something in his wine at dinner.*

His mother had said it in the same voice she used to order the linen. She had said it as though she were describing a household expense. And the terrible thing — the thing he could not look at and could not look away from — was that he understood it. He had lived in that house. He had seen what his father did to the people in it, to his wife, to his son, to the girl in the kitchen. He had been too young and too frightened to stop it, and his mother had been neither, and she had done what she had done, and she had done it in the dark, and she had carried it for twenty-four years.

He did not know whether that made her a murderer or a survivor. He suspected it made her both.

He was right. She was.

***

Cassia heard the news from three people before noon: from Hermeros, who had a cousin who was a clerk in the curia; from Comptus, who came himself, white-faced, in a litter, and stood in the atrium with his hands shaking; and from the flower seller in the Subura, who sent a boy with a message that said only, *It is done. P.*

She sent Comptus home with a cup of wine and a promise, and then she sat in the tablinum and did the arithmetic. The preliminary hearing would be in a month, perhaps less. The witnesses would be summoned. Felix had the midwife's name and the physician's assistant and the old steward; he had the story, and now he had a forum. The only thing he did not have was the box.

She called the household into the atrium and told them the truth, or as much of it as they needed.

"There is an accusation against the senator Corvinus," she said. "It will come to a hearing. Before it does, there will be men in this city asking questions about this house, and about me, and about all of you. Some of them will be magistrates. Some of them will be men who are paid to ask. Some of them will be your friends."

The household stood in a half-circle and looked at her. Twenty-three people, from Thais to the boy who fed the pigeons.

"You will tell them the truth," Cassia said. "The truth is not dangerous. What is dangerous is a lie that contradicts another lie. If a man asks you whether I received the senator Corvinus in this house, you will say yes, because I did, and because it was a public reception and half the city knew of it. If a man asks you whether I went to Tibur, you will say that I have business at Tibur. If a man asks you about the past, you will say that you were not here, or that you do not know, and you will say it the same way every time."

She looked at them.

"And if a man threatens you, you will remember that a threat is not a beating, and that a beating is not a death, and that whatever happens to you, this house will not abandon you or your families. I have written it down. Hermeros has it. If I am taken, the paper goes to the lady Tertulla, and she will see that every one of you is freed and given money and a place to go." She paused. "That is not a kindness. It is a contract. You are not being asked to be brave for nothing."

There was a long silence. Then Celer, the cook, said, "Domina, we are not going to be brave. We are going to be sensible. There is a difference." And the household laughed, and Cassia laughed with them, and then she went into the tablinum and sat down and put her face in her hands for exactly as long as it took to count to ten.

She sent for Tertulla, and for Hermeros, and for Thais. She sent a boy to the Palatine with a note that said, *I know. Do not do anything. I will come to you tonight.* She sent another to the house of Aemilia Celsa, which was the same house, and which she had never in her life written to before, and which she wrote to now in the same terms, because she had learned long ago that a message sent to two people is more likely to be delivered than a message sent to one.

Proculus came at the second hour, in a litter, with his son in attendance and a face like a man who has been asked to pay for a funeral.

"I have heard," he said. "The whole city has heard. There is not a wine shop in Rome that is not discussing it." He sat down heavily. "I have come to tell you that I cannot be seen with you for a while. Not because I do not value the friendship of this house. Because a man in my position has to be careful, and because the consul has asked me to speak for the province of Asia in the debate, and because I cannot speak for a province and be seen dining with the woman who is at the centre of a scandal."

"I am not at the centre of anything."

"You are at the centre of everything, and you know it." Proculus wiped his face. "I am sorry. I am a coward. I have always been a coward. But I am a coward with a seat in the Senate, and a seat in the Senate is worth more to you than a brave man with no seat at all." He stood. "When this is over — if you are still standing — send for me, and I will come, and I will bring the best wine I have, and we will pretend that this conversation never happened."

He went out. His son, Aulus, lingered in the doorway.

"Domina," he said. "My father is a coward. I am not."

Cassia looked at him. The boy who had put his hand on Chrysis's shoulder at a dinner in December, and who had been sent back to his seat like a child, was standing in her tablinum with his jaw set and his fists clenched, and she thought that he would either become a decent man or a dead one, and that there was no way of knowing which.

"Then do something brave," she said, "and do it quietly, and do not tell anyone you have done it. That is what courage is. It is not a speech in the Senate. It is a letter written at midnight to a man who can help, and a promise kept when it would be easier to break it." She turned away. "Go home, Aulus. Look after your father. He is more frightened than he knows."

Then she went to the strongroom and took out the box and set it on the desk and looked at it for a long time.

"Domina," Thais said, from the doorway. "The lady Aemilia is here."

Cassia turned. "Now?"

"Now. She came in a plain litter with one slave, by the garden door, and she is in the small dining room, and she has been crying."

Cassia put the box back in the strongroom and locked it.

***

Aemilia Celsa was sitting on the couch in the small dining room with her hands in her lap. She had been crying, and she had stopped, and she had wiped her face and put her hair right, and she looked, in the grey light from the garden, like a woman who had come to the end of something and had decided to keep walking.

"They are saying he killed his father," she said.

"They are saying he is accused of it. It is not the same."

"It is the same in this city." Aemilia looked up. "I have been in that house for nineteen years. I have sat at that table and listened to that woman talk about honour and the family and the masks on the wall. And this morning my son came to me and told me that there is a woman in Rome who knew my husband before I did, and that there is a child somewhere, and that my mother-in-law has been lying to all of us for twenty-four years." Her voice was steady. "I am not here to shout at you. I have done my shouting, in the litter, on the way here, at my own slave, who did not deserve it. I am here to ask you what I should do."

Cassia sat down opposite her. "You should decide what you want."

"I want my family to survive."

"That is not the same as wanting the truth."

"I know." Aemilia's hands tightened. "I have spent nineteen years wanting the truth and being told that it was not my business. Now it is my business, and I find that I do not want it. I want the house, and my children, and my husband, and the life I have made, and I do not want to know what it was built on." She looked at Cassia. "Does that make me a coward?"

"No," Cassia said. "It makes you a person who has something to lose."

"And you? What do you want?"

Cassia looked at her. The garden was grey beyond the window, and the first rain of March was beginning to fall, and somewhere in the house a door closed.

"I want the boy," she said. "I want to know whether he is alive. I want to know what he has been told and who has raised him and whether he is happy. And then I want to make sure that no one in this city can ever take him from me again." She paused. "And I want the man who paid Felix to be destroyed. Not killed. Destroyed. I want him to lose his money and his name and his friends and his seat in the Senate, and I want him to live a long time afterward, in a small house, with no one to talk to."

Aemilia stared at her. "You are not a good woman."

"No."

"You are not a bad one, either."

"I am a woman who has been pushed," Cassia said, "and who has decided to push back. Whether that is good or bad depends on who is writing the history." She leaned forward. "I will make you a bargain, Aemilia. I will do everything in my power to save your husband from this accusation, not because I love him, but because the accusation is a lie built out of a truth, and I will not let a lie win. And in return, you will do three things."

"Name them."

"You will not tell your mother-in-law anything I tell you. You will not prevent me from seeing your husband when I need to. And when this is over, you will let me know the boy — if he is alive — in whatever way he wants to be known. Not as a scandal. Not as a secret. As a person."

Aemilia was silent for a long moment. The rain came down harder, and the gutters of the house began to run, and the garden smelled of wet stone and rosemary.

"I have one condition," she said.

"Name it."

"When this is over, you will not come to my house. You will not dine at my table. You will not be seen with my husband. Whatever there is between you, it ends when this is ended." She looked at Cassia steadily. "I am not a fool. I know what he is to you and what you are to him. I have known since the portico of the library. But I will not be a wife in a house where the other woman comes to dinner. I have my dignity, and it is not much, and it is all I have."

Cassia looked at her. In the grey light, with her hair coming down and her hands clasped in her lap, Aemilia Celsa was not beautiful and not clever and not kind, and she was, in that moment, the most formidable person in the room.

"Agreed," Cassia said.

Aemilia nodded. Then she said, "What do we do?"

"We make him tell the truth," Cassia said. "Not the whole truth. The part that can be proved. The delation says your husband killed his father. We will prove that he did not, because he did not, and because the person who did is dying in a room upstairs and has been waiting twenty-four years to say it."

Aemilia's face went white. "His mother."

"Yes."

"You are sure?"

"I am sure of the cup," Cassia said. "I am sure she gave it to me. I am sure she took my son out of the room. I am not sure what she put in her husband's wine. But I am going to find out." She stood. "Go home. Tell your husband I will come tonight. Tell him to say nothing to his mother until I am there. And tell your daughter—" She paused. "Tell your daughter that I am sorry she had to find out this way, and that she was right to come to me at the festival, and that when this is over I will tell her the rest."

Aemilia rose. At the door she said, "My daughter has been asking questions about you."

"She is a clever girl."

"She is a difficult girl." Aemilia's mouth moved. "I was difficult. It did not save me, but it kept me alive." She went out, her slave behind her, and Cassia stood in the small dining room and listened to the rain.

***

Felix came at dusk, alone, with his hood up and no men behind him, and Bathyllus brought him to the tablinum and did not leave the room.

"I have thought about it," Felix said.

"And?"

"I will give you Pactus." He sat down. "Not for the money. The money is useful but I can get money. I will give you Pactus because I have been a delator for six years and no one has ever offered me a chair, and because I am tired, and because I have a boy of nine and a girl of six and I would like them to grow up in a house where their father is not spat at in the street." He looked up. "But I want something else. Something you will not want to give."

"Name it."

"I want a written agreement. Under seal. That I am under the protection of your house. That if I am ever accused, you will speak for me. That my children will never want." He watched her. "I know what your word is worth. I have spent six years learning what words are worth in this city. I want it in writing, witnessed, in a place where it cannot be burned."

Cassia looked at him. The rain had stopped, and the lamp was lit, and the black book was in the strongroom, and the box was beside it, and outside the streets were full of the noise of the evening.

"You will have it," she said. "Drawn up tonight, witnessed by my steward and by the lady Tertulla, sealed with my ring. And in exchange you will give me the name of the man who paid you, and the petition, and a written statement that you were paid, and the date of the hearing."

Felix nodded slowly. Then he said, "The man who paid me is not Pactus."

"I know."

"You know?"

"I have known since the Saturnalia. Pactus is a front. Pactus wants the Asian command, and he has debts, and he has been borrowing from a banker on the Via Lata who holds mortgages on half the Palatine." Cassia turned her cup. "What I do not know is whether the banker is using Pactus or Pactus is using the banker."

"What do they have? Not the story. The proof."

"A copy of the physician's certificate, signed by a man who is dead. A statement from the steward, taken down by a clerk and signed with his mark. A statement from the physician's assistant, Faustus, who says that he was told by his master that the old man's death was not natural." Felix turned the cup. "And the midwife. She is the one they want most. A midwife who was in the room can say what she saw, and a court will believe her over a senator any day."

"Then we will have the midwife."

"She is old, domina. She is ill. She may die before the hearing, and then they will read her statement, and a statement read by a clerk is worth more than a living witness who contradicts it, because a clerk cannot be cross-examined."

"Then we will have her alive, and we will have her statement, and we will have something better than either." Cassia leaned forward. "The petition. You said it was laid before the consul in the proper form. Who drew it up?"

"A lawyer named Crispus. He works for Sittius."

"And where is the original?"

"With the consul's clerk. There is a copy in Sittius's strongbox and a copy in Pactus's house." Felix's eyes narrowed. "You are thinking of stealing it."

"I am thinking of proving that it was paid for." Cassia stood. "A delator's petition must be sworn before a magistrate. If we can prove that the witnesses were paid, and that the payment came from Sittius, then the petition is not a petition. It is a fraud. And a man who lays a fraudulent accusation before the Senate is not a prosecutor. He is a criminal."

"Both," Felix said. "The banker is a man named Sittius. He holds the mortgage on the Sulpicius house and the vineyard in Etruria and a warehouse on the Tiber. If the senator is ruined, the estate comes to him at a fraction of its value. Pactus gets the province. Sittius gets the house. And I get paid." He looked at her. "That is the whole of it. There is no great conspiracy. There is a banker who wants a house and a senator who wants a province, and they found a man who could put a story together."

Cassia sat very still. She had known it, or something like it, but knowing a thing and hearing it said are different, and hearing it said by a man who had been paid to say it was worse.

"Then we will give them a story," she said. "And it will be true."

She called for Hermeros and the wax and the seal, and they drew up the agreement that night, and Felix signed it and she signed it and Tertulla and Hermeros witnessed it, and when it was done Felix sat looking at the tablet in his hands with an expression that Cassia had never seen on him before.

"What is it?" she said.

"I do not know," he said. "I have never had a thing like this before. I do not know what to do with it."

"Put it somewhere safe," Cassia said, "and go home to your children."

---

## Chapter Eleven

The fire began a little after the third hour of the morning, in the storeroom behind the kitchen, where the oil jars were kept.

Cassia woke because the dog was barking. There was a dog in the alley behind the house that belonged to the bakery and barked at everything, so at first she did not move; she lay in the dark and listened to it and thought about the hearing and the box and the face of the man in the Senate. Then she smelled smoke, and she was out of bed and at the door before she had decided to move.

The corridor was full of it. Grey, thin, moving along the ceiling like water. She pulled her mantle over her mouth and went down the stairs, and the house was full of noise now: doors, voices, the crackle that is not like any other sound in the world.

"Fire!" Bathyllus's voice, from the service yard. "Bucket line! Domina — the storeroom!"

She got to the bottom of the stairs and the atrium was full of people. Thais with her hair down, carrying the strongbox. Sabina in her nightgown, standing still in the middle of the floor with her hands over her ears. The two kitchen boys dragging the water barrel. Celer in his apron, his face black with smoke, shouting at a slave who was trying to carry a chest out through the front door.

"Leave the chest!" Cassia said. "Get everyone into the street. Thais, count them. Bathyllus, the strongroom."

She went through the passage to the back of the house and the heat hit her like a wall. The storeroom door was open, and inside it was a furnace: the oil jars had gone, and the flames were running along the shelves, and the ceiling above was black and moving. The yard beyond was full of men passing buckets, and the water hissed and did nothing.

"The door," she said. "Shut the door. Cut off the air."

They shut it. The fire roared behind it. Bathyllus came through the smoke with the key to the strongroom and they went together into the tablinum, and the room was already hazy, and Cassia's hands were steady as she opened the panel and the lock and pulled out the strongbox and the black book and the wooden box.

"The linen," she said. "Get the linen out. And the wills. Leave the rest."

They got the strongbox and the book and the box and three bundles of documents out into the street, and by then the vigiles had come, too late as always, with their hooks and their buckets and their axes. The neighbours were in the street in their nightclothes. The baker was there with his sons. The flower seller. The old woman from the insula opposite. Somebody had brought a ladder. Somebody was shouting that the whole block would go.

It did not go. The fire had been confined to the storeroom and the passage behind the kitchen and part of the yard, and the vigiles pulled down the shed and soaked the wall, and by dawn it was out, and the house stood, blackened and stinking, with a hole in its side where the storeroom had been.

The fire had a sound that Cassia would remember for the rest of her life: not a roar, as she had expected, but a low, busy, eating noise, like a hundred people tearing paper. It filled the passage and the yard, and the smoke came off it in waves, and the water thrown on it went up in steam and did nothing.

She carried the strongbox out herself. It was heavier than she expected; she had never carried it before, and she staggered with it through the atrium and out into the street, and Hermeros took it from her and put it in the baker's cart, and she went back in.

"Domina!" Bathyllus, in the doorway, his face black. "You cannot—"

"The papers. The wills. There are three wills in that room and if they burn, three families lose everything." She pushed past him. "The linen can burn. The silver can burn. The wills cannot."

The tablinum was full of smoke. She went in with her mantle over her face and her eyes streaming, and she found the strongroom by touch, and the key would not go in the lock because her hands were shaking, and she stopped, and made herself breathe, and put the key in, and turned it.

The black book was on the shelf where she had left it. The wooden box was beside it. She took them both, and three rolls of documents, and a bag of coin, and she went out through the smoke with her arms full and her lungs burning, and in the atrium she fell against the impluvium and lay there coughing while the household shouted her name.

"Domina. Domina." Thais, with water. "Drink. Drink."

"I am all right." She was not all right. She sat on the edge of the impluvium with the black book in her lap and the wooden box under her arm and the smoke rolling over her head, and she laughed, once, and it turned into a cough that went on for a long time.

The neighbours came. The baker's wife brought a blanket and put it around her shoulders. The flower seller stood in the street with her hands clasped and her lips moving, praying to whatever god looked after houses. The old woman from the insula opposite, who had not spoken to Cassia in six years because of a dispute about a drain, came across the street with a jug of water and stood beside her without saying anything, which was, in its way, an apology.

By dawn the street was full of people and the house was a black shell with a hole in its side, and the sun came up over the Esquiline and made the wet ash shine.

"Domina," Bathyllus said, from the passage.

She went.

That was when they found the boy.

Nolus was eleven years old. He had come to the house two years before, from the slave market, and he had been given the job of feeding the pigeons and sweeping the yard, and he slept in the passage behind the kitchen because it was warm there. He had not come out when the others came out. They found him at the far end of the passage, by the door to the yard, which had been bolted on the outside.

Cassia stood in the street and looked at the small, black, curled thing that had been a boy, and she did not look away, and she did not cry, and she thought: someone bolted that door. Someone bolted that door from the outside, and the boy went to it, and he could not get out.

"Domina," Bathyllus said, at her shoulder. "You should not be here."

"Who bolted the door?"

He was silent.

"Who bolted the door, Bathyllus?"

"It was bolted when I got there. The bolt was hot. I could not touch it." His voice was flat. "The yard door is bolted every night. It is the rule. The boy should not have been in the passage. He was supposed to sleep in the kitchen with the others."

"Then someone put him in the passage."

"Domina—"

"Someone put him in the passage and bolted the door and set the fire in the storeroom, and they did it knowing that the boy would be there, and that the house would burn, and that everyone in it would be asleep." Cassia turned. "That is not an accident. That is a message."

***

The household was taken to Tertulla's house on the Caelian, all of them, in the grey light, with what they could carry. Cassia stayed. She stood in the blackened yard with the vigiles and the smell of wet ash and looked at the storeroom, and the vigiles' captain, a thick man named Pudens who had been in the service for twenty years, came and stood beside her and said, "It was oil. There was a lamp broken on the floor and there was oil poured on the shelves. The fire started in three places. That is not an accident."

"I know."

"Someone wanted this house burned."

"Yes."

"Who?"

"If I knew that," Cassia said, "I would not need you."

Pudens looked at her. He was a man who had spent his life dealing with the small disasters of the city, and he had the flat, patient face of a man who had learned not to be surprised. "I will write it down as an accident. That is what I will write, unless you pay me to write something else, and I do not think you will, because you are not a fool." He spat. "If I write arson, there will be an inquiry, and the inquiry will be run by a man who is friends with a man who wants you ruined, and you will spend the next six months in a courtroom instead of in this house. If I write accident, the insurance will pay, and you will bury your boy, and you will find out who did it yourself. Which is what you are going to do anyway."

"You are a philosopher, Pudens."

"I am a captain of the vigiles. It is the same thing, with worse hours." He turned to go. "Bury the boy today. Do not wait. This heat will turn him by tomorrow, and the whole street will smell of it, and then people will start asking questions that you do not want asked."

They buried Nolus that afternoon, in a grave outside the Porta Esquilina, in the ground where the poor were buried and the slaves were buried and the people no one claimed were buried. Cassia paid for a stone. It was a small stone, and it said his name and his age and the words *SERVUS FAMILIAE*, and she stood at the grave with the household around her and the smell of the fire still in her hair, and she thought about a boy in a passage with a bolted door, and about a cry in the night twenty-four years before.

Thais stood beside her. "It was not your fault."

"Yes it was."

"Domina—"

"I am the mistress of that house. A boy died in it because someone wanted to hurt me, and they knew that the way to hurt me was to burn what is mine." Cassia's voice was very quiet. "I have spent my life making sure that no one could touch me. I built walls and bought favours and made myself useful to men who could destroy me. And a boy who could not have been more than eleven years old is in the ground because a man with a lamp wanted to send me a message." She looked at the small stone. "That is the price of my house. I have been paying it for twenty-four years and I did not know it."

Tertulla came out to the Caelian in the afternoon and took charge of the household as though she had been born for it. She had the women put in the upstairs rooms and the men in the stables and the strongbox put in her own strongroom behind a curtain, and she had Celer brought into the kitchen and told to make something for twenty-three people, and Celer, who had been standing in the street in his apron for six hours without speaking, looked at her and said, "I burned the fish at the Saturnalia. I have never burned a fish in my life. I have been thinking about it all night, and I cannot remember how I did it."

"Celer," Tertulla said. "It was a fish. It does not matter."

"It matters," Celer said. "If I had not burned the fish, the mistress would not have sent me to the market on the second day, and I would have been in the storeroom when it happened, and I would have smelled the oil."

"You would have died."

"Perhaps," Celer said. "And the boy would not have been alone."

Tertulla took him by the shoulders and sat him down on a stool and gave him a cup of wine and stood over him until he drank it. Then she went out into the garden and found Cassia sitting on the bench by the rosemary with the wooden box in her lap.

"The boy is asking about you," she said. "Sabina. She has not spoken since the fire. She sat in the corner all morning with her hands over her ears. Thais says she will not eat."

"I will go to her."

"In a minute." Tertulla sat down beside her. "First tell me what you are going to do."

"About what?"

"About the fire. About the accusation. About the fact that a child is dead and a man paid for it." Tertulla's voice was steady and hard. "I have known you for six years. I have never seen you like this. You are sitting in a garden with a box in your lap and your house half burned and your face is the face of a woman who has already decided something and is waiting for the courage to do it."

Cassia looked at the box.

"He is alive," she said. "The midwife says he is alive. He is a potter's son on the Via Ostiensis, and he is twenty-four years old, and he does not know that I exist." She put her hand on the lid. "And someone has been asking about him. Someone who is not Felix and not Sittius and not Pactus. Someone who knew where to send men to Tibur to ask about a boy."

"Who?"

"I do not know. But there is a patron in the palace, and Pactus is frightened of him, and a man who is frightened does things he would not otherwise do." Cassia stood. "I am going to see my son."

Tertulla looked at her. "Now?"

"Now. Before someone else does."

"Cassia—"

"I am not going to speak to him. I am going to look at him. I am going to stand in the street and look at him and see what kind of man he is, and then I am going to come home and do what has to be done." She picked up the box. "I have waited twenty-four years. I can wait another month. But I cannot go another day without knowing what he looks like."

Sabina was in the small room upstairs, sitting on the bed with her knees drawn up, in a clean tunic that someone had put on her. She had not spoken since the fire. She looked up when Cassia came in and then looked down again.

Cassia sat on the bed beside her and did not touch her.

"When I was a girl," she said, "I saw a man killed in the street. He was a baker, and a cart went over him, and I was standing on the other side of the road with a basket of linen. I was nine years old. I could not speak for two days. My mother thought I had lost my wits." She looked at the wall. "I have thought about it for thirty-three years. Not every day. Not even every year. But sometimes, when I am tired, I see the cart and the man's arm and the bread rolling in the dust."

Sabina said nothing.

"There is no good way to see a thing like that," Cassia said. "There is no trick to it. It goes into you and it stays, and you carry it, and in time you find that you can carry it and do other things at the same time. That is all I can tell you. It does not get smaller. You get stronger."

The child's shoulders began to shake. Cassia put her arms around her and held her, and Sabina cried the way children cry, with her whole body, and Cassia held her and did not say anything else for a long time.

"Was it my fault?" Sabina said, at last.

"No."

"I was awake. I heard the dog. I did not get up."

"You are twelve years old. You could not have done anything. If you had got up, you would have been in the passage with Nolus." Cassia's voice was steady. "The fault is not yours. It is not Celer's, and it is not Thais's, and it is not the household's. It is mine, because I am the mistress of this house, and because I have enemies, and because I did not think they would go this far."

Sabina pulled back and looked at her. Her face was wet and her eyes were red and she was, in that moment, entirely a child and entirely a person.

"Are we going to be sold?" she said.

"No."

"Are you going to be killed?"

"No."

"How do you know?"

"Because I am going to make sure of it," Cassia said. "That is what I do. I make sure of things. It is not a kind of magic. It is arithmetic." She wiped the child's face with her thumb. "Now. You are going to eat something, and then you are going to sleep, and tomorrow you are going to help Thais with the linen, because there is a great deal of linen and it will not fold itself."

Sabina nodded. At the door she said, "Domina. Who is going to feed the pigeons?"

Cassia stood in the corridor for a moment with her hand on the doorframe.

"I will," she said. "Until we find someone else."

Tertulla rose. "I am coming with you."

"No."

"I am coming with you, and I am going to stand in the street beside you, and when you cannot speak I am going to hold your hand, and when you want to go in I am going to hold you back." She took Cassia's arm. "That is what friends are for. It is not a very exalted thing. It is mostly standing in streets."

She turned and walked back to the city.

***

Sittius the banker kept an office behind the Basilica Julia, in a narrow building with a green door and a step worn into a curve by the feet of two generations of debtors. Cassia went there in the afternoon, alone but for Bathyllus, and she was kept waiting for a quarter of an hour in a room with a bench and a jug of water and no window, and then shown into the office.

Gaius Sittius was sixty and neat and entirely without warmth. He had a face like a closed account book and hands that did not move when he talked, and he had been a banker for forty years and had never in that time been known to lose his temper, raise his voice, or forgive a debt.

"Domina," he said. "This is an unexpected pleasure."

"Is it?"

"It is unexpected." He gestured to a chair. "Sit. You have come about the fire."

"I have come about the fire."

"I am sorry for your loss. I heard about the boy. It is a terrible thing." He said it in the tone of a man reading a weather report. "I hope you do not think I had anything to do with it."

"I think you paid for it."

Sittius looked at her without expression. "That is a serious accusation."

"It is not an accusation. It is a statement of fact, and you know it, and I know it, and the only question is what I am going to do about it." Cassia sat down. "You hold the mortgage on the Sulpicius house and the vineyard in Etruria and a warehouse on the Tiber. If the senator is ruined, the estate comes to you at a fraction of its value. You have been buying his debts for years, quietly, through intermediaries. You paid a delator to assemble a case, and you paid a man to burn my house, and you did it because you are a man who has spent his life acquiring things and has never once considered what it costs other people to be acquired."

Sittius's hands did not move. His face did not change. But something behind his eyes shifted, the way a fish moves under ice.

"You have no proof," he said.

"I have a delator who will swear to the payment. I have a copy of the petition with your lawyer's name on it. I have the statement of a clerk in the annona office who can show that your brother-in-law has been cheating the grain supply for two years." Cassia leaned back. "And I have a house full of people who have just watched a child burn to death, and who will say anything I ask them to say, because they are frightened and because they are angry and because they loved him."

Sittius was silent for a long moment. Then he said, "What do you want?"

"I want you to withdraw the accusation."

"I cannot withdraw it. It is not mine. It was laid before the consul by a Roman knight."

"You can tell your lawyer to lose the papers. You can tell your witnesses to forget. You can tell Pactus that the case is over." Cassia leaned forward. "And in exchange, I will not destroy you. I will not go to the prefect of the grain supply. I will not go to the emperor's freedman with a file of documents that would put your brother-in-law in a mine and you in a small house in the country with a very long walk to the nearest town."

"And if I refuse?"

"Then I will destroy you," Cassia said, "and I will do it slowly, and I will start with the people you love, and you will watch."

Sittius looked at her for a long moment. Then he smiled, and it was the first expression she had seen on his face, and it was worse than the blankness.

"You are a remarkable woman," he said. "I have been in this business for forty years and I have never met anyone who could do arithmetic and speak of love in the same breath." He stood. "I will consider your offer. It is a good offer. It is, in fact, the offer I would make in your position." He walked to the window. "But I did not burn your house, domina. I want you to understand that. I am a banker. Bankers do not burn houses. We foreclose on them, which is cheaper and legal and far more satisfying." He turned. "You have an enemy who is not me. And if I were you, I would find out who it is before you spend all your ammunition on a man who has no interest in killing you."

"Who?"

"I do not know. But I know that Pactus came to me a month ago with a proposal, and I know that he was more frightened than a man with a plan should be, and I know that he has a patron in the palace." Sittius smiled again. "That is the trouble with new men, domina. They always have a patron. And a patron is a man who can be blamed."

Cassia's hand tightened on the edge of the desk. "You are telling me that Pactus has a patron in the palace."

"I am telling you that Pactus has a patron, and that the patron has a reason to want the Sulpicii damaged, and that the reason is not the Asian command." Sittius's mouth moved. "The Sulpicii have a claim on a piece of land near the new forum. It was left to them by a man who died in the reign of Domitian, and the claim was contested, and the case has been in the courts for fifteen years. If the family is destroyed, the claim is worthless, and the land can be bought for a fraction of its value by a man who knows what it will be worth when the emperor extends the forum." He looked at her. "That man is not me. I am a banker. I lend money against land. I do not buy land that has a claim on it."

"Who buys land that has a claim on it?"

"A man who has been told that the claim will not survive the year." Sittius turned back to his window. "I have told you more than I should. I am telling you because I would like you to remember, when this is over, that I was useful to you, and that I did not burn your house."

Cassia rose. At the door she said, "If you did not burn my house, then who did?"

"I told you. I do not know." Sittius turned back to his window. "But I will tell you something for nothing, because you are the most interesting thing that has happened to me this year. The men who came to Tibur looking for your midwife were not mine. I sent one man, a clerk, to look at a ledger. He came back and told me that there were two other men there, and that they were not asking about a will. They were asking about a boy."

The room was very quiet. Cassia's hands were cold.

"What boy?"

"You know what boy." Sittius did not turn around. "If I were you, domina, I would go and see him before someone else does. And I would take money. A great deal of money. The kind of money that makes a man forget that he ever had another name."

She went out into the Forum with her heart going like a drum and the sun in her eyes and the smell of smoke still in her hair, and she stood on the steps of the basilica and looked across the crowded square, and for a moment the whole city seemed to her a machine made of appetite and debt and fear, grinding on and on, and she was inside it, and so was a boy on the Via Ostiensis who did not know that he had a mother.

***

They went out through the Porta Trigemina at dusk, in a plain litter, with Bathyllus walking ahead and Tertulla beside her, and the streets beyond the gate were the streets of the workshops: tanners and rope-makers and potters and men who worked in leather and men who worked in clay. The smell was of smoke and wet earth and the river. The lamps were being lit. A dog barked somewhere. A woman was singing in a yard.

The potter's shop was on the corner of the Via Ostiensis and a narrow lane, with a tiled roof and a wide door and a yard behind it where the kiln stood. There was a lamp burning in the window, and the door was open, and as they came past, a young man came out into the yard with a board of cups on his shoulder and set it down on the trestle.

He was tall and broad-shouldered and had his sleeves rolled up over his forearms, and his hands and his forearms and the front of his tunic were grey with clay. His hair was the colour of dark honey, and he had the family's nose and the family's jaw, and he moved with the unhurried competence of a man who has done the same work every day since he was a boy.

He turned to say something to someone inside the shop, and he laughed, and the laugh was a young man's laugh, surprised out of him, and it changed his face entirely.

Cassia stood in the street with her hand on Tertulla's arm and looked at her son.

She had imagined it a thousand times. She had imagined him dead, and she had imagined him as a baby, and she had imagined him as a young man in a senator's house with a senator's manners. She had not imagined this: a potter with clay on his arms, laughing at something in a doorway, in a street that smelled of the river.

She did not move. Tertulla's hand closed on hers.

"Cassia," she said, very quietly. "Do you want to go in?"

"No," Cassia said.

"Then we will stand here until you do."

They stood for a long time. The young man went back inside, and the lamp in the window moved, and someone inside the shop said something that made him laugh again. A small boy came out and chased a chicken around the yard. An older man came to the door and called the young man in to supper, and the young man said something over his shoulder, and the older man cuffed him lightly on the back of the head, and they went in together.

Cassia watched the door close. Then she turned away, and Tertulla turned with her, and they walked back up the Via Ostiensis in the dark, and Cassia did not say anything at all until they were through the gate and into the streets of the city again.

"He is happy," she said.

"Yes."

"He has a father who cuffs him on the head and calls him in to supper."

"Yes."

"I am glad," Cassia said, and then she stopped walking, and stood in the middle of the street with her hands over her face, and cried for the first time in twenty-four years, in the dark, in the street, with the noise of the city going on around her and Tertulla's arms around her, holding her up.

---

## Chapter Twelve

They left Rome before dawn on the day after the funeral, in two carriages and a cart, with Bathyllus riding ahead and two of Tertulla's men behind. The sky was grey and the streets were empty and the city smelled of wet ash and the river. Cassia sat in the first carriage with the wooden box on the seat beside her and her hand on the lid, and she did not look back at the house as they passed the end of the street, because she had learned a long time ago that a woman who looks back is a woman who is followed.

Tertulla sat opposite her with a basket of bread and cheese and a flask of wine and the expression of a woman who has decided to be practical in the face of other people's grief.

"Eat something," she said.

"I am not hungry."

"You will be. The road to Tibur is eight hours and the inn is bad." Tertulla cut a piece of cheese and handed it across. "Eat it, or I will tell the driver to stop at every milestone and I will make you walk."

Cassia ate the cheese. It tasted of nothing.

They went out through the Porta Tiburtina and onto the Via Tiburtina, and the city fell away behind them in stages: first the tombs and the market gardens, then the villas of the rich with their walls and their cypresses, then the plain, wide and green and full of larks. The sun came up. The road was crowded with carts and mules and people walking with bundles, and once they passed a procession taking a body out to a family tomb, and the mourning women were wailing in the way that is half grief and half performance, and Cassia thought about a boy in a grave outside the Esquiline gate.

They stopped at an inn at the ninth milestone to water the horses, and the innkeeper's wife brought out a jug of wine and a plate of figs and told them that the road ahead was clear and that there had been soldiers on it the day before, going north.

"Soldiers?" Tertulla said.

"Two. Not in uniform. They had good horses and they did not stop to eat." The woman wiped her hands on her apron. "They asked about a house at Tibur. The one with the fig tree."

Cassia set down her cup.

"Did they say why?"

"No. They paid for their wine and they went on." The woman looked at her curiously. "Are you going to Tibur, domina?"

"Yes."

"Then you will want to be careful. There are men on that road who are not what they seem."

At the ninth milestone there was a shrine to the springs, a small stone altar under a roof, with a cup and a jug and a great many rags tied to the branches of a tree. Travellers left offerings there for safe passage, and the rags were the clothes of sick children, and the place smelled of old water and dust.

Tertulla went to the altar and put down a coin and stood for a moment with her head bowed. Cassia stayed by the carriage.

"You should make an offering," Tertulla said, when she came back.

"To whom? The springs?"

"To whatever is listening." Tertulla climbed into the carriage. "I do not believe in the gods. I believe in the usefulness of pretending to, in front of the servants." She settled her skirts. "What are you going to do when you have the truth?"

"I do not know."

"You must have thought about it."

"I have thought about it for twenty-four years. I have thought about it so much that I have worn a groove in it." Cassia looked out at the road. "When I was in Puteoli I used to imagine finding him. I was twenty and I was nothing and I imagined walking into a house and saying, 'I am your mother,' and having him run to me. When I was thirty I imagined finding him and finding that he was dead, and I imagined standing at the grave and saying all the things I had never been able to say. When I was forty I stopped imagining it, because I had discovered that imagination is a kind of debt, and that the interest is very high." She turned. "And now I am forty-two and I am on a road to Tibur with a box in my lap, and I find that I have no imagination left at all. Only arithmetic."

"What does the arithmetic say?"

"It says that I have a son who is alive, and a household that depends on me, and an accusation that will destroy the man I love if I cannot stop it, and an enemy who has already burned my house and killed a child." Cassia's voice was steady. "It says that I cannot afford to be a mother. Not this year. This year I have to be a woman with a knife."

Tertulla was silent for a moment. Then she said, "You are not a woman with a knife."

"I am a woman with a ledger and a house full of secrets and a great deal of money. It is the same thing, in Rome."

They drove on. The sun went higher and the day grew warm and the plain gave way to the hills, and the hills were green and wooded and full of the sound of water. Cassia looked out of the window at the road and did not see it. She was thinking about a summer twenty-four years before, and a garden, and a boy who came to the kitchen for bread, and the way the light had fallen through the vines.

The inn at Tibur was a long, low building with a stable and a yard and a fig tree by the gate, and they came into it in the late afternoon with the light going gold and the hills turning blue behind them. Bathyllus had been there before and had spoken to the innkeeper, and there were rooms ready, and a boy to take the horses, and a woman in the kitchen making something that smelled of onions and thyme.

Cassia did not go into the inn. She stood in the yard with her hand on the wooden box and looked up the hill.

The villa was still there. She could see the roofline above the trees, and a corner of the portico, and the terrace where she had stood with the cushions in her arms and looked down at a young man with dust on his boots. It was a different colour now, and there was a new wing at the east end, and the vineyard had been replanted, but it was the same house, and the sight of it went through her like a spear.

"Domina," Bathyllus said. "It is owned by a wine merchant now. He is away. The steward has been told that a lady from Rome may come to see the garden, and he has agreed, for a price."

"Not today."

"No."

"Tomorrow. I will go tomorrow." She turned away. "Take me to the village."

***

She went up to the villa in the last hour of the afternoon, with Bathyllus and the steward and no one else.

The steward was a thin, nervous man named Faustillus who had been told that a lady from Rome wished to see the garden, and who had decided that a fee was a fee and that it was not his business to ask why. He walked ahead of her up the hill and talked about the vineyard and the new press and the difficulty of getting good labour, and Cassia walked behind him and did not hear a word.

The gate was the same gate. The hinge still squealed in the same place. The portico was the same portico, and the vine on it was a different vine, and the mosaic of dolphins in the bathhouse had been repaired again, badly, by someone who did not understand dolphins. The garden was smaller than she remembered, and the box hedges were leggy, and the fountain was dry.

"The mistress of the house is in Rome," Faustillus said. "She comes in the summer. The master is in Campania. The house is kept ready."

"Leave me," Cassia said.

"Domina—"

"Leave me. I will not steal the silver. I want to stand in the garden."

He went, reluctantly, and Cassia stood in the middle of the garden and turned around slowly, once, and the whole of it came back to her at once: the smell of the rosemary, the sound of the water from the falls, the way the light lay on the terrace in the late afternoon, the boy coming up from the vineyard with his boots dusty and his hair wet from the fountain.

She walked around the house. The kitchen door was the same. The bench by the door was the same bench, or a bench in the same place, and she stood and looked at it and remembered sitting on it with a book of poems in her lap and a young man beside her saying, *read me the one about the sparrow.*

The door to the corridor was open. She went in.

The cell at the back of the villa was a storeroom now. It had shelves in it and sacks of grain and a smell of mice. The small high window was still there. The whitewash had been redone, and the fish that a child had scratched into the plaster was gone, covered over by a later coat, and Cassia stood in the middle of the floor and looked at the wall where it had been and put her hand flat against the plaster.

She had been eighteen years old in this room. She had been in labour in this room. She had heard her son cry in this room.

"Domina?" Faustillus, in the corridor, nervous. "Domina, is everything well?"

"Yes," Cassia said. "Everything is well. I have seen what I came to see."

She came out of the room and walked back through the garden and out of the gate, and at the gate she stopped and looked back at the house and said, to no one in particular, "It is a good house. Tell your master that a lady from Rome says it is a good house, and that he should fix the fountain."

She went down the hill to the village with the light going and the first lamps being lit in the cottages, and she did not look back again.

***

The house with the fig tree stood at the end of a lane below the villaThe house with the fig tree stood at the end of a lane below the villa, where the ground flattened out and the stream ran between stone walls. It was small and old and had a tiled roof and a door painted a faded blue, and there was a chicken in the yard and a boy of six sitting on the step with a stick.

Marcella came out when she heard the carriage. She had walked back from Rome with Bathyllus and had been at the house for two days, and she looked tired and frightened and relieved all at once.

"She has been asking for you," she said. "She has not slept. She sits up in the chair by the fire and she will not go to bed."

"Take me in."

The room was low and dark and warm, and the smell of it was the smell of old age and woodsmoke and herbs. Psyche sat in a chair by the hearth with a blanket over her knees. She was smaller than Cassia remembered — much smaller, as though she had been left out in the sun too long and had shrunk — and her hands were twisted with the disease that old women get, and one eye was clouded. But the other eye was sharp and grey and it found Cassia the moment she came through the door.

"Domina," she said.

Cassia stood in the middle of the room and looked at her. Twenty-four years. She had imagined this meeting a thousand times, and in every version she had been eloquent, and in every version the old woman had wept, and in every version she had said the things that had been in her mouth for twenty-four years.

What she said was, "You told me he did not breathe."

The old woman's face worked. "I did."

"You told me he was buried under the fig tree."

"I did."

"You let them put me on a cart and send me to Puteoli, and you let me believe that my son was dead, and you have known for twenty-four years that he was alive." Cassia's voice was steady. "Why?"

"Because I was afraid," Psyche said.

"Of what?"

"Of her." The old woman's hands tightened on the blanket. "You do not know what she was like, domina. You were a girl in the kitchen. You saw her fan and her medicine box and her cold voice. I saw her in that room at night with the lamp in her hand and the child in her arms, and I saw her face when she told me what to do, and I have never in my life been so frightened of anyone." She looked at the fire. "She said that if I told you, she would have me killed. She said that if I told anyone, she would have my daughter sold and my granddaughter sold and my son-in-law put in a mine. She said that she had done worse things than that for less, and I believed her, because I had been in that house for twenty years and I knew what she was."

Cassia sat down on the stool by the fire. Her legs would not hold her.

"I have something else," Psyche said. "Something I did not put in the box."

She reached inside her dress and brought out a small folded cloth, and opened it on her knee. Inside was a curl of hair, fine and dark, tied with a thread.

"I cut it off him before she took him," she said. "I do not know why. I have never known why. I put it in my dress and I forgot about it, and I found it three days later when I was washing, and I have kept it ever since." She held it out. "It is all I have of him. I thought — I thought if I ever met you, I would give it to you. And then I thought you would kill me, and I did not know which I was more afraid of."

Cassia took the curl of hair. It was so light that she could barely feel it in her palm, and it was dark, dark as her own, not the honey colour of the young man in the yard on the Via Ostiensis. She closed her hand around it.

"His hair is lighter now," she said. "I saw him. I stood in the street and looked at him."

Psyche's face changed. "You have seen him?"

"Yes."

"Is he — is he well?"

"He is a potter. He has clay on his arms and a father who calls him in to supper and a laugh that I could hear from the street." Cassia put the curl of hair carefully into the box with the receipt. "He is alive, and he is well, and he does not know that I exist, and I am going to have to decide what to do about that."

"Tell him," Psyche said. "Tell him everything. He has a right to know."

"Does he?" Cassia closed the box. "He has a mother who died giving birth to him, as far as he knows. He has a father who raised him. If I go to him and tell him that his mother is a woman who runs a house of pleasure on the Esquiline, and that his grandmother is a murderer, and that his father is a senator who has spent twenty-four years believing him dead — what does he gain? A name? He has a name. A fortune? He has a trade. A family? He has one." She looked at the fire. "What he gains is the truth. What he loses is the life he has."

"That is not for you to decide," Psyche said.

"No," Cassia said. "It is not. But it is for me to offer, and I have been in this world long enough to know that a truth offered to a person who has not asked for it is not always a gift. Sometimes it is a stone thrown into a well." She put the box in her sleeve. "I will tell him. But I will tell him in my own time, and in my own way, and not because a frightened old woman in a chair thinks it would make her feel better."

"Tell me," she said. "From the beginning. Tell me everything."

And Psyche told her.

***

The night of the birth, the mistress had come to the room three times. The first time she had brought the cup. The second time she had stood in the doorway while the pains came and had said nothing at all. The third time she had come in with the steward behind her and had waited until the child was born.

"He was a fine boy," Psyche said. "Big. He came out yelling. I have delivered a hundred babies and I know a healthy one when I see it. I put him on your belly and he yelled, and you put your hand on him, and then she said, 'Give him to me.'"

"And you gave him to her."

"I gave him to her." The old woman's voice cracked. "I have thought about it every day for twenty-four years. I should have run. I should have taken him and run out of the door and down the hill. But there was a steward and there were two men in the yard and I was sixty years old and I had a daughter at home. So I gave him to her, and she wrapped him in a cloth, and she said, 'The girl is not to know. She is to be told it was stillborn. In the morning you will tell her, and you will tell her the same thing every day for the rest of your life, and if you ever tell her anything else I will know.'"

"And then?"

"And then she took him out of the room, and I gave you the poppy, and you slept. And in the morning she sent for me and she had a man with her — a dealer, a man who bought and sold children, a man named Caepio — and she gave the child to him and he gave her money and a paper, and she gave the paper to me."

"Why you?"

"Because she did not want it in the house." Psyche looked at the fire. "She said a paper in a house is a thing that can be found. She said I was to keep it, and that if I ever spoke of it she would have me killed, and that if I kept it and said nothing I would be paid a little every year, and that if I lost it she would have me killed anyway." She laughed, a dry, terrible sound. "So I kept it. I kept it under my bed for twenty-four years, and I have looked at it every year on his birthday, and I have prayed for him every day, and I have never once had the courage to go to Rome and find him."

Cassia sat very still. The fire moved. The boy outside said something to the chicken.

"Where is the grave?" she said.

"There is no grave."

"What do you mean, there is no grave?"

"There is a place under the fig tree where I dug a hole, and there is nothing in it. I put a stone there and I told the household that the child was buried, and your mistress told the same story, and the household believed it because they wanted to." Psyche looked at her. "There is no grave, domina. There never was. Your son is not in the garden at Tibur. He is in Rome."

Cassia stood up. She walked to the window and stood with her back to the room and looked out at the yard, at the chicken, at the boy on the step, at the lane and the wall and the hill beyond.

"The paper," she said.

Psyche nodded at Marcella, who went to a chest in the corner and took out a small wooden box and brought it to Cassia. It was not the box that Bathyllus had brought to Rome. That box had come from under the old woman's bed and had been sealed with a leather strap and had contained, Cassia now understood, nothing but the things a frightened old woman keeps to remind herself that she is not mad.

This box was smaller. It had a lock. Marcella gave Cassia the key, and Cassia opened it.

Inside there was a receipt.

It was a single sheet of papyrus, folded twice, brown with age, and the ink was faded but legible. It said, in a clerk's careful hand:

*Received from the household of M. Sulpicius Corvinus, for one male infant, aged one day, of good health and no defect, the sum of four hundred sesterces. The infant is delivered to Gaius Vibidius, potter, of the Via Ostiensis, outside the Porta Trigemina, in the city of Rome, to be raised as his own. No claim upon the child shall be made by the seller or by any person on the seller's behalf. Witnessed by L. Caepio.*

And below, in a different hand, a line that had been added afterward:

*The child is called Gaius Vibidius Nestor.*

Cassia read it three times. Then she folded it and put it back in the box and closed the lid and held the box against her chest with both hands, and stood in the middle of the room with the fire behind her and the light going out of the sky, and she did not cry, because she had done her crying in a street in Rome with Tertulla's arms around her, and because there was no crying left in her now, only a hard, clear, cold thing that she recognized because she had felt it once before, on a cart on the road to Puteoli.

"Marcella," she said.

"Domina."

"Go to the inn and tell the lady Tertulla to have the notary brought from the town. Tell her I want a statement drawn up and witnessed and sealed, and I want it done tonight, before the old woman sleeps. Every word she has said. Every name. The cup, the child, the dealer, the money, the paper." She turned. "And tell Bathyllus to bring the carriage to the door at first light. We are going back to Rome tomorrow."

Psyche looked up at her. "You are not going to kill me."

"No."

"You should."

"I know." Cassia looked at her. "I have thought about it for twenty-four years. I have imagined it in a hundred ways. And now I am standing in your house with your fire at my back and your granddaughter in the next room, and I find that I cannot do it, because you are an old woman with a blanket over your knees who has been afraid for twenty-four years, and because I have spent my whole life trying not to become the sort of person who kills old women in their chairs." She put the box in her sleeve. "But I will not forgive you. Do not mistake me. I will put your statement before the Senate and I will let them decide what to do with you, and if they decide to punish you, I will not lift a finger. That is not mercy. It is arithmetic."

Psyche nodded slowly. "It is more than I deserve."

"Yes," Cassia said. "It is."

***

The notary came at dusk with his tablets and his clerk and his seal, and the statement took two hours, and Psyche gave it in a steady voice with her hand on the arm of the chair and her eyes on the fire. When it was done she made her mark, and Marcella witnessed it, and Cassia witnessed it, and the notary sealed it and gave it to her with the air of a man who has just been paid a great deal of money and would like to be paid a great deal more.

They went back to the inn in the dark. Cassia did not sleep. She sat in the room with the statement on the table and the box beside it, and at midnight there was a knock at the door, and Bathyllus came in with his face set.

"Domina. There are men in the village."

"Felix's men?"

"Two of them. They came to the house with the fig tree an hour ago and asked for the old woman. Marcella told them she was asleep and would not be disturbed, and they went away, but they are at the inn at the crossroads and they have been asking about a carriage with a woman in it." He paused. "They have horses. Ours are tired. If we wait for the morning, they will follow us."

"Then we do not wait."

"Domina, the road at night is—"

"I have been on worse roads," Cassia said. "Wake the household. We leave in a quarter of an hour."

They left at midnight with the lamps lit and the horses fresh enough, and they went down the hill and onto the Via Tiburtina with the dark on either side and the stars overhead, and Bathyllus rode behind with a sword under his cloak and did not look back. They were three miles out of Tibur when the men came after them.

There were two of them, on good horses, and they came fast, and one of them shouted something that was lost in the wind. Bathyllus turned his horse and drew across the road. There was a scuffle in the dark, and the sound of hooves, and a cry, and then the carriage lurched and Tertulla caught Cassia by the arm and said, "Do not look. Do not look."

They did not stop. They went on at a gallop for a mile and then the horses were blown and they had to walk them, and Bathyllus caught them up at the milestone with blood on his face and a cut across his forearm and a riderless horse on a leading rein.

"One of them is in the ditch," he said. "The other has gone back for him. They will not follow tonight."

"You are hurt."

"It is a scratch." He wiped his face. "Domina. They were not trying to kill us. They were trying to stop the carriage. They wanted the old woman, or the paper, or both."

"They wanted the paper." Cassia put her hand on the box in her sleeve. "They will not have it."

They came into Rome at dawn, through the Porta Tiburtina, with the city waking around them and the smoke of a thousand fires going up into the grey air, and Cassia sat in the carriage with the box in her hands and the statement in her sleeve and looked at the walls of the city and thought: I have it. I have the paper, and the woman, and the words. I have the truth in my hands for the first time in twenty-four years.

And then she thought: and it is not enough.

Because the truth, in Rome, was not a thing that set you free. It was a thing that had to be proved, in public, before men who had reasons of their own, and the men who had reasons of their own had money and horses and men in the palace and a fire that could be set in the night.

She came into the house on the Esquiline at the third hour, and the house was half black and half standing, and the household came out into the yard to meet her, and Thais said, "Domina, there is a letter from the Palatine."

Cassia took it. It was sealed with a raven, and the hand was Corvinus's, and it said:

*My mother is dying. She has asked for you. Come tonight. Bring what you have.*

She stood in the burnt yard with the letter in her hand and the ash under her feet and the morning light coming over the roof, and she said, "Tell the household to rest. Tell Hermeros to bring me the black book. And tell Bathyllus to have the horses ready at dusk."

"Where are we going?" Tertulla said.

"To the Palatine," Cassia said. "To see a woman who has been waiting twenty-four years to tell me the truth, and who is going to tell it to me whether she wants to or not."

---

## Chapter Thirteen

The house on the Palatine at night was a different house from the one Cassia had walked through in the daytime. The lamps were few and far between, and the corridors were full of shadows, and the masks in the atrium looked out of their cupboards with their painted eyes and their mouths that were neither smiling nor sad. She came in through the garden door with Corvinus beside her and Aemilia waiting at the end of the corridor with a lamp, and none of them said anything for a moment.

"She is bad tonight," Aemilia said. "She has been asking for you since the afternoon. She will not take the poppy, because she says it makes her stupid, and she will not have the physician, because she says he is a fool." She looked at Cassia. "She has eaten nothing since yesterday. I do not know how she is still alive."

"People like your mother-in-law do not die until they have finished what they are doing," Cassia said.

Aemilia's mouth moved. "That is the most comforting thing anyone has said to me all week."

Young Sulpicia was sitting on the stairs.

She was in her nightgown with a mantle over it and her hair loose, and she had a lamp on the step beside her and a book in her lap that she was not reading. She stood up when she saw them, and her face in the lamplight was her grandmother's face and her father's face and, in some way that Cassia could not have explained, her own.

"I heard you come in," she said.

"You should be in bed," Corvinus said.

"I am not a child."

"You are fifteen."

"I am fifteen and I am to be married in the summer, and I am the only person in this house who is not allowed to know what is happening in it." She looked at her father.
