"Grandmother has been asking for the lady all evening. Mother has been crying in the garden. There is a notary in the tablinum, and the slaves have been told to stay out of the west wing, and there is a woman in a burnt dress standing in our atrium." She turned to Cassia. "You are the woman."

"I am the woman," Cassia said.

"You are also the woman from the wine shop."

"Yes."

Her father looked from one to the other. "You have met?"

"At the Lupercalia," Sulpicia said. "I went to see her house and she caught me in the street and gave me hot wine and told me I was too young to know the truth." She lifted her chin. "I am not too young now."

Cassia looked at her. The girl was frightened; she could see it in the way she held the lamp, in the way her free hand was clenched at her side. But she was standing in the corridor of a house full of secrets and asking to be let in, and Cassia had done the same thing, once, at eighteen, in a garden, with a boy who came to the kitchen for bread.

"Your grandmother is dying," Cassia said. "She is going to tell us some things tonight that will be difficult to hear, and some of them are about your father, and some of them are about me, and some of them are about a boy who is twenty-four years old and who has nothing to do with any of this. You may come in, if your father permits it. But you will not speak until you are spoken to, and you will not repeat a word of what you hear to anyone, and when it is over you will go to bed and you will not ask me any questions until the morning."

Sulpicia looked at her father. Corvinus looked at Cassia, and then at his daughter, and his face did something complicated.

"Come," he said.

They went up the stairs together, and the room at the end of the corridor was lit with three lamps and a brazier, and the heat of it came out into the passage like a hand. Sulpicia Varia was propped up on her pillows with her hair in two thin braids and her hands on the coverlet, and the flesh had gone from her face so completely that she looked like a carving of herself, all bone and intention.

"Sit down," she said. "All of you. You look like a delegation."

Corvinus sat on the stool by the bed. Aemilia stood by the door with her arms folded. Cassia sat in the chair at the foot of the bed, where she could see the old woman's face and the old woman could see hers.

"You have brought the paper," Sulpicia said.

"I have brought the paper."

"Let me see it."

Cassia took the receipt from her sleeve and unfolded it and held it up. The old woman looked at it for a long time. Then she closed her eyes.

"Four hundred sesterces," she said. "I remember. I thought it was cheap."

The room was very quiet. The brazier ticked. Somewhere in the house a door closed, and a slave went past in the corridor with soft feet.

"I have been trying to decide," Sulpicia Varia said, "whether I am sorry." She opened her eyes. "I have had twenty-four years to decide, and I find that I am not. I am not sorry that I did it. I am sorry that it was necessary. There is a difference, and I would like you to understand it, because I am going to die in this room and I would like at least one person in it to understand me."

"Explain it," Cassia said.

"Your son was a scandal. A bastard child of a slave girl, born in a villa, with a father who was twenty years old and a fool. If the child had lived, my husband would have sold him or exposed him, and he would have died in a ditch, or he would have been raised a slave and sold south, and you would have been sold with him. I did what I did to save the family, and I did it to save the child, and I did it to save you, though you will not believe that." She looked at Cassia. "The man I gave him to was a potter with no children and a good trade. I had him investigated. I knew his name and his wife's name and the name of the street he lived in. I chose him because he was kind. I chose him because he would raise the boy as his own and the boy would grow up with a roof and a trade and a father, instead of a brothel and a grave."

"You sold a child," Cassia said.

"I saved a child."

"You sold my son for four hundred sesterces and you told me he was dead."

"Would you have preferred the truth? Would you have preferred to be sold to Puteoli knowing that your son was alive in Rome and that you would never see him?" The old woman's voice was thin and fierce. "I gave you a mercy. I gave you a clean grief. I gave you the only thing a woman in your position could have been given, which was the ability to believe that the child was out of pain. And you have spent twenty-four years hating me for it, and I have spent twenty-four years knowing that you would hate me, and I did it anyway."

"I would have come for him," Cassia said. "I would have crawled out of Puteoli on my hands and knees. I would have found him and taken him and raised him in a room above a bakery and I would have been poor and I would have been nothing and he would have been mine."

"And he would have been a freedwoman's bastard in the Subura, with no name and no trade and no future, and every man who came to your door would have known what his mother was." Sulpicia Varia's eyes were wet. "You are a rich woman now, domina. You have a house and a fortune and the ear of half the Senate. But you were not rich then. You had nothing. You had a body and a debt. And I took the child out of that, and I gave him to a man who could raise him, and I have paid for it every day for twenty-four years, and I would do it again."

Cassia stood up. She walked to the window and stood with her back to the room and looked out at the dark garden and the lights of the city beyond it.

"His name is Gaius Vibidius Nestor," she said. "He works with his father. He has clay on his arms and a laugh that carries into the street. He is twenty-four years old and he does not know that any of us exist." She turned. "You took that from me. Not the poverty. Not the shame. The years. You took twenty-four years of him, and you cannot give them back, and no amount of explaining will make me forgive you."

"I do not want your forgiveness."

"Then what do you want?"

Sulpicia Varia looked at her for a long moment. The brazier ticked. The lamps burned.

"I have spent my life in this house," Sulpicia Varia said, "and I have watched it eat people. It ate my husband, and it ate my son's youth, and it ate my daughter-in-law's happiness, and it would have eaten you if I had let it." She looked at Cassia. "You think I am a monster. I have thought so myself, on some nights. But I am a woman who was given a house and a name and a husband, and I was told that my duty was to keep them, and I did what I had to do to keep them, and the world did not offer me any other way to be a good woman than that."

"There was another way," Cassia said. "You could have let me go. You could have given me money and a passage to somewhere and let me take the child."

"And my husband would have found you. He had men. He had money. He would have found you within the month, and he would have taken the child and had you sold, and he would have done it in a way that would have made him feel like a just man." The old woman's voice was thin. "I knew him, domina. I knew him better than anyone alive. He was a man who could not bear to be disobeyed, and he would have hunted you to the ends of the earth, and when he found you he would have made an example of you. I did not save you because I could not save you. I saved the child because I could."

"You did not save him. You sold him."

"I sold him to a potter for four hundred sesterces, and I paid the dealer another two hundred to forget the address, and I have spent twenty-four years paying that man every year to keep his mouth shut." Sulpicia Varia's hands were shaking on the coverlet. "Do you think I do not know what it sounds like? Do you think I have not lain in this bed on a hundred nights and heard it in my own voice? I sold a child. I sold your child. I did it because the alternative was worse, and I have never been able to explain to anyone why the alternative was worse, because the only person who would have understood it is dead."

Corvinus stood up. "Mother—"

"Sit down." Her eyes did not leave Cassia's. "I am not asking for your absolution. I am telling you that I know what I did, and that I would do it again, and that I am sorry for the girl in the room and not sorry for the woman in this bed. You may make of that what you like."

"I want you to save my son," she said.

***

The statement took an hour. Cassia had brought the notary from Tibur with her — the same nervous man, who had been paid enough to be brave — and he sat at the table by the window with his tablets and his seal and wrote down every word, and the old woman gave it all: the cup, the night, the dealer, the receipt, the payment, the threats, the twenty-four years of silence.

She did not confess to killing her husband. When the notary asked, she said, "I have nothing to say about that," and looked at Cassia with her cold pale eyes and said, "That is a different matter, and it belongs to a different paper, and I will give it when I am ready."

"You may not have time," Cassia said.

"Then you had better pray that I do."

The notary sealed the statement and gave it to Cassia, and Cassia put it in her sleeve with the receipt, and Sulpicia Varia lay back on her pillows and closed her eyes, and for a moment she looked like any old woman, tired and small and finished.

"Corvinus," she said, without opening her eyes.

"Yes, mother."

"Come here."

He went and knelt by the bed, and she put her hand on his head, the way a woman blesses a child, and her fingers moved once through his hair.

"You were a good boy," she said. "You were a better boy than your father deserved and a better man than I made you. Whatever happens, remember that I did what I did because I loved this house, and because I loved you, and because I did not know how to love anything else." Her hand fell back to the coverlet. "Now go away, all of you. I want to sleep."

They went out. In the corridor Aemilia said, "I will stay with her tonight," and went back in with her lamp, and Corvinus stood in the passage with his hand against the wall and his head bowed.

Cassia stood beside him and did not touch him.

"Come," she said. "I have somewhere to go."

***

The Temple of Juno Lucina stood on the Cispian Hill, in a grove of old trees, and it was one of the oldest temples in Rome. Women went there to ask for children, and to give thanks for children, and to mourn children, and the temple was full of the small terracotta figures that mothers left: babies in swaddling clothes, hands, feet, eyes, the parts of the body that had been healed. There was a stone bench outside the door and a fountain, and the priestess was a freedwoman of sixty named Calpurnia, who had been in the temple since she was fourteen and who knew more about the women of Rome than any magistrate in the city.

They came at the ninth hour, when the temple was empty and the lamps were being lit. Cassia had brought the lock of hair and a small cake and a jar of milk, because she had asked Thais what one brought to Juno Lucina and Thais had told her, and she stood at the altar with the offerings in her hands and found that she did not know what to say.

The priestess came and stood beside her.

"You have never been here before," she said.

"No."

"I would remember. I remember all of them." Calpurnia looked at the lock of hair in Cassia's palm. "How old?"

"Twenty-four years."

The priestess did not move. "And you are only now bringing him?"

"I only now know that he lived."

Calpurnia was silent for a moment. Then she took Cassia's hand and turned it over and looked at the lock of hair in it, and her face was not kind and not unkind; it was the face of a woman who has heard everything and has decided to keep listening.

"Put it on the altar," she said. "Tell the goddess what you have come to tell her. She has heard worse."

Cassia put the lock of hair on the altar with the cake and the milk. Then she stood there with her hands at her sides and said, in a voice that did not sound like her own, "I have a son. His name is Gaius Vibidius Nestor. He is a potter on the Via Ostiensis, and he is alive, and I have not seen him since the night he was born. I am told that he is well. I am told that he is happy. I do not know if that is true, because I have only seen him from the street." She stopped. The lamp on the altar moved in a draught. "I do not know what I am asking for. I do not think I am asking for anything. I think I am telling you because there is no one else I can tell."

The priestess said, "That is what the goddess is for."

"I thought the goddess was for children."

"The goddess is for women. Children are only one of the things that happen to them." Calpurnia picked up the cake and put it in the fire, and the smell of it went up with the smoke. "I had three children. Two died in the first year and one lived to be a soldier and died in Dacia. I have been a priestess for forty-six years and I have listened to women tell me things they could not tell their husbands or their mothers or their gods. Do you know what they all say, in the end?"

"No."

"They say, 'I did what I could.'" Calpurnia turned. "And they are usually right. Not always. Some of them did what they wanted and called it what they could. But most of them, most of the time, did what they could with what they had, and the world took the rest." She looked at Cassia. "You did what you could. You were eighteen years old and you were a slave and you were in a room with a bolted door. There was nothing you could have done. And now you are a rich woman with a house and a name and money, and there is a great deal you can do. That is the difference between then and now. Not the guilt. The power."

Cassia looked at the altar. The lock of hair was beginning to singe at the edges, and the smell of burning hair was in the air with the smell of the cake.

"I do not feel powerful," she said.

"No," the priestess said. "No one ever does. That is how you know you are using it well."

***

They went back to the house on the Esquiline, what was left of it, and Cassia had the strongroom opened and the black book brought out and set on the table in the small dining room with the three Graces looking down from the alcove. The burnt smell was still in the walls, and the roof over the kitchen wing was a patch of new tiles, and the storeroom was a black hole with a plank across it.

Corvinus stood in the doorway and looked at the room.

"It is a good house," he said.

"It was a good house. Now it is a good house with a hole in it." Cassia sat down and opened the black book. "Sit. We have work to do."

He sat. She turned the pages.

"Here," she said. "Felix. He has given me a statement, and the statement names Pactus and Sittius, and the payment, and the petition. Here. Psyche. She has given me a statement, and it names your mother and the dealer and the receipt, and it establishes that the child lived. Here. The receipt itself, which names the potter and the child." She looked up. "And here, at the back, everything I know about Sittius, and his brother-in-law, and the annona, and the mortgage on your house. If we put all of it before the consul at the preliminary hearing, we can have the accusation dismissed and the delation turned back on the men who paid for it."

"Can we?"

"Yes. If we do it carefully, and if your mother gives us the other paper, and if nothing else goes wrong." Cassia closed the book. "But there is a problem."

"Only one?"

"Three. The first is Felix. He has given us a statement, but he is a delator, and a delator's word is worth very little in a court, and the moment Pactus offers him more money than I have promised him, he may change his mind. So we need to make it impossible for him to change his mind. We need a second witness who can corroborate the payment, and we need it in writing."

"Sittius's clerk."

"Sittius's clerk." Cassia nodded. "The second problem is your mother. Her statement about the child is good, but it does not clear you of parricide. In fact, it makes it worse, because it establishes that there was a death in that house that night and that she was in the room when it happened. If she does not confess to the poisoning, the prosecution will say that you did it, and that she is covering for you, and there is no paper in the world that will prove otherwise."

Corvinus was silent. Then he said, "She will confess."

"How do you know?"

"Because she is dying, and because she is proud, and because she has spent her whole life arranging things so that she would be remembered well." He looked at the table. "And because I asked her, in the corridor, before we left. I asked her to give the paper. She said she would think about it."

"Then we need the third thing," Cassia said.

"What is the third thing?"

"You." She looked at him. "You have to decide what you are going to say when they ask you about the child. Not about the murder. About the boy. Because the moment the receipt is read in that chamber, every man in the Senate will know that you had a son by a slave and that your mother sold him. They will not care about the law. They will care about the scandal, and they will use it, and it will be in every wine shop in Rome by the evening."

"I know."

"Do you? Because there is a way to do this that keeps the boy out of it. We can put the midwife's statement before the court in a closed session, and the receipt can be sealed, and the court can hear the evidence without the city hearing it. The accusation would be dismissed for lack of proof, and the boy's name would never be spoken in public." She paused. "Or we can do it the other way. We can put the whole thing before the Senate and the people, and let them see what your mother did and what Sittius paid for, and we can destroy Pactus and the banker in one afternoon. But the boy's name will be in every mouth in Rome, and his father's shop will be a place that people come to look at, and he will have to decide, at twenty-four years old, whether he wants to be a potter or a scandal."

Corvinus looked at her for a long time. The lamps burned. The three Graces looked down from the alcove with their painted hands on one another's shoulders.

"What do you want?" he said.

"I want the boy left alone," Cassia said. "I want him to have his life. I have waited twenty-four years to find him and I am not going to hand him to the city to be chewed up in the Forum." She closed the black book. "But I want Pactus destroyed more, and I want the men who burned my house to pay for it, and I want the truth to be told in a place where it cannot be buried again." She looked up. "So I am going to ask you to choose, and I am going to ask you to choose as a father and not as a senator."

Corvinus sat with his hands on the table. He was forty-five years old, and he had spent his whole life in the Senate, and he had never in that life been asked to choose between his family and his name.

"Can we do both?" he said.

"No."

"Then I choose the boy," he said. "The closed session. The sealed receipt. The truth told to the men who need to hear it and to no one else." He looked up. "And afterward, when it is over, I will go to the Via Ostiensis and I will stand in the street and look at him, the way you did, and I will decide then whether I have the right to speak to him."

Cassia looked at him. Then she reached across the table and took his hand.

"All right," she said. "Then that is what we will do."

Later, when the book was closed and the household had gone to bed, he came to her room.

She was sitting on the edge of the bed with her hair down and her hands in her lap, and she did not get up when he came in. He stood in the doorway for a moment and then came and sat beside her, and neither of them said anything for a long time.

"There is a room in this house," she said, "where I have never let anyone sleep. It is the room at the top of the stairs with the window that looks over the garden. I have kept it empty for twenty-four years. I told myself it was for guests. It was not for guests."

"I know," he said.

"I do not know what to do with it now. If he does not want to come. If he does not want to know us. If he is happy in his father's shop and his father's house and the life he has made." She turned to him. "What do I do with the room?"

"You leave it empty," Corvinus said. "And you wait. And if he never comes, you give it to someone else who needs it, and you do not make it a shrine, because a shrine is a thing that eats the people who keep it."

Cassia laughed, and it hurt. "You sound like the priestess."

"I sound like a man who has spent twenty-four years keeping a shrine." He took her hand. "I have a room in my house too. It is not a room. It is a name I do not say, and a year I do not think about, and a woman I do not look at in a crowd. I have carried it so long that I do not know what I would be without it."

"You would be a senator with a wife and two children and a house that is falling down and a mother who murdered your father."

"Yes," he said. "And I would be free."

She kissed him. It was not the desperate kiss of the garden room at Tertulla's house, and it was not the kiss of the vineyard. It was slower, and sadder, and it had in it the knowledge that they were both forty-something and both damaged and both standing in a burnt house with the smell of smoke in the walls.

Afterward she lay in the dark with her head on his shoulder and listened to the house. The window was open and the night air was cold and clean, and somewhere out in the city a dog was barking, and somewhere on the Via Ostiensis a young man was asleep, and she thought about the fact that she had found him, and that she could not have him, and that there was a kind of peace in that, and a kind of grief, and that they were the same thing.

"Lucius," she said.

"Yes."

"When this is over. When the trial is done and the boy has decided what he wants and your mother is dead and the house is sold or saved. What will you do?"

He was silent for a long time. "I do not know. I have never been able to imagine a life after this. I have been living toward this for twenty-four years without knowing it." He turned his head. "What will you do?"

"I will keep the house," Cassia said. "I will keep the house and the people in it and the business and the books. And I will go to the baths on Sundays and I will dine with Tertulla and I will grow old and I will be rich and I will be lonely in the way that rich old women are lonely, and I will be all right."

"That is a very sad answer."

"It is a very honest one." She closed her eyes. "I have been lonely for twenty-four years. It is not the worst thing in the world. The worst thing is being lonely and poor."

They sat there for a long time in the burnt house with the lamps burning and the book open between them, and outside the city went on with its noise, and somewhere on the Via Ostiensis a young man was going to bed in a room over a workshop, and he did not know that two people in Rome had just decided the shape of his life.

---

## Chapter Fourteen

The games were given in April, in the new amphitheatre in the Campus Martius, and they were given by the emperor in honour of the dedication of the forum, which had been dedicated in January, and which no one in the crowd remembered by April, because a crowd remembers only the last spectacle and the next one.

Cassia had a seat in the second tier, on the women's side, with Tertulla and Thais and a cushion she had brought from home because the stone was cold in the morning. She had been to the games a hundred times. She had watched men kill each other in the morning and beasts kill each other at noon and men kill beasts in the afternoon, and she had learned, over the years, to watch the crowd instead of the sand, because the crowd was the real spectacle, and because a woman who understands a crowd can predict a great deal about a city.

The Sulpicii had a box on the senatorial side, near the front, with the other consular families. Corvinus was there in his white toga with the purple border, and Aemilia beside him in a dress the colour of wine, and Sulpicia between them, and Publius behind, and the whole box was a picture of a family that had nothing to hide. Cassia looked at it once and then looked away, and did not look at it again for a long time, because she had discovered that looking at a thing you cannot have is a way of torturing yourself, and she had had enough of that.

Pactus was three boxes along, with his wife and his brother and a group of clients, and he was laughing at something, and his laugh carried across the stone.

"He is very cheerful," Tertulla said.

"He has reason to be."

"Has he?"

"Felix was arrested this morning."

Tertulla turned her head. "What?"

"On a charge of calumny. The urban prefect's men took him from his house at dawn and sealed the door. His wife and children are with his sister on the Aventine." Cassia watched the sand. "Sittius's lawyer laid the information. They are calling it an extortion, a false accusation laid for money, and they have a witness who will swear that Felix demanded three hundred thousand sesterces from the senator Corvinus and threatened him with a prosecution if he did not pay."

"That is true."

"Yes. It is entirely true. That is the beauty of it." Cassia turned her cup in her hands. "A true thing can be used to destroy a man just as easily as a false one. They are not prosecuting him for the accusation. They are prosecuting him for the extortion. And once he is convicted of extortion, his evidence against them is worthless, because a convicted delator cannot be a witness, and the whole case against Corvinus falls apart — and with it, everything he knows about Sittius and Pactus and the money."

Tertulla was silent for a moment. "Then we have lost."

"We have lost our witness. We have not lost the case." Cassia looked at the sand. "I have the midwife's statement, and the receipt, and the notary's seal. I have the banker's own words, though I cannot prove them. And I have something else."

"What?"

"The truth," Cassia said. "It is not worth much in a courtroom. But it is worth something in a city."

The trumpets sounded. The crowd rose. The emperor came into his box with his guard behind him, and the whole amphitheatre stood and cheered, and the sound was like a wave breaking over stones, and Cassia stood with the rest and cheered too, because she was a woman who had learned that a person who does not cheer in a crowd is a person whom the crowd remembers.

"They have announced the betrothal," Tertulla said, quietly.

Cassia did not look at the box. "To whom?"

"Aulus Proculus. The herald read it out before the first fight, with the other notices. The marriage will be in June, and the dowry is the vineyard in Etruria, which is mortgaged to Sittius, and the bridegroom's father is to pay the debts." Tertulla's mouth twisted. "It is a very good match for a family with no money and a scandal coming. It is a very bad match for a girl of fifteen with a mind of her own."

Cassia looked at the box then, and at the girl in the wine-coloured dress sitting between her parents with her hands folded and her face perfectly composed, and thought about a wine shop in the Subura and a girl in a blue cloak who had wanted to know what she was marrying into.

"She knows," Tertulla said. "Look at her. She is sitting there like a Vestal at a funeral, and she is fifteen, and she knows exactly what her grandmother has sold her for."

"Then she will survive it."

"Will she?"

"Yes," Cassia said. "She has the hardness. You can see it in the way she holds her shoulders." She looked away. "It is not a good thing to be hard at fifteen. But it is better than the alternative."

The games began. In the morning there were the hunters, men with spears and nets against lions and bears, and a bull that went mad and had to be killed by six men with spears, and a leopard that escaped its handlers and ran around the arena with the crowd screaming with laughter until it was caught in a net. At noon there was an execution: three men condemned for treason, thrown to the beasts without weapons, and the crowd watched with the particular attention it reserved for death without skill. One of the men fought; the other two stood and waited, and the beasts took them quickly, and the sand was raked over, and the crowd called for the afternoon.

At the midday break a woman came down the steps of the women's gallery and sat beside Cassia without asking permission.

She was fifty, dressed in good but not expensive wool, with a plain face and red eyes and hands that would not keep still in her lap. Cassia knew her by sight; everyone in the second tier knew her by sight, because she was the wife of a man who had been a magistrate and had lost everything in a prosecution fifteen years before, and because she had spent the years since then doing penance in public.

"Domina," the woman said. "I am Junia. My husband was Lucius Junius, who was praetor in the year of the consulship of Silanus."

"I remember."

"Then you know what happened to us."

"I know that your husband was accused and that he lost his property and that he died the year after." Cassia turned. "What do you want?"

"I want to tell you something." Junia's hands twisted. "My husband was accused by Felix. Felix built the case out of nothing — a letter, a clerk, a slave who was promised his freedom. My husband was innocent. He was acquitted of everything but a technicality, and the technicality ruined him, and the state took the house, and the man who bought the house at a fraction of its value was a banker on the Via Lata." She looked at Cassia. "The same banker, domina. I have been watching him for fifteen years. I have been watching all of them."

Cassia was very still.

"Why are you telling me this?"

"Because my son is a clerk in the office of the urban prefect, and he told me last night that Felix is to be charged with extortion, and that the charge was laid by the same banker's lawyer, and that the whole thing is a trap." Junia's eyes were bright and hard. "And because I have hated that man for fifteen years and I have never had the power to do anything about it, and now there is a woman in this city who does."

"What can you give me?"

"Names. Dates. The deeds of the house he bought. The testimony of a clerk who is still alive and still frightened." Junia leaned closer. "And one thing more. Fifteen years ago, when Felix built his case against my husband, he used a forger. A man named Secundus, who lives now in a village near Fidenae, and who is dying of a disease of the lungs, and who told me last year, when he thought he was dying, that he had done it, and that he was sorry, and that he would swear to it in a court if anyone would listen."

Cassia looked at the woman beside her, and at the crowd, and at the sand where the beasts had died, and she felt the shape of the whole thing come together in her mind like a key turning in a lock.

"Write it down," she said. "Everything. Names, dates, the deed, the forger. Bring it to my house tonight, by the garden door, and I will pay you for your trouble, and your son will not be named in any of it."

Junia's face worked. "I do not want money."

"Everyone wants money. It is the only thing in Rome that everyone wants." Cassia put her hand on the woman's wrist. "Take the money. It will make you feel less like a suppliant, and it will make me feel less like a benefactor, and we will both be able to look at each other afterwards."

In the afternoon there were the gladiators.

Cassia had seen a thousand gladiators. She had seen the famous ones and the forgettable ones and the ones who died badly and the ones who died well. She had seen the crowd turn on a man who refused to die and the crowd turn on a man who killed too quickly, and she had learned that a crowd at the games is not a crowd of murderers; it is a crowd of people who have been told that this is the place where death is allowed, and who are discovering, together, what they are capable of.

The day's favourite was a retiarius named Bato, a big man with a net and a trident and a following of women who screamed his name, and he fought a murmillo called Niger who had been brought from Gaul and had a helmet with a fish on it. They fought for a long time. Bato was faster. Niger was stronger. The crowd began to shout, and the shouting turned into a rhythm, and the rhythm turned into a single voice, and the voice wanted blood.

Bato caught Niger in the net and knocked him down and stood over him with the trident at his throat, and the crowd screamed, and the referee looked to the emperor, and the emperor, who was tired, made the gesture that meant mercy, and the crowd groaned, and Bato lifted the trident and stepped back and offered his hand, and Niger, who was bleeding from the shoulder and the leg, took it, and the two men walked off the sand together to a storm of shouting and a great deal of thrown money.

"Mercy," Tertulla said. "There is a novelty."

"The emperor is in a good mood," Cassia said. "He has a forum and a bridge and a war coming in the East. He can afford to be merciful in April."

"He will not be merciful in May."

The fourth fight was between two men who had been condemned to the arena for crimes and had been given swords and no training, and it was the kind of fight that the crowd loved and that Cassia could not watch. One of them was killed within a minute. The other stood over the body with the sword in his hand and his face turned up to the crowd, and the crowd screamed at him, and he did not move, and after a long moment the referee took him by the arm and led him off the sand, and he went like a man in a dream.

"He will not live the year," Tertulla said.

"Who?"

"The one who won. They always kill themselves, the ones who are not gladiators. They cannot go back to whatever they were, and they cannot stay in the arena, and there is nothing in between." She looked at the sand. "It is a very Roman thing, to give a man a sword and then take away every reason to use it."

Cassia said nothing. She was thinking about a boy on the Via Ostiensis with clay on his arms, and about the fact that he had a trade and a father and a laugh that carried into the street, and about how thin the line was between a life like that and a life like the man on the sand.

"No," Cassia said. "He will not."

---

Between the third and fourth fights there was an interval, and the crowd stood and stretched and went to the food sellers and the latrines and the fountains, and Cassia went out into the corridor behind the women's gallery to get away from the noise.

Pactus was there.

He was standing by a column with a cup of wine in his hand and two of his clients at a distance, and he was looking at her as though he had known she would come out into that corridor at that moment. He probably had. He was a man who arranged things.

"Domina," he said. "A word."

"I have no words for you."

"Then listen to mine." He came closer. "Felix is in a cell under the prefect's house, and tomorrow he will be charged, and in a month he will be convicted, and after that he will be a man with a brand on his record and no one will ever believe a word he says again. Your witness is gone. Your case is gone. The only thing you have left is a midwife and a piece of paper, and a midwife can be discredited and a piece of paper can be called a forgery." He drank his wine. "I am offering you a way out."

"There is no way out of a hole you dug."

"I did not dig it. Your senator's father dug it, twenty-four years ago, when he put a child in a slave and his wife put a cup in his wine." Pactus's face was pleasant. "I know about the cup, domina. I have known for a month. I have a statement from the physician's assistant that says the old man's death was not natural, and I have another from the steward that says the wife was in the room, and I have been saving them, because a good prosecutor does not show his hand until the last moment."

Cassia's face did not change. "Then why are you offering me a way out?"

"Because I do not want to destroy the Sulpicii. I want the Asian command. There is a difference." He set his cup down on the ledge. "Withdraw the midwife. Seal the receipt. Let the accusation die for lack of evidence, and I will let the senator retire from public life with his name and his house and his family, and I will take the province, and everyone will be satisfied. The alternative is a trial. And in a trial, everything comes out. The cup. The child. The brothel. The boy on the Via Ostiensis." He smiled. "I have not been to see him. I have been saving him, as your friend Felix was saving him. But I will go, if I have to. And when I have finished with him, he will not be a potter any more. He will be the bastard of a senator and a whore, and every man in Rome will know it, and he will have to leave the city to escape it."

Cassia looked at him. She was aware, in a distant way, that her hands were cold and that her heart was going very fast and that the noise of the crowd in the corridor was like the noise of the sea.

"Say his name," she said.

"What?"

"Say his name. You have been talking about him for a minute. Say his name."

Pactus's smile did not change. "I do not know his name."

"Then you do not know him, and you cannot destroy him, and you have just told me that you are bluffing." Cassia stepped closer to him. "You are a new man, Pactus. You have spent your whole life being afraid that someone would find out that your grandfather was a farmer and your father was a tax collector. You have borrowed money you cannot repay and made promises you cannot keep, and you have hired a delator and a banker and an arsonist, and now you are standing in a corridor at the games telling a woman that you will destroy a boy you have never met." She smiled. "I have known men like you all my life. You are not dangerous. You are frightened. And frightened men make mistakes."

Pactus's face changed. It was a small change, a tightening around the mouth, and it was the first honest thing she had seen on it.

"You will regret this," he said.

"I have regretted worse," Cassia said, and walked away.

---

She came back to her seat and the games went on, and she did not see any of it. She sat with her hands in her lap and her eyes on the sand and her mind on the boy, and after a while Tertulla leaned over and said, "You are pale. What did he say?"

"He threatened the boy."

"Then we go to the boy."

"We cannot. If we go to him, we make him visible. If we are seen at that shop, every man in Rome will know who he is by the Ides." Cassia looked at the sand. "We need another way."

"What way?"

"I do not know yet." She stood. "I am going home. Tell Thais to meet me there. Tell Hermeros to bring the black book and the household accounts and every letter I have ever received from a man in the Senate. And tell Bathyllus to find the boy's father — the potter, Gaius Vibidius — and bring him to the house tonight, by the garden door, and tell him nothing except that a lady wishes to speak to him about his son."

"His son?"

"His son," Cassia said. "The boy is twenty-four years old and his father is a potter who loves him, and if anyone is going to protect him from the men who want to use him, it will not be me. It will be the man who raised him."

She went out through the dark corridor and down the stairs into the street, and the roar of the crowd followed her out into the daylight, and behind her, in the amphitheatre, a man was dying on the sand for the pleasure of fifty thousand people who would forget him by supper.

---

Felix's wife came at the seventh hour.

She came to the garden door with her two children and a bundle and no slave, and Bathyllus came to the tablinum with a face like a man who has been asked to choose between two duties.

"Domina. There is a woman at the door. She says she is the wife of the man Felix."

Cassia put down her pen. "Bring her in."

She was younger than Cassia had expected — thirty, perhaps, with the worn prettiness of a woman who has been pretty and has had no time for it lately — and she came into the tablinum with a child on either side and her chin up and her eyes red.

"You are the woman my husband made the agreement with," she said.

"I am."

"He is in a cell under the prefect's house. They will not let me see him. They have sealed the door of our house and taken everything, and my sister will not have us any longer than a week, and I have no money and no one to turn to." Her voice was steady. "He told me, before they took him, that if anything happened I was to come to you. He said you were the only person in Rome who had ever kept a promise to him. He said you had signed a paper with your seal on it, and that the paper said his children would never want."

Cassia looked at the two children. The boy was nine, with his father's sharp face and his mother's eyes. The girl was six, and she was holding her mother's hand and staring at the three Graces on the wall.

"Show me the paper," Cassia said.

The woman took it from her sleeve. It was the agreement, sealed with Cassia's ring, and it was creased and soft from being carried against the body.

Cassia read it, and then she folded it and gave it back.

"Thais," she said. "Take the lady and the children to the room at the top of the stairs. The one with the window over the garden. Have Narcissus look at them. Have Celer make something hot. They are to stay until I say otherwise, and no one is to know they are here."

The woman stared at her. "You are giving us the room at the top of the stairs?"

"Yes."

"I thought —" She stopped. "I thought you would give us money and send us away."

"I thought so too," Cassia said. "It seems I am not the woman I was."

The potter came at the ninth hour, alone, with clay still under his fingernails and the look of a man who has been told that a lady wishes to speak to him about his son and has spent the afternoon imagining the worst.

Gaius Vibidius was fifty and broad and grey and entirely without pretension. He had a good, plain face, the face of a man who works with his hands and sleeps well, and he stood in the tablinum of the House of the Graces with his cap in his hands and did not look at the silver or the paintings or the three Graces in the alcove.

"Domina," he said. "What has my boy done?"

"Nothing," Cassia said. "Sit down. There is wine, and there is a great deal to tell you, and none of it is his fault."

He sat. He did not drink the wine. And Cassia told him.

She told him about the villa at Tibur, and the girl in the kitchen, and the son who was born in a cell, and the woman who took him out of the room. She told him about the receipt, and the four hundred sesterces, and the dealer named Caepio, and the twenty-four years. She did not tell him everything. She did not tell him about the cup, or the trial, or the men who wanted to use the boy to destroy a senator. She told him enough.

The potter listened without moving. When she had finished, he sat for a long time with his hands on his knees.

"I bought him," he said at last. "From a man in the Forum. My wife could not have children, and we had prayed, and we had gone to the temple, and a man told us there was a child to be had, and we paid for him, and we took him home." He looked up. "I did not know he was stolen. I want you to understand that. I thought he was a foundling, or the child of a woman who could not keep him. I did not know he was taken from his mother."

"He was not stolen from me," Cassia said. "He was taken. There is a difference, and I do not hold you responsible for it."

"He is my son," the potter said.

"I know."

"I have raised him for twenty-four years. I taught him the wheel and the kiln and the price of clay. He calls me father." His voice was steady, and his hands were not. "I am not going to give him up because a lady in a big house has decided that she wants him back."

"I am not asking you to give him up." Cassia leaned forward. "I am asking you to protect him. There are men in this city who want to use him to destroy a senator, and if they cannot use him they may hurt him, and I cannot protect him from the inside of my own house because I am the woman they are trying to destroy. You can. You are his father. You can take him out of the city for a month, to the country, to a workshop somewhere, and you can keep him safe until this is over."

The potter looked at her. "And after it is over?"

"After it is over," Cassia said, "he can decide for himself whether he wants to know me. I will not come to your shop. I will not send for him. I will not tell him who I am. That is his to decide, when he is ready, and if he is never ready, then I will live with it." She paused. "But I would like to know that he is alive. That is all. A message, once a year, from you, to say that he is alive."

Gaius Vibidius looked at her for a long time. Then he picked up the cup of wine and drank it off, and set it down, and said, "You are not what I expected."

"What did you expect?"

"I do not know. A lady." He wiped his mouth. "He looks like you, you know. Around the eyes. I have seen it for years and I never knew what it was, and now I know." He stood. "I will take him to my brother's workshop at Praeneste. He will not like it; he has a girl in Ostia and he will complain about the clay. But he will go, because he is a good boy, and because I will tell him that the workshop needs him." He put on his cap. "And domina. When this is over. If he wants to know you. I will not stand in his way."

Cassia stood. "Thank you."

"Do not thank me. I have not done anything yet." He went to the door. "But I will tell you one thing. If you had come to my shop twenty-four years ago and asked for him, I would have given him to you. I would have hated it. But I would have given him to you, because he was yours before he was mine." He went out.

Cassia stood in the tablinum for a long time. Then she sat down and put her head in her hands, and Thais came in and stood beside her, and the house was quiet.

"The hearing is set," Thais said. "The consul's clerk sent word. The sixth day before the Kalends of July."

Cassia looked up. "Then we have three weeks."

"Three weeks," Thais said, "and one witness in a cell, and a banker with a lawyer, and a senator with a dying mother and a burnt house and a boy he has never met."

"Yes," Cassia said. "Three weeks."

There was a letter from the Palatine on the table, in Corvinus's hand.

*The notary has been again. My mother has given the second paper. She dictated it in the morning and signed it in the afternoon and then she slept for six hours, which the physician says is a bad sign. She has asked for you. She says she has something else to tell you and that she will not tell it to me. I do not know what else there is. I am beginning to think that I have never known my mother at all.*

*The hearing is set for the sixth day before the Kalends of July. Pactus has been seen at the palace twice. Sittius has left the city for his villa at Baiae, which means he intends to be far away when it happens, and that he expects it to happen.*

*I am not afraid of the trial. I am afraid of what will be left of us afterwards. I have spent my whole life being a Sulpicius. I do not know how to be anything else.*

*L.*

Cassia read it twice. Then she folded it and put it in the drawer with the other letter, the one that had been in the cedar box for twenty-four years, and she sat for a moment with her hand on the drawer.

Then she took out a clean sheet of paper and wrote:

*You will learn. It is not as difficult as it looks. You begin by putting one foot in front of the other and doing the next necessary thing, and you do it again the next day, and after a while you find that you have become someone else, and that the someone else is not so bad.*

*I will come to the Palatine tomorrow. Tell your mother to save whatever she has to say. I have a great deal to do and very little time, and I am not going to spend it listening to an old woman's riddles.*

*C.*

She sanded the letter and sealed it and gave it to Bathyllus, and then she turned back to the accounts and worked until the lamps burned low.

---

## Chapter Fifteen

The wedding was set for the ninth day of June, and the whole of the Palatine knew it, and by the fifth of June the house of the Sulpicii had begun to look like a house under siege.

There were deliveries at every hour: baskets of flowers, bolts of cloth, jars of wine, a new couch for the bridal chamber, a chest of linen that had been ordered in March and arrived late. There were slaves in the atrium with ladders and garlands, and the steward in the tablinum with the accounts, and Aemilia in the garden with the cook, and Sulpicia Varia in her room, dying, giving orders.

The bride herself came to the House of the Graces on the morning of the seventh, before the sun was high, alone but for one slave, by the garden door.

Cassia was in the tablinum with the black book open and the accounts in front of her, and she looked up when Bathyllus brought the girl in and did not immediately understand who she was, because the girl was in a plain cloak with her hair tied back and no paint on her face, and she looked younger than fifteen and much more tired.

"Domina," Sulpicia said. "I have come to ask you for something."

"Sit down."

"I would rather stand."

"Then stand." Cassia closed the book. "What do you want?"

The girl's chin came up. "I am to be married in two days. My grandmother has arranged it, my father has agreed to it, my mother has told me that it is a good match and that I will learn to be happy. I have met the man twice. He is not stupid and he is not cruel and he has a great deal of money, and when he looks at me he sees a girl who will run his house and give him sons and make him look settled." She stopped. "I do not want to marry him."

"Then do not."

"You know that I cannot—"

"I know that you can." Cassia stood. "You are fifteen years old and the daughter of a consular and you have been told all your life that your marriage is a matter for your family. That is true. It is also true that women in this city have refused marriages before, and that the ones who refuse them pay a price, and that the price is sometimes worth paying." She came around the desk. "But you have not come here to ask me whether you should refuse. You have come here to ask me something else. What is it?"

The girl looked at her. Her eyes were her father's eyes, and they were frightened, and they were steady.

"How do I survive it?" she said.

Cassia looked at her for a long moment. Then she took her arm and led her to the garden, where the roses were in flower and the fountain was playing and the morning light was coming through the vines.

"You survive it by keeping something," Cassia said. "Not your freedom; you will not have that. Not your money; that will be his. Something else. A room in your house that is yours and no one else's. A friend you do not tell him about. A skill you learn that he does not know you have. A purse that no one knows about, hidden where he will not look, with enough in it to buy a passage out of the city if you ever need one." She looked at the girl. "I have known women who survived forty years of marriage on nothing but a purse and a friend. I have known women who had everything and survived nothing. The difference is not love. It is not beauty. It is the knowledge that there is one thing in the world that is yours."

"And if he beats me?"

"Then you come to me."

"And if he beats me and I cannot come to you?"

"Then you put a knife in your sleeve and you learn how to use it." Cassia said it without emphasis. "I am not going to tell you that it will not happen. I am going to tell you that you are not a child, and that you are not a fool, and that a woman who knows how to use a knife is a woman who is beaten less often than a woman who does not."

The girl looked at her. Then she laughed — a short, shocked, entirely genuine laugh — and put her hand over her mouth.

"My mother would faint," she said.

"Your mother is a better woman than I am and a worse strategist." Cassia reached into her sleeve and took out a small purse and put it into the girl's hand. "This is yours. It is not your dowry and it is not your father's money and it is no one's business but yours. There are two hundred gold pieces in it. Do not tell your husband. Do not tell your mother. Do not tell your brother, because your brother cannot keep a secret, and do not tell your grandmother, because your grandmother will take it off you and put it in the family strongbox where she thinks it belongs." She closed the girl's fingers around the purse. "And if you ever need to leave, there is a house on the Esquiline with a garden door, and the woman who lives in it does not judge."

Sulpicia looked at the purse in her hand. Her eyes were bright.

"Why are you doing this?" she said.

"Because a woman gave me nothing when I was fifteen," Cassia said, "and I have spent my whole life making up for it."

---

The bride's toilette began at the second hour in the small room off the garden, with the shutters open and the morning sun coming in, and it was, in its way, the most political hour of the whole wedding.

Aemilia directed it. She had been up since before dawn and she was dressed in the wine-coloured stola she had worn at the games, and she had the keys at her belt and the household lists in her head and a face that gave nothing away. The women were hers: the two aunts from the country, the cousin from Ostia, the old nurse who had come out of retirement for the day, the hairdresser from the Subura who had been paid four times her usual fee to keep her mouth shut about the shape of the bride's head.

The bride sat on a stool in the middle of the room in her tunic with her hair loose and her hands in her lap, and let them work.

There was a ritual to it, and the ritual had been old when Rome was young. The hair was divided into six locks with the point of a spear — a spear that was kept in the house for this purpose and had been used at every Sulpician wedding for two hundred years — and each lock was coiled and pinned, and the whole was bound with woollen fillets, and the orange veil was laid over it and pinned with a brooch that had come down through four generations. The bride's tunic was woven in the old way, on an upright loom, and her shoes were the same red as the veil, and her girdle was tied with the knot of Hercules, which the bridegroom was to untie, and which the women of the household had tied, that morning, with a great deal of laughing and a great many suggestions about what the bridegroom would do when he got to it.

Aemilia watched from the corner. She had done this herself, nineteen years before, in this same room, with a different set of women and a different brooch and a great deal of hope.

"Hold still," the hairdresser said, for the fourth time.

"I am holding still."

"You are not. You are breathing."

"People do."

"Not on their wedding day. On their wedding day they hold their breath and they faint, and then we have to start again." The hairdresser put a pin in with a good deal of force. "There. Now the veil."

The old nurse, who was ninety and had the voice of a woman who has never once been contradicted, came forward with the veil and laid it over the girl's head and said, "There. You look like your grandmother."

"Thank you," Sulpicia said.

"It was not a compliment," the old nurse said. "She was a difficult woman and she made everyone around her miserable, and you have her chin."

There was a silence. Then one of the aunts laughed, and then the whole room laughed, and the bride laughed too, and for a moment she was fifteen and not a marriage portion, and Aemilia, standing in the corner, felt something turn over in her chest.

When the others had gone to see about the flowers, she came and stood in front of her daughter.

"You will not have an easy time of it," she said. "The Proculi are not cruel people, but they are vain, and vanity is harder to live with than cruelty. You will be expected to be pretty and agreeable and to produce a son in the first year, and if you do not, they will be disappointed, and they will tell you so in a hundred small ways." She straightened the veil. "You will want to be liked. Do not be liked. Be necessary. A woman who is liked is a woman who can be replaced by someone prettier. A woman who is necessary cannot be replaced at all."

"Grandmother said the same thing."

"Your grandmother was right about a great many things and wrong about the only one that mattered." Aemilia took her daughter's hands. "She was right that the family is worth everything. She was wrong that a person is worth nothing. You are not nothing, Sulpicia. You are my daughter, and you are clever, and you are going to be a great deal more than a wife in a house on the Palatine." She kissed her forehead, under the veil. "Now. Do not cry. It will ruin the paint."

The girl did not cry. She looked at her mother with her father's eyes and her grandmother's chin, and she said, "Mother. Did you ever think of leaving?"

Aemilia looked at her for a long moment. The sun was high, and the house was full of the noise of the wedding, and somewhere in the garden the flute players were tuning up.

"Every day," she said. "And every day I found a reason not to." She let go of her daughter's hands. "That is the difference between you and me. You have a purse in your sleeve and you know how to use it. I had nothing but a reason not to, and I made it my whole life." She turned to the door. "Come. They are waiting."

The wedding was at the house of the Sulpicii, in the old atrium with the masks, and Cassia was not invited, and she went anyway.

She did not go in. She stood in the street outside with the crowd, in a plain cloak with her hair covered, and watched the guests arrive: the Proculi in their litters, the senators and their wives, the clients with their gifts, the flute players, the boys with the torches. The door of the house was hung with garlands and the whole street smelled of flowers and smoke.

Tertulla stood beside her. "We are going to be seen."

"Let them see."

"You said we were not to be seen together until the trial."

"I said I would not be seen with your husband," Cassia said. "You are not my husband. You are my friend, and I am standing in the street at a wedding, and there is nothing in the law against it."

Tertulla laughed. "You have an answer for everything."

"I have had a great deal of practice."

The bride came out of the house at noon, and the crowd pressed forward. Sulpicia was in the traditional dress — the white tunic, the orange veil, the elaborate arrangement of hair with the spear in it — and she looked small and stiff and very young, and she was holding her father's arm so tightly that Cassia could see the knuckles of her hand go white.

Corvinus walked her through the crowd to the house of Aulus Proculus, and Aemilia walked behind with the bridesmaids, and Publius carried the torch, and the flute players played the wedding song, and the crowd sang the bawdy verses that were traditional and that were, that year, more than usually obscene.

Cassia watched the girl go. When she passed, for a moment, their eyes met, and Sulpicia's chin came up, and her hand went to her sleeve — to the purse that was hidden there — and she nodded, once, very slightly, and went on.

"She will be all right," Tertulla said.

"I know."

"How do you know?"

"Because she has something in her sleeve," Cassia said, "and because she knows it."

The procession went up the hill through the narrow streets, and the crowd grew as it went, because a wedding procession in Rome is a thing that gathers people the way a fire gathers people, and by the time it reached the top of the Palatine there were perhaps three hundred of them.

The flute players went first, and then the torchbearers, and then the bride with her father, and then the women, and then the clients and the neighbours and the children and the dogs. The songs were the old songs, the ones that were sung at every wedding in the city, and they were obscene in the way that the old songs are obscene, and the crowd sang them with the particular delight of people who have been given permission to be rude in public.

Cassia watched from the edge of it. She watched the torchlight on the cobbles and the garlands on the doorposts and the faces in the crowd, and she thought about all the weddings she had seen and all the ones she had not been invited to, and about the fact that she had never had one.

She had been married once, in a way. Antonius Felix had freed her in his will and left her his name and his fortune, and there had been no ceremony, no veil, no songs, no torch. There had been a lawyer and a tablet and an old man in a chair, and he had put his hand on her head and said, "You are a free woman now. Do not waste it." And she had gone out into the street with the tablet in her hand and the sun in her eyes and discovered that freedom was a thing that had to be paid for every day.

Corvinus came past with his daughter on his arm, and he did not look at Cassia, and Cassia did not look at him, and the whole exchange took a fraction of a second and contained a lifetime.

Publius came next, carrying the torch, and he did look at her — a quick, frightened, determined look — and then he turned his face back to the front and walked on.

Behind the bride, Aemilia walked with the married women, and she looked straight ahead, and her face was composed, and when she passed Cassia, she said, without turning her head, in a voice that only Cassia could hear, "The purse. Thank you."

Cassia said nothing. The procession went on up the hill.

At the door of the bridegroom's house the procession stopped, and the bridegroom came out, and the girl was lifted over the threshold amid a great deal of shouting, and the door closed behind them, and the crowd began to disperse.

Cassia turned away.

"Domina." Bathyllus, at her elbow. "There is a slave from the Palatine. He says the old woman is asking for you."

---

The house on the Palatine was quiet in the way that a house is quiet when everyone in it is somewhere else. The atrium was empty. The masks looked out of their cupboards. The garlands from the wedding were still hanging, and the floor was still scattered with flowers, and the whole place smelled of crushed petals and wine.

Cassia went up the stairs alone. The door of the old woman's room was open, and the room was dark except for a single lamp, and Sulpicia Varia was lying propped up on her pillows with her eyes open and her hands folded.

"You did not go to the wedding," she said.

"I was in the street."

"Of course you were." The old woman's voice was a whisper now. "I have been listening to it from up here. The songs. The flute. The shouting. It sounds like a funeral, a little. The songs are the same, you know. The bawdy ones. At a funeral they sing the same ones, and everyone laughs, and then everyone cries." She turned her head on the pillow. "I have been waiting for you. I have something else to give you."

"I have the two statements."

"This is not a statement." Sulpicia Varia's hand moved under the pillow and came out with a letter, folded and sealed and brown with age. "This is a letter. It was written to my husband in the year of the consulship of Domitian and Silanus, by a man named Primus, who was then a clerk in the office of the treasury and is now the emperor's freedman, the a rationibus, the man who counts the emperor's money."

Cassia took the letter. The seal was broken. The hand was small and neat and careful.

"What does it say?"

"It says that a shipment of grain was diverted, and that the money from the sale was shared among three men, and that my husband's share was paid into the account of a banker on the Via Lata. It says that the clerk will keep his part of the bargain and expects my husband to keep his. It says that if anything happens to the clerk, he has taken precautions." The old woman's eyes moved to Cassia's face. "Does it say that? I cannot read anymore. My eyes are bad. It said that when I read it, twenty-four years ago."

Cassia read the letter. Then she read it again.

"Yes," she said. "That is what it says."

"Then you understand."

"I understand that your husband was a thief."

"My husband was a great many things. He was a thief and a bully and a man who could not bear to be crossed. But he was not the only one." Sulpicia Varia's voice was very quiet. "The clerk Primus is now one of the most powerful men in Rome. He controls the emperor's purse and the emperor's accounts and a great deal of the emperor's time. He has been paying me for twenty-four years not to publish this letter. And now my son is standing for the Asian command, and the clerk has decided that the Sulpicii have become a liability, and he has found a new man — your Pactus — who can destroy the family and get the letter at the same time."

Cassia sat down on the stool beside the bed. The letter was very light in her hand. It had been written twenty-four years ago by a clerk who was now one of the most powerful men in the empire, and it was worth more than the house and the vineyard and everything in them.

"The land near the forum," she said. "The claim. The case in the courts for fifteen years."

"The land is worth a fortune, and the claim is good, and the clerk wants it. He has wanted it for years. He thought he could buy it, and my husband would not sell, and then my husband died, and I would not sell, and then my son would not sell." The old woman closed her eyes. "We are a stubborn family, domina. It is the only thing we have that is worth anything."

"You should have burned this letter," Cassia said. "This letter is a death warrant. If Primus knows you have it, he will do anything to get it. He has already burned my house. He will kill you, and he will kill your son, and he will kill the boy on the Via Ostiensis, and he will do it from a desk in the palace without ever leaving his chair."

"I know."

"Then why have you kept it?"

"Because it is the only thing I have left that is worth dying for," Sulpicia Varia said. "I have spent twenty-four years keeping this house and this name and this family together. I have done things that I will answer for. And when they came for us, I had nothing to fight with except a letter and a dying woman's word." She looked at Cassia. "I am giving it to you. Not to my son. Not to the lawyer. To you. Because you are the only person I have ever met who understands what a thing is worth."

Cassia looked at the letter in her hand.

"What do you want in exchange?"

"Nothing."

"You always want something."

"Not this time." The old woman's eyes were wet. "This time I want to do one thing that is not for the family, and not for my son, and not for the house. I want to give you the weapon, and I want to see what you do with it." She turned her head away. "Now go. My granddaughter is being married tonight, and I am tired, and I would like to die in peace."

Cassia stood. At the door she said, "I have not forgiven you."

"I know."

"I will not forgive you."

"I know that too." Sulpicia Varia's voice was almost gone. "Go and save my son, domina. And when it is over, if you ever have a daughter, do not do what I did. Do not make her a thing that can be traded. Make her a woman who can trade."

---

The door of the bridegroom's house closed at last, and the crowd stood outside it for a while, singing and shouting and waiting for the bridegroom to come out and throw nuts to them, which he did, at last, laughing, with his hair tousled and his tunic already loosened, and the crowd scrambled for the nuts and the coins and the sweets, and then, slowly, began to disperse.

Cassia stayed until the street was almost empty. Then she walked down the hill alone, with Bathyllus behind her at a distance, and the city was quiet and warm and smelled of flowers and dust and the river.

At the corner of the Clivus Victoriae a man was waiting for her.

He was a small man in a plain tunic with the look of a palace clerk: neat hands, neat hair, a face that had been trained to be forgotten. He stepped out of a doorway as she passed and said, quietly, "The lady is to know that the letter is required."

Cassia stopped. Bathyllus came up behind her, fast, and the clerk did not move.

"Which letter?" Cassia said.

"The letter from the year of the consulship of Domitian and Silanus. The one the old woman has kept." The clerk's voice was polite and entirely without threat, and that was the most frightening thing about it. "My master is prepared to pay a great deal for it. He is also prepared to be patient. He is not prepared to be disobeyed."

"Who is your master?"

The clerk smiled. "The lady knows."

He went away down the hill, and Bathyllus started after him, and Cassia said, "No. Let him go."

"Domina—"

"He is a messenger. There is no point in killing a messenger." She stood in the street with the warm wind on her face and the letter in her sleeve. "But he is right about one thing. The letter is required. By him, by Pactus, by the consul, by the whole city." She began to walk again. "The only question is who gets it first."

She went back up the hill by the garden lane and in through the door that the steward had left unbolted for her, and the house was still full of noise, and she sat in the garden with the letter in her sleeve and waited for the wedding to end.

The wedding night went on until dawn, and the noise of it carried up the hill and into the streets, and Cassia sat in the garden of the Sulpicii house with Corvinus and did not go home.

The guests were gone. The torches had burned down. The slaves were clearing the atrium, and somewhere in the house the bride and groom were behind a closed door, and the whole city was asleep except for the two of them.

"Show me the letter," he said.

She gave it to him. He read it by the light of a single lamp, and his face did not change, and when he had finished he folded it and gave it back.

"Primus," he said. "I have known him for twenty years. He was at my father's funeral. He sent a wreath." He laughed, without any humour at all. "He has been in my house a hundred times. He has sat at my table. He has advised me on the Asian command."

"He has been managing you for twenty years."

"Yes."

"He is the man who is destroying you."

"Yes."

"And Pactus is his instrument, and Sittius is his money, and the fire was his message." Cassia looked at the letter. "And your mother has been holding this over his head for twenty-four years, and he has been paying her, and now he has decided that the Sulpicii are more trouble than they are worth."

Corvinus looked at the garden. The sky over the Palatine was beginning to go grey. Somewhere in the house a woman laughed, and a door closed.

"What do we do?" he said.

"We fight," Cassia said. "Not in the Senate. Not in the courts. In the palace. We take this letter to a man who can use it — not the prefect, not the consul, not any of the men Primus has bought. To the emperor's own secretary, or to the empress, or to a man in the guard who hates him." She folded the letter and put it in her sleeve. "We do not go to the trial with a family scandal. We go to the trial with the head of the man who has been robbing the emperor for twenty years, and we put it on the table in front of the whole Senate, and we watch them eat each other."

"And if it does not work?"

"Then we lose everything," Cassia said. "And we will have lost it honestly."

"Honestly." He turned the word over. "I have been a senator for twenty years. I do not think I know what it means anymore. Every speech I have made has been a calculation. Every vote has been a favour or a debt. Every dinner has been a negotiation. I have not said a true thing in public since I was a young man, and I am not sure I said one then." He looked at her. "You are the only person I have ever told the truth to. I told it to you in a vineyard when I was twenty, and I told it to you in a room above a kitchen when I was forty-five, and both times it cost me everything."

"It cost you nothing," Cassia said. "It cost you the girl. That is not the same."

"It is exactly the same."

She looked at him. The lamp was burning low, and the garden was grey, and somewhere in the house the bride and groom were asleep behind a closed door.

"There is something I have never asked you," she said.

"Ask."

"Why did you not come back for me? Not in the first year. In the second, or the third, or the tenth. You came back to Rome. You saw the villa sold. You knew my name. You could have asked. You could have sent a man to Puteoli. There were a hundred men in this city who could have found me for the price of a dinner." Her voice was steady. "Why did you not?"

He was silent for a long time. The birds were louder now, and the sky was going from grey to gold.

"Because I believed I had killed you," he said. "Not with a sword. With my silence. I believed that I had stood in a room with my father and said nothing while he told me what he was going to do, and that you had died because of it, and that the child had died because of it, and that a man who has done a thing like that does not go looking for the woman he has killed." He looked at her. "I was twenty years old, and then I was twenty-five, and then I was thirty, and by then the silence had become a kind of house that I lived in, and I did not know how to leave it."

"You could have tried."

"Yes."

"You could have asked one question."

"Yes."

"One question, Lucius. That is all it would have taken." Cassia's eyes were bright. "I spent four years in that house at Puteoli. Four years. And every night I lay awake and thought about the fact that you did not come, and I made myself hate you, because hating you was the only thing I had that was mine. And now you tell me that you were grieving. That you were silent. That you were twenty." She stood up. "I do not know what to do with that. I have been hating you for so long that I do not know what I am without it."

"You are still you," he said. "You are the woman who built the house."

"I am the woman who built the house because of you," Cassia said. "Because of you and your father and your mother and the whole cold machine of that family. I built it to be safe. I built it so that no one could ever sell me again. And now I am standing in your garden with a letter in my sleeve that could destroy a man in the palace, and I am not safe, and I have not been safe for a year, and I find —" She stopped.

"What?"

"I find that I do not mind," she said. "I find that I would rather be unsafe with you than safe in the house alone."

He stood up. They looked at each other across the lamp, and the sky was gold now, and the first cart went past in the street below, and the day of the trial was seventeen days away.

"Then we will be unsafe together," he said.

"That is the worst speech you have ever made."

"I know. I am out of practice."

She laughed, and then she was crying, and he put his arms around her, and they stood in the garden of the house on the Palatine while the sun came up over the city and the masks in the atrium looked out of their cupboards at nothing at all.

---

## Chapter Sixteen

The strongroom was behind the panel in the tablinum, and it had a bronze door and a lock that had cost more than the couch in the dining room, and only three people in the house knew where the key was kept: Cassia, Hermeros, and Thais.

On the morning of the seventeenth of June, Cassia opened it and found the black book where she had left it, and the strongbox where she had left it, and the wooden box with the receipt where she had left it, and everything in its place. She stood in the little room with the lamp in her hand and looked at the shelves for a long moment, and then she crouched down and looked at the floor.

There was a mark in the dust by the bottom shelf. It was a small mark, the kind a knee makes when a person kneels down and then gets up again, and it was fresh, and it was not hers. She had not knelt in this room in a month.

She stood up slowly. She put the black book on the desk in the tablinum and opened it and went through it page by page, and the pages were all there, and the entries were all there, and nothing had been torn out or added. She held the book up to the window and looked at the edges of the pages, and on the last twenty pages there was a faint shine, a ghost of wax, the kind of mark that is left when a page has been pressed against a wax tablet and copied.

She sat down.

"Hermeros," she said.

He came. She showed him the book and the shine and the mark in the dust. He looked at all of it for a long time, and then he sat down opposite her and put his hands on his knees.

"When?" she said.

"Not this morning. The dust is fresh but the shine is old — three weeks, perhaps a month. It has been done more than once." He turned a page. "They were careful. They did not take the book out of the room. They came in, they copied, they went out, and they put everything back where it was."

"Who has been in this room?"

"Everyone, domina. It is the tablinum. The whole household comes through it. The women bring the linen, the boys sweep the floor, Celer comes in to argue about the price of fish, the steward comes in with the accounts." He paused. "But the strongroom is another matter. The strongroom is locked, and the key is in your room, and only three people know where it is."

Cassia looked at him.

"Four," she said. "I told Sabina where it was, two years ago, when I was ill. I told her to fetch the strongbox."

"Sabina is twelve."

"Sabina is twelve and she would die before she stole from me, and she is not strong enough to open the door in any case." Cassia closed the book. "Who else has been in the house with time and quiet? Not the kitchen. Not the women upstairs. Someone who could come and go without being noticed, who could be in the tablinum for a quarter of an hour without anyone thinking it strange."

She sat for a long moment. Then she said, "Send for Ione."

---

Ione came in with her hair half-dressed and a comb in her hand. She was to be freed at the end of the month; the papers were drawn up, and she had been making her arrangements, and she had been happier in the last three months than Cassia had ever seen her.

"You wanted me," she said.

"Close the door."

She closed it. Cassia did not ask her to sit.

"Someone has been copying the black book," Cassia said. "For a month, perhaps longer. Pages pressed onto wax, transcribed somewhere else, sold to someone. I want to know who."

Ione's face did not change. "And you think it was me."

"I think you are leaving in two weeks. I think you have been saving money for years. I think you have been seen twice in the last month with a man in the Subura, and I think you have been careful not to tell me who he is." Cassia's voice was level. "I think you have the knowledge, the opportunity, and the motive."

"The motive." Ione's voice was low. "I have been with you for nine years. I have kept your secrets and I have sat with your clients and I have smiled at men who made my skin crawl, and I have never once asked you for anything that you did not offer. And you think I would sell you for money."

"I think people do a great many things for money."
