"Yes," Ione said. "They do. I have seen it. I have been the thing that is sold." She put the comb down on the desk. "I did not take your book. I do not know who took your book. And I am not going to stand here and be questioned like a slave in a market."

"You are a slave."

The room was very quiet. Ione's face went white and then red and then white again.

"Yes," she said. "I am. Until the end of the month. And then I will be a freedwoman with forty thousand sesterces and no house and no name, and you will be a rich woman with a house and a book and a friend who has been with you for nine years, and you will have lost the friend because you could not bear to be wrong." She picked up her comb. "I will stay until the papers are signed, because I gave you my word. But I will not sleep in this house again. I will sleep in the kitchen with the boys, and I will not speak to you unless you speak to me, and when the papers are signed I will go out of that door and I will not come back."

"Ione—"

"No," Ione said. "You have made your accusation. Do not take it back because it has made you uncomfortable." She went out and closed the door quietly behind her.

Cassia sat at the desk for a long time.

---

The truth came three days later, and it came from Philumena.

She came to the tablinum in the evening, when the house was quiet, and she stood in the doorway with her hands twisted in her apron and her face the colour of old wax, and she said, "It was me, domina."

Cassia looked up from the accounts.

"It was me," Philumena said again. "I copied the book. I did not take it out of the room. I pressed the pages onto wax and I copied them at night and I gave them to a man. I have been doing it for a month. I am sorry. I am so sorry." She began to cry. "They have my brother."

"Who has your brother?"

"The man. The man who paid me. He is a steward — he works for the senator Pactus. He came to me in the spring and he said that my brother was in the vigiles in the fourth cohort, and that my brother had been caught taking money from a shopkeeper, and that if I did not do what he asked, my brother would be dismissed and charged and sent to the mines." She wiped her face with her sleeve. "And I said I would do it. And he said it would be one page, and then it was another page, and then it was the names at the back, and then it was the accounts of the payments, and I did not know what to do. I could not tell you. If I told you, they would have killed him."

Cassia sat very still. The lamp burned.

"Where is your brother now?"

"They let him go. Last week. He came to see me and he did not know anything about it, and he laughed and said he had been given a warning and a fine and he was lucky." Philumena's hands were shaking. "So it was all for nothing. I sold you for nothing."

"You did not sell me. You gave them a copy of a book that is worth more than you can imagine, and they will use it to destroy this house and everyone in it, and the fact that your brother is safe does not change that." Cassia stood. "But you are not the first person to be frightened into doing something terrible, and you will not be the last, and I am not going to have you killed."

Philumena looked up. "You are not?"

"No." Cassia came around the desk. "You are going to write down everything. The man's name, his appearance, the place where you met, the dates, the words he used. You are going to sign it. And then you are going to leave this house tonight, with money, and you are going to go to your brother and tell him what you did, and he will be angry and then he will help you, and you will go somewhere far from Rome and you will not come back."

"Domina—"

"Do not thank me." Cassia's voice was hard. "You have cost me two weeks and a friend and the safety of everyone in this house. I am not being kind. I am being practical. If I kill you, I lose the only witness I have to the man who bought you, and I need that witness more than I need the satisfaction." She held out a tablet. "Write."

Philumena wrote. It took an hour. When it was done Cassia read it through twice and then sealed it and put it in the strongroom and gave Philumena a purse and a cloak and a letter to a woman in Ostia who kept a lodging house and did not ask questions.

At the door Philumena turned and said, "Domina. The lady Ione. She did not do it."

"I know."

"She was crying in the kitchen two nights ago. She said you had accused her and she did not know how to prove that she had not." Philumena wiped her face. "She is a good woman. She is better than me."

"I know that too," Cassia said, and her voice was not steady. "Go. And Philumena — when you get to Ostia, do not write to me. Do not send word. Do not tell anyone where you are going. There are men who will be looking for you, and I cannot protect you if I do not know where you are."

Philumena went. Cassia stood in the tablinum with the lamp burning and the black book on the desk and the empty doorway.

---

She sent for Corvinus that afternoon and met him at Tertulla's house, and they sat in the garden with the bees in the rosemary and the city noise coming over the wall, and she told him everything: the copies, the steward, the arsonist, the letter from Pactus, the boy gone from Praeneste.

He listened without interrupting. When she had finished he sat for a moment with his hands on his knees.

"The steward's name," he said.

"Anthus. He has been with Pactus for eleven years. He was a slave in the household and was freed in the year of the consulship of Palumbus, and he keeps a room above the gate of Pactus's house." Cassia turned her cup. "He is the weak point. A freedman who has done his master's dirty work for eleven years will be frightened, and a frightened freedman will talk if he is offered a better master and a place to hide."

"You want to turn him."

"I want to offer him a choice. He can go to the prefect's court and tell the truth and be given a pension and a house in a town far from Rome, or he can be arrested with his master and share his fate. It is not a difficult choice."

"It is if his master has something on him."

"Everyone has something on everyone," Cassia said. "That is what makes the world go round."

Corvinus looked at the garden wall. "The hearing is tomorrow. We have no time to turn a steward."

"Then we will do it without him." Cassia took a sheet of paper from her sleeve. "Junia's son is a clerk in the prefect's office. He has given me the names of the men who will hear the case, and their connections, and their debts. Three of the five are in the pocket of the palace. One is a cousin of Pactus. One is old Servilius Paetus, who hates new men and loves a good speech." She put the paper on the table. "That is our man. He will not vote for the palace and he will not vote for Pactus, and if we give him a reason to be angry, he will make a speech that will be remembered."

"How do we give him a reason?"

"We tell him the truth." Cassia folded the paper. "Not the truth about the boy. The truth about the grain. The truth about Primus and the money and the twenty years of theft. Paetus is an old republican. He will not care about a senator's bastard, but he will care a great deal about a freedman robbing the emperor and buying the Senate."

Corvinus was silent for a moment. Then he said, "If we do this, there is no going back. Not for me, not for my family, not for you. If we accuse a man in the palace of fraud, we had better be able to prove it, because if we cannot, we will be the ones in the mines."

"Then we had better be able to prove it," Cassia said.

She went to the kitchen. Ione was sitting on the bench by the door with a blanket around her shoulders and her hands around a cup.

"Ione."

"Domina."

"I was wrong." Cassia stood in the doorway. "It was Philumena. She was frightened. Her brother was threatened. I accused you because it was easy and because you were leaving and because I was afraid, and I have treated you badly, and I am sorry."

Ione looked at her for a long moment. Then she said, "I know it was Philumena."

"You knew?"

"I have known for a week. She has been crying in the linen room and she has not been able to look at you and she has been burning the candles down to nothing in the night." Ione turned her cup. "I did not tell you because I wanted to see whether you would believe me. And you did not."

"That is not fair."

"No," Ione said. "It is not. But it is what I did, and I am not sorry." She set the cup down. "You have spent your whole life being the cleverest person in the room, domina. You told me once that you never forgot anything and that you never trusted anyone and that it was the reason you were still alive. And I have watched you for nine years, and I have seen that it is true, and I have seen what it costs." She stood up. "I am going to be free in two weeks. And I am going to leave this house and I am going to be somebody else. But I want you to know that I would have stayed, if you had asked me. I would have stayed and run the house with you and grown old with you and none of it would have been yours alone. And now I am not going to stay, because you did not ask, and because you would not have believed me if I had said yes."

Cassia stood in the kitchen doorway with the lamp in her hand.

"I am asking now," she said.

Ione looked at her. For a moment something moved in her face — grief, or anger, or the last of a long affection.

"Too late," she said, and went past her, and up the stairs.

---

The man who set the fire was called Verecundus, and Bathyllus caught him on the twenty-first of June, in a wine shop near the Porta Flumentana, drinking away the last of his fee.

He was a thin man of forty with a broken nose and a brand on his forearm, and he did not deny it. He had been paid by a steward to set a fire in a house on the Esquiline, and he had been told that the house would be empty, and he had not known about the boy in the passage. When Bathyllus told him about the boy, he put his cup down and sat looking at the table for a long time.

"I did not know," he said.

"There was a child in the passage and the door was bolted."

"I did not bolt it. I was told the door would be open. I was told the passage was empty." Verecundus wiped his mouth with the back of his hand. "I have done a great many things in my life, but I have never killed a child. I have children of my own."

Cassia came to the wine shop herself, with Bathyllus and two of Tertulla's men. She sat down opposite the arsonist and put her hands on the table and looked at him.

"You will write a statement," she said. "You will name the man who paid you and the man he works for. You will say what you were told and what you did and what you did not know. And then you will come with me to the prefect's court and you will say it again in front of a magistrate."

"And if I do not?"

"Then I will give you to my doorkeeper and my two friends, and they will take you out of the city and they will do to you what you did to my house." Cassia's voice was soft. "But I do not want to do that. I want the men who paid you. You are a tool. A tool is not worth destroying."

Verecundus looked at her. Then he picked up his cup and drank the rest of the wine and set it down and said, "Give me the tablet."

He wrote for an hour. His hand was shaking, and he could not spell, and the statement was full of holes and contradictions, but it named the steward and the steward's master, and it said the words *the senator Pactus*, and it was witnessed by Bathyllus and by the wine shop's owner and by a man at the next table who had been listening and had decided that he would rather be a witness than a suspect.

Cassia took it home and locked it in the strongroom with the others, and then she sat down at her desk and put her face in her hands.

"Domina," Hermeros said, from the doorway. "There is a message from the consul's clerk."

She looked up.

"The hearing has been moved," he said. "It will be held tomorrow."

---

The household came into the atrium at dusk, and Cassia stood on the step of the impluvium and told them what was happening.

"Tomorrow there will be a hearing in the Senate. The senator Corvinus will be accused of a crime he did not commit, and I will be called as a witness, and the men who are bringing the accusation will try to use this house and the people in it to destroy him." She looked at them. "I am not going to lie to you. Some of you may be questioned. Some of you may be taken to the prefect's house and asked the same questions over and over until you are tired enough to answer them. That is what the law allows. It is a filthy law, and it is the law we have."

Nobody spoke. The lamplight moved on the water of the impluvium.

"Thais will have a paper with the name of every one of you and the name of a person to go to if I do not come back. There is money in the strongbox for your freedom, and there is a letter to the lady Tertulla, and she will see that it is done." Cassia paused. "And if any of you wish to leave this house tonight, you may. I will not stop you and I will not hold it against you. Go to the door, tell Bathyllus, and he will give you a purse and a letter of introduction to a household in Ostia or Tibur."

There was a long silence. Then Celer said, "Domina, with respect, if I leave this house I will have to cook for strangers, and strangers do not appreciate the fish sauce."

Somebody laughed. Then somebody else. Then the whole household was laughing in the atrium, a little too loudly, the way people laugh when they are frightened, and Cassia laughed with them.

Nobody left.

Thais came to her room at the ninth hour with a cup of wine and a plate of bread and cheese and set them down on the table without asking.

"Eat," she said.

"I am not hungry."

"You never are. It is the most inconvenient thing about you." Thais sat down in the chair by the window. "I have been with you for twenty-two years. I have seen you in a brothel in Puteoli and in a room above a bakery and in this house. I have seen you buy your freedom and buy this house and buy every person in it. And I have never seen you frightened like this."

"I am not frightened."

"You are frightened and you are hiding it behind arithmetic, which is what you always do." Thais folded her arms. "What are you afraid of? Not the trial. You have been in worse rooms than a Senate."

"I am afraid of the boy," Cassia said. "I am afraid that Pactus has him, or that he has gone somewhere I cannot find him, and that the one thing I have wanted for twenty-four years is going to be taken away from me on the day before I have to stand up in front of the Senate and be called a whore." She put her hands flat on the table. "I am afraid that I have spent my whole life building a wall and that tomorrow I am going to find out that the wall was made of paper."

Thais was silent for a moment. Then she said, "Do you remember the first night you came to Puteoli?"

"I remember."

"You would not eat. You would not speak. You sat in the kitchen with your hands in your lap and you looked at the wall as though you were waiting for it to fall on you. And I said to you, 'You will not believe anything they tell you in the first month,' and you looked at me and you said, 'I do not believe anything anyone tells me.'" Thais's mouth moved. "I knew then that you would either die in that house or own it. There was no third thing."

"And now?"

"Now you are the woman who owns it, and you are sitting in the dark with a cup of wine you have not touched, and you are afraid." Thais stood. "You are allowed to be afraid. You are not allowed to be stupid. So eat the bread, and drink the wine, and tell me what you need me to do."

Cassia looked at her. Then she picked up the bread and ate it.

"The household," she said. "If I do not come back. The papers are in the strongroom. The will is in the box under the bed. The letter for Sabina is with the will. And the black book—" She stopped.

"The black book is the most dangerous thing in this house," Thais said. "If they take it, they will use it. Every name in it is a man who will be ruined, and every man who is ruined will blame you, and the ones who are not ruined will come for you to make sure they are not next."

"I know."

"Then burn it."

Cassia looked at the desk. The black book was lying there, open at a page of names.

"I have spent twenty-four years writing in it," she said. "It is the only thing I have that is entirely mine. Not the house. Not the money. The book. It is every favour and every debt and every secret and every lie, and it is the reason half the men in this city are afraid of me."

"It is the reason half the men in this city will hang you," Thais said. "Burn it."

Cassia sat for a long time. Then she picked up the book and carried it to the hearth and opened the door of the little bronze stove and put it in.

The pages caught slowly, because the book was thick and the paper was good, and she watched the names curl and blacken and turn to ash — Felix, Sittius, Pactus, Primus, a hundred others, a hundred secrets, a hundred small cruelties and large ones — and she felt nothing at all.

"Now the will," Thais said.

She wrote her will that night.

It was not a long document. The house went to Thais and Hermeros in trust for the household, with money for the manumission of everyone in it and enough to keep them for a year. The shipping venture went to Tertulla. The strongbox went to Sabina, with a letter that she was to open when she was eighteen. The black book she did not leave to anyone, because there was no longer a black book to leave.

The letter from Corvinus, the old one, the one that had been in the cedar box for twenty-four years, she did not burn. She put it in her sleeve.

Then she went up to her room and lay down in her clothes and did not sleep.

At dawn there was a knock at the garden door and Bathyllus came in with his face grey.

"Domina. The boy has gone from Praeneste. The potter's brother's workshop was visited yesterday by two men. The potter took the boy out through the back and they have not been seen since." He paused. "There is a message at the garden door. It is addressed to you."

Cassia took it. It was a single line, in a clerk's neat hand.

*The lady should consider whether a trial is worth a boy's life. P.*

She stood in the garden with the letter in her hand and the sun coming up over the burnt roof of the house, and she felt the whole of it come down on her at once — the fire, the boy, the black book, Ione, the trial, the letter in her sleeve — and for a moment she could not breathe.

Then she folded the letter and put it in her sleeve beside the other one.

"Bathyllus," she said. "Go to the Palatine. Tell the senator that the boy is missing and that Pactus has him, or knows where he is, and that I am going to the hearing, and that he is to do nothing until he hears from me." She turned. "And then go to Tertulla's house and tell her to find Junia's son, the clerk in the prefect's office, and to bring him to the court before the ninth hour."

"Domina—"

"Go."

She walked back into the house, through the burnt yard, into the tablinum, and she stood in front of the panel in the wall and looked at it for a moment.

Then she opened it and took out the receipt, and the two statements, and the letter from the palace, and the statement of the arsonist, and she put them all in a leather satchel and strapped it to her side.

"Thais," she said. "Bring me my veil. The dark one. And tell the household to bolt the doors and not to open them for anyone until I come back."

She went out into the street. The sun was up. The city was waking, and the whole of Rome was going about its business, buying and selling and arguing and dying, and somewhere in it a boy of twenty-four was hiding with a potter, and a senator was waiting in a cold house on the Palatine, and a consul was preparing to hear the most scandalous case of the year.

Cassia walked down the Clivus Suburanus toward the Forum with the satchel at her side and the two letters in her sleeve and the whole of her life behind her, and she did not look back, because a woman who looks back is a woman who is followed.

---

## Chapter Seventeen

She had been up since the fourth hour, and she had spent the first hour of the day doing something she had not done in twenty-four years: she had stood in front of the silvered mirror in her room and looked at herself as though she were a witness.

She was forty-two years old. There were lines at the corners of her eyes and a softness under her chin that had not been there at thirty, and the scar on her thumb had faded to a thin white line, and her hair, which had been black, was black still, with a few grey threads at the temples that Philumena had been allowed to pull out and that Cassia had lately begun to leave alone. She was not beautiful. She had never been beautiful, exactly. She had been striking, and then she had been handsome, and now she was a woman whose face made men in the Forum lower their voices.

"You are staring," Thais said, from the doorway.

"I am inspecting."

"You are staring, and you are frightened, and you are doing the thing you do when you are frightened, which is to look at yourself as though you were a problem that could be solved with a better dress."

"I am not frightened."

"You are frightened. I have known you for twenty-two years." Thais came in and set a cup of hot water and honey on the table. "Drink that. You will not eat, and if you faint in the Curia they will say it is a confession."

Cassia drank it. Then she put on the plain stola and the dark veil and the ring with her seal, and she stood in the atrium while the household assembled to see her off, which was a thing she had not asked them to do and could not bring herself to stop.

Sabina had made her a knot of rosemary and lavender, which was the sort of thing a child makes, and which Cassia had seen her working on for two days. She tied it to the strap of the satchel.

"For luck," Sabina said.

"Rosemary is for remembrance."

"Then it is for remembering to come back."

Cassia looked at her for a moment. Then she bent down and kissed her on the forehead, which she had done perhaps three times in the child's life.

"I will remember," she said.

She went out into the street. The morning was hot and bright and the city was already full of the noise of the day, and she walked down the Clivus Suburanus with Bathyllus behind her and the satchel on her shoulder and the smell of rosemary and lavender rising from the knot at her side.

At the corner of the Vicus Cyprius there was a crowd outside a wine shop, and she stopped, because the crowd was looking at something, and because a woman who is on her way to the Senate should know what the city is looking at.

It was Felix.

He was being taken from a cell to the prefect's house for his own hearing, and he was in chains, and he was walking between two men of the guard with his head up and his hair in his eyes, and he looked smaller than she remembered, and older, and entirely without the smile.

He saw her. He stopped, and one of the guards pushed him, and he did not move.

"Domina," he said.

The crowd turned. Cassia stood at the edge of it and looked at him.

"I kept my word," Felix said. "I gave you the statement. I told them everything I knew. And they came for me the next morning, and they took my house, and my wife is with her sister, and my children—" He stopped. "You said my children would never want."

"They will not."

"Then why am I in chains?"

"Because you were a delator for six years," Cassia said, "and because the men you served have decided that you are more useful to them in a cell than at a witness stand, and because I am not the only person in Rome who can keep a promise." She stepped closer. "I will not forget your children. I will not forget what you did. And when this is over, if I am still standing, I will come for you."

Felix looked at her for a long moment. Then he laughed — the old laugh, the one with the knife in it, but tired now, and without much hope.

"You are the only honest person in this city," he said, "and they are going to crucify you."

The guard pushed him on. The crowd closed behind them. Cassia stood in the street with the knot of rosemary and lavender at her side and her heart going like a drum, and then she turned and walked on toward the Forum.

The Curia Julia was full before the seventh hour, and the crowd outside it filled the Forum from the rostra to the basilica, and the whole city had the air of a festival that had gone wrong.

Cassia came on foot, because a litter would have been a provocation, with Bathyllus behind her and the satchel strapped to her side. She wore a plain stola and no jewellery except the ring with her seal on it, and her hair was bound up in the old way, and she had made Thais promise not to come, and Thais had promised, and Thais was somewhere in the crowd anyway.

The crowd parted for her and did not part kindly. There were voices. Somebody laughed. Somebody said a word that she had not been called in sixteen years, and Bathyllus turned his head, and she said, "No," and walked on.

At the door of the Curia the lictor stopped her.

"Women are not admitted."

"I am a witness."

"Women are not admitted."

"I am a witness summoned by the consul," Cassia said, "and if you turn me away, you will have to explain to him why the principal witness in the case of the century is standing in the Forum with the fishwives." She held out the summons. "Read it, if you can read."

The lictor read it. Then he stood aside.

The interior of the Curia was cool and dim and smelled of stone and wool and the particular smell of a great many nervous men. The benches were full. The consuls sat on their curule chairs at the end of the hall, and the clerks sat below them with their tablets, and the senators sat in their ranks in their white togas with the purple borders, and the whole thing looked, for a moment, exactly like what it was: a room full of old men who had spent their lives learning how to destroy one another with words.

Corvinus was on the front bench with his supporters. He was pale and composed, and he wore the white toga, and he had the look of a man who had not slept and had decided that it did not matter. Aemilia was not present; women were not admitted, and Cassia was admitted only because she was a witness, and she understood, walking up the aisle of that chamber, exactly how much of a favour it was and exactly how much it would cost.

Pactus sat on the other side with the prosecutor's bench, and beside him was a lawyer named Crispus with a thin face and a good voice, and behind him were the witnesses, and among them Cassia saw, for the first time in twenty-four years, a small, bent, frightened old woman in a good cloak, with a stick, and a granddaughter beside her.

Psyche looked at her. Cassia looked back. Neither of them moved.

The consul called the house to order. The clerk read the charge: that Lucius Sulpicius Corvinus, consular, augur, had procured the death of his father, Marcus Sulpicius Corvinus, by poison, in the villa at Tibur, in the year of the consulship of Domitian and Silanus; and that the said Lucius Sulpicius had done so in order to inherit his father's estate and to conceal his own misconduct with a slave of the household.

The chamber was very quiet when the clerk finished. Then the murmuring began, and the consul struck the floor with his staff and called for silence.

Crispus rose. He was a good lawyer, and he made a good speech: measured, reasonable, full of references to the dignity of the house and the sanctity of the family and the duty of the Senate to root out impiety wherever it was found. He spoke for half an hour. He did not mention the slave girl by name. He did not mention the child. He said that the prosecution would prove that the accused had been in the house on the night in question, that he had quarrelled with his father, that he had been heard to threaten him, and that after the death he had inherited the estate and had gone to Syria within the week.

All of it, Cassia thought, was true except the first and the last. Corvinus had not been in the house that night; he had been three days gone. But the rest — the quarrel, the threat, the inheritance, the flight — the rest was true, and it was the truth arranged in the shape of a lie, and she understood, watching the faces of the senators, that the shape was what mattered.

Then it was Corvinus's turn.

He rose. He did not use a written speech. He stood in the middle of the floor in his white toga and said that he had loved his father, that he had been in Syria when his father died, that he had received the news at Antioch and had wept, and that he had spent twenty-four years believing that his father had died of an apoplexy, as the physician had certified. He said that he did not know what had happened in that house on that night, and that he had come to the Senate to find out, and that he asked only for the truth.

It was a good speech. It was better than good; it was the speech of a man who had spent his life in that room, and it moved several of the older senators visibly, and Cassia saw old Paetus nodding.

Then Crispus rose again and said, "The house will hear the first witness. The midwife Psyche, of Tibur."

---

The old woman was helped to the stand by her granddaughter. She was so thin that her cloak hung on her like a shroud, and she held the stick with both hands, and when the consul told her to speak she spoke in a voice that carried, because she had spent forty years delivering babies in a village and a woman in that trade learns to be heard.

She told the truth. She told all of it: the cup, the night, the child, the cry, the mistress with the lamp, the dealer, the receipt, the twenty-four years. She did not spare herself. When Crispus asked her whether she had been paid to tell this story, she said, "I have been paid to keep silent for twenty-four years. Nobody has paid me to speak."

The chamber erupted. The consul struck the floor three times. Cassia sat very still and watched the faces of the senators, and saw what she had expected to see: not horror, not pity, but calculation. Every man in that room was working out what the story meant for him, and for his faction, and for the Asian command, and for the emperor.

When Psyche was done, Crispus rose and said, "The house has heard the midwife. The prosecution asks that the woman Antonia Cassia be called, the freedwoman of the household, the mother of the child."

The consul looked at Cassia. "The witness will come forward."

She came forward. The floor of the Curia was cold through her shoes. She stood where the old woman had stood and looked up at the consuls and then at the benches, and she thought: I have been in this city for twenty-four years and I have never once been allowed in this room, and now I am here, and I am here because of him.

"State your name and status," the clerk said.

"Antonia Cassia. Freedwoman. I keep a house on the Esquiline and I have done so for sixteen years."

"You are the mother of the child described by the witness?"

"I am."

"You were a slave in the household of Marcus Sulpicius Corvinus at Tibur in the year of the consulship of Domitian and Silanus?"

"I was."

"Tell the house what happened on the night in question."

She told it. She told it in the same voice she used for the household accounts: flat, precise, without ornament. The cup. The pain. The cry. The woman with the lamp. The morning. The cart. The house at Puteoli. She did not look at Corvinus while she spoke, and she did not look at Pactus, and she did not look at the old woman with the stick.

When she had finished, the chamber was silent.

Crispus rose. "The witness has told the house that the accused was not present at the birth."

"He was not."

"And that she was sold to a house of prostitution in Puteoli three days later."

"She was."

"And that she has spent twenty-four years building a fortune in this city, and that she now keeps a house frequented by senators, and that she has been for many years the intimate friend of the accused."

"The accused is a client of my house, as half the Senate is a client of my house," Cassia said. "If the Senate wishes to know which of its members have been in my dining room, I will be happy to provide a list. I do not think it will improve the dignity of this debate."

There was laughter, and the consul struck the floor, and Crispus's thin face tightened.

"You are a freedwoman," he said. "You are not a Roman matron. Your word in this house is worth what the word of a freedwoman is worth."

"Then it is worth exactly as much as the word of a freedman," Cassia said, "and there are three of them in this room who count the emperor's money."

This time the laughter was louder, and the consul had to stand to restore order, and Cassia saw Paetus laughing openly on the front bench.

Crispus waited for the noise to die. Then he said, "The house will note that the witness is hostile. The prosecution asks that the household of the woman Cassia be handed over for questioning, as the law allows, in order that the truth of her testimony may be tested."

The chamber went quiet.

"The law allows it," Crispus said, "because a freedwoman's household is not a Roman household. Her slaves are not the slaves of a citizen. They may be questioned under torture, and their evidence may be taken, and this house may judge for itself whether the testimony of the freedwoman is true."

Cassia stood very still. She had known it was coming. She had known it since the moment she had agreed to testify, and she had told herself that knowledge was the same as preparation.

The consul looked at her. He was an elderly man with a tired face, and he had been consul twice, and he had the look of a man who had seen a great deal and wanted to go home.

"The request is within the law," he said. "The household will be handed over to the prefect's office tonight, and the questioning will begin tomorrow. The trial will be held in thirty days." He struck the floor with his staff. "The house is dismissed."

---

The crowd outside the Curia was waiting for her.

They had heard, somehow, already — the city was like that, the city had a thousand mouths and every one of them was at the door of the Curia — and they pressed in on her as she came down the steps, and the noise was like a wave.

"Whore!" a man shouted. "Murderer's whore!"

"Where is the boy?" a woman screamed. "Where is the poor child?"

"Did you kill the old man too?"

Bathyllus put himself between her and the crowd, and two of Tertulla's men came out of nowhere and made a wall, and they got her across the Forum to the edge of the Sacra Via, and there the crowd thinned and the shouting faded and she was able to breathe.

Aemilia was waiting at the corner, in a plain litter, with the curtains drawn.

"Get in," she said.

Cassia got in. The litter moved off. Aemilia sat opposite her with her hands in her lap and her face white.

"Publius was in the gallery," she said. "He heard all of it. He came out before the crowd and he is at home now with his sister, and his sister is asking questions that I cannot answer." She looked at Cassia. "They will take your household tonight."

"I know."

"Children. Old women. The boy from the market."

"I know."

"And you will let them."

"I have no choice." Cassia's voice was steady. "If I resist, they will take them anyway and they will charge me with obstruction, and then they will have an excuse to put me in a cell, and then there will be no one to stand up in that chamber in thirty days and tell the truth."

Aemilia stared at her. "You are a cold woman."

"Yes," Cassia said. "It is the only way I have found to stay alive."

The litter stopped at the corner of the Clivus Suburanus. Cassia got out. Aemilia caught her sleeve.

"My husband did not do it," she said.

"I know."

"Then save him."

"I am trying."

"Try harder," Aemilia said, and the curtains closed, and the litter went up the hill.

---

They came at the ninth hour, as the lamps were being lit: a magistrate's clerk and eight men of the urban cohorts, with a warrant and a cart.

Cassia had prepared them. They stood in the atrium in their clean tunics with their bundles at their feet, all of them, from Thais to the smallest kitchen boy, and she had spoken to each of them alone that afternoon and told them what to say and what not to say and what to do if they were frightened.

The clerk read the warrant. Cassia listened without expression.

"The household is to be sold to the state for the duration of the questioning," the clerk said. "They will be housed in the prefect's cells and returned to you at the conclusion of the trial, less the costs of their maintenance."

"Less the costs," Cassia said. "And if they do not survive the questioning?"

The clerk looked at her. He was a thin man of fifty with the face of a clerk who has read a great many warrants.

"Then they will not be returned," he said.

Thais came forward first. She had put on her best cloak and her hair was done, and she looked, in the lamplight, like a woman going to a funeral that she had arranged herself.

"Domina," she said. "The keys."

Cassia took the keys from her belt and gave them to her. It was a small ceremony, and it meant nothing, and both of them knew it.

"Come back," Cassia said.

"I will come back," Thais said. "I am too old to die in a cell. It would be undignified."

Sabina came next. She was twelve years old and she was white to the lips, and she walked across the atrium with her bundle in her arms and her chin up, and when she reached Cassia she stopped.

"Domina," she said. "What do I say?"

"Nothing," Cassia said. "You say nothing at all. You are a child and you do not know anything and you have never heard of Tibur. If they ask you questions, you cry. If they hurt you, you cry louder. If they tell you that I have said something, you say that you do not believe them, because I would never say anything that would hurt you." She knelt down and took the child's face in her hands. "And Sabina. Whatever happens, you do not tell them about the black book. There is no black book. There never was."

"There is no black book," Sabina said.

"Good girl."

Chrysis came with Senno. The boy had been in the house for four months and had put on weight and colour and had begun to work in the garden, and he stood in the atrium with his bundle and looked at the men with the sticks and did not move.

"Domina," Chrysis said. "He is not a slave."

"He is a slave of this household. The warrant does not care that I bought him for you." Cassia looked at the boy. "Senno. You will go with your sister. You will do what she says. And when this is over, I will buy you again if I have to, and I will free you, and you will have a trade and a name."

The boy looked at her for a long moment. Then he said, "You said you did not own me."

"I did not own you," Cassia said. "The law did. It is a difference I have been explaining to people for twenty years, and I am beginning to think that no one is listening."

They went out one by one, into the street, into the cart. Celer went, and the kitchen boys, and the women from upstairs, and the old gardener, and the boy who fed the pigeons. The clerk counted them twice. The men of the cohort stood in the street with their sticks and their bored, patient faces.

Bathyllus stood in the doorway with his arms folded. He was a freedman; they could not take him.

The last to go was Thais. She stopped at the door and looked back at the house — the atrium, the impluvium, the three Graces in the alcove, the burnt patch on the wall where the fire had been — and then at Cassia.

"Twenty-two years," she said.

"Twenty-two years," Cassia said.

"Keep the lamps burning," Thais said. "I do not want to come back to a dark house."

She went out. The cart moved off. The door closed.

Tertulla came at midnight, alone, by the garden door, with a basket of food and a flask of wine and a face like a woman who had been crying and had decided not to.

"You did not eat," she said.

"I am not hungry."

"You never are. It is the most inconvenient thing about you." She set the basket down on the table in the small dining room. "I have been to the prefect's house. They are in the cells under the courtyard — the men on one side and the women on the other. The clerk says they will be questioned tomorrow morning, beginning with the oldest." She paused. "That is Thais."

Cassia sat down slowly on the couch.

"I know."

"They will question her first because she has been with you longest and knows the most, and because they think an old woman will break quickly." Tertulla sat down opposite her. "Do you want me to try to buy the clerk?"

"No. If we bribe him now, he will report it, and they will question her harder." Cassia's voice was flat. "There is a way these things are done. They will question her, and she will give them nothing, and then they will question the next one and the next, and at some point one of them will break, and that is what they are counting on. It is not a search for truth. It is a machine for producing a statement."

"And you are going to let it run."

"I am going to let it run, and I am going to be in that chamber in thirty days with the statements I have, and I am going to put the men who did this in front of the Senate and watch them explain themselves." Cassia looked up. "And I am going to hate every hour of it. Is that what you want to hear?"

Tertulla looked at her. Then she reached across and took her hand.

"No," she said. "I want to hear that you have a plan that does not require a twelve-year-old girl to be questioned by men with rods."

Cassia was silent.

"I have a plan," she said at last. "I do not know if it will work. It requires a man in the palace, and a woman I have never met, and a letter that could get us all killed." She looked at Tertulla. "And it requires me to ask you to do something that you will not want to do."

"Name it."

"I need you to go to the house of the empress's steward. Your cousin's wife is a client of his sister. I need you to get me an audience with the empress, before the trial, alone, without the knowledge of the consul or the prefect or Pactus."

Tertulla's face did not change. "The empress does not receive freedwomen."

"The empress receives whoever her steward lets through the door. And the empress is a woman who has spent thirty years married to a soldier and has no children of her own, and who has a reputation for caring about justice, and who is bored." Cassia leaned forward. "I am going to give her the letter from Primus, and I am going to tell her what her husband's money has been paying for, and I am going to ask her for one thing: that the trial be held in the Senate with the emperor's secretary present, and that the witnesses be heard without the rack."

"And if she refuses?"

"Then I will go to the trial with what I have, and I will probably lose, and my household will be sold, and the boy will be a scandal, and you will be the only person left in Rome who will speak to me." Cassia's mouth moved. "So I would prefer that she does not refuse."

Tertulla was silent for a long moment. The lamp burned. In the empty house the silence was enormous.

"I will go tomorrow," she said. "And Cassia—"

"Yes?"

"When this is over, if we are still alive, I am going to take you to Baiae for a month, and I am going to make you eat, and sleep, and sit in the sun, and you are going to be bored, and it will be the best month of your life."

Cassia laughed, and it hurt, and she said, "I do not know how to be bored."

"I know," Tertulla said. "That is why it will be the best month of your life."

Cassia stood in the empty atrium with Bathyllus and listened to the cart go down the hill, and the sound of it got smaller and smaller and then stopped, and the house was quiet in a way that it had never been quiet before.

"Domina," Bathyllus said.

"Leave me."

"Domina—"

"Leave me," Cassia said, and her voice broke on the second word, and Bathyllus went out into the yard and stood there in the dark with his back to the door, and Cassia sat down on the edge of the impluvium in the empty house and put her hands over her face.

---

## Chapter Eighteen

The prefect's house stood at the foot of the Viminal, behind a wall and a gate, and it had a courtyard with a fig tree in it and a portico and a set of cells under the north wing that the clerks called the cellar and everyone else called the question room.

Cassia came at the second hour and was not admitted. She came at the fourth hour and was not admitted. She came at the sixth hour and stood in the courtyard with the fig tree and the portico and the clerks walking past her with their tablets, and she was still not admitted, and at the eighth hour a clerk came out and told her that the magistrate would see her for five minutes.

The magistrate was a man named Valerius, fifty, thin, with the neat hands of a man who had spent his life writing down other people's words and the flat eyes of a man who had stopped reading them.

"Domina," he said. "You have been here since dawn."

"I have."

"It will do no good. The questioning is a legal procedure. It is not a spectacle and it is not a negotiation, and there is nothing you can say to me that will change what happens in that room."

"I know," Cassia said. "I am not here to change it. I am here to ask you for something."

"Ask."

"The child. Sabina. She is twelve years old and she knows nothing, and I will give you anything you want if you will question her last, or not at all." Cassia's voice was steady. "I will give you a statement. I will sign it. I will say whatever you want me to say, in my own hand, and you may put it before the Senate and I will not deny it, if you will leave the child alone."

Valerius looked at her for a long moment. He did not look surprised, or moved, or contemptuous. He looked like a man doing arithmetic.

"Your statement would be worthless," he said. "You are the principal witness for the defence. A confession from you would be read as fear for your household, and the court would discount it, and my superiors would want to know why I had accepted it." He turned a page. "The child will be questioned with the others. She will be questioned last, and she will be questioned gently. That is the most I can do, and I am doing it because I have a daughter of my own and because you have been standing in my courtyard for six hours and it is bad for the dignity of the office."

"Gently."

"Gently means the questions will be simple and the restraints will be light and the questioner will be a man who is used to children." Valerius closed his tablet. "It does not mean she will not be frightened. It does not mean she will not cry. And it does not mean she will not be hurt, if she does not answer." He stood. "Go home, domina. There is nothing for you here."

While she waited, the courtyard filled and emptied and filled again.

There were petitioners there for every kind of business: a man whose brother had been taken for debt, a woman whose son had been arrested in a brawl, a merchant with a claim against a ship owner, two slaves with a letter from their master, a young man who had been waiting so long that he had begun to write on the wall. They sat on the benches along the portico and watched the clerks and exchanged the small, bitter currency of people who have been waiting a long time: who to bribe, which door to use, which magistrate was honest and which was not, how many days it took for a letter to reach the prefect's desk.

Cassia sat among them and said nothing, and after a while the woman with the son came and sat beside her.

"You have been here since dawn," she said.

"I have."

"Who is yours?"

"My household," Cassia said. "Twelve of them. Eleven now."

The woman nodded. "They took my son in the spring. He was a porter at the docks and he was in the wrong place at the wrong time, and now he is in the cellar and I have been here every day for three months." She looked at the gate. "They will not tell me anything. They will not let me see him. I have brought him bread every day and they take it and I do not know whether he eats it."

"They take the bread and they eat it themselves," Cassia said.

"I know." The woman's hands were in her lap. "But I bring it anyway. It is the only thing I can do."

Cassia looked at her. She was perhaps fifty, with the face of a woman who had worked outdoors all her life, and she had the same flat, patient look that Cassia had seen in the faces of the people on the slave platform, and in the face of a boy in a market, and in her own face, once, in a mirror in a brothel at Puteoli.

"What is your son's name?" she asked.

"Lucius. He is twenty-six. He has a wife and a little girl."

"Then he will come home," Cassia said, "and you will bring him bread, and he will complain that it is stale, and you will tell him that it is the best bread in Rome, and he will laugh at you." She paused. "That is what happens. It is not always what happens. But it is what happens often enough that we have to keep bringing the bread."

The woman looked at her for a long moment. Then she said, "You are not from the docks."

"No."

"I thought not. You have the look of a woman who has money and is not using it." She stood. "I hope your household comes home, domina. I hope you do not have to bring bread to a gate for three months."

She went back to her bench. Cassia sat under the fig tree until the light began to go, and then she heard, from under the north wing, the sound of a door opening, and the flat, patient voice of a man reading out a list of names.

She did not go home. She sat in the courtyard under the fig tree all day, and the clerks brought her water and did not speak to her, and the sun moved across the portico, and in the afternoon she heard, faintly, from under the north wing, a sound that she would not allow herself to identify.

---

Sabina had been in the cell for a day and a half, and she had learned three things.

The first was that the cell was cold. It was June, and the city outside was hot enough to make the stones sweat, but the cell was under the ground and the walls were old and the cold came up out of the floor and into her bones.

The second was that the men were not all the same. There was the fat one who brought the food and did not look at her, and the thin one who took her to the latrine and told her to hurry, and the one with the good voice who came in the morning and asked her questions in a way that made her want to answer them.

The third was that she must not answer them.

The good-voiced man was called Rufus. He was fifty and he had a kind face and he had been doing this work for twenty years, and he began each session by asking her whether she had slept and whether she was hungry.

"Sabina," he said, on the second morning. "You have been very brave."

She said nothing.

"You have not said a single word to me. Do you know how unusual that is? Most girls your age cry and tell me everything in the first hour. Their mothers, their mistresses, what they had for dinner. Everything. But you have not said a word." He sat down on the stool opposite her. "I am going to ask you some questions, and you are going to answer them, and if you answer them truthfully, this will be over very quickly and you will go back to your cell and have something to eat. Do you understand?"

She nodded.

"Good. What is your name?"

"Sabina."

"Sabina. A pretty name. How old are you?"

"Twelve."

"Twelve. And how long have you been in the house of the woman Cassia?"

"Four years."

"Four years. That is a long time." Rufus turned a page. "And in those four years, did you ever see the woman Cassia writing in a book? A large book, with a dark cover?"

Sabina's heart began to beat very fast. She had been told, in the atrium, on the night they were taken: *There is no black book. There never was.*

"No," she said.

"No?"

"No."

"Are you sure? Because I have been told by another member of the household that there is a book, and that the woman Cassia writes in it almost every night, and that she keeps it in a room behind the tablinum." Rufus's voice was gentle. "It is not a crime to have a book, Sabina. There is nothing wrong with a book. I only want to know what is in it."

"I do not know anything about a book."

"You see, I do not believe you." Rufus sighed. "And when I do not believe a person, I have to take other measures. Do you know what those measures are?"

She shook her head.

"Then I will tell you. There is a rope, and there is a rack, and there is a man who turns the rack. It is not a pleasant thing. It is not a thing I enjoy. But it is the law, and the law is the law, and if you do not tell me the truth, I will have to use it." He looked at her. "Would you like that?"

"No."

"Then tell me about the book."

Sabina began to cry. She did not want to cry; she had promised herself that she would not cry; but the tears came anyway, and her nose ran, and she wiped it on her sleeve, and she thought about the warm kitchen and Celer's bread and Thais's hands and the way the mistress had knelt down in the atrium and taken her face in her hands and said, *You are a child and you do not know anything.*

"There is no book," she said. "There is no book. There is no book."

Rufus looked at her for a long time. Then he made a small mark on his tablet and said, "Very well. We will try again tomorrow."

They did not use the rope that day. They did not use it the next day either. They used it on the third day, when the other members of the household had given them nothing and the magistrate was beginning to ask why the questioning was taking so long, and Sabina heard the men in the corridor outside her cell arguing about it, and then the door opened, and Rufus came in with two other men and a look on his face that was almost apologetic.

"I am sorry," he said. "I truly am."

They did not rack her. They tied her hands to a ring in the wall and they raised her until her feet left the floor, and they asked her the same questions, and she could not answer them because she was crying, and after a while they let her down, and she lay on the floor of the cell and shook, and Rufus knelt beside her and said, "There. That was not so bad, was it? Now. The book."

"There is no book," Sabina said.

He looked at her. Then he stood up and said, "Take her back. We will try again tomorrow."

She was returned to the house on the ninth day, with the others, and she was thinner and quieter and she did not cry when she saw Cassia, and she did not let go of her hand for a long time.

---

Thais was questioned on the first day and on the second and on the third.

She told Cassia about it afterward, in pieces, over the following weeks, in the way that people tell the things that have been done to them: not all at once, but sideways, in the middle of other conversations, in the pauses before sleep.

They began with her name and her age and her years of service, and she answered those. They asked her about the household accounts, and she gave them the accounts — every figure, every merchant, every debt — because the accounts were honest and there was no reason to lie. They asked her about the black book, and she said there was no black book. They asked her again, and she said it again. They asked her a third time, and she said it a third time, and then they tied her hands and raised her, and the pain was worse than she had expected, and she screamed, and then they let her down and asked her again.

"There is no black book," she said.

They did it three times that day. On the second day they did it twice, and on the second night she lay in the cell and could not feel her fingers, and she thought about a kitchen in Puteoli twenty-two years before and a girl in a cart with no shoes who would not eat, and she thought: I have been in this house since before there was a house, and I am not going to be the one who ends it.

On the third day they brought in the child.

They did not question Sabina in front of Thais. That would have been too much even for the prefect's office. But they let Thais hear it: the door of the next cell, and the voices, and then the crying, and then the silence.

She told Cassia about that afterward, too. She said, "I heard her crying, and I knew that if they asked me again I would tell them everything. I would have told them about the book and the boy and the senator and the letter and the whole of it. I would have sold the house and everyone in it to make them stop." She looked at her hands. "But they did not ask me again. They had finished with me. And by the time they came back, I had remembered that the book was burned and that there was nothing to tell them, and that the child had gone quiet because she had stopped crying and not because she had broken."

Cassia sat with her in the kitchen that night and held her hands, and did not say anything, because there was nothing to say.

They questioned Celer on the second day. He was in the cellar for four hours and came out with his apron still on and his face the colour of suet, and he was questioned about the fire, and about the oil, and about who had been in the kitchen that night.

He told them the truth, which was that he had been asleep, and that the storeroom was locked at night, and that the key was on a nail by the kitchen door, and that anyone in the household could have taken it.

They asked him whether his mistress had ever spoken of burning the house.

Celer said no.

They asked him again, and then again, and on the fourth time he said, "My mistress would burn her own hand before she burned this house. She built it out of nothing. She has spent sixteen years in it. If you want to know who burned it, ask the men who paid for it."

They did not ask him again. They sent him back to the cell, and he sat on the floor with his back against the wall and shook for an hour, and then he was sick, and then he was all right.

They questioned Chrysis on the third day, and they asked her about the senator, and about the visits, and about the book, and she told them that she was a slave and did not know anything about the affairs of the household, in Greek, and then in Latin, and then in Greek again, with a blankness that Cassia would have admired if she had been there to hear it.

She was not there to hear it. She was in the courtyard under the fig tree, and she heard nothing, and that was the worst of it.

They questioned Senno on the fourth day. He was fifteen and he had been in the house for four months and he had learned to sweep the yard and carry water and read three letters of the alphabet, and when they asked him about the book he said, in his bad Latin, "I do not know. I am new. I sweep."

The questioner wrote it down. It was the most truthful thing anyone said in that cellar all week.

They came back in a cart in the evening, and Cassia was at the door with Bathyllus and Tertulla and a physician and a table laid in the atrium with bread and wine and hot water, because she had decided that they would come back and she had decided that they would be hungry.

They came back eleven. There had been twelve.

The old gardener, Marcus, had died on the fifth night. He had been sixty-eight and he had a bad chest and he had been in the cold cell with the damp and the fear, and he had died in his sleep, and the clerk had told Cassia at the gate in the tone of a man reporting a broken dish.

"He was old," the clerk said. "It happens."

Cassia looked at him for a long moment. Then she said, "His name was Marcus. He had been with me for eleven years. He had a daughter in Ostia and two grandchildren, and he sent them half his wages every month, and he was the only person in my house who could make the roses grow." She paused. "Write that in your book. Write it down, so that when the men who did this are standing in front of the Senate, someone can read it out."

The clerk looked at her and did not write anything.

Thais came out of the cart last. She walked slowly, and her hair had gone white at the temples in nine days, and she held herself carefully, the way a person holds herself when something inside is broken. Cassia went to her and put her arms around her, and Thais stood in the street with her face against Cassia's shoulder and did not speak for a long time.

"I told them nothing," she said at last. "I want you to know that. I told them nothing."

"I know."

"They asked about the book, and about the boy, and about the senator, and I told them that I was an old woman who cooked and kept the accounts and did not know anything about the affairs of my mistress." Thais pulled back. "They did not believe me, but they could not make me say anything else, because there was nothing else to say." She looked at Cassia. "They had Sabina on the rope."

Cassia closed her eyes.

"She did not break," Thais said. "She is twelve years old and she did not break. She said there was no book, over and over, and they let her down, and she said it again the next day." Her voice cracked. "She is the bravest person I have ever known, and she is twelve years old, and she will never be the same."

Cassia went into the house. Sabina was sitting on the bench in the kitchen with a cup of hot milk in her hands, and she looked up when Cassia came in, and her face was the face of a child who has been somewhere that no child should go.

"Domina," she said.

Cassia knelt down in front of her and took her hands, and they were cold, and there was a red mark around her wrist that would fade in a week and would not fade in a lifetime.

"I am sorry," Cassia said. "I am so sorry. I should have sent you away. I should have sent you all away the moment the accusation was laid, and I did not, because I was proud, and because I thought I could protect you, and I could not."

Sabina looked at her. Then she said, "Did they find the book?"

"No."

"Did I do it right?"

"Yes," Cassia said. "You did it exactly right."

"Then it was worth it," Sabina said, and drank her milk.

---

On the eighth day, a man came to the house on the Esquiline with a letter and a proposal.

He was a neat, quiet man in a good cloak, and he would not give his name, and he sat in the tablinum with his hands on his knees and said, "My master is prepared to end this."

"Which master?"

"The senator Pactus, and the men who stand behind him. They do not want a trial. A trial is expensive and public and it makes enemies. They are prepared to withdraw the accusation, release your household, and return the boy to his father, if you will do three things."

"Name them."

"You will surrender the receipt. You will surrender the two statements. And you will leave Rome with your household and not return for five years."

Cassia looked at him for a long moment. The lamp was lit, and the house was empty, and somewhere in the cellar of the prefect's house her people were being questioned, and this man was sitting in front of her with his hands on his knees, offering her a way out.

"And if I refuse?"

"Then the trial will proceed. The boy will be found and brought to Rome and his name will be read out in the Senate, and your household will be sold at the end of it, and you will be a freedwoman with a record and no house and no money." He said it without malice. "My master is not a cruel man. He is a practical one. He would rather have your silence than your blood."

Cassia sat very still. She thought about Thais with her white hair, and Sabina with the marks on her wrists, and Marcus in a grave outside the Esquiline gate, and the room at the top of the stairs with the window over the garden, and the boy in the yard with clay on his arms.

Then she thought about Sittius in his office, and Primus in the palace, and the letter in her sleeve, and the fact that a man who offers you a way out is a man who is frightened of what will happen if you stay.

"No," she said.

The man's face did not change. "You are refusing?"

"I am refusing. And you may tell your master something for me." Cassia leaned forward. "Tell him that I have been offered a way out by better men than him, and that I have refused those too, and that the reason is not courage. The reason is arithmetic. He cannot afford a trial and I cannot afford a silence, and one of us is going to be destroyed in the Senate in twenty-two days, and I have decided that it is not going to be me."

The man stood. "He said you would say that."

"Did he?"

"He said you were the most stubborn woman in Rome, and that you would rather be burned alive than give in, and that I was to tell you one more thing if you refused." He went to the door. "He said to tell you that the boy is not with his father. He said to tell you that the boy is with a man of his, in a place you will not find, and that if you appear in the Senate on the day of the trial, the boy will be sold to a farm in the south, and you will spend the rest of your life looking for him."

He went out. Cassia sat in the tablinum for a long time, and her hands were shaking, and she did not allow herself to cry.

The questioning produced one statement that mattered, and Cassia learned of it that night from Junia's son, the clerk in the prefect's office, who came to the garden door at midnight with his face grey and a copy of the summary in his sleeve.

"A kitchen boy," he said. "The youngest one. He was questioned on the fourth day and he said that you had quarrelled with a merchant in the spring, and that you had said, in his hearing, that the man would be sorry, and that you had spoken of fire."

Cassia read the summary. It was three lines long, and it was in the boy's own words, or in the words the clerk had put into his mouth.

"He did not say that," she said.

"No."

"He is nine years old. He does not know the word 'quarrel.'"

"No." The clerk looked at her. "But it is in the record, and the record is what the court will read. The prosecution will say that you burned your own house for the insurance, and that the fire killed a slave, and that you are a woman who destroys property for money. It will not be the main charge. It does not need to be. It only needs to be in the record, so that when the senators look at you they will remember that a boy died in your house and that you profited from it."

Cassia folded the paper and gave it back to him.

"Thank you," she said. "You have been a good friend to a woman you do not know."

"My mother says you are the only person who ever listened to her about my father," the clerk said. "She says you are going to destroy the man who destroyed us." He put the paper in his sleeve. "I hope she is right."

He went out. Cassia stood in the garden with the roses and the fountain and the dark house behind her, and then she went inside and found Tertulla in the tablinum with a lamp and a cup of wine and a letter in her hand.

"The empress will see you," Tertulla said. "Tomorrow, at the fourth hour, at the palace. Alone. You are to bring the letter and nothing else, and you are not to tell anyone where you are going."

Cassia looked at the letter. It was sealed with the empress's own seal, and the wax was the colour of old wine.

"Then we have one day," she said, "and one chance, and a boy in a cell and a dead gardener and a child with marks on her wrists."

"Yes."

"Then let us not waste it."

She wrote to Corvinus that night, and gave the letter to Bathyllus to carry to the Palatine before dawn.

*They have taken the household and the child has been on the rope and the old gardener is dead. I have not broken and neither have they, but I do not know how much longer I can ask them to hold. The boy is not with his father. Pactus has him, or says he has him, which in this city is the same thing.*

*I am going to the palace tomorrow to see the empress. If it works, the trial will be held in the Senate with the emperor's secretary present, and the witnesses will be heard without the rack, and Primus will be finished. If it does not work, I will be arrested at the palace gate and you will read about it in the Acta, and you will know that I tried.*

*Do not come to the house. Do not send anyone. Do not do anything brave or stupid. If I am taken, go to Tertulla and do what she tells you, because she is cleverer than both of us and she has no pride left to get in the way.*

*And Lucius. If this goes badly, and if the boy is found, do not let him be alone. Whatever it costs you. Whatever it does to your name. Sit in a room with him and tell him the truth and let him hate you for it if he wants to. A person who has been lied to all his life does not need a father. He needs a witness.*

*C.*

She sealed it and gave it to Bathyllus, and then she went to bed and slept, and in the morning she put on the plain stola and the dark veil and the ring with her seal and the knot of rosemary and lavender that Sabina had made her, and she went to the palace.

She went to bed, and slept, for the first time in nine nights, and dreamed of nothing at all.

---

## Chapter Nineteen

The palace on the Palatine was not one building. It was a hill covered with buildings, added to by every emperor for a hundred years, and by the reign of Trajan it had become a city inside the city: halls and courtyards and gardens and bathhouses and barracks and offices, connected by corridors and stairways and passages so complicated that men who had lived there all their lives still got lost in it.

Cassia had never been inside it. She had stood outside the gates on festival days, and she had seen the crowds go in and out, and she had known a dozen men who came and went through those doors every week, and she had never once been inside, because there was no reason for a woman like her to be there, and because a woman like her did not go where she was not wanted.

She went in through the gate on the Via Sacra with the empress's letter in her hand and a steward in front of her and two guards behind, and she walked through corridors of marble and porphyry and gilt with her heart going like a drum and her face as still as a painted wall.

The palace smelled of incense and wax and hot stone. There were slaves everywhere, silent, moving in and out of doorways with trays and papers and messages. There were soldiers of the guard at every turning, standing in their scale armour with their hands on their spears. There were clerks in little rooms off the corridors, bent over desks, and the sound of their pens was like the sound of insects. And there were works of art, more of them than Cassia had ever seen in one place: statues and paintings and mosaics and tables of coloured marble, and ceilings of gilded bronze, and a fountain in a courtyard that was made of a single block of porphyry and had been brought from Egypt at a cost that could not be counted.

She thought, walking through it: I have spent twenty-four years building a house that would make men lower their voices, and this is what the men who own the world live in. And then she thought: and they are just as frightened as I am. Every one of them. You can see it in their faces.

The steward stopped at a door at the end of a long corridor and said, "The empress will see you in the garden room. You will not speak until you are spoken to. You will not sit until you are told to sit. You will not touch anything. You will keep your eyes down." He looked at her. "And domina. She is not like the other one. She does not want flattery and she does not want cleverness. She wants the truth, and she has very little patience with people who take a long time to get to it."

"Then we will get on very well," Cassia said.

She waited for an hour in a room off the corridor with a bench, a fountain, and a view of a courtyard where the guard was drilling.

There was one other person waiting: a Greek of about seventy, in a clean but threadbare cloak, with a scroll case under his arm and the patient, faintly amused expression of a man who had been waiting in palaces for fifty years.

"You are here to see the empress," he said.

"I am."

"Then you will wait an hour. She is reading in the garden and she does not like to be interrupted before the fourth hour." He shifted on the bench. "I am here to see the emperor's secretary. I have been here every day for a month. I am a teacher of philosophy, and I have written a book about the education of princes, and I have been told by three different freedmen that it is excellent and by none of them that it will be read."

"I am sorry," Cassia said.

"Do not be. It is an old story." The philosopher looked at her. "You are the woman with the house on the Esquiline."

"I am."

"I have heard of you. I have heard that you keep a good table and a dangerous book and that half the Senate owes you money." He smiled. "I have also heard that you are being destroyed by a delator, and that the whole city is watching to see whether you will survive it. It is the most interesting thing in Rome this month. More interesting than the games."

"And what do you think will happen?"

"I think you will survive," the philosopher said. "You have the look of a survivor. I have seen it in soldiers and in slaves and in one or two philosophers. It is not courage. It is a kind of stubbornness that has been beaten into a person until it becomes a virtue." He stood, because a steward had appeared at the end of the corridor. "When this is over, domina, and you are rich and safe and bored, send for me. I will come and dine at your table and tell you about the education of princes, and you will tell me about the education of a woman in Puteoli, and we will both learn something."

"What is your name?"

"Epictetus," he said, and went out.

The garden room was small, by the standards of the palace, and it was open on one side to a courtyard with a fountain and a fig tree and a great many roses. The empress Pompeia Plotina was sitting in a chair by the open side with a book on her knee and a slave behind her with a fan. She was perhaps sixty, with grey hair drawn back plainly, and a long, grave, intelligent face, and she was dressed in a plain stola of undyed wool with no jewellery at all.

She looked up when Cassia came in and looked at her for a long moment without saying anything.

"Domina," Cassia said, and bowed her head.

"Sit down," the empress said. "You have been standing in corridors for an hour and you are not as young as you were."

Cassia sat. The empress closed her book and put it on the table beside her.

"You have brought the letter," she said.

"I have."

"Give it to me."

Cassia took the letter from her sleeve and handed it over. The empress unfolded it and read it, slowly, once, and then again. Her face did not change. When she had finished she folded it and put it on the table and sat for a moment with her hands in her lap.

"This is my husband's money," she said. "This is the grain of Egypt and the taxes of Gaul and the rents of the imperial estates. It has been stolen by a clerk and shared with a senator and hidden in a bank on the Via Lata, and the clerk is now the man who counts the money for the man who owns the world." She looked up. "Do you know what happens to a woman like me who brings this to the emperor?"

"I do not."

"He will be angry. He will be angry with the clerk, and he will be angry with the senator, and he will be angry with me for telling him, because I will have made him look a fool, and a man who has been made to look a fool does not thank the person who showed him the mirror." Plotina's mouth moved. "I have been married to him for thirty years. I know exactly how angry he will be, and I know exactly how long it will last, and I know that it will be worth it."

"Then you will help me."

"I will help you," the empress said, "but first I will tell you something, because you are going to hear it from someone else if I do not, and because I would rather you heard it from me." She set the letter down. "The emperor is not well. He has not been well since the Dacian war. His feet swell and his breath is short and he cannot sleep, and the physicians say it is the dropsy and the physicians say a great many other things that they do not say in front of him. He is fifty-nine years old. He has been emperor for fifteen years, and he has spent them in the saddle, and there is not much of him left."

Cassia said nothing.

"When he dies," Plotina said, "there will be a question about who follows him. There is a man in the East, a general, who has been winning battles and making friends, and there is a boy in the palace who is clever and charming and who has been waiting for twenty years for someone to die, and there is the Senate, which would like to choose one of its own and knows that it will not be allowed to." She looked at Cassia. "In the year after the emperor dies, every man who has a secret will be in danger. Every clerk, every senator, every freedman, every woman with a house and a book. The men who survive will be the ones who have made themselves useful to the right people and dangerous to the wrong ones, and who have not made a spectacle of themselves."

"Then you are telling me to be quiet."

"I am telling you to be careful," the empress said. "A trial in the Senate with the whole city watching is a very loud thing. It will make you famous, and fame in this city is a kind of debt, and the interest is collected by whoever is in power when it comes due." She leaned forward. "I can protect you for a year. I cannot protect you for ten. So take the trial, and win it, and then go home and be dull and rich and keep your household safe, and let the men in the palace destroy each other in their own time."

Cassia looked at her. In the courtyard the fountain played, and the roses moved, and somewhere beyond the wall a soldier was shouting a command.

"You are telling me to survive," she said.

"Yes."

"Everyone tells me to survive. My steward tells me to survive. My friend tells me to survive. The old woman who sold my son tells me that she did it so that I would survive." Cassia shook her head. "I have survived for twenty-four years. I have survived Puteoli and the fire and the questioning and the whole of it, and I am beginning to think that survival is a very small ambition."

The empress looked at her for a long moment.

"What would you have instead?"

"I would have justice," Cassia said. "Not for me. For the gardener who died in a cell because a banker wanted a house. For the child who was put on a rope because a delator wanted a fee. For the boy on the Via Ostiensis who has been hidden and hunted and used as a threat for six months, and who does not even know why." She stopped. "I have spent my whole life being sensible. You are asking me to be sensible again, and I will do it, because you are right and because I have a household and a house and a business and I cannot afford to be a hero. But I want you to know what you are asking."

The empress was silent. Then she said, "I know exactly what I am asking. I have been asking it of myself for thirty years." She picked up her book again. "You will have your arsonists. You will have your trial. And when it is over, you will send me a list of the men who burned your house and killed your boy, and I will make sure that they are not forgotten in whatever quiet arrangement is made about the rest." She looked up. "That is the most I can do. It is more than most women in this palace get."

"Then you will help me."
