"I will help you," the empress said, "because it is my husband's money and because I do not like thieves, and because a man who has been robbing the emperor for twenty years is a danger to the empire, and because I am bored." She picked up the letter again. "But I will not do it your way. You want a trial in the Senate, with the whole city watching and the whole story told. That is a woman's idea of justice. It is not how these things are done."

"Then how are they done?"

"Quietly." Plotina set the letter down. "The clerk will be removed from his office. He will be given a house in the country and a pension and a very clear understanding that if he ever returns to Rome he will be prosecuted for fraud. The senator Pactus will be told that his patron has fallen and that he would be wise to withdraw his accusation and retire to his estates. The banker Sittius will be investigated by the prefect of the grain supply and will pay a fine that will ruin him. And your senator will be acquitted, and his household will be released, and the boy will be left alone, and no one will ever know why."

"And the truth?"

"The truth will be known to the people who need to know it." The empress looked at her. "You are a woman who has spent her life in a house where men tell you things they would not tell their wives. You know that the truth is not a thing that is announced. It is a thing that is used."

Cassia sat very still. The fountain played in the courtyard. The roses moved in the warm wind.

"And if I refuse?" she said. "If I insist on the trial?"

"Then you will have your trial," Plotina said, "and you will probably win, because you have a letter and a receipt and a midwife and a dead gardener, and because the Senate loves nothing more than the destruction of a man who has risen too fast. And when you have won, the clerk will be executed, and the senator Pactus will be exiled, and the banker will hang himself in his bath, and the emperor will be humiliated in front of the whole city, and he will remember that it was a freedwoman with a grudge who did it to him." She paused. "And then, in a year or two, when the scandal has been forgotten, he will find a reason to destroy you. He will not do it himself. He will find a magistrate who owes him a favour, and there will be a charge, and your house will be seized, and your household will be sold, and the boy on the Via Ostiensis will be a slave before he is thirty. That is what happens to people who win too publicly in Rome."

Cassia looked at the empress. She had spent her whole life believing that the truth was a weapon, and here was a woman who had spent her whole life in the palace telling her that the truth was a tool, and that the difference between the two was the difference between a soldier and a surgeon.

"What do you want me to do?" she said.

"I want you to give the trial to the Senate," Plotina said, "with the emperor's secretary in the chamber and the emperor's name kept out of it. I want the clerk Primus to be tried and condemned for fraud, and I want it done in public, because a public condemnation is a warning to every other clerk in the palace. And I want the senator Pactus to be ruined by his own accusation, in front of the men he has spent his life trying to impress." She leaned forward. "I do not want a quiet little arrangement in a back room. I want a spectacle. But I want it to be a spectacle about the theft of the emperor's money, and not about the private life of a senator and a freedwoman and a boy on the Via Ostiensis."

Cassia understood. She understood entirely, and she felt the shape of the bargain close around her like a hand.

"You will give me the trial," she said, "and in exchange I will keep the boy out of it."

"Yes."

"And the midwife?"

"The midwife will testify to the child, because the child is the foundation of the accusation, and without the child there is no case. But she will testify in a closed session, and her evidence will be sealed, and the receipt will be read to the Senate and then sealed and put in the archive, and the name of the potter and the name of the boy will not be spoken aloud." Plotina picked up her book. "That is the bargain. It is not perfect. It is the best that can be done in this city by a woman in your position, and I am offering it to you because I have been in this palace for thirty years and I have never once been able to help a woman like you, and because I am tired of watching clever women destroyed by stupid men."

Cassia sat for a moment. Then she said, "I have one condition."

"Name it."

"The men who burned my house and killed a child will not be part of the quiet arrangement. The arsonist has given a statement, and the steward who paid him has been named. I want them tried and punished, in public, before the trial of the senator. I want the city to know that a boy died in my house and that the men who did it were not important enough to be protected."

The empress looked at her for a long moment. Then she said, "That is a very Roman condition."

"I am a very Roman woman."

"Yes," Plotina said. "I can see that." She stood. "You will have your arsonists. And you will have your trial. And now you will go home, domina, and you will tell no one where you have been, and you will wait for a message from my steward. It will come within three days."

Cassia stood. At the door she turned and said, "Domina. May I ask you something?"

"You may."

"Why are you doing this? Not the fraud. The other thing. The boy. You have no reason to care about a potter on the Via Ostiensis."

The empress looked at her. In the light from the courtyard, with the roses behind her and the book in her hand, she looked like a woman who had been beautiful once and had put it away, and who had spent thirty years learning to be useful instead.

"I had a child," she said. "A girl. She lived three days. It was a long time ago, and the emperor does not speak of it, and neither do I." She opened her book. "I have spent my life in this palace being sensible. It is a great comfort to be unreasonable once."

---

She was halfway down the corridor when a man stepped out of a doorway in front of her.

He was sixty, small, neat, with the hands of a clerk and the face of a man who had not been refused anything in twenty years. He wore a plain tunic of excellent wool and no jewellery, and he stood in the middle of the corridor with his hands clasped behind his back and said, "Domina."

Cassia stopped. The steward behind her had stopped too, and had gone very still.

"You are Primus," she said.

"I am." He inclined his head. "I have been watching your career with great interest."

"And I have been watching yours."

"I doubt that. No one watches a clerk." He smiled. "I have a proposal for you."

"I have had a great many proposals this month."

"This one is simple. You have a letter. I have a great deal of money. You give me the letter, and I give you three million sesterces and a house in Campania and a guarantee that no one in your household will ever be questioned again." He said it in the tone of a man offering a seat on a bench. "It is a good offer. It is more money than you will make in ten years of senators."

"It is a good offer," Cassia said. "But I have just spent an hour with the empress, and she has offered me something better."

The clerk's face did not change, but something behind it did — a small movement, like a fish under ice.

"The empress," he said, "is a woman with a book and no power."

"The empress," Cassia said, "is the wife of the man whose money you have been stealing for twenty years, and she is very bored, and she has just decided to be unreasonable." She stepped around him. "You should have taken the house in the country when you had the chance, Primus. It is a very pleasant house. I have seen it from the road."

She walked on down the corridor. Behind her, the clerk stood without moving, and the steward followed her, and the guards followed the steward, and the whole palace went on around them with its incense and its marble and its clerks and its silence.

At the gate on the Via Sacra, Bathyllus was waiting with his face grey.

"Domina," he said. "The household has been released. All of them. They came back an hour ago."

Cassia stopped. "All of them?"

"All of them. The prefect's clerk came himself with a paper and a cart, and he would not say why, and he would not look at anyone." Bathyllus took her arm. "And domina. There is a message from the Palatine. The old woman is dying. The senator says to come at once."

Cassia stood in the street with the sun on her face and the letter in her sleeve and the smell of the palace still in her clothes.

"Take me home first," she said. "I want to see them."

"Domina—"

"Take me home, Bathyllus. I have been in the palace for an hour and I have not seen my household in nine days and I am going to see them before I go anywhere else."

On the way home Bathyllus told her what had happened at the prefect's gate.

"The clerk came out himself," he said. "He had a paper in his hand and he read it twice, and then he told the guards to open the cells, and they brought them out one by one. He would not say who had sent the order. He would not say anything at all. He looked like a man who had been told that the ground under his feet was not as solid as he had thought."

"Was there a man with him? A small man, neat, in a good cloak?"

"There was a man in the courtyard who did not come forward. He stood by the fig tree with his hands behind his back and watched them come out, and when they were all out he turned and walked away." Bathyllus paused. "I have seen him before, domina. He was at the Curia on the day of the hearing. He was standing behind Pactus."

Cassia looked out of the litter at the streets going past.

"He was counting them," she said. "He was making sure that all twelve came out. He wanted to know that the order had been obeyed."

"Eleven," Bathyllus said. "Eleven came out."

"Yes," Cassia said. "Eleven."

He took her home. They were all in the atrium, all eleven of them, with Thais in the middle and Sabina holding her hand, and when Cassia came through the door they did not cheer and they did not weep; they stood in a line and looked at her, and Thais said, "There you are," and Cassia said, "Here I am," and then she went around the line and put her hand on each of their heads, one by one, like a woman counting her stock.

Then she went to the Palatine.

---

The house on the Palatine was full of lamps and quiet people.

They had put the sedan chair down in the street and Cassia had walked up the hill on foot, because the litter could not get through the crowd of servants and clients and neighbours who had gathered outside the door in the way that Romans gather outside a house where someone is dying, and by the time she reached the atrium she had been recognized three times and had said nothing to anyone.

Aemilia met her at the foot of the stairs. She was in a plain dress with her hair down and no jewellery, and she looked as though she had been awake for two days.

"She has had a turn," she said. "The physician says it is the end. He says she may last the night or she may last an hour, and he says there is nothing to be done but keep her warm and give her the poppy when the pain is bad." She looked at Cassia. "She has been asking for you since noon. She will not have anyone else in the room. Not me, not the children, not her son. She says she has something to say to you and that she will not say it to anyone else."

"Where is Corvinus?"

"In the garden. He has been walking up and down for an hour." Aemilia's mouth moved. "He is afraid. He has spent his whole life being afraid of that woman and now he is afraid of losing her, and he does not know what to do with either."

Cassia looked up the stairs. The corridor was lit with lamps, and the door at the end of it was open, and she could hear, faintly, the sound of a woman breathing.

"Does she know about the empress?" Cassia said.

"No one knows about the empress. You told me not to tell anyone, and I have not told anyone, not even my husband." Aemilia paused. "Did it work?"

"It worked."

Aemilia closed her eyes for a moment. When she opened them they were wet.

"Then go up," she said. "She has been waiting for you, and she is dying, and whatever there is between you, she is dying, and I have discovered in the last two days that I do not want to hate a dying woman. It is a waste of the little time I have left with her."

Cassia went up the stairs.

At the door of the room she stopped. The brazier was lit, and the lamps were low, and the old woman was propped up on her pillows with her eyes closed, and her face was the colour of the wax on the masks in the atrium below. The physician sat on a stool in the corner with his bag at his feet, and the slave who had been with her for thirty years sat by the bed with a cloth in her hand.

Cassia went in and stood at the foot of the bed.

Sulpicia Varia opened her eyes.

"You came," she said.

"I came."

"Good." The old woman's hand moved on the coverlet, a small, impatient movement. "Send them out. All of them. I have something to tell you, and I do not want it heard by a physician or a slave or my daughter-in-law, because they will all tell someone, and the someone will tell the city, and the city will tell the Senate, and by the Ides the whole of Rome will know what I did."

Cassia looked at the physician. He rose and went out without a word, and the slave followed, and the door closed, and the room was quiet except for the brazier and the old woman's breathing.

"Sit down," Sulpicia Varia said. "This will take a while."

---

## Chapter Twenty

The old woman talked for two hours.

She talked slowly, in a voice that was barely above a whisper, and Cassia sat on the stool by the bed with her hands in her lap and listened, and the brazier ticked, and the lamps burned down, and outside the window the night went on over the Palatine.

She had begun to plan it in the spring. Not the poison — the poison had been in her mind for years, in the way that a woman who has been humiliated for twenty-six years keeps a thing in her mind and takes it out sometimes and looks at it. But the plan, the actual plan, had begun in the spring, when her husband had come back from Rome in a rage about a debt and had struck her across the face in front of a slave.

"He had done it before," she said. "He had done it a hundred times. But that time there was a slave in the room, and the slave looked at me, and I saw myself in her face. I saw what I looked like. And I thought: I am fifty-three years old and I have been married to this man for twenty-six years and he will do this to me until one of us dies, and it is not going to be him."

She had a physician who was in debt. She had a steward who was in debt. She had a cook who could be frightened. She had a knowledge of herbs that she had learned from her own mother, who had learned it from hers, and she had a garden at Tibur with a bed of poppies and a bed of hellebore and a corner where the monkshood grew.

She had not used the monkshood. She had used something slower and kinder and easier to explain: a little in his wine at dinner, a little more in the posset he took before bed, a third dose in the morning when he complained of a headache. His heart was bad. The physician had told him two years before that he must not excite himself, and he had ignored it, as he ignored everything that did not suit him.

"I did not know whether it would kill him," she said. "I knew that it might. I knew that if it did not, he would beat me within an inch of my life and I would have to try again. And I lay in my bed that night and listened to him breathing in the next room and I thought: please. Please let it be tonight." She closed her eyes. "And it was."

Cassia sat very still.

"Your mother," the old woman said. "Daphne. I have not thought about her in years, and now I cannot stop."

Cassia went very still.

"She was a good servant and a bad laundress and she sang when she worked," Sulpicia Varia said. "She had a temper and a laugh and she could not sew. She died in the winter of the year I was thirty-five, of a fever, in the room over the laundry, and I paid for the funeral and I kept you in the household because she had asked me to and because I do not like to be in debt to a dead woman." Her eyes were closed. "She asked me to look after you. She said, 'Domina, she is clever, she will be worth something to you.' I said I would. And I did, in my way. I taught you to read, I put you in my own rooms, I let you sit at the foot of my chair and listen to the poets." She paused. "And then I sold you to a brothel and told you your child was dead. I have thought about that too. I have spent twenty-four years thinking about the difference between what I promised a dying woman and what I did."

Cassia said nothing. She was thinking about lavender and a song about a boat and hands that were red and cracked and always gentle, and about the fact that the old woman in the bed had known her mother better than she did.

"Did she suffer?" she said.

"No. The fever took her in four days. She was not in pain at the end; she was delirious, and she talked about a boat." The old woman's mouth moved. "She called for you. She called for you for two days, and I sat with her, and I told her that you were asleep, and I sent for the physician twice, and there was nothing to be done."

The brazier ticked. The lamp burned. Cassia sat on the stool with her hands in her lap and did not move for a long time.

"Thank you," she said at last. "For sitting with her."

"It was the least I could do."

"Yes," Cassia said. "It was."

"And the cup you gave me?"

"Poppy and honey and a little wine, and three drops of bitter almond for the taste, the way a cook puts it in a cake. Nothing more than that. I wanted you asleep, and I wanted you quiet, and I wanted the birth to happen without a great deal of noise." The old woman's mouth moved. "It was not cruelty. It was the opposite. If you had been awake, you would have fought, and they would have held you down, and you would have remembered it for the rest of your life. I gave you the only mercy I had left to give."

"And then you took my son."

"And then I took your son." Sulpicia Varia opened her eyes. "I had planned that too. Not the child — the child was a complication. But when the midwife told me that you were in labour, I understood that the child was the answer to everything. A living child would have been a scandal and a weapon. A dead child was a grief. A child given to a potter in Rome was a debt that could be paid." She looked at Cassia. "I chose the potter myself. I sent the steward to the market to find a man with a good trade and no children and a wife who could not conceive. It took him three weeks. He found Gaius Vibidius, who was known in the trade as an honest man, and who had been to the temple of Juno Lucina six times in five years and had gone home every time with nothing."

"You chose him," Cassia said, "because he was kind."

"I chose him because he was kind," the old woman said. "I want you to know that. Whatever else I have done, I did not give your son to a cruel man. I chose the kindest man I could find, and I paid the dealer to forget the address, and I have spent twenty-four years paying him to keep his mouth shut, and I have prayed every day that the boy would have a good life." She stopped. "That is not an excuse. I know it is not an excuse. It is the truth."

Cassia looked at her. In the low light the old woman's face was the colour of ash, and her hands were like birds on the coverlet, and there was nothing left of the woman who had terrified a household for fifty years except the voice and the eyes.

"Why did you not tell me?" she said. "After he was dead. After you had done it. Why did you let me go to Puteoli believing that my son was dead, when you could have told me the truth and given me money and sent me to Rome?"

"Because I was afraid," Sulpicia Varia said. "Not of you. Of the world. If you had known, you would have come back, and you would have wanted the boy, and the boy would have been a scandal, and the scandal would have destroyed my son. I had just killed my husband to save this family. I was not going to let a slave girl destroy it the week after." She paused. "And because I did not think you would survive. I thought you would be dead within the year. I thought Puteoli would kill you, or a client would, or the pox, and that the boy would be safe and the secret would die with you." Her eyes moved over Cassia's face. "You did not die. You bought the house and you bought the business and you came back to Rome and you built a fortune, and I have watched you for sixteen years, and every year I have thought: she is going to find out. She is going to come for me. And you never did, and I began to think that the gods had decided to be merciful, and then my son stood for the Asian command, and the clerk in the palace decided that we were a liability, and it all came apart."

"The clerk," Cassia said. "Primus. He knew about the child?"

"He knew about the death. He did not know about the child. He knew that my husband had died in suspicious circumstances and that the physician had signed a certificate and that the steward had been paid. He has been holding it over me for twenty-four years, the way he holds everything over everyone. When he decided to destroy the family, he gave the information to Pactus, and Pactus gave it to a delator, and the delator found the midwife." She closed her eyes. "It was not the boy they wanted. It was the death. The boy was a gift they found on the way."

Cassia sat with that for a moment.

"Tell me what he looks like," the old woman said.

Cassia looked at her.

"Please," Sulpicia Varia said. "I have never seen him. I have thought about him for twenty-four years and I have never seen him. Tell me what he looks like."

Cassia was silent for a moment. Then she said, "He is tall. Taller than his father, I think, or he will be. He has the family nose and the family jaw, and he has his hair, which is the colour of dark honey, and he has a laugh that carries." She stopped. "He works with clay. His arms are grey with it to the elbow. He has a father who calls him in to supper and cuffs him on the head, and a shop on the Via Ostiensis with a kiln in the yard, and when I saw him he was carrying a board of cups on his shoulder and laughing at something in the doorway."

The old woman's eyes were closed. Two tears ran down her face and into the pillow.

"He is happy," she said.

"He is happy."

"Then it was worth it."

"No," Cassia said. "It was not worth it. But he is happy, and that is a thing that happened, and I am not going to pretend it did not." She stood up. "You do not get to decide that it was worth it. You do not get to make this a story in which you were right. You made a choice. It cost me twenty-four years. And it gave him a life. Both of those things are true, and you will have to die with both of them, and so will I."

"What do you want from me?" she said.

Sulpicia Varia opened her eyes. They were wet.

"I want you to tell him," she said. "The boy. When this is over, when he knows who you are, and who his father is, and what was done — I want you to tell him that his grandmother chose a kind man to raise him. I want you to tell him that she was a monster and that she was sorry, and that she thought about him every day for twenty-four years, and that she hoped he was happy." Her voice cracked. "I know you will not forgive me. I am not asking for that. I am asking you to carry one true thing about me to a boy I will never meet."

Cassia looked at her for a long time. The brazier ticked. The lamp guttered.

"I will tell him," she said. "I will tell him all of it. Not the version that makes you a saint. The version that makes you a woman who did something terrible and knew it, and who chose the kindest man she could find, and who paid for it for twenty-four years." She stood. "And I will tell him that you asked me to tell him you were sorry. Because you did ask. And because it is true."

The old woman closed her eyes. Her hand moved on the coverlet, and Cassia, after a moment, took it.

"Thank you," Sulpicia Varia said.

"Do not thank me. I am not doing it for you."

"I know," the old woman said. "But I am thanking you anyway. It is the last thing I have left to do."

---

She died at dawn, with the whole family in the room.

They had come in one by one in the last hour: Aemilia first, because she was the mistress of the house now and because she had decided that she would not be kept out; then Publius, who stood at the foot of the bed with his hands behind his back and his face white; then young Sulpicia, who had come from her husband's house in the middle of the night and who sat on the floor by the bed and held her grandmother's other hand; then Corvinus, who came in last and stood by the door and did not move.

The old woman did not speak again. She lay with her eyes closed and her breathing getting smaller and smaller, and the physician came and felt her pulse and shook his head and went out, and the slave woman lit the last lamp, and the sky outside the window went from grey to rose.

At the end she opened her eyes and looked around the room, at all of them, and her mouth moved, and Cassia, who was closest, leaned down and heard her say, "The masks. Polish the masks."

Then she died.

There was a silence in the room. Then Aemilia began to cry, quietly, with her hand over her mouth, and Publius went out, and young Sulpicia put her head down on the bed and sobbed, and Corvinus stood by the door with his face in his hands.

Cassia stood up. She looked at the dead woman for a moment, and then she went to the window and opened it, and the morning came in, and the city was waking below the hill, and somewhere a cart went past on the stones.

"The masks," she said. "Someone had better polish the masks."

---

The funeral was three days later, and it was the largest funeral on the Palatine that year.

The old woman had left instructions, and the instructions were precise. The procession was to go from the house to the tomb on the Via Appia, and the ancestors were to be represented by actors wearing the wax masks, and the actors were to be hired from the best troupe in the city, and the eulogy was to be delivered by her son and not by a hired orator, and there was to be no display of grief by the hired mourners beyond what was customary, because she had always thought the professional wailing was vulgar.

The whole of the Palatine came. The whole of the Senate came. The consul came, and the prefect, and a dozen men who had been her husband's friends and had not spoken to her in twenty years, and a hundred clients who owed their fortunes to the family, and a crowd of the poor who came for the bread that was distributed in her name.

Cassia stood at the back of the procession with Tertulla and watched the masks go by: the Sulpicii, four hundred years of them, carried on the shoulders of actors who had been paid to look like them, and behind them the bier with the old woman's body, and behind that the family in black.

Corvinus walked behind the bier with his son on one side and his daughter on the other, and his face was the face of a man who had been asked to carry something too heavy for him, and who was carrying it anyway.

"He is going to be all right," Tertulla said.

"How do you know?"

"Because he is still walking."

At the tomb, the eulogy was spoken. Corvinus spoke for a quarter of an hour, and it was a good speech, and it was, Cassia thought, entirely honest: he spoke about his mother's courage and her hardness and her devotion to the family, and he did not pretend that she had been kind, and he did not pretend that she had been easy, and at the end he said, "She did what she thought was necessary, and she paid for it, and she did not ask for anyone's forgiveness. I ask it for her, because she cannot."

There was a murmur of approval. The old men nodded. The women wept. The body was put in the tomb with the ashes of her husband and the ashes of four generations of Sulpicii, and the stone was sealed, and the family went home.

Pactus was there.

He stood at the edge of the crowd in a black cloak with his clients behind him, and he listened to the eulogy with his head bowed and his hands clasped, and when it was over he came forward and put a handful of earth on the tomb and said something to Corvinus that Cassia could not hear.

"What did he say?" she asked Tertulla.

"I could not hear it either. But look at his face."

Corvinus's face was white. He said something back, short and hard, and Pactus inclined his head and moved away, and the crowd closed around him.

"He is not here for the funeral," Tertulla said. "He is here to be seen at the funeral. There is a difference. A man who is seen at the funeral of his enemy's mother is a man who is saying to the city: I am not afraid of him, and I am not his enemy, and when I destroy him it will be the law that does it and not me."

"It is a good performance."

"It is a very good performance. He has been rehearsing it for a month." Tertulla took her arm. "Come. The family is going back to the house, and there will be food, and you are not invited, and we are going to your house to eat bread and cheese and drink too much wine and talk about something other than death."

They went. But before they did, Cassia looked back at the tomb, and at the masks on their stands, and at the crowd dispersing into the Via Appia, and she thought about an old woman who had spent her life keeping a house and a name and a family together, and who had died asking for the masks to be polished.

---

The will was read that evening in the tablinum, with the family and the freedmen and the chief clients present, and the lawyer read it in the flat, careful voice of a man who has read a great many wills and has learned not to be surprised by any of them.

The house and the estate went to Corvinus, with a life interest to Aemilia. The money in the strongbox was divided among the children and the grandchildren. The vineyard in Etruria, which was mortgaged to Sittius, was to be sold and the proceeds used to pay the debts, and if there was anything left, it was to go to the poor of the Palatine district.

The slaves of the household were to be freed. All of them. Every one, with a sum of money for each, and a letter of recommendation, and the freedom of any children they might have in the future.

There was a bequest to the midwife Psyche of Tibur, of a hundred thousand sesterces, "for services rendered and silence kept."

And there was a bequest to Antonia Cassia, freedwoman, of a small wooden chest, "which is in my room and which is to be given to her unopened."

The lawyer read it out and looked up. "That is the whole of the will," he said. "There is a codicil, which is sealed, and which is to be opened only if the legatee requests it."

"I request it," Cassia said.

The lawyer broke the seal. He read the codicil to himself first, and his face did something complicated, and then he read it aloud.

*To Antonia Cassia. I have watched you for sixteen years and I have never once spoken to you. You have done what I could not do, and you have done it without a husband and without a name, and I have envied you every day. The chest contains the jewellery of my marriage, which my husband gave me and which I have not worn since the year he died. It is worth a great deal. Sell it and give the money to the boy, and tell him it is from his grandmother, and tell him that she hopes he has a good life and that he does not have to be afraid of anything.*

*I am not asking for your forgiveness. I am asking you to be better than me. You will be. You have already been.*

The lawyer folded the codicil. The room was silent. Corvinus was looking at the table. Aemilia was looking at Cassia.

Aemilia came to her as the room was emptying.

"The jewellery," she said. "It is worth a great deal. It should have gone to my daughter."

"It was hers to give."

"It should have gone to my daughter," Aemilia said again, and her voice was not angry; it was tired. "I have spent nineteen years in that house, and I have kept it running while she sat in her chair and gave orders, and I have borne her cruelty and her coldness and her contempt, and at the end she left the only thing of value to a woman she met once in a garden." She looked at Cassia. "Do you understand what that means?"

"I understand that she was trying to make amends."

"To you."

"To the boy," Cassia said. "Not to me. She could not make amends to me. She knew that. This was the only amends she could make."

Aemilia was silent. Then she said, "Sell it. Give the money to the boy. I do not want it." She turned to go, and then stopped. "But Cassia. My daughter is fifteen years old and she has been married for a month to a man she does not love, and she has just watched her grandmother die, and she is going to hear the whole story in the Senate in twelve days. When this is over, she will need someone who is not her mother and not her father and not her husband. She will need you."

"I know."

"Then do not let her down."

Cassia stood.

"Where is the chest?" she said.

---

She took it home that night and opened it in the tablinum with the lamps lit and the household around her.

It was a chest of cedarwood with a bronze lock, and inside it was every piece of jewellery the old woman had ever owned: the gold necklace with the cameo of a woman's head, the emerald earrings, the bracelet of twisted gold, the rings, the brooch with the brooch that had pinned the bridal veil at four generations of weddings. It was worth a fortune. It was worth more than the house.

Cassia looked at it for a long time. Then she closed the lid and locked it and said, "Thais. Put it in the strongroom. We will sell it in the autumn, when the trial is over and the city has stopped talking, and the money will go to the boy." She paused. "And when he is ready to hear it, I will tell him that his grandmother left it to him, and that she hoped he would not be afraid of anything."

Thais took the chest. At the door she said, "Domina. The trial is in twelve days."

"I know."

"The whole city is talking about the funeral. They are saying that the old woman's death is a judgment on the family, and that the senator will not survive the trial, and that the whole house will fall." Thais paused. "And they are saying that you were in the palace last week, and that the empress received you, and that the emperor's secretary has been asking questions about the grain accounts."

Cassia looked up.

"Who is saying that?"

"Everyone," Thais said. "It is in the wine shops. It is in the baths. It is in the market. Someone is spreading it, and it is not us."

Cassia sat for a moment. Then she laughed — a short, hard, entirely unexpected laugh.

"Primus," she said. "He is trying to make me look like the emperor's woman. He is trying to make the Senate think that the trial is a palace plot, so that the senators will vote for his man out of spite." She stood. "It is a good move. It is exactly the move I would have made."

"What do we do?"

"We do nothing," Cassia said. "We let him spread it, and we let the city talk, and in twelve days we go into that chamber with the receipt and the statements and the arsonist and the midwife, and we tell the truth in front of the whole Senate." She looked at the locked chest in Thais's arms. "And we do not mention the empress once. Not a single time. Because a woman who is accused of being the emperor's creature is a woman who is finished in this city, but a woman who says nothing and produces the documents is a woman they have to listen to."

She went to bed.

---

On the ninth day after the old woman's death, four days before the trial, the freed slaves of the Palatine house came out to the tomb for the rites, and there were forty of them, and they stood in a rough half-circle in the road and said the words and poured the wine and went away again.

Cassia went out to them. She had not intended to; she had come to the tomb alone, early, before the family, with a jar of wine and a handful of flowers, and she found them already there, cooks and porters and a woman who had been the old woman's dresser for thirty years, and a boy of about fourteen who held the dresser's hand.

"You are the lady from the Esquiline," the dresser said. "The one who came to the house at the end."

"I am."

"She talked about you." The dresser looked at the tomb. "Not to us. She talked to the steward, and we heard it through the door, the way we always heard everything through that door. She said you were the only person in Rome who had never asked her for anything, and she did not know what to do with you."

Cassia looked at the flowers in her hand.

"She gave me a great deal," she said.

"She gave everyone a great deal. That was the trouble with her." The dresser turned. "We are free, then. All of us. She put it in the will, and the lawyer has read it, and my son is free and I am free and we have money for the first time in our lives." She paused. "And I do not know what to feel. I have been in that house since I was sixteen. I have hated her and I have loved her and I have washed her and fed her and lied for her, and now she is dead and I am free and I am standing in a road outside a tomb with a boy who does not know how to be free either."

"You will learn," Cassia said.

"How?"

"The way I did. Badly, and slowly, and with a great deal of help from people who had no reason to give it." Cassia put the flowers down. "Come to my house on the Esquiline. Not today. In a month, when the noise has died down. I will find you work if you want it and I will leave you alone if you do not, and there will be no debt."

The dresser looked at her for a long moment. Then she said, "Why?"

"Because someone did it for me once," Cassia said, "and I have been trying to pay it back for sixteen years, and I am nowhere near finished."

In the morning she began, very carefully, to prepare for the trial, and she did not come out of the tablinum for three days.

---

The dresser came to the house on the Esquiline in September, when the trial was over and the city had found other things to talk about, and Cassia gave her the linen room and a wage and a contract, and she stayed eleven years.

---

## Chapter Twenty-one

The trial was held in the Senate house on the sixth day before the Kalends of August, and the city had been waiting for it for a month.

They had begun to gather before dawn. By the first hour the Forum was full, and by the third the crowd had overflowed into the basilicas and the porticoes and the steps of the temples, and the shopkeepers had put up their shutters and the wine sellers had brought out their barrels and the pickpockets had come down from the Subura in force. There were food sellers and water sellers and a man with a performing monkey and a woman selling little clay figures of the principals — Corvinus with a sword, Pactus with a scroll, Cassia with a purse — and the whole thing had the air of a festival that everyone was slightly ashamed of enjoying.

Cassia came at the fourth hour with Bathyllus and Tertulla and the leather satchel, and she was not stopped at the door this time. The lictor stepped aside. The clerk looked at her summons and nodded. And she walked up the aisle of the Curia with her head up and her hands steady, and the whole chamber turned to look at her.

Aemilia was in the gallery.

Women were not admitted to the floor, but the gallery above the benches had been opened for the families of the accused, and she was there with young Sulpicia beside her and a slave behind them, and she was dressed in black for her mother-in-law and she had the composure of a woman who had decided that she would not be seen to be afraid.

Cassia looked up at her as she came in, and Aemilia inclined her head, once, and young Sulpicia did the same, and the small movement went through the chamber like a rumour.

He is not alone, it said. Whatever else is true, he is not alone.

The benches were full. The consuls sat on their curule chairs. The emperor's secretary sat on a chair to one side with a clerk and a tablet, and he was a thin, grey, careful man who looked as though he had spent his life reading documents and had never once been surprised by one. The senators sat in their ranks. The prosecutor's bench was full of lawyers. The defence bench had Corvinus and two advocates and a row of empty seats where his supporters should have been, and the emptiness was a statement in itself.

Pactus was there, in his white toga, at the front of the prosecutor's bench. He looked rested and calm and entirely at ease.

The consul called the house to order. The clerk read the charge. And then the prosecution rose.

---

Crispus spoke for an hour and a half.

It was a good speech. It was better than good; it was the speech of a man who had been paid a great deal of money and had spent a month polishing every sentence. He spoke about the sanctity of the family, and the duty of a son to his father, and the terrible crime of parricide, which the law punished not with a fine or an exile but with the sack and the river and the viper and the dog. He spoke about the evidence: the physician's certificate, the assistant's testimony, the steward's statement, the midwife's account of the night. He spoke about the quarrel between the accused and his father, and the threats that had been overheard, and the haste with which the accused had left for Syria within the week. And then he spoke about the woman.

He did not name her. He did not need to. He spoke about a freedwoman who kept a house on the Esquiline, and who had made a fortune out of the appetites of senators, and who had been for twenty years the intimate of the accused; and he said that it was she who had corrupted the household at Tibur, she who had seduced the young man, she who had been present at the death, and she who had spent twenty-four years buying silence and influence and the friendship of men who should have known better.

"Conscript fathers," he said, "this is not a trial about a poisoned cup. It is a trial about a family that was destroyed from within by appetite and by money. The cup was the means. The woman was the cause."

He sat down. There was a murmur of approval from the benches, and a rumble of something else from the galleries, and the consul struck the floor.

The defence rose. Corvinus's advocate was a young man named Sura, who had been chosen because he was clever and because he was cheap and because he was the only advocate in Rome who had agreed to take the case.

Sura was good. He was better than good; he was brilliant, in the way that young men are brilliant when they have nothing to lose. He took Crispus's speech apart piece by piece: the physician's certificate was signed by a man who had been paid; the assistant had been paid; the steward had been paid; the midwife had been paid; every witness in the prosecution's case had been paid by somebody, and the somebody was a banker on the Via Lata who held the mortgages on the estate. He spoke about the fire on the Esquiline, and the boy who had died in it, and the arsonist who had been paid by a steward, and he said that the men who had burned a house and killed a child were the same men who had brought this accusation, and that the Senate should ask itself why.

There was a roar from the galleries. The consul struck the floor three times.

Then Corvinus rose to speak for himself.

He had not been required to. His advocate had advised him against it; a defendant who speaks is a defendant who can be trapped. But he rose anyway, and the chamber went quiet in the way that a chamber goes quiet when a man who has been silent for a long time stands up.

"Conscript fathers," he said. "I am not going to make a speech. I have made a great many speeches in this room and most of them were designed to make men vote for something, and this one is not. This one is a statement of fact." He paused. "I did not kill my father. I was in Syria when he died. I received the news at Antioch and I wept, and I have the letter I wrote to my mother in reply, and it is in the archive of this house, and any man who wishes may read it."

He looked at the benches.

"But I did something worse than kill him, and I want the house to know it, because the house is going to hear it from the prosecution before the day is out. When my father told me that he intended to sell the girl I loved and dispose of her child, I did nothing. I did not go to the governor. I did not go to the courts. I did not take her away in the night, though I could have, and though she would have come. I stood in a room and I listened to a man tell me what he was going to do, and I said nothing, and I got on a ship, and I did not come back for two years." He looked at Cassia. "That is the truth about me. It is worse than the charge. And I have carried it for twenty-four years."

The chamber was silent.

"The prosecution will say that I am a man who covered up a death to keep his inheritance. I am a man who covered up a life — the life of a woman who was sold, and the life of a child who was given away — because I was twenty years old and I was a coward, and because I believed that if I said nothing, the world would let me keep the things I had." He paused. "It did not. It never does. The world has a way of collecting."

He sat down.

In the gallery, young Sulpicia had gone very still.

She had been told, at home, in the careful words that families use, that her father had done nothing wrong and that the trial was politics. She had believed it, or had decided to believe it, which is not the same thing. And now she was sitting in a gallery above the Senate house and listening to her father say that he had stood in a room and let a man sell a girl and give away a child, and that it was worse than the charge, and she was sixteen years old and the floor of the world had moved.

Aemilia put her hand over her daughter's hand. She did not say anything. There was nothing to say that would have helped, and she was wise enough to know it.

Crispus rose at once. "The accused has made a very moving speech. But the house will observe that it is not evidence. It is an admission of moral failure, not a denial of the charge. The fact that a man is a coward does not mean he is not a murderer."

"No," Corvinus said. "But the fact that a man is a coward does not mean he is a murderer either. And the house will have to decide which of us it believes: a delator who has been convicted of extortion, a steward who has been paid, a physician's assistant who has been paid, and a prosecutor whose banker holds my mortgage — or a woman who has lost everything she had and who has no reason to lie."

The consul struck the floor. "The house will hear the witnesses."

Then the witnesses were called.

---

The midwife Psyche was called first, and she gave her evidence in a closed session, with the galleries cleared and the doors shut and the clerks sworn to silence.

She told the story again, as she had told it in June: the cup, the night, the child, the cry, the mistress with the lamp, the dealer, the receipt, the twenty-four years. She was older and weaker than she had been, and she had been brought from Tibur in a litter, and she gave her evidence sitting down, which was against the rules and which the consul allowed because she was seventy and could not stand.

When she had finished, the receipt was produced and read aloud. The clerk read it slowly, in the flat voice of a man reading a shopping list, and the chamber listened in absolute silence.

*Received from the household of M. Sulpicius Corvinus, for one male infant, aged one day, of good health and no defect, the sum of four hundred sesterces. The infant is delivered to Gaius Vibidius, potter, of the Via Ostiensis, outside the Porta Trigemina, in the city of Rome, to be raised as his own. No claim upon the child shall be made by the seller or by any person on the seller's behalf. Witnessed by L. Caepio.*

*The child is called Gaius Vibidius Nestor.*

The clerk stopped. The chamber was silent. Then the consul said, "The name of the child and the name of the potter are to be struck from the record and sealed. They are not to be spoken outside this chamber. Any man who speaks them will answer to the house for contempt."

The clerk struck them out. The receipt was sealed and put in the archive. And the Senate of Rome sat in a silence that was, for a moment, almost decent.

Then Crispus rose.

"The house has heard the midwife," he said. "The house has heard the receipt. And the house will observe that the receipt proves nothing about the death of Marcus Sulpicius. It proves that a child was born and sold. It proves that the household at Tibur was a place of vice and secrecy. It does not prove that the accused did not kill his father. In fact, conscript fathers, it proves the opposite: it proves that there was a secret in that house worth killing for."

He called the physician's assistant, Faustus. He called the old steward, who was brought in on a chair and who gave his evidence in a whisper and contradicted himself twice. He called a slave from the Tibur household who had been bought by the prosecution and who said that he had heard the young master threaten his father. He called them one by one, and each of them added a small stone to the wall he was building, and Cassia sat in her seat and watched the wall go up.

The prosecution's witnesses did not do as well as Crispus had hoped.

Faustus, the physician's assistant, was a nervous man of forty with a twitch in his left eye, and he gave his evidence in a voice that was too loud. He said that his master had told him that the death was not natural. He said that his master had been paid to sign the certificate. He said that he had been paid to leave Rome and had come back when the money ran out.

"Who paid you?" Cassia asked.

"The knight Felix."

"Out of whose purse?"

"I do not know."

"You do not know, or you do not wish to say?"

"I do not know." Faustus wiped his face. "The money came from a steward. I never met the man who paid for it."

"Was the steward's name Anthus?"

"I do not know his name."

"Was he a thin man, fifty years old, with grey hair and a mole on his cheek?"

Faustus stared at her. "Yes."

"Then you do know his name," Cassia said. "It is Anthus, and he is the steward of the senator Pactus, and he has this morning admitted in this chamber to paying for the burning of my house. The house will draw its own conclusions about the reliability of a witness who has been paid by a man who pays for fires."

There was a rumble of laughter. Crispus objected. The consul allowed the question and the chamber to draw its own conclusions.

The old steward was worse. He was eighty and he had a bad chest and he had been brought from Ficulea in a litter, and he gave his evidence in a whisper that the clerk had to repeat. He said that he had been in the house on the night of the death. He said that he had seen the mistress go into the master's room with a cup. He said that he had been paid to keep silent, and he named the man who had paid him — not Pactus, not Sittius, but a clerk in the palace whose name he did not know.

Then Cassia produced the statement of the forger.

It was a single sheet of papyrus, taken down by Junia's son in the presence of two witnesses, and it said that a man named Secundus had forged a letter in the year of the consulship of Silanus at the request of the knight Felix, and that the letter had been used to destroy the family of Lucius Junius, praetor, and that he was dying of a disease of the lungs and wished to clear his conscience before he met his maker.

The clerk read it. The chamber listened. And Crispus, for the first time that day, looked at Pactus.

Pactus did not look back.

Then the consul called a recess, and the chamber emptied into the porticoes to eat and drink and tell each other what they had heard.

Cassia went out into the colonnade with Tertulla and stood in the shade with a cup of water she did not drink. The crowd moved around her. Nobody spoke to her. A senator she had dined with four times that year went past with his eyes on the columns. A woman she had lent money to at a rate that would have made a Carthaginian blush looked at her and then looked away. Two young men in the doorway turned their backs.

"You see," Tertulla said.

"I see."

"Does it surprise you?"

"No." Cassia watched the crowd. "It surprises me a little that it does not hurt more. I have known these people for sixteen years. I have fed them and lent them money and found husbands for their daughters. And this morning I am a whore who has been bought by a banker, and by this evening I will be something worse."

"You will be the woman who told the truth to the Senate," Tertulla said. "Some of them will hate you for it, and some of them will be ashamed for the rest of their lives, and one or two will remember that you were the only person in this whole affair who did not lie." She paused. "And when it is over, half of them will come back to your table as though nothing had happened, and you will let them, because you are a businesswoman, and because you have a house to run."

"And I will let them," Cassia said.

"Yes."

They stood in the shade for a while.

At the far end of the colonnade, Pactus's wife was standing with her women. She was a small, handsome, tired woman of about forty who had been married to Pactus for twenty years and had borne him six children, and she had never once spoken to Cassia in all that time. She looked across the colonnade now, and she looked at Cassia for a long moment, and then she inclined her head — the smallest possible movement, and not a friendly one, and not an unfriendly one either. It was the nod of a woman who had heard her husband lie in a public place and had already known that he would.

"She knows," Tertulla said.

"She has known for years." Cassia set the cup down on the parapet. "That is the thing nobody tells you about the wives. They always know. They know before the mistress does."

The bell rang for the afternoon session.

Then it was her turn.

She stood in the middle of the floor with the satchel at her side and the whole chamber looking at her, and she answered the questions.

Yes, she had been a slave in the household. Yes, she had been the mistress's maid. Yes, she had been pregnant by the son. Yes, she had been sold to Puteoli. Yes, she had bought her freedom and built a house and a business. Yes, the senator had come to her house. Yes, she had received him. Yes, she had received half the men in that chamber, and if the house wished to pursue that line of inquiry she would be happy to provide a list, and she was sure the Senate would find it instructive.

There was laughter. The consul did not strike the floor.

Crispus rose again. "The witness has admitted that she is the accused's mistress."

"I have admitted that I am the accused's friend," Cassia said. "There is a difference, and it is the difference between a woman who is paid and a woman who is trusted. I have been paid by a great many men in this city. The senator Corvinus is not one of them."

"You have admitted that you were present at the death."

"I have admitted that I was present at a death in a house where I was a slave, and that I was given a cup by the mistress of that house, and that I woke to be told that my son was dead and that I was to be sold." Cassia's voice was steady. "If the house wishes to know who killed Marcus Sulpicius, I suggest it ask the woman who gave me the cup. But she is dead, and her son is on trial, and it seems to me that the prosecution has chosen the easier target."

The chamber stirred. Crispus's face tightened.

"The witness is hostile," he said. "The prosecution asks that her household be questioned again —"

"The household has been questioned," the consul said. "The questioning produced nothing. The prefect's report is before the house."

"The prefect's report is incomplete."

"Then it is incomplete," the consul said. "The house will not order a second questioning on the word of a prosecutor who has already had one. Proceed."

Crispus's mouth was a thin line. He turned back to Cassia.

"You have a document," he said. "A statement, signed by the knight Felix, who is now convicted of extortion and calumny, and whose word is worthless. You have a statement from an arsonist, who is a criminal. You have a letter that you claim was written by a clerk in the palace, and which you have not produced." He stepped closer. "You have nothing, domina. You have a story, and a ruined reputation, and a great deal of money. And you are standing in the Senate of Rome asking the Senate to believe that a banker and a senator and a clerk in the palace conspired to destroy a consular family in order to steal a house."

"Yes," Cassia said. "That is exactly what I am asking."

"Then produce your proof."

Cassia opened the satchel.

---

She produced the statement of the arsonist first.

It was read aloud by the clerk, and it named Anthus, the steward of the senator Pactus, and it said that Anthus had paid Verecundus four thousand sesterces to burn a house on the Esquiline on the night of the eleventh of March, and that he had been told the house would be empty, and that he had not known about the boy in the passage.

There was uproar. Pactus rose to his feet. The consul struck the floor.

"This is a fabrication," Pactus said. "The man is a criminal and a liar, and he has been paid by the woman to say my name."

"He has not been paid by me," Cassia said. "He has been paid by your steward. And your steward is in this chamber."

The chamber went silent.

"He is standing at the back, by the door," Cassia said. "I saw him when I came in. He has been here all morning, listening to the evidence, and he has been getting paler and paler for an hour." She turned. "Anthus. Stand up."

There was a long, terrible pause. Then, at the back of the chamber, a man in a plain tunic stood up.

He was fifty, thin, grey, with the look of a man who had spent his life in corridors, and he stood with his hands at his sides and his face the colour of clay.

"Anthus," the consul said. "Is this true?"

Anthus looked at Pactus. Pactus looked back at him, and his face was calm, and his eyes were not.

"Anthus," Pactus said. "Tell the house that you have never seen this man. Tell the house that you have never paid anyone to burn anything. Tell the house the truth."

Anthus opened his mouth. Then he closed it. Then he said, in a voice that could barely be heard, "I paid him."

The chamber erupted.

The consul struck the floor again and again, and the lictors moved in, and above the noise the emperor's secretary rose from his seat and said, in a voice that was not loud but that carried, "The house will be silent."

The chamber went silent. Every man in it turned to look at the thin grey man with the tablet.

"The emperor's household has an interest in this matter," the secretary said. "Not in the guilt or innocence of the senator Corvinus, which is for this house to decide. In the conduct of a steward who has admitted in open session to paying for the destruction of a Roman house and the death of a Roman slave. That is a crime against the peace of the city, and it will be tried, and it will be tried before this house, with the emperor's secretary present." He looked at Pactus. "The senator Pactus will be examined tomorrow on his knowledge of his steward's actions. He will not leave the city until he has been examined."

Pactus's face did not change. But he bowed, and said, "I am at the service of the house," and sat down.

The chamber erupted.

---

The consul adjourned the trial to the following day. The guards took Anthus into custody. Pactus was escorted from the chamber by two lictors, and he walked out with his head up and his toga straight and his face entirely composed, and the crowd outside, when they saw him, began to shout.

Cassia came out of the Curia into the late afternoon with Tertulla on one side and Bathyllus on the other, and the crowd pressed in on her, and this time it did not shout. It made a sound she had never heard before, a low, rising, hungry sound, and it took her a moment to understand that it was cheering.

"Say something," Tertulla said.

"No."

"Say something. They will remember it."

Cassia stopped on the steps. The crowd went quiet, or as quiet as a crowd of ten thousand people can go.

"I am not going to make a speech," she said. "I am a freedwoman and I have been on trial in this city for six months, and I am tired, and I want to go home." She paused. "But I will say one thing. A boy died in my house in March. His name was Nolus. He was eleven years old and he fed the pigeons and he was in a passage with a bolted door. The men who paid for that fire are going to be tried tomorrow, and I am going to be in that chamber, and I am going to watch." She looked at the crowd. "If you want to cheer for something, cheer for him. He is the only person in this whole affair who did nothing wrong."

Before she went out, she found Corvinus in the corridor behind the chamber, alone but for one lictor, with his back against the wall.

He looked exhausted. His toga was crumpled and his hair was out of place and there was a mark on his cheek where he had been pressing his hand.

"You spoke well," she said.

"I spoke the truth. It is easier than speaking well." He looked at her. "Tomorrow they will come for me with everything they have. Pactus will take the prosecution himself; he cannot afford not to. He will say that I killed my father and that you are my whore and that the child was a fiction invented to win sympathy."

"Then we will answer him."

"With what?"

"With the truth," Cassia said, "and with the arsonist, and with the steward, and with a letter from a clerk in the palace that I have not yet produced." She looked at him. "I am saving it. A man like Pactus will hang himself if you give him enough rope, and tomorrow he will have a great deal of rope."

Corvinus looked at her for a long moment. Then he laughed — a tired, surprised laugh — and said, "You are enjoying this."

"No," Cassia said. "I am not enjoying it. I am good at it. Those are different things, and it has taken me forty-two years to learn the difference."

She went down the steps and into the crowd, and the crowd opened for her, and the sound that followed her was not cheering any more. It was something older and stranger, and it followed her all the way to the edge of the Forum.

---

At home, the house was in an uproar. The household had heard the news before she got there — the city was like that — and they were in the atrium, all of them, and Thais was crying, and Celer was shouting, and Sabina was standing on the impluvium with her arms out.

Cassia stood in the doorway and looked at them.

"Tomorrow," she said, "is the second day. It will be worse than today. There will be more shouting, and more lies, and at some point a man will say something about me that is true and that I would rather not have said in public. And then it will be over, and we will come home, and we will eat something, and we will sleep." She paused. "And then, the day after, we are going to have a proper funeral for Nolus. Not the one we had in March. A proper one, with a stone with his name on it and a priest and a feast, and everyone in this house will be there."

Thais wiped her face. "Domina. The stone will cost money."

"Let it cost money," Cassia said. "We have a great deal of money, and we have just discovered what it is for."

She went into the tablinum and sat down and put her hands on the desk and closed her eyes. And then she opened them and took out a clean sheet of paper and began to write the speech she would make the next day, because she had learned a long time ago that the only way to survive a thing like this was to prepare for it as though it were an account.

---

## Chapter Twenty-two

She did not sleep the night before the second day.

She sat in the tablinum with the lamps burning and the papers spread out on the desk and went through them one more time, and then she put them in the satchel and strapped it and sat with her hands in her lap and listened to the house.

The house was awake too. She could hear the kitchen — Celer had been up since the third hour, making bread that nobody would eat — and the women upstairs, and the soft, endless pacing of Bathyllus in the yard. At some point in the middle of the night Thais came in with a cup of hot wine and sat down opposite her and did not say anything for a long time.

"Are you frightened?" she said at last.

"Yes."

"Good. You should be." Thais turned her cup. "A woman who is not frightened on the night before a thing like this is a woman who has not understood it. Fear is not a weakness. It is a kind of intelligence."

"I have been frightened for six months."

"Then you have been intelligent for six months." Thais looked at her. "Do you remember what I said to you in Puteoli? The first night?"

"You said not to believe anything they told me in the first month."

"And you said you did not believe anything anyone told you." Thais's mouth moved. "You were a girl in a cell with no shoes and a fever, and you looked at me as though I were a wall you were calculating how to climb. And now you are a woman with a house and a fortune and a senator and a son, and you are sitting in the dark with a cup of wine you have not touched, and you are still calculating how to climb the wall." She stood. "You will win tomorrow. I have known it since the day you bought the house. But I want you to remember something when you are standing in that chamber."

"What?"

"That you are not the girl in the cell. You are not the woman in the brothel. You are not even the woman who built this house. You are the woman who is going to walk into the Senate of Rome and tell the truth in front of the men who have been lying about her for six months." Thais put her hand on Cassia's head for a moment. "And when it is over, you are going to come home and eat something, and sleep for a week, and then you are going to be bored, and it will be the best thing that has ever happened to you."

Cassia laughed. "Tertulla said the same thing."

"Then it must be true." Thais went to the door. "Drink the wine. Sleep for an hour. And domina—"

"Yes?"

"Wear the dark veil. It makes you look like a woman who has come to bury someone."

She came to the Curia at dawn and found the woman from the prefect's courtyard sitting on the steps.

She was in the same good but not expensive wool, and she had a basket on her arm and a look on her face that Cassia recognized because she had worn it herself, in another life: the look of a person who has been waiting a long time for something and has decided to wait one more day.

"Domina," the woman said. "I heard that you won."

"I have not won anything yet."

"You were in the chamber yesterday for six hours and the whole city knows what happened." The woman stood up. "My son is out. They let him out the day after they let out your household. He came home and he ate a whole loaf of bread and then he slept for two days, and now he is back at the docks and he is complaining about the weight of the sacks." She held out the basket. "I brought you bread. It is not much. It is what I have."

Cassia looked at the basket. Then she took it.

"What is your name?" she asked.

"Marcia."

"Marcia. I will eat this bread today, in the chamber, while a man is telling the Senate that I am a whore." Cassia tucked the basket under her arm. "And it will remind me of what I am actually doing."

The woman laughed — a surprised, unguarded laugh — and went down the steps, and Cassia stood in the doorway of the Senate house with the basket on her arm and the satchel at her side and the sun coming up over the rooftops.

The second day of the trial began with Pactus on his feet.

He had spent the night preparing, and it showed. He spoke for two hours, and he did not use a single note. He spoke about the sanctity of the family and the duty of a son; he spoke about the poisoned cup and the sack and the river; he spoke about the woman on the Esquiline and the house of pleasure and the fortune made out of the appetites of senators. He spoke about the child, and he said that the story of the child was a fiction invented by a clever woman to win the sympathy of the house, and that the receipt was a forgery, and that the midwife was a paid witness, and that the arsonist's statement had been obtained by threats.

And then he said something that changed the temperature of the room.

"The house will observe," he said, "that the woman Cassia has been in the palace. She was received by the empress in June, alone, without the knowledge of the consul or the prefect. She has been seen since in the company of the empress's steward. And the emperor's secretary sits in this chamber today, not as an observer, but as a participant." He turned. "I ask the house: is this a trial, or is it a palace arrangement? Is the Senate judging a man, or is it being told what to think by a woman who has the ear of the empress?"

The chamber stirred. The emperor's secretary did not move.

"The senator Pactus will confine himself to the evidence," the consul said.

"The house will observe that this woman has made a career out of the weaknesses of senators. She has kept a house of pleasure for sixteen years. She has taken money from men who could not afford it. She has collected secrets the way a banker collects interest, and she has used them to make herself the most powerful woman in Rome who has never held an office and never been elected to anything." He looked around the chamber. "And now she has climbed into the palace, and she has persuaded the empress to receive her, and she has brought the emperor's secretary into this chamber to sit in judgment on a Roman senator. If this house acquits the senator Corvinus on the word of such a woman, then it is not the Senate of Rome. It is the antechamber of the palace, and we are all of us servants of a freedwoman's ambition."

The chamber stirred. Several senators nodded. Cassia, watching from her seat, understood exactly what he was doing: he was not trying to prove that Corvinus had killed his father. He was trying to make the Senate afraid of her.

She rose.

"The senator Pactus has said that I am the most powerful woman in Rome who has never held an office," she said. "I am flattered. But I would remind the house that the last woman who was accused of ruling Rome was Livia, and she was the wife of an emperor, and she had an army. I have a house on the Esquiline, a cook, and a bookkeeper." She paused. "If that is power, then power in this city is even cheaper than I thought."

The chamber laughed. Pactus's face tightened.

"The house may also observe," Cassia went on, "that the senator Pactus has spent the last six months telling anyone who would listen that I am the emperor's creature, and the last ten minutes telling the house that I am a whore. I would like to know which of those accusations he would like the house to believe, because they cannot both be true, and because a man who makes two contradictory accusations in the same speech is a man who is not interested in the truth."

"I am confining myself to the evidence. The evidence is that this woman has spent six months buying witnesses, bribing clerks, and climbing into the palace through the empress's private door. The evidence is that she has a document that she says was written by a clerk in the palace, and that she has not produced it, because it does not exist." Pactus turned to Cassia. "Produce it, domina. Produce your letter. Or admit to this house that you have been lying."

Cassia stood.

"The house has asked for the letter," she said. "The house shall have it."

She took it from her sleeve and gave it to the clerk.

---

The clerk read it aloud.

It was not long. It was four hundred words, in a neat clerk's hand, dated in the year of the consulship of Domitian and Silanus, and it concerned a shipment of grain that had been diverted at Ostia and sold at a profit in Campania, and it named three men: the clerk Primus, the senator Marcus Sulpicius Corvinus, and a banker on the Via Lata named Sittius. It said that the money had been divided, and that the senator's share had been paid into an account, and that the clerk would keep his part of the bargain.

And it said, at the end, in the same neat hand: *If anything happens to me, I have taken precautions.*

The chamber was silent when the clerk finished.

"The letter is a forgery," Pactus said. "It is a clumsy forgery. The hand is not the hand of the emperor's secretary."

"The hand is the hand of a clerk," Cassia said. "The secretary was a clerk twenty-four years ago. He was not the man he is now. He was a slave in the treasury, and he was bought and freed by the emperor's father, and he has been in the palace ever since." She turned. "The house may compare the letter with the secretary's own hand. There are a hundred documents in the archive with his signature on them. The clerks of this house can do it in an hour."

The consul looked at the emperor's secretary. The secretary rose.

"The letter is mine," he said.

The chamber erupted.

---

It took an hour to restore order, and by the time it was restored, the shape of the trial had changed entirely.

The secretary did not deny it. He stood in the middle of the chamber, a thin grey man with a tablet in his hand, and he said, "I was a slave in the treasury in the year of the consulship of Domitian and Silanus. I was twenty-four years old. I was offered money to divert a shipment of grain and I took it, and I have regretted it every day since. The senator Marcus Sulpicius was my partner in the fraud. The banker Sittius held the money. And I have spent twenty-four years paying the old woman at Tibur to keep silent, and twenty-four years waiting for the day when it would come out."

He turned to the Senate.

"I have served the emperor for forty years. I have counted his money and kept his accounts and I have never taken a copper since that year. I am not asking for mercy. I am telling the house the truth, because the truth is going to come out anyway, and because I would rather tell it myself than have it dragged out of a midwife and a delator and a woman who has been trying to bury it for a quarter of a century." He paused. "The trial of the senator Corvinus is a fraud built on a fraud. The accusation was paid for by Sittius, who wanted a house. It was brought by Pactus, who wanted a province. And it was concealed by me, because I wanted to keep my place. That is the whole of it. The house may do with me what it likes."

He sat down.

There was a long silence. Then Pactus rose, and his face was white, and he said, "This is a conspiracy. The secretary has been bought by the woman —"

"By whom?" the consul said.

"By the woman Cassia, by the empress, by —"

"The senator will be silent." The consul struck the floor. "The senator will sit down and he will remain silent until he is called to answer for his steward, his banker, and his accusation."

Pactus sat down.

---

Anthus was called next.

He had spent the night in a cell under the Curia, and he came into the chamber in the morning grey-faced and shaking, and he told the truth. He told it in a flat, exhausted voice, without ornament, without excuses. He told them about the payments to Felix, the payments to the forger, the payments to the arsonist. He told them about the visit to Tibur, and the men at the inn, and the old woman in the house with the fig tree who would not be frightened. He told them about the boy.

"The boy was taken from the workshop at Praeneste on the fifteenth of June," he said. "He is being held at a farm near Aricia, on the estate of the banker Sittius. He has not been harmed. He has been told that he is being kept for his own safety, and that his father will come for him when it is safe."

"Who gave the order?" the consul asked.

"The senator Pactus."

Pactus did not move. His face was the face of a man who had been carved out of stone.

"Have the boy brought to Rome," the consul said. "At once, and safely, and with a physician. And have the senator's steward taken to the prefect's house and held on a charge of arson and murder." He struck the floor. "The house will vote."

---

The vote took an hour.

The senators voted one by one, in the old way, each man rising and speaking his verdict aloud, and the clerks wrote them down. Cassia stood at the back of the chamber with her hands folded and listened to the words, and the words were, in the end, exactly what she had expected: not a verdict on the truth, but a verdict on the politics.

Corvinus was acquitted of parricide. The vote was overwhelming; no man in the chamber was willing to convict a consular on the word of a convicted delator and a steward who had confessed to arson.

But the house did not stop there. Old Paetus rose and said that while the senator was innocent of his father's death, he was not innocent of the concealment, and that a man who had allowed a woman to be sold and a child to be given away, and who had spent twenty-four years in the enjoyment of an inheritance that had been obtained by fraud, was not fit for the Asian command. He moved that the senator be censured by the house and that his name be struck from the list of candidates for the proconsulship.

The motion passed.

Pactus was convicted of calumny, of suborning witnesses, of procuring arson and murder, and of conspiracy to defraud. He was condemned to exile, his property was confiscated, and his name was struck from the rolls of the Senate. He heard the verdict standing, and when it was read he did not speak, and he did not look at anyone, and he walked out of the chamber between two lictors with his head up and his face like a mask.

Sittius was condemned in his absence. His bank was confiscated, his house on the Via Lata was sealed, and a warrant was issued for his arrest. The prefect's men found him three days later at his villa at Baiae, in his bath, with his wrists opened and a letter on the table that said, *I have been a banker for forty years and I have never lost an argument. I do not intend to begin now.*

Primus was removed from his office by the emperor's order. The order was read in the Senate by the secretary himself, in the same flat voice in which he had confessed, and it said that the emperor thanked the house for its diligence, that the clerk would be retired to a house in Campania with a pension, and that no further action would be taken against him. The Senate received it in silence. No one was surprised.

The arsonist Verecundus was condemned to the mines. His companion, a man named Rufus who had held the lamp, was condemned to the games. Anthus, who had confessed and given evidence, was condemned to exile and the confiscation of his property.

And the household of Antonia Cassia, freedwoman, was declared free of all suspicion, and the costs of the questioning were remitted by order of the house.

Cassia stood at the back of the chamber while all of it was read, and when it was over she did not cheer and she did not weep.
