She turned and walked out into the sunshine, and the crowd in the Forum was waiting for her, and this time it made the sound she had heard on the steps the day before, and this time she let it wash over her.

---

The emperor's secretary was waiting for her in the portico.

He was standing by a column with his tablet in his hand and his clerk three paces behind him, and he had the look of a man who has been waiting for some time and does not intend to be kept waiting much longer.

"Domina," he said. "A word."

"Secretary."

"I have been in the service of three emperors," he said, "and I have read a great many records of a great many trials, and I have never in forty years seen a house vote a man's name out of a document." He looked at her. "The names are struck. The sheet is sealed. It is in the archive, and it will stay there, and in fifty years a clerk will open it and find two lines cut out of a receipt and a note in the margin in my hand, and he will wonder what was so dangerous about a potter."

"Will the seal hold?"

"The seal will hold as long as the men who ordered it are alive, and after that it will hold because nobody will care." He put the tablet under his arm. "I am going to Campania in the autumn. I am told there is a house and a pension and a garden, and that I am to be grateful. I am grateful. I have spent my life counting other men's money, and now I am to spend the rest of it counting figs." He paused. "I have one thing to say to you before I go, and then I will not trouble you again."

"Say it."

"You won," he said. "You won because you were the only person in that chamber who did not want anything. The prosecutor wanted a conviction. Pactus wanted a province. The banker wanted a house. The senator wanted to be forgiven. Even the old woman in the bed wanted to be told that she had done the right thing." He looked at her. "You wanted the boy, and you wanted the truth, and you were willing to lose both. That is why they could not touch you. It is also why you will never be safe in this city again, because the one thing Rome cannot forgive is a person who does not want what it is selling."

Cassia looked at him for a long moment.

"That is the most honest thing anyone has said to me in sixteen years," she said.

"I am a good accountant," the secretary said. "Honesty is a kind of bookkeeping." He inclined his head. "Goodbye, domina. I hope you find him."

He walked away down the portico with his clerk behind him, and Cassia stood by the column and watched him go.

---

Aemilia was waiting in the portico with her daughter.

She had heard the verdict from a clerk before it was read, because the wives of senators always hear things before they are read, and she stood with her hands folded and her face composed, and when Corvinus came out she looked at him for a long moment and then said, "You are acquitted."

"I am."

"And censured."

"And censured." He stopped in front of her. "And finished. The Asian command is gone. The augurate will go too, in a month, when the house votes on it. I will be a consular who cannot hold an office and a senator who cannot speak without being reminded of his father's fraud and his mother's poison and the boy he did not raise." He looked at his wife. "I am sorry. I am sorry for all of it. For the years I was not there. For the lies. For my mother. For the house. For all of it."

Aemilia looked at him. Then she said, "Do you know what I have been thinking, all through this trial? I have been thinking that I have spent nineteen years in a house where no one told me the truth, and that I would rather have a husband who is finished and honest than a husband who is successful and a lie." She took his hand. "You are not finished, Lucius. You are forty-five years old. There is a great deal of life left, and I intend to spend it with a man who has finally stopped pretending to be his father."

Young Sulpicia came forward. She was fifteen, and married, and she had been in the gallery for two days, and she looked at her father with her grandmother's eyes.

"The boy," she said. "Is he alive?"

"He is alive. He is on a farm near Aricia and Bathyllus is already on the road."

"Then bring him home," Sulpicia said. "And let me meet him. He is my brother. I have a right to meet him."

Corvinus looked at his daughter for a long moment. Then he said, "You have a right to a great many things, and I have given you almost none of them." He put his hand on her head. "I will bring him home. And then we will see what he wants."

An hour later Corvinus came and found her standing by the fountain at the edge of the Forum with Tertulla and Bathyllus. He had sent his wife and daughter home in the litter and had walked the length of the Forum alone, and he looked ten years older than he had that morning.

His toga was crumpled and his face was grey and there was something in his eyes that she had not seen there before: not relief, not triumph. Emptiness.

"Well," he said.

"Well."

"I am acquitted."

"You are."

"And censured, and struck from the list, and finished." He laughed, without any humour. "I have spent my whole life wanting the Asian command. I have mortgaged the house and borrowed from a man who wanted to destroy me and married a woman I did not love and lied to my mother and lied to my wife and lied to myself, and I have got what I wanted, which is to be alive and free and to have nothing left to want."

Cassia looked at him. The crowd was going past them, and the sun was on the fountain, and somewhere a man was selling the little clay figures again, and a woman was shouting that the senator was innocent and the gods were just.

"You have a son," she said. "Two sons. And a daughter who is married to a fool and a wife who has been braver than either of us. And a house that is falling down and a name that has been dragged through the Forum and a woman who has been in love with you since she was eighteen years old." She paused. "You have a great deal left to want."

He looked at her. Then he put his hand over his face and stood in the middle of the Forum and wept, and the crowd went past and did not notice, because in Rome a man weeping in the Forum is not a remarkable thing.

Cassia stood beside him and did not touch him, and waited.

After a while he wiped his face and said, "The boy. Anthus said Aricia."

"Bathyllus is already on the road. He will be there by nightfall."

"Then I am going with him."

"Lucius—"

"I am going with him," Corvinus said. "I have spent twenty-four years not going. I am not going to spend another night in this city while my son is on a farm in Aricia being held by a banker's men." He looked at her. "You said I had to choose. I am choosing."

Cassia looked at him for a long moment. Then she said, "Take the fast horses. Take Bathyllus and two of Tertulla's men. And Lucius—"

"Yes?"

"Bring him home," she said. "Not to me. To his father. Bring him home to the Via Ostiensis, and tell him what he needs to know, and let him decide what happens next." She paused. "And when it is done, come back and tell me."

He nodded. Then he turned and went, and the crowd closed around him, and Cassia stood by the fountain with Tertulla and watched him go.

"He will bring the boy home," Tertulla said.

"Yes."

"And then?"

"And then we will find out what the truth is worth," Cassia said. "We have spent six months fighting for it. Now we have to live with it."

---

They buried Nolus on the third day after the trial, in the ground outside the Porta Esquilina where the poor and the slaves were buried, and this time it was not a small stone and a hurried prayer.

Cassia paid for a proper funeral. There was a procession from the house to the gate, with the household in front and the flute players and the mourning women, and there was a stone with his name on it and his age and the words *A GOOD BOY, WHO FED THE PIGEONS*, which Cassia had chosen herself, and there was a feast afterward in the courtyard of the house for everyone who had come.

The whole household was there. Thais and Hermeros and Celer and Bathyllus and Sabina and Chrysis and Senno and the kitchen boys and the women from upstairs and the old gardener's daughter, who had come from Ostia with her two children and who cried through the whole of it and then ate a great deal of bread and cheese.

Tertulla came. Aemilia came, with young Sulpicia, who stood at the back and looked at the stone and did not say anything. Corvinus was still on the road to Aricia, and Cassia stood at the grave and thought about the fact that he had never met the boy in life and was now going to meet him in a farmhouse with a physician and a banker's men.

When the priest had finished and the earth had been put in, Cassia said a few words. She had not prepared them.

"Nolus was eleven years old," she said. "He came to this house two years ago from the market, and he was given the pigeons and the yard, and he did it well. He was not a clever boy and he was not a beautiful boy and he did not have a great future ahead of him. He had a broom and a bucket and eleven pigeons, and he fed them every morning before he ate his own breakfast, because he said they were always hungry and he was not." She paused. "He died because someone wanted to hurt me. That is the truth of it, and I am not going to pretend otherwise. He was killed to send me a message. And the men who killed him have been sent to the mines and to the games, and that is justice, of a kind, and it does not bring him back, and it never will."

She looked at the stone.

"But he is not a message. He is a boy who fed the pigeons. And every year, on the day of his death, this house will make an offering for him and say his name, and the pigeons will be fed, and anyone who wants to remember him will be welcome at my table." She paused. "That is all. That is the whole of it. A boy lived and a boy died and we are going to remember him, because that is the only thing any of us can do for the dead."

They went back to the house and ate and drank and told stories about Nolus, and Celer got drunk and wept and was put to bed by Thais, and Sabina fell asleep on the bench by the fountain with her head on Chrysis's shoulder, and the sun went down over the garden, and the house was full of people who had come through something together and were still, all of them, alive.

Cassia sat on the bench by the roses and watched them.

"Domina," Thais said, sitting down beside her. "The boy. What will you do if he does not want to come?"

"I do not know."

"You must have thought about it."

"I have thought about nothing else for six months." Cassia looked at the dark garden. "If he does not want to come, I will let him go. I will leave him the money from the chest and a letter, and I will not go to the shop, and I will not send anyone. And once a year I will have a message from his father to say that he is alive, and once a year I will send him something that he can throw away if he wants to." She paused. "And I will be the woman with the house on the Esquiline, and he will be the potter on the Via Ostiensis, and the city will go on, and that will be the end of it."

"And if he does want to come?"

"Then I will have a son," Cassia said, "and a house with a room at the top of the stairs that is no longer empty, and a great deal of explaining to do." She looked at Thais. "And I will probably make a mess of it."

"Probably," Thais said. "That is what mothers do."

Cassia laughed, and then, unexpectedly, she cried — briefly and furiously, with her hands over her face, the way she had not allowed herself to cry since the morning in the prefect's courtyard. Thais sat beside her and did not say anything and did not touch her, and after a while Cassia stopped and wiped her face and said, "Well. That was undignified."

"It was very dignified," Thais said. "You did it in the dark, in your own garden, in front of one person. That is the most dignified way there is."

---

## Chapter Twenty-three

The city forgot the trial in three weeks, which was longer than Cassia had expected.

At first it was everywhere: the wine shops and the baths and the barbers and the steps of the temples, and every man in Rome had an opinion about the woman who had brought down a consul and a banker and a clerk in the palace, and half of them had been in her house and the other half had wanted to be. For a fortnight she could not walk down the Clivus Suburanus without being pointed at, and there were people who came to the garden door and asked to see her, and one man who came with a lyre and wanted to sing her praises, and whom Bathyllus removed with a firmness that was almost courteous.

Then the emperor dedicated another monument, and the games were announced, and a grain ship went down off Sicily with a cargo of Egyptian wheat, and the city found something else to talk about.

That was how it went. Cassia had known it would. She had lived in Rome for twenty-four years, and she had seen the city forget a fire, a plague, a war and a murder in less time than it took to forget a trial. It was not cruelty, exactly. It was appetite. There was always something new to eat.

The consequences did not go away, though. They arrived one by one over the following months, like the bills after a wedding.

The first was the clients. On the day after the verdict, half the men who had dined at her table for years did not send their usual messages. By the end of the week, two of them had sent letters explaining that they were going to the country for the summer. By the end of the month, the house was taking a third of what it had taken in the spring, and Celer had begun to complain about the waste of food, and Thais had begun to look at the accounts in a way that Cassia did not like.

The second was Corvinus. The Senate stripped him of the augurate in September, by a vote that was not close. The Asian command went to a man named Verus, who had no qualifications except a good name and a wife with connections. Corvinus took the news quietly. He had expected it. But Cassia saw him afterward, in the garden of the Palatine house, standing under the masks with his hands behind his back, and she knew that something in him had gone out.

The third was the house on the Palatine. The mortgage had been held by Sittius, and Sittius was dead, and his heirs — a nephew and a cousin and a bank in Alexandria — had no interest in the Sulpicii. They called in the loan in October. Corvinus could not pay it. The house was sold at the end of November to a man who had made his money in bricks and who intended to knock it down and build an insula on the site, and the family moved into a smaller house on the Esquiline, two streets from Cassia's, with a garden and a portico and no history at all.

The masks went into the new atrium. There was nowhere else to put them.

---

Young Sulpicia came in September, alone, in a plain cloak, with a basket of figs.

She was sixteen now and she had been married for three months, and she had the look of a woman who had discovered that marriage was not the end of the world and not the beginning of one either, but a long room that had to be furnished. She sat down in the tablinum and put the figs on the desk and said, "I have come to ask you something, and I want you to tell me the truth."

"I will try."

"Did my grandmother kill my grandfather?"

Cassia looked at her. The girl's face was steady and her hands were steady and she had her grandmother's chin.

"Yes," Cassia said.

"Did my father know?"

"No. He suspected, at the end, but he did not know until she told him."

"Did you know?"

"I knew that she gave me a cup on the night my son was born, and that my son was taken from me, and that she was in the room. I did not know about your grandfather until the spring." Cassia turned her cup. "Your grandmother told me herself, in the last week of her life, and I have not told anyone what she said except your father and the Senate. I am telling you now because you asked and because you are old enough to know and because a person who is lied to by her family for sixteen years has a right to the truth."

Sulpicia was silent for a long time. Then she said, "I loved her."

"I know."

"She was cruel to my mother and cold to my father and she arranged my marriage without asking me, and I loved her. She used to let me sit in her room when I was small and she would tell me about the masks. She knew the name and the office of every one of them. She used to say that a family is a thing you carry, like a lamp, and that it is your job to carry it without dropping it." Her eyes were wet. "And she killed my grandfather and sold your son. How do I hold both of those things?"

"You hold them the way you hold everything," Cassia said. "You stop trying to make them into one thing. She was a woman who loved you and who did something terrible. Both of those things are true. You do not have to choose."

Sulpicia wiped her eyes. "My husband is a fool," she said.

"Yes."

"He is not a bad man. He is just — he talks about the games all the time. All the time. He has an opinion about every gladiator in Rome and no opinion about anything else." She blew her nose. "My mother says I should be patient and manage the house and have a son and he will improve. My grandmother would have said the same. What do you say?"

"I say have the son, and manage the house, and take the money, and keep the purse I gave you, and learn something that he does not know you can do." Cassia leaned forward. "And when you are thirty, and he is still talking about the games, you will have a life of your own that he has never once looked at, and you will be able to be kind to him, because you will not need him to be anything other than what he is."

Sulpicia looked at her. "That is a very cold answer."

"It is a warm answer that has been in the cold for a long time." Cassia picked up a fig and ate it. "Now. Tell me about the house. Is the cook any good?"

Ione came back in October.

She came to the garden door in the evening, alone, with a bag over her shoulder and a cloak that had seen better days, and Bathyllus came to the tablinum with a face that was trying very hard to be neutral.

"There is a woman at the door," he said. "She says you will know her."

Cassia put down her pen.

"Show her in."

Ione came in. She was thin and brown from the sun and she had cut her hair short in the way of a woman who has decided to stop being decorative, and she stood in the middle of the tablinum with her bag still on her shoulder and looked at Cassia.

"I heard," she said. "About the trial. About the house. About the old woman. About the boy." She set the bag down. "I was in Baiae. I was keeping a house for a man who did not want a house, and it ended badly, as those things do. I have spent the summer being somebody else and I find that I do not like her very much."

Cassia stood up.

"I am sorry," she said. "For what I said in the kitchen. For what I did not say. For —"

"Stop." Ione held up her hand. "I did not come back for an apology. I came back because I heard that you had lost half your clients and that the house was in trouble and that Thais was doing the accounts on her own, and because I am good at accounts, and because I have nowhere else to go, and because — " She stopped, and her voice changed. "Because I have been free for four months and I have discovered that freedom is a great deal of work and very little fun, and that I was happier in your kitchen than I have been anywhere since."

Cassia looked at her for a long moment. Then she said, "You are hired. The pay is bad and the hours are worse and the household is full of people who have been through too much, and the cook is impossible."

Ione's mouth trembled. "That is the best offer I have had all year."

"Then take it." Cassia came around the desk. "And Ione. I was wrong. I was wrong about the book and I was wrong about you and I was wrong to accuse you, and I have thought about it every day since you left. You were the best thing in this house and I did not see it until you were gone."

Ione looked at her. Then she put her bag down on the floor and said, "Well. I am back now. You will have to be cleverer than that if you want to get rid of me."

They embraced in the middle of the tablinum, and Thais came in and saw them and stopped, and then came and put her arms around both of them, and the household gathered in the doorway and someone began to laugh, and someone else began to cry, and Celer said, "Does this mean I have to cook for one more?" and Ione said, "Yes, Celer, and I want the fish tonight, with the sauce, not the other sauce," and the whole house settled back into itself like a thing that had been holding its breath.

---

Aemilia came in the last week of October, to the new house, and asked Cassia to help her hang the masks.

The new house on the Esquiline was smaller than the old one and newer and had no history at all, and the atrium was bright and low and the cupboards for the masks had been made by a carpenter in the Subura who had never made one before. Aemilia stood in the middle of the floor with her hands on her hips and looked at the wall.

"They do not fit," she said. "The old ones were made for a wall three feet higher. They look like a row of provincial ancestors."

"They are the same ancestors."

"They are the same wax and the same names and they look ridiculous." Aemilia sighed. "My mother-in-law would have had the carpenter flogged."

"Your mother-in-law is dead."

"Yes," Aemilia said. "That is the only good thing about this house."

Cassia laughed. She had not expected to like Aemilia, and she had discovered over the summer that she did, in the way that two women who have been through the same war like each other: not with affection, exactly, but with a shared and slightly grim understanding.

"The jewellery," Aemilia said, when the masks were hung. "Have you sold it?"

"Not yet. I have a buyer in mind for the winter. The emeralds are worth more than the rest put together, and there is a woman in the Subura who deals in old stones and does not ask questions."

"Give the money to the boy."

"I intend to."

"Give it to him as money, not as a story," Aemilia said. "A potter with a workshop needs a kiln and a cart and a boy to turn the wheel. He does not need a chest of his grandmother's jewellery and a letter about the old days. Give him the tools of his trade and let him think that the lady in Rome is a patron with more money than sense." She paused. "And when he is older, and when he asks, tell him the rest."

Cassia looked at her. "You have thought about this."

"I have thought about nothing else since the funeral. I have a son who is twenty-two and a daughter who is sixteen and a husband who has lost everything, and I have discovered that the only thing I am good at is arranging things so that the people I love are not hurt any more than necessary." Aemilia straightened a mask. "It is not a noble talent. But it is mine."

The potter came in November, on a wet afternoon, with clay under his nails and a letter in his hand.

Gaius Vibidius had aged in the six months since Cassia had last seen him. He was greyer and thinner and there was a tremor in his hands that had not been there before, and he sat down in the tablinum and put the letter on the desk between them.

"He is home," he said. "They brought him back from Aricia in August. He was not hurt. They had told him that he was being kept for his own safety, and he believed them, because he is a trusting boy and because he had no reason not to." He paused. "He knows there is something wrong. He has known since the men came to the workshop at Praeneste. He has been asking questions, and I have been lying to him, and I am not a good liar."

Cassia looked at the letter. It was unsealed and it was addressed to her, in a careful, awkward hand.

"He wrote that," the potter said. "Himself. He asked me to bring it to you. He does not know who you are. He thinks you are the lady who paid for his apprenticeship, or a patron of the shop, or something of that kind. He has been told that a great lady in Rome took an interest in him, and he has decided that he wants to thank her." The potter's mouth twisted. "He is a good boy. He does not know that he is in the middle of anything."

Cassia picked up the letter.

*Domina. My father says you are the lady who paid for my apprenticeship and for the new kiln. I do not know why you did it and I do not know who you are, but I want to thank you. The kiln is very good. I have made a set of cups that I am proud of, and if you would like them I will send them to you. My father says I am not to ask questions, and I am trying not to, but I would like to know your name, if it is not a secret. Yours, Gaius Vibidius Nestor.*

She read it twice. Then she put it down on the desk and looked at the potter.

"What have you told him?"

"That you are a patron. That he should be grateful. That he should not ask questions." The potter's hands were on his knees. "I have lied to him for four months, domina, and it is eating me alive. He is twenty-four years old. He has a right to know who he is. But if I tell him, he will want to know why I did not tell him before, and then he will want to know what happened to his mother, and then —" He stopped.

"And then he will want to know about me."

"Yes."

Cassia sat for a moment. The rain came down on the roof. Somewhere in the house Ione was arguing with Celer about the fish.

"There is a thing I have not told you," she said. "And I am going to tell you now, because you have earned it and because I cannot ask you to lie to your son for me."

She told him about the trial. She told him about the receipt and the sealed record and the names struck out and the promise she had made to the empress. She told him about the chest of jewellery and the money that was waiting for the boy. And she told him what she had decided.

"I am not going to take him from you," she said. "Not now, not ever. He is your son. You have raised him and fed him and taught him the wheel and the kiln and the price of clay, and I will not undo twenty-four years of that because I happen to have given birth to him." She paused. "But he has a right to know. Not today. Not tomorrow. But some day, when he is ready, and when you are ready, and when I can tell him the whole truth in a room with a door that locks and a cup of wine in his hand. And then he can decide what he wants to do with it."

The potter looked at her for a long time. Then he said, "And if he wants to know you?"

"Then I will be here."

"And if he does not?"

"Then I will be here anyway," Cassia said. "That is what a mother is. It is not a thing you earn or a thing you deserve. It is a thing that happens to you, and then you spend the rest of your life standing in a room waiting for someone to come through the door."

The potter wiped his eyes with the back of his hand and said, "I am sorry. I am not a man who cries in front of ladies."

"You are not in front of a lady," Cassia said. "You are in front of a woman who has been crying in kitchens for twenty-four years." She stood. "Now. Tell me about the kiln. And tell me about the cups. And then go home and tell your son that the lady in Rome was very pleased with his letter, and that she would like the cups, and that she will write to him herself."

---

She wrote to him that night.

It took her four hours and eleven drafts, and the letter she finally sent was three lines long.

*Gaius Vibidius Nestor. I am glad the kiln is good. I would like the cups. Send them to the house on the Esquiline with the green door, and tell the doorkeeper that they are from the potter on the Via Ostiensis, and he will bring them to me. Your name is not a secret. It is a good name and you should be proud of it. A friend.*

She sealed it and gave it to Bathyllus to take to the Via Ostiensis in the morning, and then she sat in the tablinum with the lamps burning and the rain on the roof and her hands in her lap.

Thais came in with a cup of hot wine and set it down.

"You have written to him," she said.

"I have written to him."

"What did you say?"

"Nothing. Everything. I do not know." Cassia picked up the cup. "I said I would like the cups and that he should be proud of his name."

Thais sat down opposite her. "It is a beginning."

"It is not a beginning. It is a letter about cups."

"Everything is a letter about cups," Thais said, "until it is not."

---

The house on the Esquiline began to fill again in November.

It was not the same as before. The great men did not come back; they had found other houses, or they were afraid of the empress's shadow, or they had simply moved on to the next amusement. But the men who came now were different: merchants with money and no name, equestrians who had made their fortunes in the provinces, a young senator from Gaul who wanted to be seen at the right tables, two of the new consuls' clerks, a shipowner from Ostia with a wife who was cleverer than he was.

They came because the house had been through the fire and had come out the other side, and because in Rome a house that has survived a scandal is more interesting than a house that has never had one.

Cassia did not enjoy it. She had enjoyed the old house, in the old days, when she had been building something and every dinner was a brick. Now she sat at the head of the table and watched the new men talk and thought about the fact that she had spent twenty-four years building a wall and that the wall had burned and been rebuilt and that she was still, at forty-two, sitting at the head of a table, listening to men.

The cups came at the end of November, four days before the Kalends of December.

There were six of them, wrapped in straw in a wooden box, and they were red-glossed and thin-walled and good, better than anything in Cassia's kitchen, and on the bottom of each one, pressed into the clay before firing, there was a small mark: a bird.

Bathyllus brought the box into the tablinum and set it on the desk, and Cassia took the cups out one by one and set them in a row and looked at them.

"They are very good," Thais said, from the doorway.

"They are better than good. They are the work of a man who knows what he is doing and is proud of it." Cassia turned one over in her hands. "He has been making cups since he was a boy. He has been making them for fifteen years, in a yard on the Via Ostiensis, and no one in this city knows his name, and he is better at his trade than half the men in the Senate are at theirs."

She put the cup down.

"There is a letter," Bathyllus said.

It was short, and it was in the same careful, awkward hand.

*Domina. These are the cups. I hope they are what you wanted. My father says I am to thank you again for the kiln and for the apprenticeship and for everything, and I am thanking you. I would still like to know your name. Not because I am ungrateful, but because a man likes to know who his friends are. Yours, Gaius Vibidius Nestor.*

Cassia read it twice.

"Are you going to tell him?" Thais said.

"Not yet."

"He has asked you twice."

"I know." Cassia folded the letter and put it in the drawer with the other one, the old one, the one that had been in the cedar box for twenty-four years. "A name in a letter is a strange thing. It arrives without a face. He would read it in his yard and look up and see nothing but the racks and the kiln, and he would have to imagine the woman who wrote it, and he would imagine someone smaller and quieter and kinder than I am." She closed the drawer. "There is a great deal that has to be said before he knows my name, and I am not going to say it in a letter about cups. I am going to say it to his face, in a room, with his father there."

"So what will you write?"

"That the cups are the best I have ever owned. That is true, and it is not the whole truth, and it will do for now."

She wrote the letter that night. It was four lines long: the cups, the price of the clay, a request that he send six of the red-glossed ones, and a line at the end that took her an hour and said only that his father was a good man and that she hoped the kiln was drawing well. She did not sign it with her name. She signed it as she had signed the first: *a friend.*

She sealed it and gave it to Bathyllus, and then she went and stood in the garden in the cold, with the roses cut back to stumps and the fountain turned off, and she thought about a boy in a yard with clay on his arms, and about the fact that he had asked her name twice and she had given him nothing but cups.

The house on the Palatine was sold on the last day of November, and Cassia went to see it empty.

Corvinus took her through it himself: the atrium with the empty cupboards where the masks had been, the tablinum where his father had told him what he was going to do, the garden where he had stood with his wife and watched the light go out of the sky, the room where his mother had died.

"It is a good house," he said. "It was a good house for four hundred years. And now a man who sells bricks is going to knock it down and build a block of flats, and in fifty years no one will remember that it was ever anything else."

"That is what happens to houses," Cassia said.

"Yes." He looked around the empty atrium. "That is what happens to everything."

They went out together into the street, and the door was closed behind them, and a workman was already chalking a mark on the wall.

"Where are you going?" Cassia said.

"To the new house. Aemilia has been there since Tuesday, arranging the furniture and complaining about the drains." He paused. "And then, when the potter agrees to see us, to the Via Ostiensis. To meet my son."

"And what will you say to him?"

"I do not know." Corvinus looked at the street. "I have been a senator for twenty years and I have made a hundred speeches, and I do not know what I will say to a potter of twenty-four who has my eyes and my father's jaw." He looked at her. "What will you say to him?"

"I wrote about cups," Cassia said.

He laughed — a real laugh, the first she had heard from him in months — and took her arm, and they walked down the hill together through the crowds and the noise and the ordinary business of the city.

---

"You are melancholy," Tertulla said, one night in December, when the guests had gone.

"I am tired."

"You are melancholy. I have known you for seven years and I can tell the difference." Tertulla sat down beside her. "What is it?"

"I do not know." Cassia looked at the three Graces in the alcove. "I have everything I wanted when I was thirty. The house, the money, the name, the freedom. And I sit at the table and I listen to a man from Ostia talk about his ships and I think: is this it? Is this what I spent twenty-four years building?"

"What else would you have?"

"I do not know." Cassia turned her cup. "I think I would like to be bored. I have never been bored. Not once, in forty-two years. And I think that a person who has never been bored has not really been alive."

Tertulla looked at her for a long moment. Then she said, "In January I am going to Baiae. There is a house there, with a garden and a view of the sea and a cook who is not as good as Celer and a great many old women who talk about their ailments. Come with me. For a month. We will do nothing. We will sit in the sun and be bored and you will complain about it every day."

"And the house?"

"The house will keep. It kept while you were in the palace and in the Senate and in the prefect's courtyard. It will keep while you are in Baiae." Tertulla took her hand. "You have spent your whole life being necessary. Try being unnecessary for a month. It will not kill you."

Cassia looked at the garden, and the roses, and the three Graces with their painted hands on one another's shoulders.

"Perhaps," she said. "In the spring. When the boy has decided."

"Then in the spring," Tertulla said, and poured her another cup.

They sat up for another hour, talking about the shipping venture and the price of Egyptian wheat and a senator's wife who had run off with a charioteer, and then Tertulla called for her cloak and her litter and went home, and the house settled.

After she had gone, Cassia went out into the garden with a lamp.

It was a clear night and cold enough for a cloak, and the roses were cut back and the fountain was off and the fig tree stood in the corner like a man with his hands in his pockets. She set the lamp on the wall and stood for a while in the dark, and then she went back inside and up the stairs and opened the door of the room at the top.

It had been dusted that morning, because it was dusted every morning. There was a bed with clean linen on it and a stool and a chest and a small window that looked out over the roof of the bakery next door, and on the chest there was a cup: one of the six, red-glossed and thin-walled, with a bird pressed into the bottom of it.

She had put it there in November, on the day the box came, and she had not told anyone. It was not a shrine. It was not a claim. It was a cup on a chest in a room that nobody used, waiting for a man who had asked her name twice and had been given nothing but cups.

"Some day," she said, to the room, to the cup, to nobody at all. "Some day I will tell him."

Then she shut the door and went down to the accounts.

---

## Chapter Twenty-four

There was no speech. She had thought about writing one, and had got as far as the second line, and had put the tablet in the fire, because she had learned that speeches are for the Senate and that a room with a potter in it is not the Senate.

She spent the night before in the kitchen with Thais and Ione and Celer, and they drank wine and did not talk about the visit, and at midnight Celer made a cake that nobody wanted, and at one in the morning Sabina came down in her nightgown and sat on the bench and said, "Is he nice?"

"I do not know," Cassia said. "He makes cups and he laughs at things in doorways and he has a father who calls him in to supper."

"That does not tell you if he is nice."

"No," Cassia said. "It tells you that he is loved. It is not the same thing, but it is a good start."

At two in the morning Thais sent everyone to bed and sat with Cassia in the empty kitchen.

"There is a room at the top of the stairs," she said. "It has been empty for twenty-four years. If he comes, it will not be a room for him. He has a room. He has a house and a father and a trade. He does not need a room."

"I know."

"Then why have you been up there all week, dusting?"

Cassia looked at her hands. "Because it is the only thing I can do," she said. "I cannot give him back the twenty-four years. I cannot make his mother alive again. I cannot undo what was done to him or to me or to his father. I can dust a room and put clean linen on the bed and open the window, and that is all, and it is ridiculous, and I am going to do it anyway."

Thais reached across the table and took her hand.

"It is not ridiculous," she said. "It is the most sensible thing you have done in a year."

It had taken three weeks to arrange, and it had been arranged in the way that everything important in Cassia's life was arranged: by letter, by patience, and by other people's courage.

She had written to Gaius Vibidius three days after the cups came, in the last week of November, and he had not answered. She had written again a week later, and he had come to the house in the rain at the beginning of December and stood in the tablinum with his apron in his hands and said, "I cannot do it. I have tried four times. The words will not come out of my mouth."

"Then I will say them," Cassia said.

"He will hate me."

"He will not hate you. He may hate me. He has a right to." She had looked at him across the desk. "But he is twenty-four years old, and he has a father who has lied to him for four months, and every day that we wait is a day that he spends knowing that something is wrong and being told that it is not. He is not a child. He is a man with a trade and a girl in Ostia and a great deal of patience, and he deserves better than a lie."

The potter had cried then, quietly, into his apron, and Cassia had sat with him until he stopped.

"Bring him to the shop on the day before the Ides," she said. "I will come in the morning. Tell him that a lady is coming who has been his patron, and that she has something to tell him, and that he is to hear her out and not to speak until she is done. And be there. Whatever he says to me, be there, because he will need to look at a face he trusts."

The potter had nodded and gone out into the rain.

The Via Ostiensis was loud in the morning. Carts going down to the river and carts coming up from it, porters with sacks on their shoulders, a man with a wheelbarrow full of fish, two women arguing over a basket of onions, a dog asleep in the middle of the road and refusing to move. The potter's shop stood on the corner of a lane with a fig tree beside the door and a yard behind it where the kiln was, and the smoke from the kiln went up into the grey December air and smelled of hot clay and wood ash.

Cassia came on foot, with Bathyllus behind her and Corvinus beside her, and they stopped at the corner of the lane and looked at the shop.

"I do not know if I can do this," Corvinus said.

"You can."

"I have spoken in the Senate a hundred times. I have stood in front of the emperor. I have been accused of murdering my father and I did not shake." He looked at the door. "And I am standing in a street on the Via Ostiensis and my hands are shaking."

"That is because the Senate did not matter," Cassia said. "This does."

Gaius Vibidius came out of the shop and saw them and wiped his hands on his apron and came to meet them.

"He is in the yard," he said. "I have told him that there is a lady who has been his patron, and that she has something to tell him, and that it is important and that he is to listen. He thinks it is about money." The potter's mouth twisted. "He asked me if he was to be apprenticed somewhere else. He asked me if I was selling the shop."

"Did you tell him anything else?"

"No. I said that I would be there, and that his mother would have wanted him to hear it, and that was all." He looked at Corvinus. "You are the senator."

"I am."

"You look like him." The potter said it without warmth. "He has your jaw. I have been looking at it for twenty-four years and I did not know what it was until this summer."

Corvinus said nothing. The potter turned and led them through the shop and out into the yard.

---

The yard was small and busy: racks of drying pots, a table with tools, a boy of twelve turning a wheel in the corner, a shed with the kiln in it, and a great heap of clay under a wet cloth. The young man was standing at the table with a cup in his hand, turning it over, looking at the rim.

He looked up when they came in.

He was tall — taller than Corvinus, taller than Cassia had remembered — and he had his sleeves rolled up over his forearms and his hands and his arms were grey with clay to the elbow, and his hair was the colour of dark honey and needed cutting. He had the family jaw and the family nose and his father's eyes, and he was, Cassia thought, the most beautiful thing she had ever seen.

"Domina," he said, and put the cup down. "My father says you have something to tell me."

"Sit down," Cassia said.

"I would rather stand."

"Then stand." She looked at him. "My name is Antonia Cassia. I keep a house on the Esquiline. I am a freedwoman and I have been free for sixteen years, and before that I was a slave, and before that I was a girl in a kitchen at a villa in Tibur." She paused. "And twenty-four years ago, in the autumn, I gave birth to a son in a cell at the back of that villa, and I was told that he had died, and I was sold to a house in Puteoli three days later."

The young man looked at her. His face did not change.

"I do not understand," he said.

"You are the son," Cassia said. "Your name is Gaius Vibidius Nestor, and you were born on the twenty-eighth of September in the year of the consulship of Domitian and Silanus, and you were sold to Gaius Vibidius, potter, of the Via Ostiensis, for four hundred sesterces, and you have been raised by him as his own." She paused. "And I have spent twenty-four years believing you were dead."

The yard was very quiet. The boy at the wheel had stopped turning. The kiln hissed. The young man looked at her, and then at his father, and then at Corvinus, and his face was doing something complicated: not disbelief, not grief, but the slow, terrible work of a mind rearranging everything it had ever known.

"My mother," he said. "My mother is dead. She died when I was small."

"Your mother is the woman who raised you, and she is dead, and I am sorry for it," Cassia said. "I am not her. I am the woman who gave birth to you. There is a difference, and I do not know yet what it is worth."

"Who are you?" He turned to Corvinus. "Who is he?"

Corvinus stepped forward. "My name is Lucius Sulpicius Corvinus. I am a senator. I was twenty years old in the summer of eighty-eight, and I was in love with the girl in the kitchen, and I was told that she had died in childbirth and that the child had died with her." His voice was steady. "I did not know you existed until this year. I did not know that she was alive. I have spent twenty-four years believing that I had killed you both."

The young man looked at him for a long time. Then he said, "You are my father."

"Yes."

"And you"—he turned to Cassia—"are my mother."

"Yes."

"And my father"—he turned to Gaius Vibidius—"is my father."

"Yes," the potter said. "I bought you, and I raised you, and I have loved you every day since I carried you through that door in a basket, and I did not know that you had a mother who was alive, and if I had known I would have told you." His voice broke. "I would have told you. I want you to believe that."

The young man looked at the three of them standing in the yard.

"I need to sit down," he said.

---

They sat in the back room of the shop, on stools, with the door open to the yard and the smell of clay coming in. The potter brought a jug of water and a cup for each of them, and they sat in a row like people at a funeral.

Cassia told him everything. She did not spare herself and she did not spare Corvinus and she did not spare the old woman. She told him about the vineyard and the store-room and the book of poems. She told him about the night and the cup and the cry. She told him about Puteoli, and the green door, and Thais, and the four years. She told him about the fire and the trial and the empress and the sealed record. She told him about the chest of jewellery and the money that was waiting for him and the letter the old woman had written with a shaking hand.

It took an hour.

The young man listened without interrupting. Twice he got up and walked to the door and stood looking out at the yard, and came back and sat down. Once he put his face in his hands and sat like that for a long time.

"Tell me about her," he said, when Cassia had finished the first part. "My mother. The one who raised me. What did she look like?"

Cassia looked at Gaius Vibidius. The potter's face was working.

"She was small and brown and she had a laugh that carried," the potter said. "She could not have children of her own and she prayed at the temple of Juno Lucina for six years, and when I brought you home in a basket she sat down on the floor of the kitchen and cried for an hour, and then she got up and made you a bed in a drawer." He wiped his face. "She died when you were seven. She had a fever and it took her in three days. She called for you at the end. She made me promise to keep you and raise you and never tell you that you were bought, because she was afraid that if you knew, you would think that she had loved you less."

The young man's face was wet.

"I do not remember her," he said. "I remember a woman singing. I remember a hand on my forehead."

"That is her," the potter said. "That is all any of us get."

Cassia sat very still. She had spent twenty-four years imagining this room, and in every version of it she had been the mother, and the boy had been hers, and the woman who had raised him had been a shadow. She had not imagined this: a boy with two mothers and a father who had bought him, and all of them sitting in a room with a jug of water, and nothing to be done about any of it.

"Tell me about the girl," Cassia said, when the worst of it was out. "Your father says there is a girl in Ostia."

The young man looked up, startled, and then his face changed in a way that Cassia would remember for the rest of her life: the face of a man who has been braced for blows and is offered a chair.

"Her name is Vibia," he said. "She is a potter's daughter too. Her father has a yard by the river and he is getting old, and she runs the accounts and the slaves and the carting, and she shouts at the porters." He stopped. "She is not beautiful. She is better than beautiful. She is the sort of woman who walks into a yard and everyone stops arguing."

"She will need to be," Cassia said.

"She does not know anything about any of this."

"Then you will have to tell her. Not tonight. But you will have to tell her, because a secret like this one does not keep, and because if you marry her and she finds out from someone else, she will never forgive you, and she will be right." Cassia paused. "And when you tell her, tell her all of it. Not the version that makes me look well. The version that is true."

The young man looked at her for a moment. Then he said, "You are very like my father. He says the same thing. He says, 'Tell it all, and let them decide.'"

"Then he is a wise man, and you should listen to him."

He was quiet for a while. The kiln hissed, and somewhere beyond the wall a cart went by, and the boy at the wheel began to sing under his breath.

"Did you look for me?" he said.

"Yes," Cassia said. "Every day. For twenty-four years. I did not know your name or where you were, and I looked for you in every crowd, in every street, in every face. I have a room at the top of my house that I have kept empty for twenty-four years because I could not bear to give it to anyone else." She paused. "And I did not look hard enough. I should have gone to every potter in Rome. I should have walked the Via Ostiensis twenty years ago. I did not, because I was afraid, and because I had built a house and a business and a wall, and because I told myself that the child was dead and that looking for him was a kind of madness." She looked at him. "I was wrong. I have been wrong for twenty-four years, and I am sorry."

He looked at her for a long time.

"Why did you give me up?" he said.

"I did not give you up. You were taken from me while I was asleep. I woke up and the room was clean and the midwife told me that you had never breathed, and I was on a cart to Puteoli three days later." Cassia's voice was steady. "I have spent twenty-four years believing that you were dead. I did not give you up. I was robbed of you. There is a difference, and I need you to understand it, because if you go away from this room thinking that I chose to leave you, then the woman who sold you will have won."

The young man put his face in his hands and sat like that for a long time.

"I am angry," he said, at last. "I do not know at who. At her, I suppose. At the old woman. At the banker and the senator and the men with the farm. At you, a little. At my father, a little. At the whole of it." He looked up. "Is that allowed?"

"It is allowed," Cassia said. "It is the most sensible thing you have said all morning."

Then he said, "What do you want from me?"

"Nothing," Cassia said.

"Everyone wants something."

"Yes," she said. "Everyone does. But I am not going to ask you for anything today. I am going to tell you that I am here, and that I have been here for six months, and that I will be here in the spring, and that if you want to know me, you know where I live. And if you do not want to know me, I will understand, and I will not come to this shop again, and once a year your father will send me a message to tell me that you are alive, and that will be enough."

The young man looked at her. His eyes were his father's eyes, and they were wet.

"You have been here for six months," he said.

"Yes."

"You paid for the kiln."

"Yes."

"You wrote to me about the cups."

"Yes."

"And you did not tell me your name."

"No."

"Why?"

"Because I did not want to give you a name that you had not asked for," Cassia said. "Because a person who is told that his whole life has been a lie does not need a stranger at the door with a story. He needs time." She paused. "And because I was afraid."

The young man laughed — a short, broken, entirely unexpected laugh. "You were afraid?"

"I have been afraid every day for twenty-four years," Cassia said. "I was afraid in the cell. I was afraid in Puteoli. I was afraid in the Senate. And I was afraid of you, because you are the only person in the world whose opinion of me matters, and because I did not know whether you would hate me."

He looked at her for a long moment. Then he said, "I do not hate you."

"You may, in a week. It is a great deal to take in."

"I know." He stood up. "I need to work. I need to put my hands in the clay and make something, and think about it, and I cannot do that with three people looking at me." He looked at each of them. "I am not going to say anything today. I am not going to decide anything today. I am going to go out into that yard and make cups, because that is what I do when I do not know what to do."

"That is a very good way of dealing with it," Cassia said.

"It is the only way I know." He stopped at the door. "Domina. Cassia. If I come to the house on the Esquiline. If I want to know you. What do I call you?"

Cassia looked at him. In the doorway, with the light behind him and the clay on his arms and his father's jaw and his grandmother's eyes, he looked like the answer to a question she had been asking for twenty-four years.

"You call me whatever you like," she said. "I have been called a great many things. I have been called a whore and a freedwoman and a patron and a friend. I have never once been called a mother." She paused. "You may call me Cassia, if you like. That is my name. It is the only thing I have that no one gave me."

He nodded. Then he went out into the yard and picked up a lump of clay and sat down at the wheel, and the boy who had been turning it moved aside, and the wheel began to spin.

---

They came out into the shop, and Gaius Vibidius followed them to the door.

"Domina," he said. "A word."

Corvinus went out into the lane. The potter stood in the doorway of his own shop with his apron in his hands and his face grey.

"I have had him for twenty-four years," he said. "I taught him to walk and to read and to throw a pot. I sat up with him when he was sick and I beat him when he was lazy and I have been prouder of him than of anything I have ever made with my hands." He stopped. "And now there is a lady in Rome and a senator on the Palatine and a chest of jewellery and a story about a villa at Tibur, and I am a potter on the Via Ostiensis with a bad chest and a workshop that is mortgaged to my brother."

"You are his father," Cassia said.

"I know that. I am telling you that knowing it does not help." He wiped his face with his apron. "I am afraid that he will go to your house and see the silver and the paintings and the three Graces and the cook and the garden, and he will think: this is what I could have had. And he will look at me and see a man with clay under his nails."

Cassia looked at him. Then she said, "Do you know what he said when I asked him what he does when he does not know what to do?"

"No."

"He said, 'I go out into the yard and make cups, because that is what I do.'" She paused. "That is not a thing he learned from a senator. That is not a thing he learned from a woman with a garden and a cook. That is a thing he learned from a man who taught him to throw a pot." She put her hand on his arm. "You have given him the only thing that cannot be taken away from him. Not the shop. Not the trade. The knowledge that when the world falls apart, there is something he can do with his hands. I could not have given him that. I could have given him a name and a fortune and a great deal of trouble, and you gave him a craft. Do not tell me that you are less than me. You are the reason he is the man he is, and if he goes to my house and sees the silver, he will come home to yours and tell you about it."

The potter looked at her for a long moment. Then he nodded, once, and went back into the shop.

Cassia and Corvinus stood in the lane outside the shop.

"Well," he said.

"Well."

"He did not throw us out."

"He did not." Cassia looked at the door. "He is a good man. He has a trade and a temper and a father who loves him, and he is going to go out into that yard and make cups, and in a week or a month or a year he will come to the house on the Esquiline and we will have to explain it all over again." She paused. "And I am going to make a mess of it."

"Probably."

"You are supposed to tell me that I will not."

"I have known you for twenty-four years," Corvinus said. "You will make a mess of it, and he will forgive you, and you will make a mess of it again. That is what families are." He looked at her. "I should know. I have been making a mess of mine for twenty years."

They walked back up the Via Ostiensis in the cold, and at the gate of the city Cassia stopped and looked back at the lanes and the workshops and the smoke.

"I am not going to Baiae," she said.

"What?"

"Tertulla wants me to go to Baiae in the spring. To be bored. To sit in the sun." Cassia turned. "I am not going. I am going to stay in Rome, and I am going to be here when he comes, and if he does not come, I am going to be here anyway."

Corvinus looked at her. Then he took her arm, and they went through the gate together into the city.

That evening Corvinus came to the house, and they sat in the tablinum with the lamp between them and did not say anything for a while.

"He asked me what I did," Corvinus said. "In the yard, when you were talking to his father. He asked me what a senator does all day, and I said that a senator listens to other men talk and then talks himself, and he looked at me as though I had told him that I spent my days counting clouds." He laughed. "He asked me if I had ever made anything with my hands. I said that I had made speeches. He said that was not the same thing, and he was right."

"He is twenty-four," Cassia said. "At twenty-four everyone is certain of everything."

"At forty-five I am certain of nothing." He turned the cup. "I have lost the Asian command and the augurate and the house on the Palatine and the name that my father spent his life protecting. My mother is dead and my father is a fraud and my son is a potter who thinks I am useless. And I am sitting in a room with a woman I have loved since I was twenty, and I am happier than I have been in twenty years." He looked up. "What does that make me?"

"A man who has finally got what he wanted," Cassia said.

"I did not want any of this."

"No," she said. "That is why it is funny."

Two days later, a boy came to the green door on the Esquiline with a parcel wrapped in cloth.

There was no letter. Inside the cloth there was a cup — the same red-glossed ware, thin-walled, with the bird on the bottom — and round the rim, scratched into the clay before firing, in a rough and unpractised hand, were four words.

*For the house. From Gaius.*

Cassia stood in the kitchen with the cup in her hands and looked at it for a long time. Then she set it on the shelf above the hearth, next to the cups that had cost her a fortune, and she said, "Nobody is to use this cup. Nobody is to wash it. It is to stay on that shelf until I die, and then it is to be given to my son."

Thais looked at the cup. "He did not call you mother."

"No."

"He did not call you Cassia either."

"No."

"He called himself Gaius."

"Yes," Cassia said. "He called himself by his own name. He did not call himself my son and he did not call himself yours. He called himself Gaius, and he wrote *from* Gaius, as though the house were his to send things from." She turned the cup over. "That is more than I had this morning."

She went out into the garden. The roses were black stumps and the fountain was off and the sky was the colour of pewter, and she stood in the cold with her hands in her sleeves and looked at the fig tree that she had planted there six years before for no reason she had ever been able to explain to anyone.

Young Sulpicia came on the morning of the Saturnalia, and asked to meet her brother.

She came to the house with her mother, and she sat in the tablinum and said, "He is my brother. I have a right to meet him."

"You have a right to ask," Cassia said. "He has a right to refuse."

"Then ask him."

"I will ask him. But you will have to be patient, and you will have to understand that he has had a great deal to take in in a very short time, and that he does not owe you anything." Cassia paused. "And Sulpicia. If he agrees to meet you, you will not call him your brother. Not the first time. You will call him Gaius, and you will ask him about his work, and you will not tell him about the masks or the house on the Palatine or the trial. He knows about all of it. He does not need to hear it from a girl of sixteen who has had a comfortable life and a good marriage and a grandmother who loved her."

Sulpicia's chin came up, and then went down again.

"That is fair," she said.

"Yes."

"I do not like it."

"No," Cassia said. "But you will do it, because you are clever, and because you have discovered this year that being clever is not the same as being right, and because you want to meet him more than you want to be right."

Sulpicia looked at her. Then she said, "You are a very difficult woman."

"So I am told."

When they had gone she went and sat with Thais and Ione, who were arguing about the seating for that evening, and took the list out of Thais's hand and began to write.

---

## Chapter Twenty-five

The Saturnalia began on the sixteenth day before the Kalends of January, and by the morning of the first day the house on the Esquiline smelled of pine and baking and hot wine and the particular smell of a household that has decided to be happy.

The whole house had been turned upside down, which was the point of the festival. The slaves were served at table by their masters; the children were allowed to give orders and were obeyed with exaggerated solemnity; the cooks were praised and the doorkeepers were given the best seats and the women wore whatever they liked. There were garlands of pine and ivy on every door, and lamps in every window, and a great many small gifts wrapped in cloth and straw and piled on a table in the atrium, and a clay figure of a little man — a sigillum, for the season — on the hearth, which Celer had made and which was, Cassia thought, the ugliest thing she had ever seen.

"You are not to say that in front of him," Thais said. "He is very proud of it."

"It has no face."

"It has a face. It has a face in the style of the old sigilla. It is a Saturnalian figure and it is not supposed to have a face."

"It has one eye and a nose and no mouth."

"That is how you know it is Saturnalian," Thais said, and went to see about the wine.

Cassia stood in the atrium and watched the household. Sabina was thirteen now, and she had been given the keys to the linen for the day and was taking her authority extremely seriously. Chrysis and Senno were arranging the presents, and Senno was arguing with her about the order in which they should be given, in a mixture of Latin and his own language that only the two of them understood. Celer was in the kitchen, shouting. Ione was at the accounts, because Ione had discovered that she liked accounts and that a woman who controls the accounts controls the house. Bathyllus was at the door in a new cloak, greeting the guests, because at the Saturnalia the doorkeeper was the master of ceremonies and Bathyllus had taken to the role with a gravity that was entirely unironic.

She had not always loved the Saturnalia.

When she was a girl in the kitchen at Tibur, the festival had been a day of extra work: the good dishes brought out, the wine mulled, the household fed and the slaves given a cup of the cheap wine and a piece of meat and told to be merry. When she was in Puteoli, the festival had been the busiest night of the year, and she had learned to smile through it and to count the hours until the green door closed. When she had bought the house, she had made the festival her own: the first year, there had been four of them at the table — Cassia, Thais, Hermeros, and a cook who had been paid too much and had left in the spring. Now there were thirty.

She stood in the atrium and watched them arrive and thought about the fact that she had spent twenty-four years making this day into something, and that the something had turned out to be a house full of people who were not obliged to be there.

There was a letter on the table by the door, brought by a palace slave at dawn, and she had not opened it. She opened it now.

It was from the empress's steward, in a careful, correct, wholly impersonal hand. It said that the emperor was well and that the preparations for the Eastern expedition were proceeding. It said that the clerk Primus had died in Campania in November, of a disease of the liver, and that his estate had been confiscated and his name struck from the rolls. And it said that the empress had heard how the household of Antonia Cassia had conducted itself in the summer, and that she had been minded, in the autumn, to write and say so, and had not, because a letter from the palace is a gift with a price on it, and she did not wish to put a price on that one.

And it said: *The empress has read the record of the trial. She thinks the boy has his mother's sense and his father's jaw, and she wishes him a good life. She asks me also to say that a house which frees its people is a house that will be spoken of for a hundred years, and that she is glad to hear that yours has done it.*

Cassia folded the letter and put it in her sleeve.

"Who is it from?" Thais said.

"A woman I have met twice," Cassia said. "She sends her regards, and a suggestion, and she is too well bred to call it one."

The first guests arrived at the fourth hour. Tertulla came with a basket of figs and a new book of poems and a bottle of wine from her own estate. Aemilia came with Corvinus and with young Sulpicia and Aulus, who had grown a beard and had become, since the summer, slightly less of a fool. Publius came late, with a girl on his arm whom nobody had met and whom he introduced as Calpurnia, and who turned out to be the girl from the Subura, the sailor's promised bride, who had not gone to Ostia after all.

Cassia looked at Publius. Publius looked back at her, and his chin came up, and his arm tightened around the girl's waist.

"Domina," he said. "This is Calpurnia. She is my wife."

There was a silence in the atrium.

"Since when?" Cassia said.

"Since the Kalends of November. We were married quietly. My father knows. My mother does not, and I would be grateful if no one told her until after the wine." He swallowed. "I paid the sailor. It took everything I had and a loan from my grandmother's jewellery, and I have not regretted it for a moment."

Cassia looked at the girl. She was perhaps twenty, with a plain, honest, frightened face and a good cloak and her hand in Publius's, and she was looking at the three Graces on the wall as though she expected them to come down and eat her.

"Calpurnia," Cassia said. "Come here."

The girl came. Cassia took her hands.

"Are you happy?" she said.

"Yes, domina."

"Does he make you laugh?"

"He makes me laugh a great deal. He is very foolish."

"Good," Cassia said. "A husband who cannot make you laugh is a husband who will make you cry." She looked at Publius. "You have done a stupid, brave, expensive thing, and I am proud of you, and your father is going to be impossible about it later, because he will be relieved and he does not know how to be relieved without giving a speech."

Publius laughed. Calpurnia looked at Cassia as though she had been told that the monster under the bed was actually a friend of the family.

Aemilia had not moved from her couch.

"Mother," Publius said.

"Calpurnia," Aemilia said, and her voice was perfectly level. "Come here."

The girl went. Aemilia looked at her for a long moment — the plain face, the good cloak, the hands that had been in a poor house and were not ashamed of it — and then she said, "You paid the sailor."

"Publius paid the sailor."

"With my mother-in-law's jewellery."

"With a loan," Publius said, from across the room, "which I am repaying at a rate that would make a Carthaginian blush."

Aemilia closed her eyes for a moment.

"Then you had better be good to her," she said, "because she is the only thing in this family that has not been bought and sold." She held out her hand to Calpurnia. "Sit by me. I have a great many questions, and I intend to ask all of them, and then I intend to be very kind to you for the rest of my life, because I have discovered that being kind is cheaper than being right."

Corvinus, at the head of the table, put his face in his hand.

"You see," he said to Cassia, "this is what my family is. We arrive at a Saturnalia dinner with a secret wife and a scandal and no money, and my wife decides to be gracious about it, and now I have to make a speech."

"You do not have to make a speech."

"I am going to make a speech."

"Please do not make a speech."

He made the speech. It was short and it was very bad, and it ended with the words "and I love all of you, even the ones I have not met," and the whole room drank to it, and Calpurnia cried, and Publius looked at his father with an expression that Cassia had not seen on his face before and would see on it many times afterward.

---

Aemilia found her in the garden before dinner, standing by the dry fountain with a cup of wine she had not drunk.

"I have sold the jewellery," Cassia said. "The emeralds went to a woman in the Subura for a sum that would have bought a small house. The rest went to a dealer on the Via Sacra. The money is in the strongbox, and it will go to the boy in the spring, when he has decided what he wants to do with the shop."

"He will be rich," Aemilia said.

"He will be comfortable. There is a difference." Cassia turned. "I have been thinking about your mother-in-law."

"So have I. I think about her every day. I think about her sitting in that chair and giving orders and never once apologising for anything." Aemilia's mouth moved. "And I think about the fact that she left the jewellery to you and not to my daughter, and that I have been angry about it for six months, and that I am not angry any more."

"What changed?"

"You did. You sold it and gave the money to the boy, and you did not keep a single piece, and you did not make a speech about it." Aemilia looked at her. "I would have kept the emeralds. I would have told myself that I was keeping them for my daughter, and I would have kept them, and I would have been wrong." She paused. "You are not a good woman, Cassia. But you are a better one than I am, and I have decided to stop pretending otherwise."

Cassia laughed. "That is the nicest thing anyone has said to me all year."

"It is not a compliment."

"No," Cassia said. "It is better."

The dinner was in the garden room, because the painted dining room was still being repaired, and the garden room had been hung with so many lamps and garlands that it looked like a cave in a fairy tale. There were thirty people at three tables, which was too many, and the food was too much, and the wine was excellent, and at some point in the second course Bathyllus stood up and made a speech about the dignity of doorkeepers that reduced the whole table to tears of laughter.

Cassia sat at the head of the middle table with Corvinus on one side and Tertulla on the other, and she looked down the room at the faces: Aemilia, who had become, over the year, something between a friend and an ally and a sparring partner; young Sulpicia, who was arguing with her husband about a gladiator; Publius and Calpurnia, who were feeding each other figs in a way that would have been nauseating if it had not been so obviously sincere; Sabina, who had been allowed a glass of wine and was behaving as though she had been given the keys to the city; Chrysis and Senno, who were sitting together and laughing at something Celer had said; Ione, who was wearing a dress the colour of a peacock and looking, for the first time in years, entirely at home.

"It is a good house," Corvinus said, quietly.

"It is a good house," Cassia said.

"Last year at this time I was in my mother's atrium, listening to her complain about the arrangements, and you were here, eating with your household, and neither of us knew that the other was alive." He turned his cup. "It has been the worst year of my life."

"And?"

"And the best. Both. I do not know how that is possible."

"It is possible," Cassia said, "because the worst things and the best things are usually the same things, seen from different ends."

At the end of the meal, when the tables had been cleared and the lamps were burning low, Cassia stood up and said, "I have a gift for the household. It is not the sort of gift that is usually given at the Saturnalia, and I am going to give it now because I am the mistress of this house and because I am too old to wait for the last day."

She took a roll of papers from her sleeve.

"These are manumission papers. There are nine of them. Thais and Hermeros and Bathyllus are already free and have been for years. But Sabina, Chrysis, Senno, Celer, and the five others whose names are on these papers are free as of today, by my hand, before witnesses, with the money for their freedom paid out of my own account and a sum for each of them to start a new life." She paused. "You may stay in this house, all of you, as free people, with wages and a contract and the right to leave. Or you may go, with my blessing and my recommendation and my friendship. There is no condition. There is no debt. There is nothing to be paid back."

The room was silent.

Sabina was the first to move. She stood up, white-faced, and said, "Domina. Are we to be turned out?"

"No," Cassia said. "You are to be free. It is not the same thing."

"But where will I go?"

"Wherever you like. You are thirteen years old and you can read and write and do sums and you have a purse of your own and you will always have a room in this house." Cassia's voice was not quite steady. "You can stay here until you are grown and then you can go and be a bookkeeper or a merchant or the wife of a ship's captain or anything you like, and no one will ever be able to sell you, and no one will ever be able to put you on a rope, and you will never have to stand in a market with a placard around your neck."

Sabina stared at her. Then she burst into tears and ran around the table and threw her arms around Cassia, and then the whole room was on its feet, and Celer was crying openly, and Chrysis had her hand over her mouth, and Senno was standing very still with a look on his face that Cassia would remember for the rest of her life.

Bathyllus did not move from the door. He stood with his hands folded in front of him, in his new cloak, with the gravity of a man who has been given the keys to the city.

"Bathyllus," Cassia said.

"Domina."

"You have been free for eleven years. Why are you still standing at my door?"

"Because it is my door," Bathyllus said. "I have been offered better doors. There was a man in Ostia who wanted me to run his warehouse, and a man on the Caelian who offered me twice the money and a house of my own, and I turned them both down and did not tell you, because you would have made a speech about it." He looked at the room, at the crying and the laughing and the papers in people's hands. "A freedman who works for wages at a door he chose is not the same as a slave who stands at one he was given. I wanted to choose it. I have chosen it. I will go on choosing it until I am too old to open the door."

Cassia looked at him. Then she said, "You are the best man in this house."

"I am the doorkeeper," Bathyllus said. "It is nearly the same thing."

Thais came and stood beside her and said, quietly, "You did not tell me."

"I did not want you to talk me out of it."

"I would not have talked you out of it." Thais looked at the room. "You have just given away your entire household."

"No," Cassia said. "I have just hired a household. It is more expensive. It is also better."

---

The boy came at the end of the evening, when the lamps were low and the guests were going and the house was full of the warm, tired, satisfied noise of a feast that has gone well.

He came to the garden door with his father, and he was wearing a clean tunic and his hair was wet from being combed, and he stood in the doorway of the garden room with a parcel in his hands and looked at the thirty people and the lamps and the three Graces on the wall.

"Gaius," Cassia said.

"Domina." He held out the parcel. "I made this. For the Saturnalia. It is not much."

She unwrapped it. It was a small clay lamp, well made, with a handle and a spout and a pattern of vine leaves around the rim, and on the bottom, pressed into the clay, was the bird.

"It is beautiful," she said.

"It is a lamp," he said. "It is the thing I make most of. Everyone needs a lamp." He looked around the room again. "My father said I should come. He said that a man who is invited to a house at the Saturnalia and does not go is a man who is afraid."

"Are you afraid?"

"Yes." He looked at her. "But I came."

Cassia looked at him. Then she set the lamp down on the table and said, "Come and meet the household. There are a great many of them and they have all been asking about you, and they will all want to tell you their opinions, and you do not have to listen to any of them."

She took him into the room. Thais was the first to come forward, and then Celer, and then Sabina, who had been crying and had washed her face and was now very composed, and then Ione, who looked at him for a long moment and said, "You have your mother's eyes," and then young Sulpicia, who had been waiting all evening and who came forward and said, "I am Sulpicia. I am your sister. I have been told not to say that, but I am going to say it anyway."

He looked at her. Then he laughed — the same laugh, the young man's laugh, surprised out of him — and said, "I have never had a sister."

"Now you have two," young Sulpicia said. "I am the difficult one."

The room filled up around him. Somebody gave him a cup of wine. Somebody asked him about the kiln. Somebody else asked him about the price of clay, and he began, hesitantly at first and then with more confidence, to explain the difference between the clay from the Tiber and the clay from the hills, and the whole room listened as though he were describing the campaigns of Caesar.

Cassia stood at the edge of it and watched.

"Look at him," Corvinus said, at her shoulder.

"I am looking."

"He is talking about clay to a room full of senators and their wives."

"Yes."

"And they are listening."

"Yes," Cassia said. "That is because he is the only person in this room who knows what he is talking about."

---
